Preface

Lao She (1899～1966), born Shu Qingchun, courtesy name Sheyu, was a native of Beijing. He lost his father at a young age and grew up in poverty, deeply influenced by the life, values, and aesthetic sensibilities of the lower-class urban residents of old Beijing. In 1924 he went to England to teach and began his literary career, drawing attention for his humorous style. After returning to China in 1930, he wrote works such as Camel Xiangzi and Divorce, establishing himself as the foremost writer of urban commoner literature in the history of modern Chinese literature. During the War of Resistance, he led the All-China Federation of Writers and Artists in Resistance to the Enemy and wrote the million-word novel Four Generations Under One Roof. After the founding of New China, he produced powerful stage works such as Dragon Beard Ditch and Teahouse, and was honored with the title "People's Artist."

The novel tells the tragic story of a rickshaw puller named Xiangzi in old Beijing.

Xiangzi drifted from the countryside to the city and became a rickshaw puller. Young and strong, with no bad habits, he was full of confidence about the future. His one and only wish was to buy his own rickshaw and become an "independent" and "free" puller. But three rounds of setbacks in trying to buy a rickshaw shattered his life's dream and plunged him into despair.

In his personal life, Xiangzi also lost control of his own fate. Huniu, the daughter of Liu Siye, the owner of the rickshaw depot, took a liking to Xiangzi. Although Xiangzi found her old and ugly and disliked her, he still couldn't resist her seduction and trickery, and ended up marrying her. After the wedding, Xiangzi used Huniu's private savings to buy a used rickshaw from Er Qiangzi. But before long, Huniu died in a difficult childbirth, and Xiangzi was forced to sell the rickshaw to pay for her burial. This loss of home and family left Xiangzi devastated. After Huniu's death, the devoted care of Xiaofuzi made him genuinely fall in love with this unfortunate girl. It seemed as though he had found the courage to go on living, but fate still loved to toy with him. Driven by hardship, Xiaofuzi was forced into prostitution, and before Xiangzi could "make something of himself," she hanged herself. This turned Xiangzi's last glimmer of hope into nothing.

From then on, Xiangzi completely broke down and fell into depravity. He drank, gambled, visited prostitutes, cheated and schemed, and even sold out others' lives, until he finally sank into being a social dregs, a walking corpse, waiting quietly for death.

Camel Xiangzi is written in Beijing vernacular and dialect, with vivid depictions of old Beijing's local customs and culture. It reads with a warm, natural, and rhythmic quality, and stands as a classic work of modern vernacular Chinese fiction.

1

What we are about to tell is the story of Xiangzi, not of a camel. "Camel" is simply a nickname for Xiangzi. So let us start with Xiangzi himself. As for the connection between "Camel" and Xiangzi, we'll mention it in passing as we go along, and leave it at that.

Among Beijing's rickshaw pullers there were many types: the young and strong, with nimble legs and feet, preferred to rent good-looking rickshaws and pull "full days," free to head out or call it quits whenever they liked. Once out on the road, they would park at a regular stand[1] or outside a wealthy residence and wait for passengers who wanted a fast ride. On a good day, they might earn a dollar or two in one go; on a bad day, they might waste the whole day without even covering the rental fee — but they didn't much care. The hopes of this crowd generally came down to two things: either to land a private hire job, or to buy their own rickshaw. Once they had their own rickshaw, it didn't matter much whether they took monthly fares or individual passengers — the rickshaw was theirs, after all.

Those a few years older than this group, or those whose physical condition made them a bit slower, or those with family obligations who couldn't afford to waste a whole day, mostly pulled rickshaws that were about eighty percent new. Both the person and the vehicle were reasonably presentable, so they could still maintain a certain dignity when quoting fares. These pullers might work "full days" or "half days." In the latter case, since they still had decent energy, they would always "pull evenings"[2] regardless of the season. At night, of course, more alertness and skill were required, but naturally more money could be earned as well.

Those over forty or under twenty were unlikely to find a place in either of the first two groups. Their rickshaws were worn out, and they didn't dare "pull evenings," so they could only head out early, hoping to keep going from dawn until three or four in the afternoon, earning enough to cover the rental fee and their own food. Their rickshaws were old and slow, so they had to cover more ground and charge less. Hauling goods to the melon market, the fruit market, the vegetable market — that was their lot. The pay was low, but at least they didn't need to run fast.

Among those under twenty — some started as young as twelve or thirteen — very few managed to become presentable pullers after the age of twenty, because injuries sustained in childhood made it hard to grow up strong and healthy. They might pull rickshaws their whole lives without ever making a name for themselves even in that trade. As for those over forty, some had already been pulling for eight or ten years; the wear on their muscles had made them content to fall behind, and they gradually came to understand that sooner or later they would die face-down on the road. Their pulling posture, their quick thinking when haggling over fares, their knowledge of shortcuts and detours — all of this was enough to remind them of their past glory, and they would flare their nostrils at the younger generation. Yet this bit of glory did nothing to lighten the darkness ahead, and they themselves, while wiping away sweat, would often sigh softly. Still, compared to another group of pullers also in their forties, they seemed not to have hit rock bottom quite yet. This other group had never imagined they would ever have anything to do with a rickshaw, and only took up the handles when the line between life and death had grown blurry. Dismissed policemen or school janitors, small vendors who had spent their last penny, or unemployed craftsmen — when there was nothing left to sell and nothing left to pawn, they gritted their teeth, held back their tears, and stepped onto this road of no return. These men had already sold away the most vigorous years of their lives, and now they were dripping the blood and sweat wrung from steamed buns onto the road. No strength, no experience, no friends — even among their fellow pullers they couldn't catch a break. They pulled the most broken-down rickshaws, with inner tubes that went flat who knows how many times a day. While still carrying a passenger they had to beg for the person's patience, even though fifteen copper coins was already considered a good deal.

Beyond this, differences in background and knowledge set another segment of pullers apart into their own category. Those born in Xiyuan or Haidian naturally found it more convenient to run routes to the Western Hills, Yenching, or Tsinghua. Similarly, those outside Andingmen ran to Qinghe and Beiyuan; those outside Yongdingmen ran to Nanyuan… These were the long-haul pullers, who didn't want to take short fares, because each trip was a full trip, and they looked down on scraping together three or five copper coins at a time. Yet even they couldn't match the staying power of the rickshaw pullers in the Legation Quarter. Those who specialized in foreign clientele prided themselves on pulling in one stretch from the Legation Quarter all the way to Yuquan Mountain, the Summer Palace, or the Western Hills. Endurance aside, the main reason ordinary pullers couldn't compete for this business was that these men who "ate foreign rice" had a certain knowledge that set them apart from everyone else: they could speak foreign languages. British soldiers, French soldiers, the Summer Palace, the Lama Temple, the "Eight Great Hutongs" — they knew them all. They had their own set of foreign phrases, which they kept to themselves and never taught others. Their running style was distinctive too: a steady four-six gait, neither fast nor slow, heads down, eyes straight ahead, hugging the edge of the road, giving off an air of minding their own business while having their own specialty. Because they pulled foreigners, they were exempt from wearing the numbered vest, and instead wore uniformly small white long-sleeved jackets, white or black trousers with especially wide legs tied at the ankle with thin cords, and wide double-faced, thick-soled black cloth shoes — clean, neat, and sharp. At the sight of this outfit, other pullers would never come over to compete for passengers or race them; they seemed to belong to an entirely different trade.

With this brief analysis in mind, we can now describe Xiangzi's place among the pullers with the precision of — we hope — identifying a particular bolt in a piece of machinery. Xiangzi, before he became associated with the nickname "Camel," was a relatively free rickshaw puller. That is to say, he belonged to the category of the young and strong who owned their own rickshaw: his own vehicle, his own livelihood, all in his own hands — a top-tier puller. This was by no means an easy thing to achieve. One year, two years, at least three or four years; one drop of sweat, two drops of sweat, countless tens of thousands of drops of sweat — that was what it took to earn that rickshaw. Gritting his teeth through wind and rain, denying himself food and drink — that was how he scraped together that rickshaw. That rickshaw was the sum total and reward of all his struggles and hardships, like a medal on the chest of a battle-hardened warrior. When he was still renting other people's rickshaws, he went from morning to night, from east to west, from south to north, spinning like a top whipped by someone else's hand — he had no self. Yet in the midst of all that spinning, his eyes never blurred and his heart never wavered. He always kept his sights on a rickshaw far off in the distance, one that could give him freedom and independence, one that would be as much his own as his hands and feet. With his own rickshaw, he would no longer have to put up with the attitude of the people who rented out vehicles, nor would he need to flatter anyone. With his own strength and his own rickshaw, he could open his eyes each morning and know there would be food to eat.

He wasn't afraid of hard work, and he lacked the bad habits common among rickshaw pullers — habits one might forgive but wouldn't want to imitate. His intelligence and diligence were more than enough to turn his ambitions into reality. Had his circumstances been a little better, or had he received a bit more education, he certainly would never have ended up among the "rubber-tire crowd." Whatever he might have done, he would never have wasted his opportunities. As it happened, he had no choice but to pull a rickshaw — and even in that trade, he proved his ability and intelligence. It was as if he could be a decent ghost even in hell. He had grown up in the countryside, lost his parents and a few meager acres of land, and at eighteen had come to the city. Bringing with him the sturdy health and honesty of a country boy, he had done nearly every job that could be done by sheer physical labor. But before long he could see that pulling a rickshaw was a better way to earn money. Other kinds of hard labor brought in a fixed income; rickshaw pulling offered more variety and opportunity — you never knew when or where you might come across a fare that paid more than you'd hoped. Of course, he also understood that such luck wasn't entirely a matter of chance: both the person and the rickshaw had to look sharp and presentable, so that someone who knew quality would recognize it when they saw it. Thinking it over, he believed he had what it took: he was strong, and he was young. What he lacked was experience on the road and the confidence to start out pulling a good-looking rickshaw right away. But these were not insurmountable obstacles. With his body and strength as a foundation, he figured that after ten days or half a month of practice he would surely be pulling with some style. Then he could rent a new rickshaw, and before long he might well land a private hire job. After that, living frugally for a year or two — three or four at most — he would certainly be able to buy his own rickshaw, the finest one there was! Looking at his own powerful muscles, he felt it was only a matter of time. This was an ambition and a goal he would definitely reach — no mere daydream!

His build and muscles had developed ahead of his years. In his early twenties, he was already very large and tall. Though his frame had not yet been fully set by the years, he already looked like a grown man — a grown man whose face and body still carried a boyish, mischievous air. Watching the top-tier pullers, he planned how to cinch in his waist to better show off his chest, broad as an iron fan, and his straight, firm back. He turned his head to look at his own shoulders — how wide they were, how imposing! Once the waist was cinched just right, he'd put on a pair of wide-legged white trousers, tied at the ankle with a thin cord, showing off those oversized feet of his! Yes, he could undoubtedly become the most outstanding rickshaw puller there was. He laughed at himself like a fool. He had no particular looks to speak of, but what made him appealing was the spirit in his face. His head wasn't very large; he had round eyes, a fleshy nose, two short thick eyebrows, and a head that was always shaved smooth and shiny. There was no excess flesh on his cheeks, but his neck was nearly as thick as his head. His face was always ruddy, and especially prominent was a sizable scar between his cheekbone and his right ear — left from when he had fallen asleep under a tree as a child and been bitten by a donkey. He didn't pay much attention to his looks. He liked his face the same way he liked his body — solid and tough. He more or less counted his face among his four limbs: as long as it was sturdy, that was good enough. Indeed, after coming to the city, he could still do a handstand and hold it for half a day. Standing like that, he felt, he was very much like a tree — not a single part of him from top to bottom that wasn't upright and strong.

He really was a bit like a tree — sturdy, quiet, and full of life. He had his own plans and his own thoughts, but he wasn't one to share them with others. Among rickshaw pullers, personal grievances and hardships were public conversation fodder. At the stand, in the small teahouses, in the crowded courtyards, everyone reported, described, or argued about their own affairs, which then became common property, passed from place to place like folk songs. Xiangzi was a country man,His speech wasn't as nimble as city folk; if quick talk comes from natural talent, he was born unwilling to say much, so he had no desire to pick up the city people's habit of idle chatter. His own affairs he kept to himself, and he didn't like discussing them with others. Because his mouth was often idle, he had time to think, and his eyes seemed always to be looking inward at his own heart. Once his mind was made up, he would follow the path his heart had laid out; if the path was blocked, he could go a day or two without a word, biting down hard, as if biting on his own heart. He decided to pull a rickshaw, so he went and pulled one. He rented a beat-up rickshaw and started training his legs. The first day he didn't earn much. Business was decent on the second day, but then he was laid up for two days, his ankles swollen like two gourds, unable to lift them at all. He endured it, no matter how much it hurt. He knew this was unavoidable, a threshold every rickshaw puller had to cross. Until he crossed it, he couldn't run with confidence.

Once his feet healed, he dared to run. This made him feel wonderful, because there was nothing else to fear: he knew the street names well, and even if he took a longer route sometimes it didn't matter much, since he had plenty of strength to spare. As for the technique of pulling a rickshaw, drawing on his experience with pushing, pulling, carrying, and hauling, it wasn't all that difficult to grasp. Besides, he had his own approach: stay alert, don't try to outdo others, and things would generally go fine. When it came to haggling over fares and competing for passengers, his tongue was slow and his temper strong, and he couldn't get the better of those seasoned veterans. Knowing this weakness, he simply avoided the main rickshaw stands; wherever there were no rickshaws, that's where he'd position himself. In these quiet spots, he could negotiate fares at his own pace, and sometimes he wouldn't even name a price, just saying, "Hop in, pay what you think is fair." His manner was so honest, his face so simple and likable, that people seemed to have no choice but to trust him, and couldn't imagine that this big, guileless fellow would overcharge them. Even if they had doubts, they could only suspect he was a country bumpkin newly arrived in the city who probably didn't know the roads and so couldn't name a price. When people asked, "Do you know the way?" he would smile in a way that was half playing dumb and half playful, leaving people not quite knowing what to make of him.

Within two or three weeks, he had broken in his legs. He knew his running style looked good. Running style was proof of a rickshaw puller's skill and standing. Those who splayed their feet, shuffling along like a pair of fans fanning the ground, were undoubtedly fresh arrivals from the countryside. Those who hung their heads low, scuffing their feet, moving at barely more than a walking pace while making a show of running, were the men over fifty. Those with plenty of experience but little strength had their own method: chest caved inward at a deep angle, knees lifted high, rocking forward with each step; this gave the impression of great effort, while in fact they were no faster than anyone else — they relied on "style" to maintain their dignity. Xiangzi naturally had no intention of adopting any of these postures. His legs were long and his strides wide, his waist rock-steady as he ran; he made little noise, each step with a slight spring to it, the rickshaw handles perfectly still, giving passengers a sense of safety and comfort. When told to stop, no matter how fast he was going, he'd give two light scrapes of his big feet on the ground and come to a halt; his strength seemed to reach every part of the rickshaw. Back slightly bent, hands loosely gripping the handles, he moved with ease, precision, and fluency — looking unhurried yet running very fast, fast yet without danger. Even among rickshaw pullers, this was something to be prized.

He switched to a new rickshaw. From the day he made the switch, he had already found out clearly that a rickshaw like the one he rented — soft springs, quality brass fittings, large rain curtain, double lamps, slender neck and big brass horn — was worth just over a hundred yuan; if the lacquer work and brass fittings had a bit of iron mixed in, a hundred yuan would cover it. Roughly speaking, as long as he had a hundred yuan, he could get himself a rickshaw. At first thought, if he could save ten cents a day, a hundred yuan would take a thousand days — a thousand days! Stacking a thousand days together, he could barely fathom how far away that was. But he made up his mind: a thousand days, ten thousand days if need be, he was going to buy a rickshaw. The first step, he decided, was to hire himself out on a monthly contract. If he landed an employer with lots of social engagements and dinner parties — averaging ten or more dinners a month — he could pocket an extra two or three yuan in meal money. On top of that, if he saved another yuan or so each month, maybe three to five yuan, he could put away fifty or sixty yuan in a year. That way, his goal would be much closer. He didn't smoke, didn't drink, didn't gamble, had no vices of any kind, and no family to weigh him down; as long as he was willing to grit his teeth, there was nothing he couldn't accomplish. He swore an oath to himself: within a year and a half, he — Xiangzi — would have his own rickshaw, freshly made, not some old one that had seen better days.

He really did land a monthly contract. But reality doesn't always cooperate with hope. True, he had gritted his teeth, yet when a year and a half had passed, he still hadn't fulfilled that vow. He had indeed taken on contract work and been careful and conscientious about everything; but unfortunately, the world doesn't work in just one direction. He could be as careful as he liked, yet his employer might still let him go; whether after two or three months or just ten days or so, it could all fall apart. He'd have to go looking for work again. Naturally, he had to look for work on one hand and take individual fares on the other; he couldn't afford to sit idle. During these stretches, he often made mistakes. He still forced himself to keep going, not just to scrape together daily food money, but to keep saving toward the rickshaw. Yet forcing yourself to keep going is never a sound approach: when pulling the rickshaw, he couldn't focus fully on running, always seeming to be turning something over in his mind, and the more he thought, the more anxious and resentful he became. If things kept going like this, when would he ever buy a rickshaw? Why was it like this? Was he really someone without ambition? Lost in these tangled thoughts, he forgot his usual caution. The rubber tire caught a piece of broken metal or ceramic and blew out; he had to call it a day. More seriously, he sometimes bumped into pedestrians, and once, in his rush to squeeze through, he knocked off a wheel hub cap. If he had been on a monthly contract, none of these mistakes would have happened; once he was between jobs, his mood soured and he became a bit reckless. Damaging the rickshaw meant paying for repairs, which only made him more agitated, adding fuel to the fire; afraid of causing even bigger trouble, he sometimes just lay in bed a whole day. When he finally opened his eyes, the whole day had slipped away for nothing, and he'd be filled with regret and self-reproach. What's more, during these periods, the more anxious he grew the harder he pushed himself, eating and drinking with less and less regularity; he thought of himself as made of iron, but it turned out he could get sick too. When sick, he begrudged spending money on medicine and just toughed it out; the result was that the illness grew worse and worse, and he ended up not only having to buy medicine but also needing to rest for several days at a stretch. These difficulties made him grit his teeth and push harder, yet the money for the rickshaw didn't accumulate any faster because of it. It took him a full three years to scrape together a hundred yuan.

He couldn't wait any longer. The original plan had been to buy the most complete, most up-to-date, most ideal rickshaw; now he had to work with what a hundred yuan could get. He couldn't wait any longer; what if something came up and he lost a few more yuan? As luck would have it, there was a freshly made rickshaw — custom-ordered but unclaimed for lack of funds — that was close enough to what he'd hoped for; it was originally worth over a hundred, but since the deposit had been forfeited, the shop was willing to take a little less. Xiangzi's face flushed red, his hands trembling, as he slapped down 96 yuan: "I want this rickshaw." The shop owner tried to push for a round number, talking at length, pulling the rickshaw out and then back in, opening the canopy and folding it down again, pressing the horn, each action accompanied by a string of the finest superlatives; finally he gave the steel spokes a couple of kicks: "Listen to that sound — rings like a bell. Take it away; even if you smash the thing to pieces, if a single spoke goes soft, bring it back and throw it in my face. A hundred yuan, not a cent less, or no deal." Xiangzi counted the money again: "I want this rickshaw, 96." The shop owner could see he was dealing with a man of one mind; he looked at the money, looked at Xiangzi, and let out a sigh: "Let's call it friendship — the rickshaw is yours. Six-month guarantee: unless you smash the frame, I'll repair everything for free. Here's the warranty, take it."

Xiangzi's hands were trembling even more now; he tucked away the warranty, took hold of the rickshaw, and nearly burst into tears. He pulled it to a quiet spot and examined his rickshaw carefully, even trying to see his own reflection in the lacquered panel. The more he looked, the more he loved it; even the parts that fell a little short of his ideal were forgivable now, because it was his own rickshaw. When he felt he'd looked at it enough for the moment, he sat down on the new rubber footrest, gazing at the gleaming brass horn on the handles. It suddenly occurred to him that he was twenty-two this year. Because his parents had died early, he had forgotten which day his birthday fell on. Since coming to the city, he hadn't celebrated a single birthday. Well then, today he had bought a new rickshaw — let this be his birthday, both his and the rickshaw's, easy to remember; and since the rickshaw was the fruit of his own blood and sweat, there was really no reason not to count the person and the rickshaw as one.

How to celebrate this "double birthday"? Xiangzi had it all figured out: the very first fare had to be someone well-dressed, and absolutely not a woman. Best of all would be a fare to Qianmen, or else the Eastern Peace Market. Once he got there, he should treat himself to a good meal at the finest food stall — something like a hot sesame cake stuffed with stir-fried lamb. After eating, if a good fare came along he'd take one or two more; if not, he'd call it a day. This was his birthday.

From the day he got this rickshaw, his life grew more and more spirited. Whether on a monthly contract or taking individual fares, he no longer had to worry every day about the "rickshaw fee" — whatever he earned was entirely his own. With his heart at ease, he was friendlier to people, and business went more smoothly. After half a year of pulling, his hopes grew even bigger: at this rate, working two more years — three at most — he could buy another rickshaw, then one more, two more… he could even open his own rickshaw yard.

But hopes more often than not come to nothing, and Xiangzi's were no exception.

[1] Rickshaw stand, i.e., a designated stopping place for rickshaws.

[2] Pulling the night shift means going out after four in the afternoon and working until before dawn.

[3] Chewing grain, meaning food and daily expenses.

2

Ever since buying the rickshaw, Xiangzi ran faster. He had a little more money in hand, and his confidence grew along with it. Yet he was always especially careful with his own rickshaw, and whenever he looked at himself and then at his rickshaw, something felt off if he wasn't running fast.

Since coming to the city, Xiangzi had grown more than an inch taller. He could feel it himself, and it seemed like he still had more growing to do. True, his build and features had become sturdier and more defined, and a small mustache had appeared on his upper lip; but he felt he ought to grow a bit taller still. Whenever he came to a low doorway or a gate and had to duck way down to get through, he said nothing, but felt a quiet pleasure inside, because he was already this tall and still seemed to be growing — he felt at once like a grown man and a child, which struck him as rather wonderful.

A man this big, pulling such a fine rickshaw — his own rickshaw — with springs so soft they trembled, the very handles swaying gently; the carriage so gleaming, the cushion so white, the horn so loud; how could he not run fast? How could he do justice to that rickshaw? This wasn't vanity — it felt more like a responsibility. Only by running fast, by flying, could he fully express his own strength and the rickshaw's beauty. That rickshaw was truly something to love; after half a year of pulling it, it seemed to have developed awareness and feeling in every part. A twist of Xiangzi's waist, a bend of his knees, or a straightening of his back, and it responded instantly, giving him exactly the help he needed — not the slightest awkwardness or friction between them. When he came to a stretch of flat road with few people, Xiangzi could hold the handles with one hand, the softly humming rubber wheels urging him on like a swift little breeze, fast and smooth. By the time he reached his destination, Xiangzi's clothes could be wrung out, the sweat pouring off him as if he'd just been pulled from a washbasin. He felt tired, but it was a satisfying, pride-worthy kind of tiredness — like having galloped a fine horse for dozens of li. If boldness isn't the same as carelessness, then Xiangzi, when running at full speed, was not careless at all. Not running fast would be letting people down; running fast and damaging the rickshaw would be letting himself down. The rickshaw was his life, and he knew how to take care of it. Caution and boldness held together, he grew more and more self-assured, firmly believing that both he and his rickshaw were made of iron.

Because of this, he not only dared to run at full speed, he also didn't give much thought to when he went out. He felt that earning a living by pulling a rickshaw with his own strength was the most dignified thing in the world; he wanted to go out, and no one could stop him. He didn't pay much attention to rumors: that soldiers had arrived again at Xiyuan, that fighting had broken out again at Changxindian, that people were being conscripted outside Xizhimen, that Qihuamen had been closed for half a day — none of it registered with him. Of course, when the shops along the street had all put up their shutters and the roads were lined with armed police and security forces, he had no reason to go looking for trouble, and like everyone else he quickly put away his rickshaw. But rumors — he didn't believe them. He knew how to be careful, especially since the rickshaw was his own, but he was a country man at heart and not like city people, who heard the wind and called it rain. Besides, his physical strength made him confident that even if he were unlucky enough to find himself in the wrong place at the wrong time, he would find a way out and wouldn't come off too badly; he wasn't easy to push around — a man that big, with shoulders that broad.

News of war and rumors of fighting rose up almost every year alongside the spring wheat, and wheat ears and bayonets could be taken as symbols of the hopes and fears of people in the north. When Xiangzi's new rickshaw had just passed the half-year mark, it was the season when the wheat needed spring rain.Spring rain does not necessarily fall according to people's wishes, but war always comes whether anyone wishes for it or not. Rumors or truth, Xiangzi seemed to have forgotten he had once worked the land; he didn't much care how war destroyed the fields, nor did he pay much attention to whether the spring rain came or not. He only cared about his rickshaw. His rickshaw could produce flatbread and all kinds of food — it was a plot of all-purpose land that followed him obediently, a living piece of ground, a treasure. Because of the drought and the news of war, grain prices had all gone up; this, Xiangzi knew. But like the city folk, all he could do was complain about the high prices without any idea what to do about it; grain was expensive, so be it — who had the power to make it cheap? This attitude kept him focused only on his own livelihood, pushing every calamity and disaster to the back of his mind.

If city people can do nothing about anything, they will make up rumors — sometimes completely out of thin air, sometimes blowing one part truth into ten — just to show they aren't foolish or idle. They're like little fish, and when they have nothing to do they put their mouths to the surface of the water and blow out a few completely useless bubbles, feeling rather pleased with themselves. Among all rumors, the most interesting are those about war. Other kinds of rumors tend to stay rumors forever, like ghost stories and fox tales — you can talk and talk and never actually run into a ghost. With war, it's precisely because there's no accurate information that rumors can produce immediate results. The details may differ greatly from the truth, but as for whether war itself will happen, eight or nine times out of ten the rumors are right. "There's going to be a fight!" — once those words are spoken, sooner or later a fight will come; as for who fights whom and how, that's a different story for every person. Xiangzi wasn't unaware of this. Still, people who did hard labor — rickshaw pullers included — though they wouldn't welcome war, didn't necessarily have to suffer when it came. Whenever war arrived, the ones who panicked most were the wealthy. The moment they caught wind of trouble, they immediately thought of fleeing; money had come to them fast, and it would carry them away fast too. But they couldn't run on their own, because their legs were weighed down too heavily by their wealth. They had to hire many people to be their legs — trunks needed carriers, old and young, men and women needed rickshaws; at times like these, the hands and feet of the brothers who sold nothing but their physical labor all became more valuable at once: "Qianmen, East Station!" "Where?" "East — Station!" "Alright, straight up one dollar forty! No bargaining — it's wartime chaos!"

It was under these circumstances that Xiangzi pulled his rickshaw out of the city. The rumors had been going around for over ten days, prices had already risen, but the fighting still seemed far away and unlikely to reach Beiping anytime soon. Xiangzi kept pulling his rickshaw as usual, not slacking off on account of the rumors. One day, after pulling into the western part of the city, he noticed something was off. At the west end of Huguo Temple Street and at Xinjieko, there wasn't a single person calling out "Xiyuan? Qinghua?" He wandered around near Xinjieko for a while. Word was that rickshaws no longer dared to leave the city — outside Xizhimen they were seizing vehicles of all kinds, carts, mule carts, and rickshaws alike. He thought about getting a bowl of tea and heading south; the unusual quiet at the rickshaw stand hinted at real danger. He had a fair amount of nerve, but there was no point in deliberately walking into a dead end. Just at that critical moment, two rickshaws came from the south carrying what looked like students. The pullers called out as they walked:\n"Anyone going to Qinghua? Hey, Qinghua!"

The few rickshaws at the stand gave no response. Some looked at those two rickshaws with a thin, unhurried smile; others sat there with their small pipes in their mouths, not even lifting their heads. The two rickshaws kept calling: "All gone mute? Qinghua!"

"Two yuan and I'll go!" A young, shaved-headed short fellow, seeing no one else speak up, answered half-jokingly. "Come on over! Find another one!" The two rickshaws stopped.

The young shaved-head hesitated for a moment, seeming unsure what to do. Everyone else still didn't move. Xiangzi could tell there must be danger outside the city — otherwise why would nobody jump at two yuan to Qinghua, a trip that normally cost only twenty or thirty cents? He didn't want to go either. But the shaved-head young fellow seemed to have made up his mind that if someone would come along for the run, he'd go for it; he set his eyes on Xiangzi: "Big guy, what do you say?"

The words "big guy" made Xiangzi smile — it was a kind of compliment. His mind started turning: for a compliment like that, it seemed only right to back up this short but bold shaved-head; and besides, two yuan was two yuan, not something you came across every day. Danger? Would he really be that unlucky? Besides, just a couple of days ago someone had said the Temple of Heaven was packed with soldiers; he'd gone to see for himself and there wasn't a single soldier in sight. Thinking it over like this, he pulled his rickshaw forward.

By the time they reached Xizhimen, there were almost no pedestrians in the city gate tunnel. Xiangzi's heart sank a little. The shaved-head could also see things didn't look good, but he still smiled and said: "Let's go for it, buddy! What's meant to be will be — today is today!" Xiangzi knew things were going to go badly, but he'd been making his way on the streets for years now — he couldn't go back on his word, couldn't act like a crybaby!

Once outside Xizhimen, they truly didn't encounter a single other rickshaw; Xiangzi lowered his head, not daring to look left or right along the road. His heart felt like it was pressing against his ribs. When they reached Gaoliang Bridge, he glanced around — not a single soldier — and he relaxed a little. Two yuan was two yuan after all, he figured; without a bit of nerve you'd never land such a good deal. He wasn't usually one for talking, but right now he felt like saying a few words to the shaved-head short fellow — the street was quiet in a way that was truly frightening. "Should we take the dirt road? On the main road —" "Goes without saying," the short fellow guessed what he meant, "once we get onto the side road, we'll have a bit of cover!"

Before they even reached the side road, Xiangzi and the shaved-head short fellow were captured along with their rickshaws by a dozen or so soldiers!

Although it was already the season for the Miaofeng Mountain temple fair and pilgrimages, the night chill was still more than a single unlined jacket could keep out. Xiangzi had nothing on him at all — just a gray unlined military jacket on top and a pair of blue cloth military trousers, both soaked through with sweat and reeking — they had smelled that way even before they ever came to him. From these ragged military clothes, he thought of his own original white cotton undershirt and that indanthrene blue lined jacket and trousers; how clean and presentable those had been! True, there were many things in the world more presentable than indanthrene blue, but Xiangzi knew how hard it had been for someone like him to get himself looking that clean and put-together. Smelling the stench of stale sweat on himself now, he saw his past struggles and achievements as all the more glorious — ten times more glorious than they had seemed before. The more he thought about the past, the more he hated those soldiers. His clothes, shoes, and hat, his rickshaw, even the cloth sash around his waist — all had been taken from him; they had left him nothing but a body covered in bruises and a pair of feet full of blisters. But clothes — those were nothing; the wounds on his body would heal before long. His rickshaw, though — the one he had earned through years of blood and sweat — was gone! From the moment they were taken to the barracks it had vanished! He could forget all his past hardships and difficulties in the blink of an eye, but he could not forget that rickshaw!

Hardship he didn't fear; but getting hold of another rickshaw wasn't something you could manage just by saying so — it would take at least a few more years of work! All his past success had been for nothing; he'd have to start all over from scratch. Xiangzi wept! He didn't just hate those soldiers — he hated everything in the world. What gave anyone the right to bully a person to this degree? What gave them the right? "What gives them the right?" he cried out. That outburst — though it gave him some relief — immediately reminded him of the danger he was in. Never mind everything else for now — getting away with his life was what mattered!

Where was he? He couldn't even answer that accurately himself. All these days he had been running with the soldiers, sweat pouring from his head all the way down to his heels. When they marched, he had to carry, haul, or push the soldiers' things; when they stopped, he had to fetch water, tend fires, and feed the animals. From morning to night all he knew was how to put his last bit of strength into his hands and feet; his mind had gone blank. At night, the moment his head touched the ground he was out like the dead, and the thought of never opening his eyes again was not necessarily a bad thing.

At first, he seemed to remember the soldiers retreating toward the Miaofeng Mountain area. Once they reached the back of the mountain, all he could do was climb, constantly thinking that at any moment he might slip and fall into a mountain ravine and have his flesh picked clean by wild eagles — there was no room to think of anything else. After wandering through the mountains for many days, one day the mountain paths grew fewer and fewer, and when the sun was at his back he saw flat land in the distance. The bugle call for evening mess summoned the soldiers back to camp, and a few armed men led several camels in.

Camels! Xiangzi's heart stirred, and all of a sudden he could think again — like a person lost on the road who suddenly spots a familiar landmark and rapidly pieces everything together. Camels couldn't cross mountains, so he must have reached flat ground. From what he knew, he was aware that areas west of Beijing — places like Balizhuang, Huangcun, Beixin'an, Moshikou, Wulitun, Sanjiadian — all had people who kept camels. Could it be that after all this wandering, he had ended up at Moshikou? What kind of strategy this was — if these soldiers who knew nothing but running and looting could even be said to have a strategy — he didn't know. But he was certain that if this really was Moshikou, the soldiers must have been unable to find a way out of the mountains and had come down to the foothills looking for a way forward. Moshikou was a good place: heading northeast you could get back to the Western Hills; heading south you could make for Changxindian or Fengtai; going straight out through the pass heading west was also a way out. As he worked this out for the soldiers, he also mapped out a route for himself: it was time for him to escape. If the soldiers retreated back into the wild mountains, even if he slipped out of their grasp he'd still risk starving to death. If he was going to escape, he had to seize this opportunity. From here, he believed, one run would take him straight back to Haidian! Even though there was so much ground in between, he knew it all; close his eyes and he had a map: here was Moshikou — God, it had to be Moshikou! — he'd turn northeast, pass Jinding Mountain, Liwang Tomb, and reach Badachu; from Sipingtai head east toward Xingzikou and arrive at Nanxinzhuang. For better cover, he'd best follow along the mountain, from Beixinzhuang heading north, through Weijia Village; north again, through Nanhetan; further north to Hongshangtou, Jiewang Mansion; Jingyi Garden! Once he found Jingyi Garden, he could find his way to Haidian with his eyes closed! His heart was about to leap out of his chest! All these days his blood had seemed to flow entirely into his limbs; now, in this moment, it all seemed to rush back to his heart; his heart burned hot while his limbs turned cold; the burning hope made his whole body tremble!

He still couldn't close his eyes when midnight came. Hope made him glad, fear made him anxious; he wanted to sleep but couldn't, his limbs lying spread out on some dry straw as if they had come loose. Not a sound anywhere, only the stars in the sky keeping company with his heartbeat. A camel suddenly let out two mournful cries, not far from him. He liked that sound — it made him feel sad the way a rooster crowing in the night suddenly does, yet also brought a measure of comfort.

In the distance came the sound of cannon fire — very far away, but unmistakably cannon fire. He didn't dare move, but the camp immediately fell into chaos. He held his breath — the moment had come! He knew for certain that the soldiers would have to retreat again, and most likely back into the mountains. His experience of these past days had taught him that these soldiers fought the way a bee trapped in a room does — just blundering around in all directions. With cannon fire, the soldiers would surely run; and that meant he himself had better be sharp. Slowly, holding his breath, he crawled along the ground, his aim being to find those few camels. He knew perfectly well that the camels couldn't help him in any practical way, but since they were fellow captives, he felt he owed them some sympathy. The camp grew more chaotic; he found the camels — crouching in the darkness like several mounds of earth, not a stir except for their heavy breathing, as if the whole world were perfectly at peace. This gave him a bit more courage. He crouched beside the camels the way a soldier hides behind sandbags. Very quickly a thought came to him: the cannon fire was coming from the south, and even if it wasn't a genuine battle, it was at least a warning that the road south was blocked. So these soldiers would have to flee back into the mountains. If they really went up into the mountains, they couldn't take the camels with them. In that case, the camels' fate and his own fate were one and the same. If the soldiers didn't abandon these animals, he was finished along with them; if they forgot about the camels, he could escape. He pressed his ear to the ground and listened for the sound of approaching footsteps, his heart pounding furiously.

He didn't know how long he waited — no one ever came for the camels. He gathered his nerve and sat up, peering through the gap between the camels' two humps; he could see nothing, the darkness was total on all sides. Escape! Whatever came of it, good or bad — escape!

[1] 一边儿, meaning "likewise" or "at the same time."

[2] 招呼吧, meaning "let's do it" or "let's go for it."

[3] 今儿个就是今儿个, meaning that when a critical moment arrives, success or failure all comes down to today.

3

Xiangzi had only run a few dozen steps before he stopped. The truth was, he couldn't bear to leave those few camels behind, because right now all he had was his bare life. Even a rope lying on the ground he'd have been glad to pick up — useless as it might be, it would give him a little comfort; at least he'd have a rope in his hand and wouldn't be completely empty-handed. Escaping with his life was important, but what good was a bare life with nothing to show for it? He had to take these animals with him. He still hadn't figured out what use the camels would be, but they were at least something — and not a small something at that.

He got the camels to their feet. He didn't know much about handling camels, but he wasn't afraid of them — having grown up in the countryside, he was comfortable getting close to animals.The camels rose slowly, very slowly. He had no time to check carefully whether they were all tied together; feeling that he could lead them forward, he set off at a stride, whether he was pulling just one or the whole bunch.

The moment he took that first step, he regretted it. Camels — accustomed to carrying heavy loads in the interior — simply could not walk fast. Not only did they have to go slowly, they had to go with extreme care, for camels were afraid of slipping; a puddle of water, a patch of mud, could make them do the splits or twist a knee. A camel's entire value lay in its four legs; once the legs were gone, everything was gone. And Xiangzi was trying to flee for his life!

Still, he refused to let them go. He had left everything to fate, and camels that had come to him for free were not something he could release.

From years of pulling a rickshaw, Xiangzi had developed a decent sense of direction. Even so, his mind was somewhat muddled now. When he had found the camels, his whole attention had seemed to be on them; but once he got them moving, he could no longer tell where he was. The night was so dark, his heart so anxious, that even if he had known how to read the stars and adjust his course, he would not have dared to stop calmly and do so. The stars — in his eyes — seemed even more frantic than he was, jostling and bumping one another in the black sky. Xiangzi dared not look up again. He lowered his head and pressed forward, heart racing but feet refusing to hurry. He thought of this: since he was leading camels, he had to follow the main road and could no longer hug the hillside. From Moshikou — if this was indeed Moshikou — to Huangcun was a straight road. It was the main road for camel traffic, and it did not detour at all. "No detour" carried great weight in the mind of a rickshaw puller. But this road offered no cover. What if he ran into soldiers again? Even if he did not, could anyone believe, looking at his tattered military clothes, his mud-caked face, and his long matted hair, that he was a camel driver? No, not at all — he looked nothing like a camel driver. He looked far more like a deserter. A deserter caught by the authorities would be the lesser trouble; caught by villagers, he would be buried alive at the very least. At that thought he began to tremble, and the soft padding of the camels' hooves behind him suddenly made him jump. If he truly wanted to escape with his life, he ought to abandon these burdensome animals. Yet he still could not bring himself to let go of the rope through the camels' noses. Walk on, just walk, go wherever the road leads, deal with whatever comes; if he lived, he would have earned a few animals; if he died, it was fate.

Still, he stripped off the military jacket and tore the collar away with one yank; the pair of brass buttons that still clung to their duty were ripped off and flung into the darkness without so much as a sound. Then he draped this collarless, buttonless tunic diagonally across his body, tying the two sleeves together in a knot at his chest, the way one might wear a bundle on one's back. This, he figured, would reduce the suspicion that he was a defeated soldier; he also rolled his trousers up a notch. He knew it still did not look quite like a camel driver's outfit, but at least it no longer looked entirely like a deserter's. With the mud on his face and the sweat on his body, he reckoned he could pass for a coal hauler. His thinking was slow, but thorough, and whatever he thought of he acted on at once. In the dark of night no one could see him; there was really no need to do all this right away. But he could not wait. He had no sense of the time — dawn might come at any moment. Since he was no longer following the mountain path, he would have no place to hide in daylight; if he intended to keep moving in the daytime, he had to make people believe he was a coal hauler. Having thought of it, he did it immediately, and his heart felt a little lighter, as though the danger had already passed and Beijing lay just ahead. He had to get to the city steadily and quickly, for he had not a single coin on him, not a scrap of food, and could not afford to waste any more time. At that thought, he considered riding one of the camels to save his strength and hold off hunger a little longer. But he did not dare. Even if it were perfectly safe, he would first have to make the camel kneel before he could mount; time was precious and he could not afford the trouble. Besides, if he were up that high, it would be even harder to see the ground beneath him, and if the camel fell, he would go down with it. No, he would just keep walking.

He had a rough sense that he was following the main road, but his bearings and his exact location were both hazy. The night had grown late; days of exhaustion and the terror of his escape left him wretched in body and mind. As he walked on, his steps fell into such an even, unhurried rhythm that he gradually began to feel drowsy. The night was still very dark, the air damp and cold with mist, and his heart felt all the more desolate. He strained to look at the ground, which kept seeming to rise and fall in low ridges, yet when he set his foot down it was perfectly flat. This constant wariness and constant disappointment made him more unsettled, almost irritable. He gave up watching the ground altogether, kept his gaze level, and shuffled forward with his feet barely leaving the surface. There was nothing to see in any direction; it was as though all the darkness in the world had been waiting for him — stepping out of the dark, stepping back into the dark, with those silent camels following behind.

He grew accustomed to the outer darkness, and his mind seemed to stop working; his eyes closed of their own accord. He could not tell whether he was still moving forward or had already stopped. He felt only a rolling, surging sensation inside him, like a heaving black sea; darkness and his own consciousness merged into one — both boundless, both rising and falling, both dreamlike. Suddenly something stirred in his heart, as though he had just remembered something, or perhaps heard some sound — he could not say which — and his eyes opened again. He was indeed still walking; he had forgotten what he had just thought of, and there was no movement anywhere around him. His heart pounded for a moment, then gradually settled. He told himself not to close his eyes again, not to let his mind wander; getting to the city as fast as possible was the one thing that mattered. But when his mind had nothing to hold onto, his eyes would close again all too easily. He had to keep something in his thoughts, had to stay awake. He knew that once he went down, he could sleep for three days straight. What to think about? His head was a little dizzy, his body clammy and uncomfortable, his scalp itching, his feet aching, his mouth dry and bitter. He could think of nothing else — only of pitying himself. But even that he could not sustain in any detail; his head was so hollow and swollen that the moment he thought of himself he seemed to forget himself again, like a candle about to go out that can no longer even illuminate itself. The darkness all around deepened this feeling, making him sense that he was drifting inside a mass of black vapor. He knew he still existed, still kept putting one foot in front of the other, yet there was nothing else to confirm where exactly he was walking — very much like floating alone on a desolate sea, unable to trust his own existence. He had never before experienced this agonizing uncertainty, this absolute, suffocating solitude. In ordinary life, though he was not one for making friends, he was a man alone in the sunlight, with the sun warming his limbs and all manner of things before his eyes, and he was not afraid. Now he was not afraid either — it was simply the inability to be certain of anything that he could not bear. If the camels had been as restless as mules or horses, they might at least have kept him alert and watchful; but camels were so docile, so docile that it exasperated him. In his most disoriented moments he would suddenly doubt whether the camels were still behind him, and the thought would give him a start. He half believed these great beasts could quietly slip away down some dark side path without his knowing a thing, the way a block of ice held in one's hand can melt away to nothing.

At some point he sat down. If he had died right there, even with consciousness after death, he would not have been able to remember how he sat down or why. He sat for five minutes, or perhaps an hour — he did not know. He did not know whether he sat down first and then fell asleep, or fell asleep first and then sat down. Most likely he fell asleep first and then sat down, for his exhaustion had already reached the point where he could fall asleep standing up.

He woke with a start. Not the natural, gradual waking from sleep, but a sudden jolt, as though he had leapt from one world into another in the instant of opening his eyes. He still saw only darkness, but he heard a rooster crow with perfect clarity — so clear it was as if something hard had drawn a line across his brain. He came fully awake. The camels — he had no thought for anything else. The rope was still in his hand, and the camels were still beside him. His heart grew calm. He felt too lazy to get up. His body was stiff and heavy; he did not want to rise, yet he dared not sleep again. He had to think — think carefully — come up with a good plan. It was at this moment that he thought of his rickshaw and cried out, "Why? Why?"

"Why?" But crying out into the void was utterly useless. He reached out to feel the camels, still not knowing how many he had brought with him. He counted carefully: three in all. He did not feel this was too many or too few. He focused his thoughts on these three animals, and though he had not yet settled on exactly what to do, a vague notion came to him that his entire future depended on these three beasts.

"Why not sell them and buy a rickshaw?" He nearly leapt to his feet. But he did not move, as though he felt he ought to be ashamed for not having thought of something so obvious and straightforward sooner. Joy overcame shame, and he made up his mind: had he not just heard a rooster crow? Even if roosters sometimes crowed at one or two in the morning, dawn could not be far off. Where there was a rooster there had to be a village — perhaps it was Beixin'an? There were camel keepers there. He had to move quickly; if he could arrive by daybreak, sell the camels, and get into the city, he could buy a rickshaw right away. In times of war and chaos, rickshaws would surely be cheaper. He thought only of buying the rickshaw, as though selling the camels would be no trouble at all.

Thinking of the connection between the camels and the rickshaw, his spirits rose, and it was as if his body had never been uncomfortable at all. If he had thought that these three camels could buy him a hundred mu of land, or be traded for a few pearls, he would not have been nearly so happy. He sprang to his feet, grabbed the camels' lead, and set off. He had no idea what camels were fetching these days; he had only heard that in the old days, before the railways came, a single camel was worth one large silver ingot, because camels were strong and ate less than mules or horses. He did not hope for three ingots — he only hoped to get a hundred or eighty yuan, just enough to buy a rickshaw. With every step the sky grew lighter; sure enough, the brightness was ahead of him — he was indeed heading east. Even if he had taken a wrong road, his direction was right; the mountains were to the west, the city to the east, and he knew that much. The uniform blackness all around gradually began to show gradations of depth; though colors were still indistinguishable, fields and distant trees had taken on shapes within the general gray. The stars thinned, and the sky was draped in a layer of gray haze, neither quite cloud nor quite fog — dim, yet much higher than before. Xiangzi felt he could almost lift his head. He began to smell the grass along the roadside, and heard a few birds call; because he could now make out the faint shapes of things, his eyes and ears and nose and mouth all seemed to recover their proper functions. He could also see himself — ragged and wretched as he was, the sight of his own body let him believe he was truly still alive, the way one feels after waking from a nightmare, suddenly aware of how precious life is. Having looked himself over, he turned to look at the camels behind him — as sorry-looking as he was, and just as dear. It was the season when animals shed their coats; the camels had already exposed their grayish-red skin in patches, with only scattered, limp, loose tufts of long hair hanging here and there, ready to fall at any moment — like enormous beggars among beasts. Most pitiable of all were those long, bare necks — so long, so naked, curved and clumsy, stretching out far ahead, like gaunt, dejected dragons. But Xiangzi did not find fault with them. However ungainly they were, they were living things. He acknowledged himself to be the luckiest man in the world: heaven had sent him three living treasures worth a rickshaw, and this was not something one came across every day. He could not help but laugh.

A tinge of red appeared in the gray sky, and the ground and distant trees looked darker by contrast; the red gradually blended with the gray, turning some patches grayish-purple, making others especially vivid, while most of the sky became the color of grape. After a little while, a bright golden yellow broke through the red, and every color began to glow; then suddenly everything became very clear. The rosy dawn in the east deepened to a rich crimson, and the sky overhead turned blue. The rosy clouds broke apart, and shafts of golden light shot outward — horizontal bands of cloud, vertical beams of light — weaving across the southeastern sky into a vast and glorious spider's web: the green fields, trees, and wild grass all shifted from dark green to luminous jade. The trunks of old pines were touched with gold and red, the wings of birds in flight flashed with golden light, and everything seemed to smile. Xiangzi wanted to shout aloud at that blaze of red light. Since the soldiers had taken him away, he felt as though he had not seen the sun; his heart had been full of curses, his head always bowed, forgetting that there was still a sun and moon, forgetting heaven itself. Now he walked freely, and with every step the world grew brighter. The sun gave a glint of gold to the dewdrops on the grass blades, and lit up Xiangzi's brows and hair, and warmed his heart. He forgot all hardship, all danger, all pain. No matter how ragged and filthy he was, the sun's light and warmth had not excluded him — he lived in a universe that had both light and warmth. He was happy, he wanted to cheer.

He looked at his tattered clothes, then turned to look at the three molting camels behind him, and smiled. With just four such disreputable creatures — man and beast alike — he thought, they had somehow escaped danger and were walking toward the sun again. Truly remarkable. There was no need to think about who was right or wrong anymore; it was all heaven's will, he felt. His heart at ease, he walked on at a leisurely pace. As long as heaven watched over him, there was nothing to fear. Where had he gotten to? He no longer cared to ask, though men and women were already out working in the fields. Walk on,Even if he couldn't sell the camels right away, it didn't seem to matter much anymore; he'd get to the city first and figure things out from there. He longed to see the city again. Even though there were no parents or relatives there, no property of any kind, it was still his home — the whole city was his home. Once he got there, he'd find a way. In the distance there was a village, a fairly large one. The willow trees outside the village stood like a row of tall green sentries, looking down over the low-roofed houses, wisps of cooking smoke drifting above them. The barking of village dogs could be heard from far away, and it sounded wonderful. He headed straight for the village, not expecting anything good to come of it — it was more like he was showing that he wasn't afraid of anything. He was a decent person, and decent people had nothing to fear from the honest folk of a village. Right now, everyone was living under the bright and peaceful sun. If possible, he wanted a drink of water; but even if he couldn't get any, it didn't matter. Since he hadn't died out in the hills, what was a little more thirst?

The village dogs barked at him and he paid them little mind; but the stares of the women and children made him uncomfortable. He must look like a very strange camel driver, he thought — otherwise, why would everyone be gaping at him like that? He felt deeply embarrassed: the soldiers hadn't treated him like a human being, and now that he'd come to the village, everyone was looking at him like he was some kind of freak. He didn't know what to do with himself. His size and strength had always been a source of pride and self-respect, yet over these past days he had suffered humiliation and hardship for no reason at all. He looked past the rooftops of the houses and saw the bright sun again, but it no longer seemed as lovely as it had a moment ago.

Along the village's one main road, pig urine, horse urine, and wastewater had pooled into several foul-smelling little puddles. On the north side of the road stood a household that was better off than the rest — tile-roofed rooms in the back, but the front gate was just a wooden fence with no wooden door and no gatehouse. Something stirred in Xiangzi's mind: tile roof — a man of means; wooden fence but no gatehouse — a camel keeper. Good enough. He'd rest here a while. Who knew, maybe there'd be a chance to get rid of the camels.

"Sè! Sè! Sè!" Xiangzi called for the camels to kneel. Of all the commands for handling camels, the only one he knew was that "sè…" meant kneel. He used it with great satisfaction, deliberately letting the villagers see that he was no amateur. The camels did kneel, and he himself sat down casually under a small willow tree. People watched him, and he watched them back; he knew that was the only way to ease the villagers' suspicions.

After sitting for a while, an old man came out of the courtyard — blue cloth jacket hanging open at the chest, a bright, ruddy face — clearly a well-off country landowner at a glance. Xiangzi had made up his mind. "Old timer, got water handy? I'll have a bowl."

"Ah!" The old man rubbed a roll of dirt between his hands on his chest and sized Xiangzi up, then looked the three camels over carefully. "There's water. Where'd you come from?"

"The west." Xiangzi didn't dare name a place, since he wasn't sure himself.

"There are soldiers out west?" The old man's eyes fixed on Xiangzi's military trousers.

"Got swept up by the troops, just managed to escape."

"Ah! Was it dangerous bringing the camels out through the western pass?"

"The soldiers have all gone into the hills. The road's clear."

"Hmm." The old man nodded slowly. "Wait here, I'll get you some water."

Xiangzi followed him inside. In the courtyard he saw four camels. "Old timer, take my three and make it a full string?"

"Hmph! A full string? Thirty years ago I had three strings! Times have changed — who can afford to keep camels anymore!" The old man stopped and stared blankly at the four animals. After a long pause: "Just the other day I was thinking of teaming up with a neighbor to send them out to the northern pastures for the summer. But there are soldiers causing trouble to the east, soldiers to the west — who'd dare make the trip! Keeping them here through the summer, it just breaks your heart to look at them, just breaks your heart — look at all these flies. Come the real heat, add the mosquitoes on top of that, and you'll watch perfectly good animals suffer for nothing. Ah!" The old man kept nodding, as if he had no end of grievances and frustrations.

"Old timer, take my three and make a full string, then send them out to the northern pastures for the summer. Fine healthy animals like these, stuck here all summer — the flies and mosquitoes will half kill them for sure." Xiangzi was almost pleading.

"But who has the money to buy them? These aren't times for keeping camels." "Take them anyway, whatever you can give is fine. I just need to get them off my hands so I can go to the city and make a living."

The old man looked Xiangzi over carefully once more and decided he was no criminal. Then he turned and looked at the animals outside the gate. In his heart he seemed to genuinely like those three camels — he knew perfectly well there was no practical benefit in buying them, but a book lover sees books and wants to buy them, a horse man sees horses and can't bear to walk away, and a man who once had three strings of camels was the same way. Besides, Xiangzi had said he'd sell cheap; a knowledgeable person who gets a bargain tends to forget whether the thing is actually useful.

"Young man, if I had the money to spare, I'd truly want to keep them." The old man spoke honestly.

"Just go ahead and take them — we'll work it out." Xiangzi was so sincere that the old man felt a little embarrassed.

"To be straight with you, young man — thirty years ago these would have been worth three big silver ingots. In times like these, with soldiers and chaos everywhere, I — you'd better go try your luck somewhere else." "Whatever you can give is fine." Xiangzi couldn't think of anything else to say. He understood the old man was being straight with him, but he didn't want to go wandering all over trying to sell camels — he might not find a buyer, and something else might go wrong on top of that.

"You see, twenty or thirty yuan — it's hard to even say it out loud, and it's truly not easy to scrape together either. That's just how things are these days."

Xiangzi's heart sank a little. Twenty or thirty yuan? Still a long way from buying a rickshaw. But first, he wanted to be done with this quickly, and second, he didn't believe he'd be lucky enough to run into another buyer. "Old timer, whatever you can give is fine."

"What do you do for a living, young man? I can tell this isn't your trade."

Xiangzi told him the truth.

"So you risked your life for these animals." The old man felt genuine sympathy for Xiangzi, and his mind was put at ease — they hadn't been stolen. Well, it wasn't far off from stealing, but there was still a layer of soldiers in between. After a disaster brought on by troops, you couldn't judge anything by ordinary standards.

"Here's what I'll do, friend — I'll give you 35 yuan. If I said that's not a bargain, I'd be a dog. And if I could give even one yuan more, I'd be a dog too. I'm over 60. Hmph, what more can I say."

Xiangzi was at a loss. When it came to money, he had never been willing to let a single coin slip by. But after those days among the soldiers, hearing the old man's sincere and heartfelt words, he couldn't bring himself to argue. Besides, 35 silver dollars in hand seemed more reliable than the ten thousand he'd once dreamed of — even if it was true that one life had only bought 35 yuan, which was indeed too little. Just for three big live camels alone, it couldn't be — it absolutely couldn't be — worth only 35 silver dollars. But what could he do. "The camels are yours, old timer. I just have one more thing to ask — find me a shirt and something to eat."

"That's no problem."

Xiangzi drank a long draft of cold water, then took his 35 bright silver dollars, two cornmeal flatbreads, and a tattered white shirt that barely reached his chest, and set off to cover the distance to the city in a single stride.

[1] 谱儿, meaning appearance or manner, with a sense of resemblance.

[2] 大宝, a silver ingot weighing 50 taels.

[3] 放青, letting livestock out to graze on fresh grass.

Four

Xiangzi lay in a small shop in Haidian for three days, his body alternating between chills and fever, his mind drifting in and out of consciousness. A row of purple blisters had formed on his gums, and all he wanted was water — he had no appetite for food. Three days without eating, and the fever finally broke, leaving his body as limp as taffy. It was probably during those three days that someone overheard him mumbling about the three camels in his sleep or delirium. By the time he came to his senses, he was already "Camel Xiangzi."

Ever since he'd come to the city, he'd just been "Xiangzi," as if he had no surname at all. Now, with "Camel" tacked on in front of "Xiangzi," nobody cared even less what his last name was. Whether he had a surname or not, he didn't much care himself. Still, three animals had only fetched him a few yuan, and all he'd gotten out of it was a nickname — he felt like he'd gotten the short end of the deal.

As soon as he could drag himself to his feet, he wanted to go out and have a look around. He hadn't expected his legs to be so useless — he made it to the doorway of the shop and his knees buckled, and he sat down on the ground. He sat there in a daze for a good long while, cold sweat beading on his forehead. He held on a bit longer, then opened his eyes. His stomach let out a rumble, and he felt a twinge of hunger. He got up very slowly and found a wonton peddler. He ordered a bowl of wontons and sat back down on the ground. He took a sip of the broth and felt nauseous, held it in his mouth for a long moment, then forced it down — he didn't want any more. But after a while, the warm broth threaded its way down to his belly like a thin line, and he let out two loud belches. He knew he was going to live.

With a little food in his stomach, he had the presence of mind to look himself over. He'd lost a lot of weight, and his tattered trousers were as dirty as dirty could be. He felt too lazy to move, but he wanted to get himself cleaned up right away — he refused to go back into the city looking like a ghost. But getting cleaned up cost money: a haircut, a change of clothes, shoes and socks — all of it cost money. The thirty-five yuan in his pocket had to stay untouched, every last coin, and even untouched it was still far short of what he needed to buy a rickshaw. But he felt sorry for himself. Though he hadn't been held by the soldiers for very long, looking back on it now, the whole thing felt like a nightmare. That nightmare had aged him considerably, as if he'd suddenly grown several years older all at once. He looked at his big hands and big feet — clearly his own, yet somehow as if he'd just found them somewhere. He felt deeply miserable. He didn't dare think about all the hardship and danger he'd been through, but even without thinking about it, it was still there, the way you know the sky is dark during a stretch of overcast days without even looking up. He felt that his body was something precious, something he shouldn't put through too much suffering anymore. He stood up, knowing full well he was still weak, but felt an urgent need to clean himself up — as if a haircut and a change of clothes would make him strong again on the spot.

After getting himself sorted out, he'd spent only two yuan and twenty cents in all. A plain coarse-cloth shirt and trousers, something close to canvas, cost one yuan; cloth shoes, eighty cents; knitted socks, fifteen cents; and a straw hat, twenty-five cents. He traded his old rags for two packs of matches. Matches in hand, he set off westward along the main road toward Xizhimen. He hadn't gone far before he felt weak and exhausted. But he gritted his teeth. He couldn't take a rickshaw — there was no way he could take a rickshaw, from any angle: what kind of country person would treat eight or ten li as a real distance, let alone someone who pulled rickshaws for a living. Never mind that — to be brought down by such a trifling illness, with his size and strength, would be a joke. Unless he keeled over and couldn't get back up, he'd roll into the city on the ground before he'd give in. If he couldn't walk into the city today, he thought, then Xiangzi was finished. He trusted only his own body, no matter what illness came his way.

He swayed but kept his stride. Not far out of Haidian, stars began to flash before his eyes. He steadied himself against a willow tree and stood there collecting himself for a long while. The world spun and lurched around him, but he refused to sit down. Gradually the spinning settled, and his heart seemed to drift back from somewhere far away and land back in his chest. He wiped the sweat from his forehead and started walking again. He'd already gotten a haircut, already put on new clothes and new shoes — he felt he'd done right by himself. So his legs had to do their part. Walk. He walked all the way to Guanxiang in one stretch. Seeing the bustle of people and vehicles, hearing the jumbled, grating sounds, smelling the dry, stale odors, feeling the soft, filthy grey dust under his feet, Xiangzi wanted to drop down and kiss that grey, foul-smelling earth — that beloved earth, that earth where silver dollars grew. No parents, no brothers, no relatives of any kind — his only friend was this ancient city. This city had given him everything. Even going hungry here was better than being in the countryside. Here there was something to see, something to hear, light and color everywhere, sound everywhere. As long as you were willing to work, there was no end of money here, no end of good things to eat and wear. Even begging here could get you a bowl of rich broth; back in the countryside there was nothing but cornmeal. He'd barely reached the west side of Gaoliang Bridge when he sat down on the riverbank and let fall a few hot tears.

The sun had shifted to the west. The old willows along the river leaned crookedly, their tips catching a glint of gold. There wasn't much water in the river, but it was thick with green algae — like a long, greasy green ribbon, narrow and deep green, giving off a faint, fishy dampness. The wheat on the north bank had begun to ear, short and withered, its leaves coated with a layer of grey dust. On the south bank, the small, delicate lotus leaves floated weakly on the water's surface, tiny bubbles rising and popping around their edges. On the bridge to the east, people and vehicles passed back and forth, looking especially hurried in the slanting sunlight, as if everyone felt a restlessness at the approaching dusk. All of this was deeply interesting and dear to Xiangzi's eyes and ears. Only a little river like this could really be called a river. Trees like these, wheat like this, lotus leaves like these, a bridge like this — only these could truly be called trees, wheat, lotus leaves, and a bridge. Because they all belonged to Beiping.

Sitting there, he felt no rush. Everything before him was familiar and dear. Even if he were to sit there and die, he felt he wouldn't mind. After resting for a good long while, he went to the bridgehead and had a bowl of soft tofu: vinegar, soy sauce, Sichuan pepper oil, and chopped chives, all scalded by the hot, snow-white tofu into a wonderfully fragrant steam — so fragrant that Xiangzi had to hold his breath. Cradling the bowl, gazing at the deep green bits of chive, his hands trembled uncontrollably. He took a bite, and the tofu burned a path open through his insides. He ladled in two more small spoonfuls of chili oil himself. By the time he finished the bowl, sweat had soaked through his waistband. He half-closed his eyes and held out the bowl: "Another one!"

When he stood up, he felt like a person again. The sun still hung low in the west, the river tinged faintly red by the evening glow. He felt so good he wanted to shout. He touched the smooth scar on his face, felt the money in his pocket, took one more look at the sunlight on the corner tower, and then forcibly put his illness out of his mind, put everything out of his mind. As if there were something he'd set his heart on, he decided to walk into the city.
