The city gate tunnel was packed with all kinds of vehicles and all kinds of people. Nobody dared move fast, yet everyone wanted to get through quickly. Whip cracks, shouts, curses, horn blasts, bell rings, and laughter all merged together in the tunnel — which amplified everything like a loudspeaker — into one continuous hum, as if everyone was making some kind of sound, all buzzing together. Xiangzi's big feet stepped left and right, his hands pushing and parting the crowd on both sides, and like a lean, long fish leaping with the current, he squeezed his way into the city. His eyes landed immediately on Xinjieko — the road was so wide, so straight. His eyes lit up, as bright as the glare reflecting off the rooftops to the east. He gave a nod.

His bedroll was still at the Renhe Rickshaw Yard on Xi'anmen Street, so naturally that was where he headed. Having no family, he'd always lived at the yard, even when he wasn't pulling the yard's own rickshaws. The owner of Renhe, Liu Fourth Master, was nearly seventy years old. Old in years, but not in cunning. In his younger days he'd been a warehouse guard, run gambling dens, trafficked in people, and lent money at ruinous rates. All the qualities and skills that line of work required — strength, street smarts, ruthlessness, connections, reputation — Liu Fourth had them all. Back in the Qing dynasty, he'd fought in gang brawls, abducted women, and knelt on iron chains in prison. Kneeling on those iron chains, Liu Fourth hadn't so much as furrowed a brow or begged for mercy. He'd toughed out the whole ordeal — that was his reputation. When he got out of prison, the Republic had just begun, and the power of the police was growing. Liu Fourth could see that the era of street heroes was over — even if Huang Tianba himself were reborn, there wouldn't be much opportunity for him. So he opened a rickshaw yard. Having come up from the rough side of life, he knew how to handle poor people — when to tighten the screws, where to ease up a little. He had a natural talent for managing people. No rickshaw puller dared try to pull a fast one on him. One glare from him, or a hearty laugh, could leave a man completely disoriented, feeling as if one foot was in heaven and the other in hell, with no choice but to let Liu Fourth do as he pleased. By now he had over sixty rickshaws, the worst of them still in seventy or eighty percent condition — he didn't keep broken-down vehicles. His rental rates were higher than other yards, but at the three major festivals he gave two extra days off from payments. Renhe Yard had living quarters, and single men who pulled his rickshaws could stay for free — but they had to keep up with their rent. Anyone who fell behind and tried to drag things out with him would have their bedroll confiscated and be tossed out the door like a broken water jug. But if anyone had an urgent matter or sudden illness, they only had to say the word — he wouldn't hesitate, and would help wholeheartedly through fire and flood. That was his reputation.

Liu Fourth Master had the look of a tiger. Nearly seventy, he still stood straight, and could walk ten or twenty li without trouble. Two large round eyes, a big nose, a square mouth, a pair of prominent canine teeth — when he opened his mouth he looked just like a tiger. He was nearly as tall as Xiangzi, kept his head shaved smooth, and wore no beard. He fancied himself a tiger, but unfortunately had no son — only a daughter in her late thirties with the same tiger spirit. Anyone who knew Liu Fourth Master would also know Tiger Girl. She too had a bold, fierce look about her, which frightened men off. She was a capable hand at helping her father run the business, but no one dared take her as a wife. She was just like a man in every way, even her cursing had a man's directness, and sometimes with more variety. Liu Fourth handled outside affairs, Tiger Girl handled inside ones, and together father and daughter ran Renhe Yard like an iron barrel. Renhe Yard had become an authority in the rickshaw world, and the methods of the Liu family were quoted among rickshaw pullers and owners the way scholars quote the classics.

Before buying his own rickshaw, Xiangzi had pulled rickshaws for Renhe Yard. He'd kept his savings with Liu Fourth for safekeeping. When he'd saved up enough, he took the money back and bought that new rickshaw.

"Liu Fourth Master, take a look at my rickshaw!" Xiangzi brought the new rickshaw to Renhe Yard. The old man glanced at it and nodded: "Not bad."

"I'll still be staying here though — once I land a monthly hire, then I'll move to a private household!" Xiangzi said with some pride.

"Fine." Liu Fourth nodded again.

And so it went: when Xiangzi had a monthly hire, he'd go stay at the employer's home; when that fell through and he went back to pulling individual fares, he'd stay at Renhe Yard.

That Xiangzi could stay at Renhe Yard without pulling Liu Fourth's own rickshaws was, in the eyes of the other pullers, quite an unusual thing. Some even speculated that Xiangzi must be a relative of old Liu's. Others said that old Liu had probably taken a liking to Xiangzi and was thinking of getting him as a live-in son-in-law for Tiger Girl. Mixed in with the envy in these guesses was the thought that if it really were true, then when Liu Fourth died, Renhe Yard would surely go to Xiangzi. This kept them to idle speculation — they didn't dare say anything unpleasant to Xiangzi's face. In truth, old Liu's favorable treatment of Xiangzi had a different account behind it. Xiangzi was the kind of person who, in a new environment, still kept his old habits. If he'd gone and joined the army, he certainly wouldn't have put on that tiger-skin uniform and immediately started bullying people by playing dumb. At the yard, he never sat idle — the moment his sweat dried, he'd find something to do. He'd wipe down the rickshaws, pump up the tires, dry the rain covers, oil the parts... without anyone telling him to, he did it of his own accord, and did it cheerfully, as if it were the finest kind of entertainment. The yard had around twenty pullers living there more or less permanently. After turning in their rickshaws, the others would either sit around chatting or sleep with their heads covered. Xiangzi — only Xiangzi's hands were never idle. At first, everyone assumed he was trying to curry favor with Liu Fourth, fawning like a dog. After a few days, they could see he had no intention of ingratiating himself — he was simply genuine and natural about it, and there was nothing more to say. Old Liu had never praised him once, never given him a second glance. But the old man kept his own tally in his head. He knew Xiangzi was a capable hand, and even if he wasn't pulling his rickshaws, he still wanted Xiangzi around the yard. With Xiangzi there, for one thing, the courtyard and the entrance were always swept spotlessly clean. Tiger Girl liked this big, simple fellow even more. Whatever she said, Xiangzi always listened carefully and never argued back. The other pullers, worn down by hardship, always spoke with an edge. She wasn't afraid of them, but she didn't want to bother with them either. So her words were all saved for Xiangzi. When Xiangzi went off to do a monthly hire, both father and daughter seemed to lose a friend. And when he came back, even old Liu's cursing seemed somehow more spirited and good-natured.

Xiangzi walked into Renhe Yard with his two packs of matches. It wasn't dark yet, and the Liu family father and daughter were eating dinner. When she saw him come in, Tiger Girl put down her chopsticks: "Xiangzi! Did a wolf carry you off, or did you go to Africa to dig for gold?" "Hmph!" Xiangzi couldn't get any words out.

Liu Fourth Master's big round eyes swept over Xiangzi and said nothing. Xiangzi, wearing his new straw hat, sat down across from them.

"If you haven't eaten yet, join us!" Tiger Girl said, as if welcoming a good friend.

Xiangzi didn't move, but suddenly felt a warmth inside him that he couldn't quite name. He'd always thought of Renhe Yard as home: when pulling a monthly hire, the employer kept changing; when pulling individual fares, the passengers changed from one moment to the next;Only here had always let him stay, and people here had always chatted idly with him. Now he had just barely escaped with his life, and here he was back among familiar faces, being fed a meal on top of it — he almost suspected they were trying to trick him, yet he also almost felt like crying.

"I just had two bowls of tofu!" he said, making a small gesture of politeness.

"Where have you been?" Liu Si-ye's big round eyes were still fixed on Xiangzi. "Where's the rickshaw?" "The rickshaw?" Xiangzi spat.

"Come eat a bowl of rice first! It won't kill you! What good are two bowls of tofu?!" Tigress grabbed him and pulled him over, the way an older sister-in-law might dote on a younger brother-in-law. Xiangzi didn't reach for a bowl — he first pulled out his money: "Si-ye, hold onto this for me, thirty yuan." He put the loose change back in his pocket.

Liu Si-ye raised an eyebrow to ask, "Where'd it come from?"

Xiangzi ate while telling the story of how he'd been taken by soldiers.

"Hmph, you foolish boy!" Liu Si-ye shook his head when he finished. "Bring them into the city and sell them to the slaughterhouse — that's over ten yuan a head; if it were winter when the camel wool is full, three of them would fetch sixty yuan!"

Xiangzi had already felt a little regret, and hearing this made him feel even worse. But then he thought about it — selling three living animals to the slaughterhouse to be butchered felt wrong somehow; he and the camels had all escaped together, so they all deserved to live. He said nothing, and his heart grew calm.

Tigress cleared away the dishes, and Liu Si-ye tilted his head back as if trying to recall something. Suddenly he smiled, revealing two tiger teeth that only seemed to grow sturdier with age: "You fool, you said you fell ill in Haidian? Why didn't you just come straight back along the Huangcun road?"

"I came back by way of the Western Hills — I was afraid of being caught on the main road. If the people in the village had figured things out, they might have taken me for a deserter!"

Liu Si-ye smiled, his eyes turning inward for a moment. He was worried there might be something fishy about Xiangzi's story — what if those thirty yuan had been stolen? He didn't want to be holding stolen goods for someone. In his own youth he had done all manner of unlawful things; now he considered himself reformed, and knew he had to be careful — and knew exactly how to be careful. Xiangzi's account had only this one loose thread, but Xiangzi explained it away without the slightest hesitation, and the old man was reassured.

"So what do you want to do?" the old man said, pointing at the money.

"Whatever you think!"

"Buy another rickshaw?" The old man showed his tiger teeth again, as if to say: "You buy your own rickshaw and still live here for free?!"

"Not enough! If I'm buying, I'm buying new!" Xiangzi wasn't looking at Liu Si-ye's teeth — he was only looking into his own heart.

"I'll lend it to you — one percent interest. Anyone else would pay two and a half!"

Xiangzi shook his head.

"Borrowing from a rickshaw depot at loan-shark rates is worse than paying me one percent!"

"I'm not borrowing at any rate," Xiangzi said absently, "I'll save up slowly, and when I've got enough, I'll pay cash for cash goods!"

The old man looked at Xiangzi the way you might look at a strange character in a book — infuriating, yet impossible to get angry at. After a moment he picked up the money: "Thirty? Don't try to pull a fast one on me!"

"It's right!" Xiangzi stood up. "I'm going to sleep. Here's a box of matches for you, old man!" He set a box of matches on the table, then paused: "No need to tell anyone — about the camels!"

Five

Old Liu hadn't actually spread the word for Xiangzi, but the camel story traveled quickly from Haidian into the city. Before, though no one could find fault with Xiangzi, his stubborn streak had made them think of him as somewhat unsociable, a bit of an odd one out. But after "Camel Xiangzi" spread around, even though Xiangzi still kept his head down and worked without much warmth, people began to see him in a different light. Some said he had picked up a gold watch, some said he had gotten three hundred silver dollars for nothing, and those who claimed to know the most accurate version nodded and said he had hauled thirty camels back from the Western Hills! The stories differed, but the conclusion was the same — Xiangzi had come into a windfall! For someone who had come into a windfall, no matter how much of a "loner" the fellow was, people naturally showed respect. Earning money through hard labor was so difficult that everyone hoped to stumble into a windfall. And since windfalls were so rare, anyone lucky enough to get one must be extraordinary — blessed with great fortune and a charmed life. So Xiangzi's silence and unsociability transformed into the reticence of a man of distinction. He was supposed to be that way, and they were the ones who ought to be currying favor with him. "Come on, Xiangzi! Tell us, tell us how you made your fortune!" He heard words like these every day. He never said a word. Only when pushed to the limit, when the scar on his face began to redden, would he say, "Fortune — where the hell is my rickshaw?"

Right, that was true — where had his rickshaw gone? People began to think about it. But worrying on someone else's behalf never comes as naturally as rejoicing for them, so people forgot about Xiangzi's rickshaw and went back to thinking about his good luck. After some time, when everyone saw that Xiangzi was still pulling a rickshaw and hadn't changed trades or bought a house or land, they cooled toward him somewhat, and when they mentioned Camel Xiangzi, they no longer asked why he was called "Camel" — as if that had simply always been his name.

Xiangzi himself had not so easily brushed the matter aside and forgotten it. He was desperate to buy a new rickshaw as soon as possible, and the more anxious he felt, the more he thought about his original one. He worked tirelessly all day long without complaint, but as he worked, the memory would come back to him. When it did, he felt a heaviness in his chest, and he couldn't help thinking — what good was striving? The world didn't become any fairer just because he strove. What right did anyone have to take his rickshaw from him like that? Even if he got another one right away, who was to say the same thing wouldn't happen again? The past felt like a nightmare, and it almost made him afraid to hope for the future. Sometimes he watched others drinking, smoking, and visiting brothels, and he almost felt a twinge of envy. If striving was useless, why not enjoy the present? They were right. He — even if he didn't go to brothels just yet — ought to at least have a couple of drinks and take it easy. Tobacco and alcohol now seemed to hold a particular pull for him; he felt these two things cost little and would surely comfort him, letting him keep pushing forward through hardship while forgetting the pain of the past.

But still he didn't dare touch them. He had to save every extra coin he could — only that way could he buy his own rickshaw sooner. Even if he bought one today and lost it tomorrow, he still had to buy one. This was his ambition, his hope, even his religion. Without his own rickshaw, he felt he was living for nothing. He never thought about becoming an official, getting rich, or acquiring property. His ability was pulling a rickshaw; his most reliable hope was to buy one — without buying his own rickshaw, he couldn't face himself. He thought about this day and night, calculating his money; if he ever forgot about it for a moment, he felt he had forgotten himself, and felt he was nothing but a running animal with no prospects and no humanity. No matter how fine a rickshaw it was, if it was rented, he never pulled it with any spirit — it felt as unnatural as carrying a stone on his back. Even with a rented rickshaw he didn't slack off, always keeping it spotlessly clean for the owner, never bumping or crashing it carelessly; but this was only caution, not joy. Yes — tending to your own rickshaw, like counting your own money, was true happiness. He still had to go without tobacco and alcohol, and he couldn't even afford to drink decent tea. At the teahouse, a respectable rickshaw puller like him, after a hard sprint, would normally treat himself to a ten-copper packet of tea with two packets of white sugar, to restore energy and cool the body's heat. When he ran until sweat dripped from his earlobes and his chest felt a burning sensation, he truly wanted to do the same; it wasn't a matter of habit or show — he genuinely needed those two bowls of tea to settle himself. But even when the thought came, he still drank the one-copper packet of tea dust. Sometimes he truly wanted to scold himself for being so hard on himself; but what choice did a rickshaw puller have if he wanted to save a little money each month? He hardened his heart. Buy the rickshaw first, buy the rickshaw first! Having a rickshaw would make up for everything!

As tight-fisted as he was with spending, Xiangzi was equally relentless about earning. Without a monthly contract, he pulled all day — out early, back late — and he wouldn't call it a day until he had pulled in a set amount of money, regardless of the time, regardless of his legs; sometimes he pushed straight through, pulling for a full day and night. Before, he had been unwilling to steal business from others, especially the old and the weak; with his body and his rickshaw, competing with them for fares — could they even get a share? Now he no longer cared much about that. He only saw money — every extra coin counted — and he didn't care whether the work was easy or hard, or who he was taking business from; he just grabbed a fare and went, like a starving wild beast. Once he had a fare he ran, and his heart felt a little easier, feeling that only by never standing still could he have any hope of buying a rickshaw. Little by little, the reputation of Camel Xiangzi fell far short of what it had been when he was simply Xiangzi. Many times he snatched a fare and ran, with a stream of curses following behind him. He didn't talk back — he just lowered his head and sprinted, saying to himself: "If it weren't for buying a rickshaw, I'd never stoop this low!" He seemed to use these words to seek forgiveness from everyone, yet he wouldn't say them aloud. At the rickshaw stand or in the teahouse, he saw people glaring at him; he wanted to explain himself, but seeing how cold they were toward him, and given that he had never joined them for drinks, gambling, chess, or idle talk, his words stayed locked inside him with nowhere to go. Embarrassment gradually turned to shame and anger, and his temper rose; they glared at him, and he glared back. Thinking of how respectfully everyone had treated him when he first came back from the hills, and how lightly they now regarded him, he felt even worse. Alone with a pot of tea — if he happened to be at a teahouse — or alone counting the copper coins he had just earned — if he was at the rickshaw stand — he used every ounce of strength to press his anger down. He didn't want to fight, though he wasn't afraid of it. The others weren't afraid of fighting either, but picking a fight with Xiangzi was something worth thinking twice about — none of them was a match for him one-on-one, and a gang beating one man wasn't exactly honorable. Forcing himself to hold his temper, he could think of no other way but to endure for now, and wait until he had bought his rickshaw — then things would be different. With his own rickshaw, he wouldn't have to worry about the daily rental first thing every morning, and he could naturally carry himself with ease, no longer offending people by snatching fares. With this thought settled, he glanced at the others as if to say: we'll see!

For his own sake, he shouldn't have been pushing himself so hard. After escaping back to the city, he had started pulling a rickshaw before he had fully recovered from his illness; though he never showed any weakness, he often felt exhausted. Exhausted as he was, he didn't dare rest — he always figured that sweating out a few more runs would shake off the fatigue. As for food, he didn't dare be picky, but he didn't dare eat more or eat better either. He could see he had lost a lot of weight, but his frame was still tall and large, his bones still hard and sturdy, and he felt reassured. He always assumed that because he was bigger than others, he could endure more hardship than others — as if it never occurred to him that a larger frame, bearing more strain, needed more nourishment. Tigress had already warned him several times: "If you keep this up, you cough up blood and that's your own problem!"

He understood perfectly well that this was good advice, but because things weren't going his way and his body was poorly nourished, he was short-tempered. He narrowed his eyes slightly: "If I don't push like this, when will I ever buy a rickshaw?"

If anyone else had narrowed their eyes at her like that, Tigress would have cursed them out for half a day at least; with Xiangzi, she was genuinely, wholeheartedly patient and caring. She just pursed her lips: "Buying a rickshaw can wait — do you think you're made of iron! You should take a proper three days off!" Seeing that Xiangzi wasn't taking it in: "Fine, you have your own stubborn ideas — just don't blame me when you're dead!"

Liu Si-ye also had some misgivings about Xiangzi: Xiangzi's relentless grind, out at dawn and back at dusk, was naturally hard on his rickshaw. Although renting by the full day meant no set time limit — you could go out and come back whenever you liked — if everyone worked it like Xiangzi, a rickshaw would wear out at least half a year sooner; no matter how sturdy a thing was, it couldn't hold up under constant hammering! On top of that, Xiangzi was so focused on his mad dash that he rarely had time to help wipe down the rickshaw or anything like that — another loss. The old man was a little displeased. But he said nothing — renting by the full day with no time limit was standard practice; helping to maintain the rickshaws was a favor, not an obligation; and given his own standing and reputation, he couldn't afford to make a fool of himself by saying something to Xiangzi. He could only let a hint of dissatisfaction show at the corner of his eye while keeping his mouth firmly shut. Sometimes he was quite tempted to throw Xiangzi out; then he'd look at his daughter and not dare. He had no intention whatsoever of treating Xiangzi as a prospective son-in-law, but since his daughter was fond of this stubborn young man, he couldn't very well make trouble. He had only this one daughter, and it was clear she had no hope of getting married — he couldn't drive away her friend on top of that. Truth be told, Tigress was so capable and useful that he genuinely didn't want her to marry; this selfish thought made him feel he had rather let her down, and so he was somewhat afraid of her. The old man had feared nothing in heaven or earth his whole life, yet in his old age he had come to fear his own daughter — he worked out a kind of reasoning for himself in his mild embarrassment: as long as he feared someone, it proved he was not entirely lawless. With this fact established, perhaps he would not meet a bad end when his time came. Very well — having acknowledged that he ought to fear his daughter, he was not about to drive Xiangzi away. This was not to say, of course, that he would simply let his daughter do as she pleased, to the point of marrying Xiangzi. No. He could see that his daughter might not be without such intentions, but Xiangzi had not dared to make any move toward her.So he just needed to be a bit more careful — no point in upsetting his daughter first. Xiangzi hadn't noticed the old man's expression; he had no time to pay attention to such idle matters. If he had any desire to leave Renhe Garage, it was definitely not out of spite, but out of hope for a monthly contract job. He had grown a little tired of pulling random fares — partly because scrambling for business made others look down on him, and partly because his daily income was unpredictable, more today, less tomorrow, with no way of knowing when he'd save up enough to buy his own rickshaw. He wanted something he could count on in his heart. Even if the amount left over was small, as long as he could rely on a fixed sum each month, he'd feel hopeful and at ease. He was the kind of person who liked everything in its proper place.

He landed a monthly contract job. Hmm, just as frustrating as pulling random fares! This time it was at the Yang household. Mr. Yang was from Shanghai, the first Mrs. Yang was from Tianjin, and the second Mrs. Yang was from Suzhou. One husband, two wives, with accents from all over the place and more children than anyone could count. On his very first day of work, Xiangzi nearly lost his mind. First thing in the morning, the first Mrs. Yang took the rickshaw to the market to buy groceries. When she returned, he had to take the young masters and misses to school one by one — some in middle school, some in elementary school, some in kindergarten. Different schools, different ages, different faces, but all equally insufferable, especially when sitting in the rickshaw; even the best-behaved one had more tricks up their sleeve than a monkey. After sending all the children off, he took Mr. Yang to his office. After dropping him off, he rushed back to take the second Mrs. Yang to Dong'an Market or to visit relatives and friends. Then he went back to pick up the children for lunch at home. After lunch, he sent them off again. When he returned from dropping off the children, Xiangzi thought he could finally eat, but the first Mrs. Yang called out in her Tianjin accent, telling him to haul water. The Yang household had drinking water delivered, but the brackish water for washing clothes was the rickshaw puller's job to carry. This task was outside the agreed terms, but Xiangzi, wanting to keep things smooth, didn't dare argue and silently filled the water vat without a word. He had just set down the water buckets and was about to pick up his rice bowl when the second Mrs. Yang called him to go buy things. The first and second Mrs. Yang had never gotten along, yet when it came to running the household, their policies were perfectly aligned — one being that servants must never be idle for a moment, and the other being that they couldn't stand watching servants eat. Xiangzi didn't know this, and assumed the household just happened to be unusually busy on his first day, so again he said nothing, and dug into his own pocket to buy a few sesame flatbreads. He treasured money like his own life, but to keep the job, he had to steel himself.

When he returned from buying things, the first Mrs. Yang told him to sweep the courtyard. The master, the first wife, and the second wife of the Yang household all dressed impeccably when going out, yet the inside of the house and the courtyard were one big garbage heap. Xiangzi felt sick looking at the courtyard, so he just focused on cleaning it and forgot that a rickshaw puller wasn't supposed to double as a handyman. After the courtyard was tidied up, the second Mrs. Yang asked him to go ahead and sweep the rooms inside as well. Xiangzi didn't refuse that either — what astonished him was how two such well-dressed and presentable women could live in rooms so filthy you could barely set foot in them. After tidying up the rooms too, the second Mrs. Yang handed him a little mud-caked demon of a child who had just turned one year old. He was at a loss. He was capable of any kind of physical labor, but he had never held a baby. He cradled the little young master in both hands — if he held too loosely, he was afraid the child would slip; if he held too tight, he was afraid of hurting the little bones. He broke into a sweat. He thought of handing this precious bundle over to Zhang Ma — a big-footed woman from Jiangbei. He found her, and she immediately gave him a good tongue-lashing. The Yang household went through servants every three to five days; the master and mistresses always treated servants as household slaves, as if only by wringing the life out of poor people could they feel they were getting their money's worth. Zhang Ma was the only one who had stayed with them for five or six years, and the sole reason was that she dared to curse anyone out loud — master or mistress, it didn't matter; cross her and she'd let loose. Mr. Yang had his Shanghai-style curses, vicious and cutting; the first Mrs. Yang had her Tianjin voice, powerful and booming; the second Mrs. Yang had her Suzhou lilt, smooth and flowing — they had always been unbeatable. But when they met Zhang Ma's wild ferocity, they began to feel a sense of meeting their match, a warrior encountering a worthy rival, and so they rather appreciated her and kept her on as a trusted aide.

Xiangzi had grown up in the rural north and had a strong aversion to casual cursing in the streets. But he didn't dare hit Zhang Ma — a real man doesn't fight with women — and he didn't want to talk back either. He just glared at her. Zhang Ma fell silent, as if she sensed some danger. Just at that moment, the first Mrs. Yang called for Xiangzi to go pick up the children. He quickly took the muddy little one back to the second Mrs. Yang. The second Mrs. Yang took it as a deliberate slight and burst out cursing him on the spot. The first Mrs. Yang had never been happy about Xiangzi holding the second Mrs. Yang's child in the first place; hearing the second Mrs. Yang curse him, she too opened up her smooth, oily voice and joined in the cursing — also directed at him. Xiangzi had become a shield for everyone's abuse. He hurriedly pulled the rickshaw out and left, seemingly forgetting even to feel angry, because he had never seen anything like this before, and being hit with it all at once left him genuinely dizzy.

He ferried the children back in batches, and the courtyard became livelier than a marketplace — the cursing of three women, the crying of a pack of children, as chaotic as Dashilan when a show lets out, and chaotic in a way that made no sense at all. Fortunately he still had to go pick up Mr. Yang, so he rushed out again; the shouting of people and horses on the street seemed somehow easier to bear than the chaos inside the house.

It wasn't until midnight that Xiangzi found a moment to heave a sigh. He felt more than just physical exhaustion — his head kept buzzing. The Yang household's young and old had all gone to sleep, yet his ears still seemed to ring with the master's and mistresses' shouts and curses, like three different gramophones spinning at once inside his chest, making him feel frantic. Too tired to think anymore, he just wanted to sleep. The moment he stepped into his little room, his heart sank and his drowsiness vanished. It was a gatehouse room divided in two by a wooden partition, with two doors. Zhang Ma lived on one side, he on the other. There was no lamp inside; a small window about two feet wide in the wall facing the street happened to sit directly below a streetlamp, letting in a little light. The room was damp and foul-smelling, the dirt floor as thick as a copper coin, and against the wall lay a set of bed planks with nothing else. He felt the planks and realized that if he laid his head down flat, his feet would press against the wall, and if he stretched his legs out, he'd have to sit halfway up. He didn't know how to sleep curled up like an ingot. After thinking it over, he pulled the planks diagonally so that both ends pointed toward the corners, and that way he could lay his head flat and let his legs dangle off the edge — good enough for one night.

He dragged his bedding in from the doorway, spread it out roughly, and lay down. His legs hung in the air; it felt unnatural and he couldn't sleep. He forced his eyes shut and consoled himself: sleep, you've got to be up early tomorrow. You've endured every kind of hardship — why balk at this one? Never mind the poor food and the exhausting work; maybe there'll be card games, hosting guests, dinner parties[1] from time to time. What did you come out here for, Xiangzi? Isn't it for money? As long as the money comes in, you've got to put up with anything! With that thought, he felt much more at ease, and even the smell of the room didn't seem as bad as before. He slowly drifted off to sleep. Half-asleep, he sensed bedbugs, but couldn't be bothered to deal with them.

Two days passed, and Xiangzi's heart had gone completely cold. But on the fourth day, female guests arrived and Zhang Ma was busy setting up the mahjong table. His heart felt like a small frozen lake suddenly touched by a spring breeze. Once the mistresses started playing mahjong, all the children were handed over to the servants; since Zhang Ma had to attend to tea, cigarettes, and hand towels, that whole pack of little monkeys naturally fell under Xiangzi's command. He disliked those monkeys, but he snuck a glance into the room and saw the first Mrs. Yang keeping track of the money, looking quite serious about it. He said to himself: never mind how fierce this woman is — maybe she's not foolish, maybe she knows to use moments like this to get the servants an extra thirty or fifty cents. He summoned an extra measure of patience with the little monkeys; for the sake of the tip money, he had to treat these little brats like proper young masters and misses.

When the mahjong game broke up, the mistress told him to take the guests home. Two female guests needed to leave at the same time, so an extra rickshaw had to be hired. Xiangzi called one over, and the first Mrs. Yang fumbled all over her robes looking for money, ready to pay the guests' fare on their behalf. The guests politely declined a couple of times, and the first Mrs. Yang shouted as if her life depended on it: "What are you doing, dear! You're at my place — can't I even cover a rickshaw fare! Dear! Get in!" Only then did she finally fish out one dime.

Xiangzi watched it all clearly — when she handed over that one dime, the mistress's hand was trembling slightly.

After seeing the guests off and helping Zhang Ma put away the mahjong table and everything else, Xiangzi glanced at the mistress. The mistress told Zhang Ma to fetch some hot water, and once Zhang Ma had stepped out the door, she took out a dime: "Here, take it — stop staring at me like that!"

Xiangzi's face suddenly flushed dark red. He straightened up, as if his head might hit the ceiling, snatched the bill, and slapped it against the mistress's fat face: "Give me four days' wages!" "What's gotten into you?" the mistress said, then looked at Xiangzi again and fell silent, and paid him his four days' wages. He had barely stepped out the front gate with his bedroll when he heard a torrent of cursing erupt from inside the courtyard.

[1] Dinner party, meaning a banquet.

6

On an early autumn evening, amid starlight and leaf shadows, a gentle breeze stirred. Xiangzi raised his head, gazed at the high and distant Milky Way, and let out a sigh. The night was so cool and his chest was so broad, yet he felt as though the air wasn't enough — his chest was terribly stifled. He wanted to sit down and cry his heart out. With his physique, his endurance, his drive to better himself — to be treated like a pig or a dog, to be unable to hold down a single job. He didn't just resent the Yang household; he felt a vague, formless sense of hopelessness, a fear that he might never amount to anything for the rest of his life. Carrying his bedroll, he walked slower and slower, as if he were no longer the Xiangzi who could pick up his legs and run ten miles without a thought.

Out on the main street, there were few pedestrians, but the streetlamps were bright, and he felt all the more adrift and lost, not knowing where to go. Where to? Back to Renhe Garage, of course. Yet his heart ached a little at the thought. For those in business or doing physical labor, it wasn't the lack of work that hurt — it was having a steady client and then losing the job, like a customer walking into a restaurant or barbershop, taking one look, and walking back out. Xiangzi knew perfectly well that starting and quitting jobs was a common thing, that if one place wouldn't keep you, another would. But he had swallowed his pride to hold onto this job, had sacrificed his dignity for the sake of buying a rickshaw, and in the end it had lasted only three and a half days — just like those seasoned veterans who drifted from household to household. That made him feel wretched. He almost felt too ashamed to walk back into Renhe Garage and become a joke for everyone: "Look at that — turns out Camel Xiangzi also got the boot in three and a half days, hah!"

If not Renhe Garage, then where? To keep himself from brooding over it, he walked straight toward Xi'an Men Street. The front of Renhe Garage faced the street with three shop-front rooms; the middle one served as the office, where rickshaw pullers could come in to settle accounts or handle business, but they weren't allowed to cut through freely, since the east and west rooms were the sleeping quarters of Liu and his daughter. Beside the west room was a carriage gate — two large green-lacquered doors, with a thick iron bar arching above them and a very bright, shade-less electric light hanging beneath it, and below the light, four gilded characters on an iron plate: "Renhe Rickshaw Garage." Pullers came and went through this gate when taking out or returning rickshaws. The deep green lacquer of the gate and the gold characters above it all gleamed under that stark white electric light. The rickshaws coming and going were equally handsome — black-lacquered or yellow-lacquered, all glossy and shining, paired with snow-white seat covers, and even the pullers themselves felt a certain pride, as if they considered themselves the aristocracy among rickshaw men. Through the main gate, past the west room of the front section, lay a large square courtyard with an old scholar tree in the middle. The rooms along the east and west sides were open-fronted, used for storing rickshaws; the south rooms and the few small rooms in the small yard behind them were all quarters for the pullers.

It was probably past eleven. Xiangzi spotted Renhe Garage's very bright yet strangely solitary lamp. The office and the east room were dark, but the west room still had its light on. He knew Tiger Girl hadn't gone to sleep yet. He wanted to slip in quietly without being seen by her; precisely because she had always thought well of him, he didn't want her to be the first to witness his failure.

He had just pulled the rickshaw up to her window when Tiger Girl came out through the carriage gate:

"Oh, Xiangzi? How come—" She was about to ask more, but seeing Xiangzi's dejected look and the bedroll on the rickshaw, she swallowed the rest of her words.

What you fear most is what finds you — the shame and frustration inside Xiangzi congealed into a lump, and he stopped dead in his tracks, standing there in a daze. Unable to say a word, he stared blankly at Tiger Girl. She looked different today — whether it was the electric light or that she had powdered her face, it was much whiter than usual; with her face a shade lighter, much of her fierce air was softened. Her lips were indeed touched with a little rouge, and even Tiger Girl carried a hint of allure. Seeing this, Xiangzi felt strangely unsettled and even more flustered inside, because he had never thought of her as a woman before, and now, suddenly confronted with those red lips, he felt an unexpected twinge of self-consciousness. She wore a light green silk short jacket on top and a pair of wide-legged thin trousers in dark crepe below. The green jacket shimmered under the electric light with a soft, faintly melancholy silken sheen; being short, it revealed a tiny strip of white trouser waistband, making the green stand out all the more clean and bright. The wide black trousers below stirred gently in the breeze, like some shadowy presence trying to shake free of the glaring light and merge with the darkness of the night. Xiangzi didn't dare look any longer and lowered his head in a daze, the small glowing image of the green jacket still lingering in his mind. Given the Liu family's means, she could easily have worn silk and satin every day, but since she dealt with rickshaw pullers all day long, she always wore plain cotton clothes; even when there was some pattern, on cotton it didn't draw the eye. Xiangzi felt as though he were seeing something entirely new — familiar, yet new,So his heart was in a bit of a muddle.

Already troubled inside, and now under the glare of that powerful light he had encountered this strange, living creature — he was at a loss. He himself couldn't bring himself to move, yet he hoped Tiger Girl would hurry inside, or give him something to do. He simply couldn't bear this kind of torment — a torment that resembled nothing in particular, yet was deeply distressing.

"Hey!" She stepped forward, her voice low: "Don't just stand there! Go put the rickshaw away, then come right back. I've got something to say to you. Inside."

He was used to doing things for her, so he had no choice but to obey. But today she was different from usual, and he very much wanted to think it over. Standing there trying to think felt awkward and stiff. With no idea what to do, he pulled the rickshaw in. He glanced at the south room — no lights, everyone was probably asleep, or maybe there were still some rickshaws not yet put away. He parked the rickshaw, then turned back to her door. Suddenly, his heart began to pound.

"Come in, I've got something to tell you!" She poked her head out, half smiling, half annoyed. He walked in slowly.

On the table sat a few not-quite-ripe white pears, their skin still tinged with green. A wine pot, three white porcelain cups. A large platter piled with half a soy-braised chicken, along with smoked liver, spiced tripe, and other such food.

"Look," Tiger Girl pointed him to a chair, waited for him to sit down, then said: "Look, I'm treating myself today, so you eat too!" As she spoke, she poured him a cup of liquor. The sharp bite of the baijiu mingled with the smell of smoked and braised meat, making the air feel especially thick and heavy. "Drink up, and eat some of that chicken. I've already eaten, no need to be polite! I just did a divination with dominoes and knew you'd be back — how's that for accuracy?"

"I don't drink," Xiangzi stared blankly at the cup.

"Then get out. I'm being kind and you don't appreciate it? You big dumb camel! It won't kill you! Even I can drink four liang. Don't believe me? Watch!" She picked up the cup, poured more than half of it down her throat, squeezed her eyes shut, and let out a breath. Holding the cup out: "You drink! Or I'll grab your ear and pour it in myself!"

Xiangzi was full of pent-up grievances with nowhere to vent them, and faced with this teasing, he really wanted to glare at her. But he knew Tiger Girl had always treated him well, and she was straightforward with everyone — he shouldn't offend her. Since he didn't want to offend her, he thought again, and figured he might as well pour out his troubles to her. He had never been much of a talker, but today it felt as though a thousand words were bottled up inside him, and he wouldn't feel right until he let them out. Thinking this, he felt that Tiger Girl wasn't mocking him at all, but looking out for him in her blunt way. He took the cup from her and drained it. A wave of heat moved slowly, precisely, and forcefully downward. He stretched out his neck, straightened his chest, and let out two rather uncomfortable belches.

Tiger Girl laughed. He had barely managed to get that mouthful of liquor down when he heard her laugh, and quickly glanced toward the east room.

"Nobody there," she stifled her laughter, though the smile remained on her face. "The old man went to his aunt's birthday celebration — he'll be gone two or three days. His aunt lives in Nanyuan." As she spoke, she refilled his cup.

Hearing this, something shifted in his mind, and he sensed that somewhere something wasn't quite right. At the same time, he couldn't bring himself to leave. Her face was so close to his, her clothes so clean and smooth, her lips so red — all of it stirred in him a new kind of excitement. She was still the same old homely woman, yet she seemed to have gained a certain vitality, as if she had suddenly become someone else — still her, but with something more. He didn't dare examine this new something too closely, and yet he couldn't casually accept it, nor could he bring himself to refuse it. His face flushed. As if to bolster his own courage, he drank another mouthful. A moment ago he had wanted to confide his troubles to her — now he had forgotten all about that. Red-faced, he couldn't help stealing more glances at her. The more he looked, the more confused he felt inside, the more that something he couldn't quite name seemed to radiate from her, the more a hot, burning force seemed to pass between them, until gradually she became something abstract. He warned himself to be careful, yet he also wanted to be bold. He drank three cups in a row and forgot what caution even meant. Gazing at her in a daze, he felt, for no reason he could name, an immense sense of release and daring — a fierce, courageous urge to seize some new experience and pleasure right then and there. Ordinarily he was a little afraid of her. Now there was nothing frightening about her at all. He himself had become the one with authority and strength, as if he could hold her in his hands like a cat. The lamp in the room went out. The sky above was very dark. Now and then a star or two pierced the Milky Way, or streaked into the darkness, trailing tails of reddish or whitish light — drifting lightly or cutting sharply, plunging straight down or sweeping sideways, sometimes flickering and trembling, stirring the sky with warmth and motion, scattering flashes of brilliance through the dark. Sometimes one or two stars, sometimes several at once, fell together, making the still autumn sky shiver faintly, throwing the ten thousand stars into momentary confusion. Sometimes a single great star slashed across the corner of the sky, its tail enormously long, scattering sparks — red, then fading to yellow — and in its final thrust, blazed a white streak across the sky's edge in a kind of wild ecstasy, as if it had torn through layer upon layer of darkness and let in a lingering wash of milky light. As the last glow faded, the darkness seemed to sway a few times, then closed back in, and the quiet, languid stars returned to their places, smiling faintly in the autumn breeze. On the ground, autumn fireflies drifted in search of mates, playing their own game of stars.

The next day, Xiangzi rose very early, took up the rickshaw shafts, and headed out. His head and throat ached a little — the result of drinking for the first time — but he paid it no mind. Sitting at the entrance of a small alley, the cool morning breeze blowing against his head, he knew the headache would pass before long. But there were other things weighing on his heart, making him feel stifled, with no way to shake them loose for the moment. What had happened the night before left him confused, ashamed, troubled, and with a sense of something dangerous.

He couldn't make sense of Tiger Girl. She was no longer a virgin — Xiangzi had only learned this a few hours ago. He had always respected her, and had never heard anything improper about her. Though she was casual and straightforward with everyone, no one had ever gossiped about her behind her back. Even among the rickshaw pullers, those who spoke ill of her only called her fierce — nothing else. So why had last night happened?

This being unclear, Xiangzi also had doubts about the night's events. She had known he wasn't at the rickshaw yard — how could she have been waiting specifically for him? If it could have been anyone at all... Xiangzi lowered his head. He had come from the countryside, and though he had never given much thought to marriage, he had always had a rough plan in mind: if he ever got his own rickshaw, settled into a more comfortable life, and decided he wanted to marry, he would go back to the village and find a young, strong girl — one who could endure hardship, wash clothes, and do housework. Boys his age, even those with someone keeping an eye on them, who didn't sneak off to the brothels? Xiangzi had never gone along with that. For one thing, he thought of himself as a man of self-respect who couldn't throw money away on women. For another, he had seen with his own eyes the fools who wasted their money — some barely eighteen or nineteen — standing in the latrine with their foreheads against the wall, unable to pass water. And finally, he had to keep himself clean to be worthy of his future wife, because when the time came to marry, he would marry a girl of spotless reputation, so he had to be the same kind of person himself. But now, now... When he thought of Tiger Girl as a friend, she was genuinely decent. But as a woman — she was ugly, old, vicious, and shameless! Even thinking of those soldiers who had stolen his rickshaw and nearly cost him his life didn't fill him with as much hatred and disgust as thinking of her did. She had destroyed that last trace of freshness he had brought with him from the countryside. He had become a man who sneaked around with women.

And then there was this: if word got out and Liu Si found out, what then? Did Liu Si know his daughter was damaged goods? If he didn't know, wouldn't Xiangzi be left holding the blame alone? If he had known all along and hadn't bothered to rein in his daughter, then what kind of people were this father and daughter? And if Xiangzi had gotten mixed up with people like that, what did that make him? Even if both father and daughter were willing, he still couldn't take her. It didn't matter if old Liu had sixty rickshaws, or six hundred, or six thousand. He had to leave Renhe Yard immediately and cut all ties with them. Xiangzi had his own abilities — he would buy a rickshaw with his own hands, marry a wife on his own terms, and do it all above board and with his head held high. Thinking this, he lifted his head and felt himself to be a man of worth — nothing to fear, nothing to worry about. As long as he worked hard, he would surely succeed.

He missed two fares in a row, neither of them panning out. That nagging, unsettled feeling came rushing back. He didn't want to think anymore, yet his heart felt blocked. This matter seemed unlike anything else — even if there were a way to resolve it, it wasn't something you could simply forget. It wasn't just that something seemed to have stuck to his body; it was as if a dark stain had appeared in his heart too, one that could never be washed away. No matter how much he resented her, no matter how much he despised her, she seemed to have a hold on his heart. The harder he tried not to think of her, the more suddenly she would leap out from inside him — a bare, unguarded her, handing over everything ugly and everything good all at once, whole and complete, like buying a pile of junk where among the broken copper and rusted iron there are one or two small glinting, colorful objects that you can't bring yourself to throw away. He had never been this close to anyone. Even though it had come out of nowhere, even though it had been a kind of trick and seduction, a connection like this couldn't simply be forgotten. Even if you tried to set it aside, it would naturally wind itself around your heart, as if it had taken root. For him this was not only an experience but also a kind of disturbance he couldn't put into words, leaving him not knowing what to do. Toward her, toward himself, toward the present and the future — he was helpless, like a small insect caught in a spider's web, already too late to struggle free.

In a daze he pulled a few fares. Even while running, he couldn't get this matter out of his mind — not as a clear, beginning-to-end recollection, but as a recurring sense of something, a faint taste, a vague feeling, all of it hazy yet strangely intimate. He very much wanted to go off alone and drink until he knew nothing — maybe then he could feel some relief, and stop suffering this torment. But he didn't dare drink. He couldn't let this thing ruin him. He tried to think about buying a rickshaw again, but he couldn't concentrate. Something always blocked his thoughts. Before he could even get to the rickshaw, that something would slip out and take over his heart, like a dark cloud covering the sun, cutting off the light. By evening, when he thought about returning the rickshaw, he felt even worse. He had to go back to the yard, yet he truly dreaded going. What if he ran into her — what would he do? He pulled his empty rickshaw through the streets in circles, twice or three times getting close to the yard, then turning back and heading elsewhere, very much like a child skipping school for the first time who can't bring himself to go home. Strangely, the more he wanted to avoid her, the more he also wanted to see her. The darker the sky grew, the stronger this feeling became. A boldness and a bewilderment — knowing full well it was wrong, yet very much wanting to try — gripped his heart tightly. It was just like when he was small and used a pole to poke at a wasp's nest: frightened, yet his heart leaping with the urge to try, as if some wicked force were driving him on. Vaguely he felt a force stronger than himself, wanting to knead him into a ball and hurl him into a roaring fire. He had no way to stop himself from moving forward.

He circled back to Xi'an Men. This time he didn't want to hesitate any longer — he would go straight in and find her. She was no longer anyone in particular. She was simply a woman. His whole body grew hot. Just as he reached the entrance, a man of about forty walked into the lamplight. He seemed to recognize the man's face and manner, yet didn't dare call out to him. Almost by instinct, he said: "Rickshaw?" The man paused: "Xiangzi?" "Yes," Xiangzi smiled. "Mr. Cao?"

Mr. Cao smiled and nodded. "Listen, Xiangzi, if you're not working for a household right now, why not come back to my place? The man I'm using now is too lazy — he never wipes down the rickshaw, though he does run pretty nimbly. Will you come?"

"Of course I'll come, sir!" Xiangzi seemed to have forgotten how to smile properly, and kept wiping his face with a small towel. "Sir, when do I start?"

"Well," Mr. Cao thought for a moment, "the day after tomorrow."

"Yes, sir!" Xiangzi also thought for a moment: "Sir, let me take you home." "No need. You know I went to Shanghai for a while — when I came back I moved out of the old place. I'm on North Changstreet now. I just came out for an evening walk. See you the day after tomorrow." Mr. Cao told Xiangzi the house number, then added: "Still using my own rickshaw."

Xiangzi felt so good he could have flown. All the misery of these past days was suddenly swept clean all at once, like a white stone road after a heavy rain. Mr. Cao was his former employer. Though they hadn't been together long, the feeling between them was the best. Mr. Cao was an exceptionally easygoing man, and his household was small — just a wife and a young son. He pulled the rickshaw and headed straight for Renhe Yard. The light in Tiger Girl's room was still on. The moment he saw that light, Xiangzi froze in his tracks.

He stood there for a long time, then decided to go in and see her — tell her he had found a live-in job again, hand over the past two days' rickshaw fees, collect his savings, and from this point on cut all ties. That last part naturally couldn't be said outright, but she would understand.

He went in and put the rickshaw away first, then came back and called out with forced boldness: "Miss Liu." "Come in!"

He pushed open the door. She was lying sideways on the bed, dressed in her everyday clothes, barefoot.Still leaning to one side, she said: "So? Got a taste for the good life, have you?"

Xiangzi's face turned as red as the eggs given out when someone has a baby. He stood there dumbstruck for a long while, then slowly said: "I found another job. I start the day after tomorrow. They have their own rickshaw..."

She cut him off: "You don't know what's good for you!" She sat up, half smiling half annoyed, pointing at him: "You've got food here, you've got clothes here. You just can't get enough of sweating it out on the streets, is that it? The old man can't control me, and I'm not going to spend my whole life like a widow. Even if the old man really digs his heels in, I've got my own savings. The two of us could get two or three rickshaws going, bring in a dollar or so a day — isn't that better than you running your legs off all over the streets? What's wrong with me? Sure, I'm a bit older than you, but not by that much. I can look after you, take care of you!"

"I want to pull a rickshaw!" Xiangzi couldn't find any other argument.

"Thick as a steamed bun! Sit down first, I'm not going to bite you!" She finished speaking and smiled, showing a pair of fangs.

Xiangzi sat down, the veins on his neck bulging. "What about my money?"

"The old man has it. It won't go anywhere, don't worry. And don't go asking him for it — you know what he's like? Once there's enough to buy a rickshaw, ask him then, and he won't short you a single cent. Ask now, and it'd be a miracle if he didn't scare the soul right out of you. He treats you well. It won't go anywhere — if a single coin is missing I'll pay you back double. You country bumpkin! Stop making me talk down to you!"

Xiangzi had nothing to say again. He lowered his head and dug around for a long time, then pulled out two days' worth of rickshaw rent and set it on the table: "Two days." Then he thought of something: "Today counts as returning the rickshaw. I'll take tomorrow off." He had no desire at all to take a day off, but it seemed cleaner this way. Once he returned the rickshaw, he would never stay at Renshun Garage again.

Tiger Girl came over, grabbed the money, and stuffed it back into his pocket: "These two days the rickshaw and the man were both on the house! You've got some luck, you do! Just don't be ungrateful!" With that, she turned and locked the door from the inside.

[1]

 主儿 refers to a person. Here it means the owner of a hired rickshaw.

[2]

 吹 means to fall apart, to be finished.

[3]

 白房子, the lowest class of brothel.

[4]

 麻利 means quick, nimble.

Seven

Xiang Zi moved into the Cao household.

He felt a little ashamed about Tiger Girl. But since the whole thing had come from her seduction, and since he had no intention of taking her money, he figured that cutting things off with her completely wasn't really doing her any great wrong. What worried him was Liu Si Ye holding onto his savings. Going to demand them back right away might make the old man suspicious. If he just stopped seeing the two of them altogether, Tiger Girl might fly into a rage and say something bad to her father, getting that money "burned to nothing." But if he kept on having the old man hold his money for him, he'd run into her every time he went to Ren He Garage, and that would be terribly awkward. He couldn't think of a good solution, and the more helpless he felt, the more uneasy he became.

He thought about asking Mr. Cao for advice, but what would he say? The part about Tiger Girl wasn't something he could tell anyone. Thinking about it, he truly regretted it. He was beginning to understand that this was not something you could simply cut off clean. This kind of thing could never be washed away, like a dark scar in the flesh. He'd lost his rickshaw for no reason, and now for no reason he'd gotten himself tangled up in this mess. He felt that his whole life was probably finished, that no matter how hard he pushed himself, it had all been for nothing. Turning it over and over in his mind, he could see one thing clearly: in the end, he'd probably still have to swallow his pride and go back to Tiger Girl — not for her sake, but for those few rickshaws, wasn't it? "A cuckold gets a couple of stir-fried pork dishes!" He couldn't stand it, but when the time came, it might be the only way. He could only keep pushing forward, working toward the good and bracing for the bad. He no longer dared to be as confident as before. His size, his strength, his spirit — none of it counted for anything. His life was his own, yet it was controlled by others. Controlled by the most worthless kind of people.

By rights he should have been quite content, because the Cao household was, of all the places he'd worked, the most pleasant. The wages at the Cao house weren't higher than elsewhere, and beyond the bonuses at the three festivals there wasn't much extra money, but Mr. Cao and Mrs. Cao were both very kind and treated everyone like a real person. Xiang Zi wanted to earn more money, to work himself to the bone for it, but he also wanted a decent place to live and enough food to eat his fill. The Cao house was clean everywhere, even the servants' quarters. The food there was good, and they never fed the servants anything rotten. He had a spacious room of his own and could eat three meals in peace, and on top of that the employers were considerate — even Xiang Zi, even he, stopped thinking only about money. Besides, with food and lodging both comfortable and the work not too tiring, keeping himself in good health was no loss either. If he were paying for his own meals, he'd never eat this well. Since the food was already prepared, and since eating it didn't cost him anything out of pocket, why not eat his fill? Food was bought with money, and he understood that arithmetic very clearly. And although the Cao family didn't play mahjong, rarely had guests, and didn't give out much extra cash, doing some odd job here and there could still earn him a dime or two. For instance, when the mistress sent him to buy medicine for the child, she always gave him an extra dime for the rickshaw fare, even though she knew perfectly well he could run faster than anyone. That little bit of money was nothing much, but it made him feel a kind of human warmth, a kind of consideration, that lifted his spirits. He'd worked for quite a few employers, and nine out of ten of them would delay paying wages by a day if they could, as if the ideal arrangement were to use servants for free, and as if servants were basically cats and dogs, or maybe worse than cats and dogs. The Cao family were an exception, and that was why he liked being here. He would tidy the courtyard and water the flowers without being told, and whenever they saw him doing these things they always said something kind, and on such occasions they would also dig out some worn-out items and tell him to go exchange them for matches — even though those things were still perfectly usable, and he would just keep them for himself. Here, he felt something like human dignity.

In Xiang Zi's eyes, Liu Si Ye could be counted as a Huang Tianba. Fierce, yes, but a man of face, a man with a name, never entirely crooked. The respectable figures in his mind, besides Huang Tianba, had to include Confucius. He had no clear idea what kind of person Confucius actually was, but supposedly he knew a great many characters and was quite reasonable. Among the households he'd worked in, some were military types and some were scholarly types. Among the military ones, not a single one could match Liu Si Ye. Among the scholarly ones, there were professors at universities and officials with good posts in government offices — they certainly knew plenty of characters — but he'd never met a single one who was reasonable. Even when the master was somewhat reasonable, the mistress and the young ladies were very hard to please. Only Mr. Cao both knew his characters and was reasonable, and Mrs. Cao was also proper and well-liked. So Mr. Cao must be Confucius, and if Xiang Zi couldn't picture what Confucius looked like, then Confucius must look like Mr. Cao — whether Confucius liked it or not.

In reality, Mr. Cao was nothing particularly remarkable. He was simply a middling sort of person who sometimes taught a few classes and sometimes did other things. He considered himself a "socialist" and was also an aesthete, having been influenced somewhat by William Morris. In politics and in art he had no profound insights. But he had one virtue: the little he did believe in, he could put into practice in the small matters of daily life. He seemed to understand that he had no extraordinary talent for doing earth-shaking things, and so he arranged his work and his household according to his own ideals. Though it did nothing to improve society, he at least wanted his words and actions to be consistent, and not to be a hypocrite. For this reason he paid close attention to small things, as if to say that as long as one's little household was made beautiful, the rest of society could do as it pleased. This sometimes made him feel ashamed of himself, and sometimes pleased with himself. He could see it plainly enough: his household was a small oasis in the desert, able to offer a little clean water and food to those who passed through, and nothing more.

Xiang Zi had arrived at just this small oasis. After wandering through the desert for so many days, he felt it was a miracle. He had never before met anyone like Mr. Cao, and so he regarded this man as a sage. Perhaps this showed his limited experience, or perhaps such people were genuinely rare in the world. When he pulled Mr. Cao's rickshaw, Mr. Cao's clothes were so understated and elegant, the man himself so lively and gracious, and Xiang Zi himself so neat and trim, so tall and strong, that he ran with particular joy, as if only he was worthy of pulling Mr. Cao. At home, everything was so clean, always so quiet, making him feel comfortable and settled. Back in the countryside, he used to watch old men sitting in silence on winter days or under an autumn moon, pipes in their mouths, not saying a word. He was too young then to do the same, but he loved watching them sit so still, sensing — he imagined — that there must be some flavor to it. Now, though he was in the city, the quiet of the Cao house was enough to make him think of the countryside, and he truly wished he could light a pipe and savor whatever that flavor might be.

Unfortunately, that woman and that money gave him no peace of mind. His heart was like a green leaf being wrapped up by a worm in silk, being made into a cocoon. Because of this, he couldn't settle himself. With others, even with Mr. Cao, he was constantly drifting off, answering questions with unrelated answers. This troubled him deeply. The Cao household went to bed early, and by past nine in the evening there was nothing left to do. He would sit alone in his room or in the courtyard, turning things over and over in his mind — always these two matters. He even thought about getting married right away, figuring that would surely put an end to Tiger Girl's ideas. But how could a rickshaw puller support a family? He knew the hardships of the men in the tenement yards — the men pulling rickshaws, the women doing piecework sewing, the children picking up coal cinders, eating watermelon rinds off rubbish heaps in summer, and lining up at the charity porridge kitchens in winter. Xiang Zi couldn't bear that. And besides, if he got married, he'd never get back the money Liu Si Ye was holding. Would Tiger Girl let him off so easily? He couldn't give up that money — he'd traded his life for it!

His own rickshaw had been bought in early autumn of the previous year. More than a year had passed, and now he had nothing — only thirty-some yuan he couldn't get back, and a tangle of trouble. The more he thought about it, the more miserable he felt.

It had been more than ten days since the Mid-Autumn Festival, and the weather was gradually turning cool. He figured he needed to add a couple of pieces of clothing. More money again! If he bought clothes, he couldn't keep the money at the same time, and the hope of buying a rickshaw was something he hardly dared hope for anymore. Even if he kept on doing monthly hire work, what would his whole life amount to?

One evening, Mr. Cao came back from the east side of the city a little late. To be safe, Xiang Zi kept to the main road the whole way past Tiananmen. The road was wide and open, with few people about, a gentle cool breeze, and quiet lamplight. He found his stride. The oppression that had weighed on his heart for so many days was temporarily forgotten. Listening to the sound of his own footsteps and the soft creak of the rickshaw shafts, he forgot everything. He unbuttoned his collar and let the cool breeze rush against his chest. He felt a surge of freedom, as if he could just keep running like this, running to some unknown place, and it would be fine to run himself to death. The faster he ran, the better. Ahead was another rickshaw, and he called out to pass it, overtaking it in no time. He was past Tiananmen before long. His feet felt like two springs, barely touching the ground before bouncing back up. The rear wheels were spinning so fast you couldn't make out the spokes, and the rubber tires seemed to have left the ground, as if the whole man and rickshaw had been swept up by a sudden gust of wind. The cool breeze must have half-lulled Mr. Cao to sleep, otherwise he would surely have told Xiang Zi to slow down. Xiang Zi had hit his stride, and in the back of his mind he thought vaguely that if he worked up a good sweat, he'd sleep soundly tonight and wouldn't lie awake brooding over things.

Not far from Bei Chang Street now, the northern half of the road was very dark, shadowed by the locust trees outside the red walls. Xiang Zi had just thought to slow down when his foot struck something raised in the road. His foot hit it, and so did the wheel. Xiang Zi was thrown forward. A crack — the shafts broke. "What happened?" Mr. Cao tumbled out along with his own words. Xiang Zi made no sound and scrambled up from the ground. Mr. Cao also sat up quickly. "What happened?"

A freshly dumped pile of road-repair stones, with no red warning lantern set out.

"Are you hurt?" Xiang Zi asked.

"No. I'll walk back, you pull the rickshaw." Mr. Cao remained calm, feeling around on the stones to see if anything had fallen out.

Xiang Zi felt the broken shaft end: "It's not too bad, sir, please get back in, I can still pull it!" As he spoke, he yanked the rickshaw free from the stones. "Please get in, sir!"

Mr. Cao didn't want to get back in, but hearing that Xiang Zi's voice was on the verge of tears, he climbed in anyway.

At the lamppost at the entrance to Bei Chang Street, Mr. Cao saw that the skin had been scraped off his right hand. "Xiang Zi, stop!"

Xiang Zi turned around. His face was covered in blood.

Mr. Cao was frightened and couldn't think of what to say. "You, quickly, quickly —"

Xiang Zi had no idea what he meant, and thinking he was being told to run fast, he grabbed the shafts and ran all the way home in one go.

He set down the rickshaw and saw the blood on Mr. Cao's hand. He rushed into the courtyard to find the mistress and get some medicine.

"Never mind me, look at yourself first!" Mr. Cao hurried inside. Xiang Zi looked himself over and began to feel the pain — both knees, his right elbow, all scraped raw. What he had thought was sweat on his face turned out to be blood. Without thinking about anything else, he sat down on the stone step in the entryway and stared blankly at the rickshaw with its broken shaft. The brand-new black-lacquered rickshaw, with a section of the shaft snapped off, exposed two raw white splinters of wood — jarringly out of place, ugly, like a beautifully made paper figure that hadn't yet had its feet attached, with two bare sorghum stalks poking out. Xiang Zi stared blankly at those two white splinters of wood. "Xiang Zi!" Gao Ma, the Cao family's maidservant, called out in her clear, bright voice. "Xiang Zi! Where are you?"

He sat without moving, his eyes fixed unblinking on the broken shaft. Those two white splinters seemed to be jabbing straight into his heart.

"What's the matter with you! Not a sound, hiding here — look, you gave me a fright! The master is calling for you!" Gao Ma's words always mixed practical matters with feeling, making everything seem both complicated and touching. She was a widow of thirty-two or thirty-three, clean, straightforward, quick and careful in her work. In other households, some people found her too outspoken, too full of opinions, always up to some little scheme or another. The Cao family liked clean, bright-spirited people and didn't pay much attention to those small faults, so she had been with them for two or three years already, and whenever the whole Cao family moved elsewhere they always brought her along. "The master is calling for you!" she repeated. When Xiang Zi stood up, she saw the blood on his face. "Lord have mercy, my goodness! What happened to you? Aren't you going to change? You'll get tetanus, that's no joke! Come on! The master has medicine!"

Xiang Zi walked ahead, Gao Ma chattered behind him, and together they went into the study. Mrs. Cao was there too, wrapping medicine around the master's hand. When she saw Xiang Zi come in, she let out a startled "oh."

"Ma'am, he's really taken quite a fall this time." Afraid the mistress couldn't see for herself, Gao Ma hurried to pour cold water into the washbasin and talked even faster: "I always knew it — once he gets running he doesn't care about his life, sooner or later something was bound to go wrong. Sure enough! Aren't you going to wash up? Wash first and then put on some medicine, honestly!"

Xiang Zi cradled his right elbow and didn't move. The study was so clean and tasteful, and here he stood, a big man with a face full of blood — completely out of place. Everyone seemed to sense that something was not quite right, and even Gao Ma fell silent.

"Sir!" Xiang Zi lowered his head, his voice quiet but firm. "Sir, please find someone else. Keep this month's wages to repair the rickshaw — the shaft is broken, and the glass on the left lamp is cracked. Everything else is fine."

"Wash up first, put on some medicine, then we'll talk about the rest." Mr. Cao said, looking at his own hand.The mistress was slowly wrapping the gauze upward.

"Wash it first!" Gao Ma remembered something else to say. "The master didn't say anything — don't go turning it around on him!"

Xiang Zi still didn't move. "No need to wash it, it'll be fine in a bit. A monthly hire driver, threw his passenger, damaged the car, no face left to..." His words weren't enough to fully express what he meant, but his feelings had been completely vented — he was just short of breaking into tears. Quitting, forfeiting his wages — to Xiang Zi that was about the same as killing himself. But responsibility, dignity — at this moment they seemed more important than life itself, because the one he'd thrown wasn't just anyone, it was Mr. Cao. If he'd thrown that Mrs. Yang, well, thrown was thrown, served her right. With Mrs. Yang he could put on the rough street attitude, because she didn't treat him like a person, so he had no reason to be polite either; money was everything, there was no talk of dignity or rules. Mr. Cao was nothing like that — he had to sacrifice the money to preserve his dignity. He had no energy to hate anyone else, only to hate his own fate. He had almost made up his mind: once he left the Cao household, he would never pull a rickshaw again. His own life might be worthless, he could throw it away — but what about someone else's life? If he really killed someone, what then? He hadn't thought about this before, but because this time he had injured Mr. Cao, he had come to understand this truth. Fine then — he could go without the wages, change trades from now on, and never again do work that carried someone's life on his back. Pulling a rickshaw was his ideal profession; giving it up was the same as abandoning hope. He felt his whole life would just muddle along in a wretched mess — not even becoming a good rickshaw puller was worth thinking about anymore, all for nothing that he'd grown so tall and strong. When he used to pull casual fares outside, he had shamelessly "poached" [2] business, been mocked and cursed by everyone — but that kind of shamelessness was precisely because he wanted to do well, wanted to buy his own rickshaw, and he could forgive himself for it. But pulling a monthly hire and causing trouble like this — what did he have to say for himself? If people found out — Xiang Zi threw his passenger, damaged the car — what kind of rickshaw puller is that, what a piece of work! Xiang Zi had no way out. He couldn't wait for Mr. Cao to dismiss him — he'd better just get out on his own.

"Xiang Zi," Mr. Cao's hand was already bandaged, "go wash up. No need to talk about quitting just yet. It wasn't your fault — if you put a stone in the road you ought to put a red lantern there. Let it go — wash up and put on some medicine."

"That's right, sir," Gao Ma remembered more to say, "Xiang Zi just can't let it go. Well, naturally — throwing the master like that! But since the master says it wasn't your fault, you don't need to be so stubborn about it. Look at him — big and strong as he is, still acting like a child, genuinely all worked up. Mistress, say a word and put his mind at ease!" Gao Ma's words were like a gramophone record, going around in circles, drawing everyone in, with no trace of any formal structure.

"Hurry up and wash — the blood frightens me!" That was all the mistress said.

Xiang Zi's mind was in turmoil, but at the end he heard the mistress say the blood frightened her, and it seemed he had found something he could do to comfort her. He carried the washbasin out and washed up a few times at the study doorway. Gao Ma stood inside the door waiting for him with the medicine bottle.

"What about your arm and leg?" Gao Ma dabbed medicine on his face for a while. Xiang Zi shook his head. "It's nothing."

The Cao couple went to rest. Gao Ma, carrying the medicine bottle, followed Xiang Zi out. When they reached his room, she set the bottle down and stood just inside the doorway: "Put some on yourself later. I'm telling you, there's no need to be so obsessed over something like this. Back when my old man was still alive, I used to quit jobs all the time. For one thing, I was out there working hard while he wouldn't make any effort, which made me furious. For another, I was young and hot-headed — one wrong word and that was it, done. We sell our labor for money, we're not slaves.
