You've got your stinking money, and even a clay figure has its earthy pride — the old mistress can do without being served. Now I'm much better — once the old man died, I had nothing to worry about anymore, and my temper improved a bit. Here — I've been here nearly three years now. That's right, I started on the ninth day of the ninth month — the pocket money is a bit low, but they treat people decently enough. We sell our strength, we do it for money — just saying nice things doesn't count for much. But then again, there's something to be said for taking the long view: if you're changing jobs every few days and end up taking six months off a year, that doesn't add up either. Better to find a reasonable employer — even if the pocket money is a little less, you can't beat steady work day after day, and over time you can still save something. As for today's business — since the master didn't say anything, just let it go, why bother. Not that I'm talking down to you, but you're still young, easy to flare up. There's no need at all — a hot temper won't put food on the table. Honest and straightforward as you are, settling in here and getting by for a while is better than flying all over the place with nowhere to land. I'm not taking their side at all — I'm saying this for your sake. We all get along pretty well here." She caught her breath. "Alright, see you tomorrow. Don't be pigheaded — I speak straight from the heart, one word for one word."

Xiang Zi's right elbow hurt badly, and he couldn't sleep until the middle of the night. Turning it over every which way, he felt Gao Ma's words made sense. Everything else was false — only money was real. Save money, buy a rickshaw. A hot temper won't put food on the table. Thinking this, a little peaceful drowsiness came over him.

[1] 过节儿, details, small rules of etiquette.

[2] Snatching away someone else's ongoing business was called "poaching."

Learning resource sharing 8

Mr. Cao had the rickshaw repaired and did not dock Xiang Zi's wages. Mrs. Cao gave him two pills of "Sanhuang Baola," which he didn't take. He didn't bring up quitting again. Although he felt awkward about it for several days, Gao Ma's words won out in the end. After some time, life fell back into its groove, and he gradually forgot the incident — all his hopes sprouted anew. Sitting alone in his room, his eyes would light up as he calculated how to save money and buy a rickshaw, his lips muttering constantly, as if he had something weighing on his mind. His arithmetic wasn't very sophisticated, but he kept reciting "six sixes are thirty-six" in his heart and on his lips — it had little to do with the actual amount of money he had, but just saying it made him feel more grounded inside, as if he really had a proper ledger.

He had a fair amount of admiration for Gao Ma, feeling that this woman had more resourcefulness and ability than most men, and that her words went straight to the root of things. He didn't dare go out of his way to chat with her, but when they ran into each other in the courtyard or at the gate and she had a moment to say a few words, he was always happy to listen. Each time she said something, it was enough to keep him thinking for half a day — so whenever he ran into her, he would give a simple, earnest smile to let her know he admired what she said, and she would feel a little pleased with herself and, even if she was busy, would still manage to say a few words.

However, when it came to how to handle money, he didn't dare just blindly follow her ideas. Her ideas, he thought, were actually not bad — but they carried a certain amount of risk. He was very willing to listen to her talk, to learn more tricks and feel more at ease in his mind; but in practice, he stuck to his old principle — don't let go of money lightly.

Indeed, Gao Ma had her methods: ever since she was widowed, she had been lending out whatever small amount she could save each month — one yuan was a loan, two yuan was a loan — to fellow servants, lower-ranking policemen, and small traders, at an interest rate of at least three percent. These people were often so desperate for a single yuan that their eyes turned red going around in circles, and even if someone lent them one yuan and counted it as two, they would still reach out and take it. Apart from this, money would never come their way — the money they did see was poisoned, and taking it would drain the blood from them, yet they still had to take it. Anything that could give them a moment's breathing room, they had the nerve to pick up; life was about getting one more breath and then talking, deal with tomorrow when tomorrow comes. Gao Ma, while her husband was still alive, had herself suffered from this poison. Her husband would come home drunk and demand money from her — nothing less than one yuan would send him away; without it, he would make a drunken scene outside the gate. She had no choice but to borrow that yuan immediately, no matter how high the interest. From this experience she learned this method — not out of any desire for revenge, but because she regarded it as reasonable, almost a charitable act of helping in emergencies. There were those who urgently needed money, and those willing to lend it out — Zhou Yu striking Huang Gai, one willing to strike and one willing to take it.

In principle, since she saw nothing wrong with it, she had to be tough in practice — she couldn't let the money float away like water on a surface; whatever you're doing, you do it properly. This required sharp judgment, skill, caution, and boldness, so as not to end up losing everything [1]. She put in no less effort than a bank manager, because she needed even more care and prudence. Capital may differ in size, but the principle is the same — because this is a capitalist society, like an enormous fine sieve, sifting money downward bit by bit, with less and less the further down it goes. At the same time, it also sifts principles downward, but those are equally plentiful at every level, because principles, unlike money, are not afraid of small sieve holes — they are formless and can slip through even the tiniest opening. Everyone said Gao Ma was tough, and she admitted it herself. Her toughness had been forged through hardship and suffering. Whenever she thought of the bitterness of the past — even her own husband being so heartless and unreasonable — she would clench her teeth. She could be very gentle, and she could be very ruthless; she knew that without this, there was no surviving in this world.

She also advised Xiang Zi to lend his money out, entirely out of goodwill, and if he was willing, she could help him: "I'm telling you, Xiang Zi, if you keep it in your pocket, one coin will always be one coin. But lend it out, and money will breed money. No mistake — what are our eyes for? Look carefully before you let go of the money, don't release a hawk with no tail feathers. If a policeman doesn't pay the interest on time or doesn't return the principal, go find his superior officer. One word — his job will be on the line, dare he not pay. Find out when they get their pay, and catch them right at the source. If they don't repay, new loan on top of old. One to ten — whoever you lend to, you've got to know their background. Fine, lend it out, but fishing blind in the sea — does that work? Listen to me, guaranteed no mistakes."

Xiang Zi didn't need to say anything — his expression was enough to show he greatly admired Gao Ma's words. But when he thought it over alone, he felt that money in his own hands was more secure than anything. True, this way it was dead money, money that wouldn't breed money — but it also couldn't be lost, and that was real. He would quietly take out the few yuan he'd saved over the past two or three months — all silver dollars — and turn them over one by one, careful not to make a sound. Silver dollars were so bright, solid, and eye-catching that he felt even more strongly he could never let them go, except to buy a rickshaw. Everyone had their own way of doing things — he didn't have to follow Gao Ma in everything.

Earlier, at a family named Fang, the entire household — master, mistress, children, and servants alike — all had a savings passbook at the post office. Mrs. Fang had also advised Xiang Zi: "One yuan is enough to open a passbook — why don't you open one? As the saying goes, when you have plenty, think of times of want; don't wait until you have nothing to wish you had something. Young as you are, if you don't save a little while you're young and strong, you can't count on three hundred and sixty days of sunshine every year. It's no trouble, it's reliable, it earns interest, and you can withdraw some whenever you need it — what could be more convenient? Go on, go get a form. You can't write, I'll fill it in for you — I mean it kindly."

Xiang Zi knew she meant well, and he knew that the cook Wang Liu and the wet nurse Qin Ma both had passbooks, so he genuinely wanted to try. But one day the young Miss Fang asked him to go deposit ten yuan for her, and he looked carefully at that little passbook — it had writing on it, little red stamps — altogether, hmm, it weighed about as much as a small stack of toilet paper. He handed the money in, and they wrote a few more characters in the passbook and pressed a small stamp on it. He felt this wasn't a swindle — and yet it had to be a swindle. Bright white silver dollars handed over, and all it took was a few strokes of someone's pen to call it settled — Xiang Zi wasn't falling for that. He suspected the Fang family had some connection with the post office business — he always thought of the post office as a business with branches everywhere, probably a very old establishment, at least on a par with Ruifuxiang or Hongji — and that was why they were so enthusiastic about drumming up customers. Even if that wasn't the case, cash in hand was still far better than figures in a little passbook — far better. The money in the passbook was just a few written characters.

As for banks and money houses, he only knew them as places where you could pick up fares, and if the police didn't stop you from parking your rickshaw there, you were sure to get a ride. What went on inside, he couldn't figure out. True, there must be a great deal of money in there — but why people came specifically to these places to stir up [2] money, he didn't understand. He himself was unlikely to have much to do with them, so there was no point troubling himself to think about it. There were so many things in the city he didn't understand, and listening to friends debate them in teahouses only confused him more, because everyone had a different take and none of them got to the bottom of it. He didn't want to listen anymore, didn't want to think too hard about it. He knew that if you were going to rob someone, the best target was a bank — but since he had no intention of becoming a bandit, he might as well hold on to his own money, and not worry about anything else. He considered this the most seasoned approach.

Gao Ma knew he was burning with the desire to buy a rickshaw, and she offered him another idea: "Xiang Zi, I know you don't want to lend money out — you want to buy your own rickshaw as soon as possible, and that's a plan too. If I were a man, if I pulled a rickshaw, I'd pull my own rickshaw. Work for yourself, answer to no one — if you can do that, I wouldn't trade it for a county magistrate's post. Pulling a rickshaw is hard work, but if I were a man with some strength in my arms, I'd rather stubbornly pull a rickshaw than go be a policeman. Through winter and summer alike,"""Standing around on the street all day, earning that little bit each month, no extra income, no freedom; grow a beard and they'll just blow you off, not a bit of progress. I'm saying, look, if you want to buy a rickshaw fast, I've got a good idea for you: start a rotating savings club, ten or so people, twenty at most, two dollars each per month, and you get the first payout; isn't that forty-some dollars right there? You must have some savings too, scrape it all together and get yourself a rickshaw to pull — just go for it! Once you've got the rickshaw, you run a black-seal club, no interest, and it's a respectable thing to do — it'll suit you just right! If you really want to start a club, count me in for one share, I won't back out! What do you say?"

This really made Xiangzi's heart beat a little faster! If he could really scrape together thirty or forty dollars, plus the thirty-some that Liu Fourth had, and the few he had on him now, wouldn't that come to nearly eighty? Not enough to buy a brand-new rickshaw, but an eighty-percent-new one should be doable! And on top of that, he could go ask Liu Fourth to return his money, so it wouldn't just be sitting there doing nothing. Eighty-percent-new was fine — pull it for now, and swap it out when he had more to spare.

But where would he find twenty people? Even if he could, this was a matter of face — if he started a club just because he needed money, wouldn't people come asking him to join theirs later? Starting a club, in times this hard, often ended in collapse! A real man doesn't beg; plain and simple, if he was meant to buy a rickshaw, he'd buy it; no begging!

Seeing that Xiangzi didn't react, Gao Ma really wanted to tease him, but thinking of his sincerity, she felt it wasn't quite right: "You're something else! 'Pig in a narrow alley — straight through'; fine by me!"

Xiangzi said nothing. After Gao Ma left, he gave himself a little nod, as if acknowledging that his stubborn persistence was admirable, and felt rather pleased with himself.

It was already early winter. In the evenings, the alley vendors called out sugar-roasted chestnuts and peanuts, and now added the low, mournful cry of "night pots." The night-pot peddler's carrying pole had clay-sealed earthenware jars, and Xiangzi bought a large one. It was the first sale of the day, and the peddler couldn't make change, so Xiangzi's eye fell on the tiniest little green night pot — it was a charming thing, glossy green, with a little pursed spout — "Keep the change, I'll take this one!" He set down the earthenware jar and carried the little green pot inside: "Young master still up? Here's something fun for you!"

Everyone was watching little Wen — the Cao family's young boy — take his bath, and when they saw the thing they all burst out laughing. Mr. and Mrs. Cao said nothing, probably feeling that though the gift was a bit silly, Xiangzi's good intentions deserved acknowledgment, so they both smiled at him in thanks. Gao Ma's mouth, of course, couldn't stay still: "Look at that, really, Xiangzi! A big fellow like you, coming up with such a brilliant idea; how ridiculous!"

Little Wen loved the thing and immediately started scooping water from the tub into the little pot with his hands: "This little teapot, big spout!"

Everyone laughed even harder. Xiangzi straightened himself up — not knowing what to do with himself when pleased — and walked out. He was very happy; this was something he had never experienced before, all those smiling faces turned toward him, as if he were someone quite important. Smiling, he brought out those silver coins again and gently dropped them one by one into the earthenware jar, saying to himself: this is safer than anything! When there's enough, smash it against the wall — crack, there'll be more silver coins than broken pottery!

He decided he would not ask anyone for anything anymore. Even someone as reliable as Liu Fourth could be awkward at times — the money wasn't going anywhere, sitting with Liu Fourth, but he always felt a little uneasy about it. Money was like a ring — always better on your own hand. This decision made him feel good, as if he had cinched his belt one notch tighter, letting him stand straighter and stronger.

The weather grew colder and colder, yet Xiangzi seemed not to notice. With a clear purpose in his heart, the world ahead seemed brighter; in that brightness, he felt no cold. Ice crystals began to appear on the ground, the dirt on the footpaths hardened, everything looked dry and firm, the dark soil taking on a faint yellowish tinge, as if all the moisture had been driven out. Especially in the early morning, the ridges of earth churned up by carts were edged with frost, a sharp little wind scattered the early dawn glow to reveal a sky impossibly high, blue, and crisp; Xiangzi liked to head out early with his rickshaw, the cool wind rushing into his sleeves, making his whole body shudder and tingle like a cold-water bath. Sometimes a fierce gust would knock the breath out of him, but he lowered his head, clenched his teeth, and drove forward like a great fish swimming against the current; the stronger the wind, the harder he pushed back, as if locked in a fight to the death with the gale. A sudden blast would cut off his breath entirely — he'd clamp his mouth shut, hold it, then let out a long belch, like surfacing from a deep dive. After that belch, he pressed on, charging forward, nothing in the world able to stop this giant; every muscle in his body was taut, like a green caterpillar besieged by ants, his whole frame straining in resistance. What a sweat! When he finally set down the rickshaw, straightened his back, let out a long breath, and wiped the yellow sand from the corners of his mouth, he felt invincible; watching the dust-wrapped wind sweep past him, he gave a nod. The wind bent the trees along the road, tore apart the cloth shop banners, stripped the posters from the walls, blotted out the sun, singing, howling, roaring, echoing! Then it would suddenly charge straight ahead like a maddened great spirit, pulling at heaven and earth as it raced; then suddenly fall into chaos, swirling in all directions like a demon that, not knowing what else to do, decided to crash into everything; then suddenly sweep sideways, catching everything on the ground off guard, snapping branches, lifting roof tiles, severing electric wires; but Xiangzi stood there watching — he had just come through the wind, and the wind had done nothing to him! Victory was Xiangzi's! When the wind was at his back, he only needed to hold the shafts steady; he didn't have to run at all — the wind would push the wheels for him, like a good friend.

Of course, not being blind, he must have seen those old and frail rickshaw pullers. They wore rags that a light breeze could pierce through and a strong gust could shred; their feet wrapped in who knows what. At the rickshaw stands they shivered, their eyes darting about like thieves, and no matter where a person appeared from, they would scramble to ask: "Rickshaw?!" When they got a fare, they warmed up, sweat soaking through those thin, tattered clothes. The moment they stopped, the sweat on their backs turned to ice. In the wind, they could barely lift a foot, yet had to drag the rickshaw forward by sheer force; when the wind came from above, they had to tuck their heads into their chests; when it came from below, their feet lost the ground; when it came from the front, a raised hand would send them flying like a kite; when it came from behind, they had no way to control the rickshaw or themselves. Yet they tried every trick, spent every ounce of strength, and hauled the rickshaw to its destination by brute force, risking their lives for a few copper coins. After one trip, the dust mixed with sweat into mud plastered on their faces, leaving only three frost-reddened circles — the eyes and mouth. The days were so short, so cold, the streets so empty; a whole day of this bitter struggle might not earn enough for one full meal; yet the old ones had wives and children at home; the young ones had parents and siblings! In winter, they were entirely in hell, with one more breath of life than ghosts, but none of a ghost's ease and freedom; ghosts didn't have to work as hard as they did! Dying like a dog in the street was the greatest peace they could hope for; frozen corpses, it was said, had a slight smile on their faces.

How could Xiangzi not have seen all this. But he had no time to worry or think about them. Their suffering was his too, except that he was young and strong, able to endure hardship, unafraid of cold or wind; at night he had a clean place to sleep, by day a decent set of clothes, so he felt he could not be compared to them — though he suffered alongside them now, the degree of suffering was not entirely the same; he suffered less now, and in the future he could still escape from all this; he told himself that when he grew old, he would never end up pulling a broken-down rickshaw, starving and freezing. He believed his present advantages would guarantee his future success. Just as when, outside a restaurant or a wealthy household, rickshaw pullers encountered chauffeurs, they wouldn't stand around chatting together; the chauffeurs felt it beneath them to have anything to do with rickshaw pullers. The chauffeur's attitude toward the rickshaw puller was much like Xiangzi's toward those old and broken-down men; all in hell, but on different levels. It never occurred to any of them that they ought to stand together — instead each went his own way, each person's individual hopes and efforts blinding their eyes, each one feeling they could build a life from nothing with their bare hands, each groping alone in the dark for their own path. Xiangzi didn't think about others, didn't care about others; he thought only of his own money and his future success.

The streets were gradually taking on the feel of the approaching new year. On clear, windless days, though the air was dry and cold, more color appeared along the roadsides: new year prints, gauze lanterns, red and white candles, silk hair flowers, offerings of all sizes — all laid out on display, making the heart feel bright yet also a little uneasy; for no matter who you were, the new year made you think of a few days of happiness, yet everyone had their difficulties, large or small. Xiangzi's eyes brightened when he saw the new year goods along the road; he thought the Cao household would surely be sending gifts soon — with each delivery there'd be a few coins of tip money for him. The holiday bonus was a fixed two dollars, not much; but when guests came to pay their new year's respects, he'd go along to see them off, and each trip would earn him twenty or thirty cents. It all added up; he didn't mind small amounts, as long as the money kept trickling in; his earthenware jar would never cheat him! In the evenings when he had nothing to do, he'd stare at this clay friend that only swallowed money and refused to give it back, and softly coax it: "Eat more, eat more, old friend! When you've eaten your fill, I'll be all set!"

The new year drew closer and closer, and in a blink it was already the eighth day of the twelfth month. Joy or anxiety drove people to plan and prepare; it was still twenty-four hours a day, but these days were different from ordinary ones — they wouldn't let anyone pass them idly, something had to be done, and everything had to be done with the new year in mind, as if time had suddenly gained awareness and feeling, making people think and bustle along with it. Xiangzi stood on the side of happiness — the bustle of the streets, the vendors' calls, the hope of bonuses and extra coins, the rest of the new year, the thought of good food — all of it made him as giddy and eager as a child. He decided he'd spend a dollar or so to buy a small gift for Liu Fourth. A modest gift carries sincere intentions — he had to bring something; first, as an apology for not having visited the old man lately, since the household had been so busy; and second, he could take the opportunity to get back those thirty-some dollars. Spending a dollar to recover that sum was a good deal. With this plan in mind, he gently shook the piggy bank, imagining how much deeper and more satisfying it would sound with thirty more dollars added inside. Yes, once he got that money back, he'd have nothing left to worry about!

One evening, just as he was about to give the treasure jar another shake, Gao Ma called out to him: "Xiangzi! There's a young lady at the gate looking for you; I just came back from the street and she kept asking me about you." When Xiangzi came out, she added in a low voice: "She looks like a big black tower! Quite frightening!"

Xiangzi's face suddenly flushed red as if a ball of fire were wrapped inside it — he knew things were about to go wrong!

[1] "Lost the hawk" means losing everything.

[2] "Gu dou" means to repeatedly toy with or manipulate.

Nine

Xiangzi could barely find the strength to step over the front threshold. His head was spinning; his feet were still inside the doorway when, by the light from the street, he already made out Liu's daughter. Her face was probably powdered again, and under the lamplight it showed a grayish-green tinge, like frost clinging to blackened, withered leaves. Xiangzi didn't dare look at her directly.

Huniu's expression was complicated: her eyes held a glimmer of longing to see him; her mouth was slightly open, showing a trace of cold smile; her nose was creased with lines folding together disdain and urgency; her brows were furrowed, standing out against the strange powder on her face with a look that was both seductive and domineering. Seeing Xiangzi come out, her lips twisted a few times, and all the different expressions on her face, unable to find a proper place to settle, left her staring blankly at the ground. She swallowed, seemed to press down the tangle of feelings and emotions, summoned the bold manner she had inherited from Liu Fourth, and with a half-angry, half-laughing air, pretending not to care much, threw out a jab: "Well, aren't you something! Throw a meat bun at a dog and it never comes back!" Her voice was loud, just like when she used to argue with the rickshaw pullers at the yard. The moment those words were out, every trace of a smile vanished from her face, and she suddenly seemed to feel a kind of shame and wretchedness — she bit her lip.

"Keep it down!" Xiangzi seemed to concentrate all the strength in his body into his lips, and those two words burst out — barely audible, yet with tremendous force.

"Hmph! I'm not scared!" She laughed with spite, yet as if against her own will, lowered her voice slightly. "No wonder you've been avoiding me — turns out there's a pretty little maid here like a little vixen; I always knew you were no good, don't let that big dumb look fool anyone, you're a Tartar pulling a pipe — playing dumb on purpose!" Her voice rose again. "Keep it down!" Xiangzi was afraid Gao Ma might be inside listening. "Keep it down! Come over here!" He spoke while walking toward the road.

"I'm not afraid wherever we go, this is just how loud I am!" Her mouth protested, but she followed along.

They crossed the road and came to the east footpath, pressed against the red wall of the park. Xiangzi — not having forgotten his country habits — squatted down. "What did you come for?" "Me? Hmph, plenty to talk about!" ""She stood with her left hand on her hip, belly pushed out a little. She glanced down at him, thought for a moment, as if she'd taken pity on him out of some small kindness: "Xiangzi! I need to talk to you about something important!"

That soft, low "Xiangzi" scattered much of his anger. He raised his head and looked at her — she still had nothing particularly appealing about her — yet that "Xiangzi" was still faintly echoing in his heart, warm and tender, as if he'd heard it somewhere before, stirring up feelings he couldn't deny and couldn't quite let go of. His voice was still low, but gentler now: "What is it?"

"Xiangzi!" She leaned in closer: "I'm in trouble!"

"What kind of trouble?" He was confused for a moment.

"This kind!" She pointed at her belly. "You'd better figure something out!"

Dazed and slow-witted, he let out an "ah," and then suddenly understood everything. Ten thousand things he'd never imagined came rushing into his mind all at once — so many, so fast, so tangled — that his mind went completely blank, like a film reel that had suddenly snapped. The street was very quiet. Gray clouds drifted across the moon. A light wind stirred now and then, rustling dead branches and withered leaves. Somewhere in the distance, a cat let out a few sharp cries. Xiangzi's mind went from chaos to blankness; he didn't even hear those sounds. His hand propped up his jaw as he stared blankly at the ground, staring so hard it seemed about to move. He couldn't think of anything, and didn't want to think of anything. He only felt himself growing smaller and smaller, yet unable to sink entirely into the earth. His whole being seemed to rest on this single point of misery — nothing else existed. Only then did he feel the cold, his lips trembling faintly.

"Don't just squat there, say something! Get up!" She seemed to feel the cold too, and wanted to move around a bit.

He stood up stiffly and followed her northward, still unable to find any words. His whole body felt numb, as if he'd just been thawed out from a freeze.

"You've got no idea what to do?" She shot Xiangzi a glance, her eyes carrying a look of tender concern.

He had nothing to say.

"By the twenty-seventh — the old man's birthday — you need to come by." "I'm busy, it's the end of the year!" Even in the midst of his turmoil, Xiangzi hadn't forgotten his own affairs. "I know you're the type who responds to pressure, not kindness — talking nicely to you is a waste of breath!" Her voice rose again, and the stillness of the street made it ring out especially clear, making Xiangzi especially uncomfortable. "You think I'm afraid of anyone? What are you planning to do? If you don't want to listen to me, I haven't got time to waste my breath on you! If it comes to a falling out, I'll stand at your gate and curse you for three days and three nights! Wherever you go, I'll find you! I've still got nothing to lose!"

"Can you keep it down?" Xiangzi stepped away from her.

"Afraid of noise? You should've thought of that before you took advantage! You got what you wanted and now you want me to take the blame alone — open your dead eyes and see who you're dealing with!"

"Say what you have to say, I'm listening!" Xiangzi had been feeling cold, but the scolding suddenly made him flush with heat, the warmth pushing against his frozen skin, his whole body prickling, his scalp especially irritated.

"Now that's more like it! Don't make things hard on yourself!" She curled her lip, showing her two prominent canine teeth. "Honestly, I really care about you — don't be ungrateful! Cross me, and there's nothing good in it for you, I'm telling you!"

"I'm not…" Xiangzi wanted to say something like "don't slap me and then rub it better," but he couldn't quite get it out. He knew plenty of Beijing witticisms, but couldn't say them smoothly himself — he understood them when others said them, but couldn't produce them on his own.

"Not what?"

"Go on, say your piece!"

"I've got a good plan for you," Tiger Girl stopped and faced him directly: "Look — if you sent a matchmaker, the old man would never agree. He runs a rickshaw yard, you pull rickshaws — he wouldn't want to marry down. I don't care about that. I like you, and liking you is enough — what does anything else matter! No matchmaker would work for me either. The moment anyone went to propose, the old man would think they were after his fleet of rickshaws. Even men of higher standing than you wouldn't do. This is something I have to handle myself. I've chosen you, and we've already done things the other way around. Either way, it's done — neither of us can run now! But we still can't just walk in and announce it outright. The older the old man gets, the more muddled he becomes. The moment we let anything slip, he'd go off and take a young wife and throw me out by force. The old man's still got it in him — don't let his nearly seventy years fool you. If he really took a young wife, I won't say how many, but I'd bet he could still produce two or three children — believe it or not!"

"Let's walk and talk." Xiangzi noticed the policeman on duty had already walked past twice and felt uneasy.

"We'll talk right here, who's going to stop us!" She followed Xiangzi's gaze and spotted the policeman too: "You're not even pulling a rickshaw — why are you afraid of him? He can't just bite someone's × off for no reason — now that would be something strange! Let's keep talking! Look, here's what I'm thinking: on the twenty-seventh, the old man's birthday, you go and kowtow to him three times. Then when the new year comes around, go pay your respects again and get on his good side. When I see he's in a good mood, we'll get some liquor and let him drink his fill. When he's about seven or eight cups in, we strike while the iron's hot — you just come right out and ask him to be your godfather. After that, I'll gradually let him know I'm not feeling well. He'll interrogate me, and I'll give him the 'Xu Shu entering Cao Cao's camp — not a word.' When he's really worked up, I'll give him a name — I'll say it was that Qiao Er who died recently — the second manager of the funeral parlor east of us. He had no family, and he's already buried in the common cemetery outside Dongzhimen. Where would the old man even begin to investigate? With no idea what to do, the old man will be at a loss, and then we slowly work on him — best case, he gives me to you. You'd already be his godson, and then his son-in-law too — it's not that far off. Going with the flow saves everyone from embarrassment. What do you think of my plan?"

Xiangzi said nothing.

Feeling she'd reached a natural pause, Tiger Girl began walking north, her head slightly bowed, as if savoring her own words, or perhaps giving Xiangzi a chance to think things over. Just then, the wind tore open a gap in the gray clouds, letting through a shaft of moonlight. The two of them had reached the north end of the street. The water of the Imperial Canal had long since frozen solid — still, gray-bright, flat, and firm — bearing the walls of the Forbidden City. Not a sound came from within. The delicate corner towers, the gilded archways, the vermilion gates, the pavilions on Jingshan Hill — all were hushed, as if listening for sounds that could never be heard again. A light wind passed through like a sigh, drifting softly among the halls and towers, as if trying to whisper some message from history. Tiger Girl turned west, and Xiangzi followed her to Jin'ao Yudong Bridge. There were almost no passersby on the bridge. The faint moonlight fell cold and still on the two great stretches of ice on either side, and in the distance the pavilions loomed as dark silhouettes, frozen silent above the lake, only the yellow roof tiles catching a faint glimmer. The trees stirred slightly, making the moonlight seem even more dim and distant. The White Pagoda rose high into the clouds, starkly white, casting a cold desolation over everything. The entire Sanhai area, shaped by human craft, revealed the bleak chill of the north. At the bridgehead, the cold rising from the ice on both sides made Xiangzi shudder, and he didn't want to go any further. On ordinary days, pulling his rickshaw across the bridge, he kept all his attention on his feet, afraid of making a mistake, never sparing a glance to either side. Now he was free to look — but what he saw filled him with a kind of dread: the gray, cold ice, the faintly swaying tree shadows, the ghastly white pagoda — all so silent they seemed on the verge of suddenly letting out a wild cry or breaking into a mad run. Even the great white stone bridge beneath his feet seemed unusually empty, unusually pale, the lamplight itself tinged with a kind of sorrow. He didn't want to walk any further, didn't want to look any more, and least of all did he want to keep her company. He truly wanted to leap down headfirst, crack through the ice, sink below, and freeze there like a dead fish.

"See you tomorrow!" He suddenly turned and walked back the way he came.

"Xiangzi! That's the plan then — see you on the twenty-seventh!" she called after his broad, straight back. When she finished, she glanced up at the White Pagoda, let out a sigh, and walked west. Xiangzi didn't even turn his head. As if something were chasing him, he covered the distance to the Round Fortress in a few quick strides, moving so fast he nearly ran into the city wall. One hand braced against the wall, he felt the urge to cry welling up. He stood there dazed for a moment, when from the bridge came a call: "Xiangzi! Xiangzi! Come here! Xiangzi!" Tiger Girl's voice!

He shuffled back toward the bridge with agonizing slowness. Tiger Girl was leaning forward slightly, coming down toward him, her mouth open a little: "Xiangzi, come here — I've got something for you!" Before he'd taken more than a few steps, she was already in front of him: "Here — the thirty-odd yuan you'd saved. There were some small change, so I rounded it up to a full yuan. Take it! Not for any other reason — just to show you how I feel. I think about you, I care about you, I look out for you! Never mind anything else — just don't be ungrateful. Take it! Hold onto it carefully — if you lose it, don't come blaming me!"

Xiangzi took the money — a wad of banknotes — and stood there for a moment, unable to find any words.

"Alright, see you on the twenty-seventh — I'll be there without fail!" She smiled. "The good deal is yours — work it out for yourself!" She turned and walked back.

He clutched the wad of notes and stared after her until the arc of the bridge swallowed her head from view. The gray clouds covered the moon again. The lamps grew brighter, and the bridge was exceptionally white, empty, and cold. He turned, quickened his pace, and walked back — like a man gone mad. By the time he reached the gate of his lodgings, that image of the pale, desolate bridge was still lodged in his mind, as if only the blink of an eye had passed.

Back in his room, he counted the banknotes first. He counted them two or three times, the sweat on his palm making them sticky, and still couldn't get a clean total. When he finished, he put them in the clay piggy bank. Sitting on the edge of the bed, staring blankly at the earthenware jar, he resolved not to think about anything. Money meant options — he trusted deeply that this piggy bank would solve everything for him, and there was no need to think further. The Imperial Canal, Jingshan, the White Pagoda, the bridge, Tiger Girl, the belly… all of it was a dream. And when the dream was over, the piggy bank held thirty-odd yuan more — real and solid!

He'd looked at it long enough. He hid the piggy bank away and decided to sleep — even the greatest troubles could be slept through, and tomorrow was another day.

He lay down, but couldn't close his eyes. Those thoughts swarmed like a hive of bees — one going out, another coming in, each one with a sting at its tip.

He didn't want to think about it, and truly had no way to think about it either — Tiger Girl had blocked every path, and there was no escape.

The best thing would be to pack up and leave. Xiangzi couldn't leave. He'd be willing to go guard the White Pagoda at Beihai — anything but leave for the countryside. Another city? He couldn't imagine anywhere better than Beijing. He couldn't leave. He'd rather die here.

Since he wasn't going to leave, there was no point spending any more energy thinking about the rest. Tiger Girl said what she meant and meant what she said. If he didn't go along with her plan, she really would follow him around making trouble. As long as he was in Beijing, she'd find him. With her, you had to be straight — there was no point trying to be clever. Push her too far, and she'd bring Liu Siye into it. And if Liu Siye decided to sell someone out — no need to exaggerate — he could have Xiangzi's life taken in some quiet corner without any trouble.

He went over Tiger Girl's words from beginning to end and felt like he'd fallen into a trap, his hands and feet caught in a snare, with no way out at all. He couldn't pick apart her plan point by point, so he couldn't find any crack in it. He only felt that she'd cast an airtight net — not even a fish an inch long could slip through. Since he couldn't think through it all in detail, he lumped it together into one solid mass, pressing down on his head like a thousand-pound sluice gate. Under this irresistible weight, he felt the whole fate of a rickshaw puller's life was contained in two words — rotten luck. A rickshaw puller, being a rickshaw puller, ought to want nothing else, not even to get tangled up with a woman — one tangle and you'd make a catastrophic mistake. Liu Siye had his fleet of rickshaws to lord over him, and Tiger Girl had her rotten × to bully him with! He didn't need to think any further. If he was going to accept his fate — fine, go kowtow and call someone godfather, then wait to marry that foul creature. If he wasn't going to accept it, he'd have to risk his life.

At that thought, he set Tiger Girl and her words aside. No — this wasn't about her cunning. This was simply the fate of a rickshaw puller, just as a dog is bound to be beaten and abused, and even children will hit it a couple of times for no reason at all. What was the point of a life like this? Throw caution to the wind, then!

He stopped trying to sleep, kicked off the covers with one foot, and sat up. He decided to go get some liquor and drink himself senseless. What rules, what obligations — × all of you! Drink, then sleep. The twenty-seventh? He wouldn't go kowtow on the twenty-eighth either — let anyone try to get the better of Xiangzi!

He threw on his heavy cotton jacket, picked up the small rice bowl he used as a teacup, and ran out. The wind had picked up a little. The gray clouds had scattered, and the moon was small, casting a cold light. Xiangzi had just come out of a warm bed and kept drawing in sharp breaths. The street was practically deserted. Along the roadside sat one or two rickshaws, their pullers cupping their hands over their ears, stamping their feet beside the carts to keep warm. Xiangzi ran straight to a small shop to the south. To keep in the warmth, the shop had put up its door panels, taking money and passing goods through a small window. Xiangzi asked for four liang of baijiu and three copper coins' worth of peanuts. He carried the bowl of liquor level in both hands, not daring to run, walking quickly instead like a sedan-chair bearer, and made it back to his room. He hurried under the covers, his upper and lower teeth chattering for a while, and didn't want to sit up again. The liquor sat on the table giving off its sharp, pungent smell — he didn't much like the scent, and seemed to have no heart even for the peanuts. That blast of cold air had been like a bucket of cold water thrown over him, sobering him up. His hands were too lazy to reach out, and his heart was no longer burning hot. He lay there for a long time,His eyes glanced again from the edge of the quilt at the bowl of wine on the table. No, he couldn't let that nagging entanglement ruin him, couldn't break his vow of abstinence over this. Things were indeed difficult to handle, but there was always a crack somewhere he could slip through. Even if there was truly no escape, he shouldn't be the one to roll himself into the mud first; he had to keep his eyes open, watch clearly and steadily, and see exactly how others would push him in.

He turned off the lamp, pulled the covers completely over his head, and tried to just fall asleep. Still he couldn't sleep. He lifted the quilt and looked out — the window paper was tinged blue by the moonlight from the courtyard, as if dawn were approaching. The tip of his nose felt the cold of the room, and mixed into the chill was a faint smell of wine. He suddenly sat up, grabbed the bowl, and swallowed a big mouthful.

Ten

Xiangzi wasn't clever enough to solve things one at a time. He didn't have the guts to settle the whole account at once. So he had no way out at all, and spent his days going around in circles with a belly full of grievances. Just like every living thing, after taking a hit, all he could do was try to pick up the pieces on his own. Like a cricket that's lost its hind legs, still trying to crawl with what's left. Xiangzi had no fixed plan — he just wanted to get through it slowly, day by day, one thing at a time, crawling as far as he could, with no thought of ever jumping back up.

There were still more than ten days until the twenty-seventh, but his whole mind was fixed on that date. What he thought about, what he muttered to himself, what he dreamed about — it was all the twenty-seventh. As if once the twenty-seventh passed, he'd have a way to solve everything, even though he knew full well he was only fooling himself. Sometimes he'd think further ahead — like taking the few dozen yuan in his pocket and heading to Tianjin. Once there, maybe he'd get lucky and switch trades, stop pulling a rickshaw altogether. Could Huniu really chase him all the way to Tianjin? In his mind, anywhere you had to take a train to was very far away, and no matter what, she couldn't follow him there. It sounded good, but his conscience told him this was only a last resort — if he could stay in Beiping, he'd rather stay in Beiping. And so he'd circle back to thinking about the twenty-seventh again. It was simpler and easier that way — as long as he got through this one hurdle, maybe he could slip past the whole mess without anything falling apart. Even if he couldn't shake everything off cleanly, getting past one obstacle was still getting past one.

How was he going to get through this hurdle? He had two ideas: one was to ignore the whole thing and simply not show up for the birthday celebration. The other was to do exactly as she had told him. The two ideas were different, but the outcome was the same — if he didn't go, she definitely wouldn't let it go. If he did go, she wouldn't let him off either. He still remembered when he first started pulling a rickshaw, imitating others by ducking into every alley he saw to cut corners, only to end up in a circular hutong. He'd gone around in a loop and come right back to the original street. Now he'd wandered into that same kind of alley again — it seemed like no matter which end you took, you ended up in the same place.

With no other option, he tried to look on the bright side — what would be so wrong with just taking her as his wife? But no matter how he looked at it, he felt suffocated. Thinking about her looks, he could only shake his head. Never mind the looks — thinking about her behavior, hmph! A man like him, so proud and proper, marrying a worn-out piece of goods like that — he'd never be able to face anyone again, not even his parents after death. And who could say for sure the child in her belly was even his? Sure, she'd bring a few rickshaws with her — but could you count on that? Old Liu wasn't someone to be trifled with. Even if everything went smoothly, he couldn't stand it. Could he ever get the better of Huniu? She only had to crook her little finger and she'd have him spinning so dizzy he wouldn't know north from south. He knew how fierce she was. If he was going to start a family, there was simply no way it could be with her — nothing more to say about it. Take her, and there'd be no more him — and he wasn't a man who looked down on himself. No way out.

With no way to deal with her, he turned his anger on himself and felt a strong urge to slap himself across the face a few times. But honestly, he hadn't really done anything wrong. She had set the whole thing up, just waiting for him to walk into the trap. The problem seemed to be that he was too honest — and being honest meant getting taken advantage of, with no reason or fairness to appeal to.

What made it even harder to bear was that he had nowhere to go and pour out his grievances. He had no parents, no brothers, no friends. In ordinary times, he felt like a real man standing under the open sky with nothing tying him down. Now he finally understood, finally came to his senses — a person can't live alone. Especially toward his fellow rickshaw pullers, he now felt a kind of fondness for them. If he'd made a few friends over the years, he thought — big fellows like himself — even with a Huniu on his hands, he wouldn't be afraid. They'd help him think things through, stand up for him and put in the effort. But he had always been alone, and trying to grab onto friends at the last minute wasn't easy. He felt a kind of fear he had never felt before. The way things were going, anyone could bully him. One person alone couldn't hold up the sky.

This fear made him start to doubt himself. In winter, when his employer had a dinner engagement or went to the opera, he would tuck the water canister for the carbide lamp inside his coat as a matter of course, since leaving it on the rickshaw would freeze it solid. He'd just been running, sweating all over, and the moment that ice-cold little canister pressed against his chest, he'd shudder instantly. It would take a good while before the canister warmed up at all. But in ordinary times, he never thought there was anything wrong with this. Sometimes when he tucked it in, he even felt a kind of superiority — those pulling broken-down rickshaws didn't even get to use carbide lamps. Now it seemed to him that for the little money he earned each month, he had to endure every hardship there was — even a small water canister couldn't be allowed to freeze, but had to be held against his chest. His chest, broad as it was, seemed worth less than that little canister. Before, he had thought pulling a rickshaw was his ideal life, that through it he could build a home and make something of himself. Now he shook his head quietly. No wonder Huniu bullied him — he was, after all, a man worth less than a little water canister.

On the third evening after Huniu had come looking for him, Mr. Cao went with a friend to a late-night film showing, and Xiangzi waited in a small teahouse with that ice-like little canister tucked against his chest. It was bitterly cold. The doors and windows of the small teahouse were shut tight, thick with coal fumes, the smell of sweat, and the stale smoke of cheap cigarettes. Even so, a layer of frost flowers had formed on the windows. Nearly all the tea drinkers were monthly-hire rickshaw pullers. Some leaned their heads against the wall, eyes closed, dozing in the warmth of the room. Some held a bowl of baijiu, offered it around to everyone, then slowly drank it down with a story from start to finish — after each sip, lips smacking above, a loud release of cold air below. Some gripped a rolled-up flatbread, biting off half in one go, necks bulging thick and red. Some wore tight, grim expressions, complaining to everyone in general about how they hadn't stopped moving since early morning, how their clothes had soaked through and dried and soaked through again, more times than they could count. Most of the others were chatting idly among themselves, but when they heard those words, everyone fell quiet for a moment — then, like birds startled from a nest, they all remembered their own day's grievances and wanted to tell everyone about them. Even the one eating the flatbread cleared just enough room in his mouth to work his tongue, swallowing bread and talking at the same time, the veins on his forehead jumping: "You think pulling a monthly hire is easy?! I've been at it since — hic! — two in the morning and haven't had a bite or a drop! They keep saying Qianmen to Pingzemen — hic! — I've done three round trips for them! In this weather, my ass crack's frozen solid, just keeps letting air!" He looked around at everyone, gave a nod, and took another bite of bread.

This turned everyone's talk back to the weather, and with the weather as the center, each person told of their own hardships. Xiangzi hadn't said a word the whole time, but he listened carefully to everything they said. Though the tone, pitch, and details of what everyone said were all different, it all came down to cursing and resentment. These words, meeting the grievances in his own heart, were like raindrops falling on bone-dry earth — soaked straight in. He had no way, and didn't know how, to tell his own story from beginning to end to everyone. He could only absorb the bitter taste of life from other people's words. Everyone was suffering, and he was no exception. Recognizing himself, he also wanted to feel for the others. When people got to the sad parts, he furrowed his brow. When they got to something funny, he'd twist his mouth a little. This way, he felt he was one of them — they were all fellow sufferers, and it didn't much matter that he hadn't said a word. Before, he had thought they were all just loose-tongued and foul-mouthed, that no matter how much they talked all day long, they'd never get rich. Today, for what felt like the first time, he sensed that they weren't just talking for the sake of it — they were speaking for him, giving voice to the hardships of him and every rickshaw puller.

Everyone was in the middle of lively talk when the door suddenly swung open and a gust of cold air rushed in. Nearly everyone glared toward the door, wanting to see who was so inconsiderate as to push it open. The more impatient everyone got, the slower the person outside moved, as if doing it on purpose.[1] The teahouse attendant called out, half annoyed and half laughing: "Hurry it up, will you, my dear uncle! Don't let out what little warmth we've got left!"

Before he'd finished saying that, the person outside came in — also a rickshaw puller. He looked to be over fifty, wearing a padded jacket that was neither short enough nor long enough, shaped like a lotus pod, with cotton stuffing poking out at the lapels and elbows. His face looked like it hadn't been washed in many days — you couldn't make out the color of his skin — only his two ears were frozen bright red, red as fruit about to drop from a branch. His sparse white hair stuck out in all directions beneath a tattered little cap. His eyebrows and short mustache were hung with beads of ice. The moment he came in, he felt for a bench and sat down, struggling to get out the words: "A pot of tea."

This teahouse had always been a gathering place for monthly-hire pullers. An old rickshaw man like this one would never have come in on an ordinary day.

Everyone looked at him, and all seemed to feel something even deeper than what had just been said. No one wanted to speak. On a normal day, there would always be one or two thoughtless young men who'd find a few clever remarks to poke fun at a customer like this. Today, not a single person made a sound.

The tea hadn't even been poured yet when the old rickshaw man's head began to slowly droop lower and lower, until his whole body slid down.

Everyone jumped to their feet at once: "What's wrong? What's wrong?" they said, all wanting to rush forward.

"Don't move!" The teahouse owner had experience. He held everyone back and went over alone, loosened the old man's collar, propped him up where he sat, braced a chair against his back, and gripped both his shoulders with his hands: "Sugar water, quick!" Then he leaned in and listened along the old man's neck, murmuring to himself: "Not a stroke!"

No one moved, but no one sat back down either. They all stood there in the smoke-filled room, blinking, looking toward the door. It was as if they all silently said to themselves without any prior agreement: "This is what's in store for us. When our hair turns white, every one of us has a fall like this waiting for us."

The sugar water had just been brought to the old man's lips when he let out two low groans. Still with his eyes closed, he raised his right hand — black and gleaming, as if lacquered — and wiped his mouth with the back of it.

"Drink some water!" the owner said close to his ear.

"Hm?" The old man opened his eyes. Seeing that he was sitting on the floor, he bent his legs and tried to stand.

"Have some water first, no rush," the owner said, letting go of him. Nearly everyone rushed over.

"Oh, oh!" The old man looked around at everyone, cupped the bowl in both hands, and sipped the sugar water slowly.

When he had slowly finished the sugar water, he looked at everyone again: "Oh, I've troubled you all!" It came out so gently and warmly, nothing like what you'd expect from that stubbly face. Then he tried to stand again, and three or four people hurried over to help him up. A faint smile came to his face, and he said just as gently: "It's fine, it's fine, don't worry. I was just cold and hungry — got a little dizzy for a moment. Nothing serious!" His face was caked with grime, but that little smile made everyone feel as though they were looking at a kind, clean face.

It seemed to move everyone. The middle-aged man who had been holding a bowl of liquor had already drained it, his eyes bloodshot, and now there were tears in them: "Another two liang!" When the liquor arrived, the old man was already seated in a chair against the wall. The middle-aged puller was a little drunk, but he set the liquor down properly in front of the old man: "My treat, please drink up. I'm past forty myself — not going to hide it, pulling a monthly hire is just getting by, one year at a time, and only your legs know how long they'll last. In another two or three years, I'll be in the same boat as you. You must be close to sixty?"

"Still young — fifty-five!" The old man took a sip of liquor. "It's cold, can't get any fares. Me, well — my stomach's empty. I had a few coins and drank them away for a bit of warmth. I was walking along and I really couldn't hold on anymore, so I thought I'd come in and get warm. It was too hot in here, and I hadn't eaten — must have fainted. It's nothing, it's nothing! I've troubled you all, brothers!"

At that moment, the old man's dry, straw-like gray hair, the grime on his face, his charcoal-black hands, his tattered cap and padded jacket — all of it seemed to give off a kind of pure light, like a statue in a ruined temple: broken, yet still dignified. Everyone looked at him as if afraid he might leave. Xiangzi had not said a word the whole time, standing there in a daze. When he heard the old man say his stomach was empty, he suddenly bolted out the door and came flying back, holding ten mutton-filled steamed buns in a cabbage leaf. He brought them straight to the old man, said "Eat up," then went back to his seat, lowered his head, and seemed utterly exhausted. "Oh!" The old man seemed both happy and on the verge of tears, nodding to everyone. "After all, it's your brothers who come through for you. You pull a fare, give everything you've got, and at the end of it, asking for even one extra coin feels like too much to ask!" As he said this, he stood up and made to leave.

"Eat first!" nearly everyone called out at once.

"I need to get little Xiao Ma'er — my little grandson, he's outside watching the rickshaw!" "I'll go, you sit down!" the middle-aged puller said. "The rickshaw won't go anywhere here, don't worry — there's a police box right across the way." He opened the door a crack: "Xiao Ma'er! Xiao Ma'er! Your grandpa's calling you! Leave the rickshaw here and come in!"

The old man touched the buns several times but never picked one up. The moment Xiao Ma'er stepped through the door, he picked one up: "Xiao Ma'er, good boy, here you go!" Xiao Ma'er was only about twelve or thirteen, with a thin face but a well-padded body, his nose frozen bright red and two white streams of snot hanging from it,Wearing a pair of worn ear flaps on his ears. Standing beside the old man, he took the steamed buns with his right hand, while his left hand automatically picked up another one, taking a bite out of it.

"Hey! Slow down!" The old man placed one hand on his grandson's head and picked up a bun with the other, slowly bringing it to his mouth. "Two is enough for grandpa, the rest are all yours! Once we're done eating, we'll pack up the cart and head home, no more rides today. If it's not this cold tomorrow, we'll head out earlier. Right, little Ma'er?"

Little Ma'er nodded at the buns and sniffled: "Grandpa should eat three, and the rest are mine. I'll pull grandpa home later!" "No need!" The old man smiled proudly at everyone: "We'll still walk back later, it's too cold sitting in the cart."

After finishing his share, the old man drained his cup of wine and waited for little Ma'er to finish the buns. He pulled out a worn piece of cloth, wiped his mouth, and nodded to everyone again: "My son went off to be a soldier and never came back; my daughter-in-law——"

"Don't talk about that!" Little Ma'er's cheeks puffed up like two little peaches, talking between bites to stop his grandfather.

"It's fine to talk about it! We're all family here!" Then he lowered his voice to everyone: "The child takes things to heart, you can't imagine how proud he is! The daughter-in-law left too. The two of us get by on this cart; the cart's a wreck, but it's ours, and at least we don't have to worry about rent every day. Whether we earn more or less, the two of us muddle through, what can you do! What can you do!"

"Grandpa," little Ma'er had nearly finished the buns and tugged at the old man's sleeve, "we still need to do one more run, we don't have money to buy coal tomorrow morning! It's all your fault, just now twenty coins to pull to the back gate, I said let's go, but you wouldn't! No coal tomorrow morning, let's see what you do!"

"There's a way, grandpa will go beg some coal briquettes on credit."

"And some kindling too?"

"Right! Good boy, eat up; when you're done, we should get moving!" With that, the old man stood up, turned to everyone and said: "Thank you all, gentlemen!" He reached out to pull little Ma'er along, and little Ma'er stuffed the remaining unfinished bun whole into his mouth. Some people stayed seated without moving, others followed them out. Xiangzi was the first to follow out, wanting to take a look at that cart.

It was an extremely worn-out cart, the paint on the cart board had cracked open, the handles had been rubbed down to bare wood grain, a rattling broken lantern, and the support rods of the cart canopy were tied with hemp rope. Little Ma'er fished out a match from inside his ear flap, struck it on the sole of his shoe, cupped it with two small black hands, and lit the lantern. The old man spat into his palm, let out a grunt, grabbed the handles, "See you tomorrow, friends!"

Xiangzi stood blankly outside the door, watching the old man, the young boy, and that broken cart. The old man kept talking as he walked, his voice rising and falling; the lamplight and shadows on the road shifted between light and dark. Xiangzi listened and watched, feeling a kind of sadness he had never felt before. In little Ma'er, he seemed to see his own past; in the old man, he seemed to see his own future! He had never easily let go of a single coin, but now he felt a surge of relief, and bought ten buns for the old man and the boy. Only after they had disappeared from sight did he go back inside. Everyone started talking and laughing again, and he felt restless, paid for his tea, walked back out, and pulled his cart to wait outside the cinema for Mr. Cao.

It was truly cold. Gray dust floated in the air, the wind seemed to be rushing somewhere above, the stars were barely visible, only the larger ones trembling faintly in the sky. There was no wind on the ground, but cold air seeped in from all directions, the cart ruts already showing long frozen cracks, the earth gray-white, as cold and hard as ice. Xiangzi stood outside the cinema for a while and already felt the chill, but he didn't want to go back to the teahouse. He wanted to quietly think things through on his own. The old man and the boy seemed to have shattered his greatest hope — the old man's cart was his own! From the very first day he pulled a rickshaw, he had decided to buy his own cart, and he was still grinding away every day for that same dream; with his own cart, he had believed, he would have everything. Hmph, just look at that old man!

He didn't want Tiger Girl, wasn't it precisely because of his dream of buying a cart? Buy a cart, save up money, then marry a decent woman with a clean slate; hmph, just look at little Ma'er! If he had his own son someday, it might turn out just the same.

Thinking this way, resisting Tiger Girl's pressure seemed pointless; after all, he couldn't escape the cycle, what kind of woman couldn't he take? Besides, she might even bring a few carts with her — why not enjoy a few days of ready-made comfort! Once you see yourself clearly, there's no need to look down on others. Tiger Girl is Tiger Girl, nothing more to say!

The film let out, and he quickly set up the small water flask, lit the lantern. He even took off his small padded jacket, left with just a thin shirt, wanting to run at full speed, run until he forgot everything — dying in a crash wouldn't matter much!

[1]

 磨烦, meaning to drag things out.

[2]

 横是, meaning probably.

Chapter Eleven

Whenever he thought of the old man and little Ma'er, Xiangzi wanted to give up all hope and just enjoy each day as it came — why spend every single day gritting his teeth and making life hard for himself?! The fate of the poor, he seemed to have figured out, was like a date pit, pointed at both ends: when you're young, it's a blessing just not to starve to death; when you're old, not starving is nearly impossible. Only the middle stretch, when you're young and strong, unafraid of hunger or hard work, can you live something like a human life. During that stretch, when you should be enjoying yourself you don't dare to — that's being a real fool; once you pass that village, there's no inn ahead! Thinking this way, he stopped worrying even about the whole business with Tiger Girl.

But when he looked at that sealed clay jar, his thoughts shifted again. No, he couldn't be reckless; he was only a few dozen yuan away from buying a cart, he couldn't throw away all his hard work; at the very least he couldn't waste the savings in that jar, they had been so hard to put aside! He still had to stay on the right path, for certain! But what about Tiger Girl? Still no solution, still had to worry about that hateful twenty-seventh.

Worrying until there was nothing left to do, he held the clay jar and muttered to himself: do whatever you want, this money is mine no matter what! Nobody can take it away! With this money, Xiangzi feared nothing! With money, his legs would carry him anywhere!

The streets grew livelier and livelier, candy melons for the Kitchen God Festival filled the stalls, and everywhere you went you could hear the calls of "candy here, candy." Xiangzi had been looking forward to the New Year, but now he felt no excitement at all; the more chaotic the streets, the tighter his heart grew, that dreaded twenty-seventh was right around the corner! His eyes sank deeper, even the scar on his face seemed to darken. Pulling his cart through the busy, slippery streets, he had to be extra careful. The weight of his worries and the need to stay alert pressed on him from both sides, leaving him feeling mentally drained; thinking of one thing made him forget another, and he would suddenly startle from time to time, his body prickling like a child breaking out in heat rash in summer.

On the afternoon of the Kitchen God Festival, a steady east wind brought in a sky full of dark clouds. The weather suddenly turned a little warmer. By the time the lamps were being lit, the wind had died down further, and sparse snowflakes began to fall from the sky. The candy melon vendors all grew anxious — the warmth, combined with the snowflakes, had everyone frantically dusting white powder onto the candy, still afraid it would all stick together. The snowflakes didn't fall for long before turning into small pellets of sleet, rustling softly as they fell, whitening the ground. After seven o'clock, shops and households began their Kitchen God offerings, and amid the incense glow and firecracker smoke fell a dense flurry of fine snow, giving the festive scene an eerie undertone. People on the streets all looked hurried and anxious — those on foot and those in rickshaws alike rushed to get home to worship the god, yet the wet and slippery ground kept them from walking too fast. The street vendors selling candy called out breathlessly, trying to move their seasonal goods, their cries sounding strangely unsettling to the ears.

It was around nine o'clock when Xiangzi pulled Mr. Cao home from the western part of the city. Past the busy stretch at Xidan Pailou, heading east onto Chang'an Street, the people and traffic gradually thinned out. The flat asphalt road was dusted with a thin layer of snow that glinted faintly under the streetlights. Occasionally a car would pass, its headlights reaching far ahead, the sleet pellets catching the light with a faint golden shimmer, like a scattering of ten thousand grains of gold. Near Xinhua Gate, the road was already exceptionally wide, and with the thin snow it felt even more open and refreshing, while everything seemed to take on a more solemn air. The Chang'an Pailou, the gate tower of Xinhua Gate, the red walls of Nanhai — all wore white caps, set against vermilion pillars and red walls, quietly displaying the dignity of the old capital beneath the lamplight. In that moment and place, one felt as though Beijing had no inhabitants at all, as if it were nothing but a palace of jade and crystal, with only ancient pines silently receiving the snowflakes. Xiangzi had no time to take in the scenery; one look at the "jade road" ahead and he only wanted to be home in a single stride; the straight, white, still road seemed to lead his eyes directly to his front door. But he couldn't run fast — though the snow wasn't thick, it clung to his feet, and in no time a thick layer would build up on the soles of his shoes; stamp it off, and it would stick again. The sleet pellets were tiny but heavy and substantial, clinging to his feet and stinging his eyes, keeping him from running at full speed. The pellets didn't melt easily on his body either, and a thin layer had already accumulated on his shoulders — not much, but the dampness made him uncomfortable. There were few shops in this stretch, but the sound of firecrackers in the distance kept going without pause, with double-bangers and five-ghost crackers shooting up into the dark sky from time to time. The sparks scattered and fell, making the sky above seem even darker, dark enough to be almost frightening. He listened to the firecrackers, watched the sparks and darkness overhead, and wanted to be home immediately. But he didn't dare let his legs go — it was just too awkward!

What made him even more uneasy was that since leaving the western city, he had sensed a bicycle following behind him. Once on West Chang'an Street, with the road quieter, the trailing presence became even more noticeable — the wheels pressing over the thin snow made only a faint sound, but it was unmistakable. Xiangzi, like other rickshaw pullers, had a deep dislike of bicycles. Cars were obnoxious, but they were loud and you could get out of the way long before they reached you. Bicycles squeezed through any gap and wobbled from side to side, dizzying just to look at. On top of that, you had to be careful not to make any mistakes, because if something went wrong it was always the rickshaw puller's fault — the police had it figured out in their heads that no matter what, a rickshaw puller was easier to deal with than a cyclist, so they always pinned the blame on the rickshaw puller first. Several times Xiangzi was tempted to suddenly brake and send the person behind him flying. But he didn't dare — rickshaw pullers had to swallow their anger everywhere they went. Whenever he needed to stamp the snow off his soles, he had to call out: "Braking!" When they reached the front gate of Nanhai, the road was so wide, yet that bicycle still clung close behind. Xiangzi grew even more irritated, and deliberately stopped the cart to brush the snow off himself. He stood still, and the bicycle squeezed past beside the cart. The rider even looked back. Xiangzi deliberately dragged his feet, waiting until the bicycle had gone far ahead before picking up the handles again, muttering: "Annoying!" Mr. Cao's "humanitarianism" meant he refused to put up the windproof cotton canopy, and even the canvas canopy was not to be raised unless it was actually raining heavily, all to spare the rickshaw puller some effort. He felt a little snow like this didn't warrant putting up the canopy, and besides, he was enjoying the view of the snowy night. He had also noticed the bicycle, and after Xiangzi finished grumbling, he said quietly, "If he keeps following us, don't stop at our gate — go to Mr. Zuo's place on Huanghua Gate; don't panic!"

Xiangzi felt a twinge of panic. He had only thought of the cyclist as annoying, not realizing there was something frightening about it — if even Mr. Cao didn't dare go home, this person must be no ordinary figure! He ran a few dozen paces and caught up with the man, who had apparently been waiting deliberately for him and Mr. Cao. The bicycle let Xiangzi pass, and Xiangzi took a glance at the rider. One glance was enough — plainclothes detective. He had often run into people from the detective squad at teahouses, and though he'd never spoken to them, he knew their manner and dress. This one's outfit looked familiar: a blue padded jacket, a felt hat pulled down low.

At the corner of South Changstreet, Xiangzi used the turn as an opportunity to steal a glance behind him — the man was still following. He almost forgot about the snow underfoot and quickened his pace. The long, straight, white-lit road, with nothing but cold lamplight, and a detective trailing behind! Xiangzi had never been through anything like this, and he broke into a sweat. By the back gate of the park, he looked back again — still there! At his own gate, he didn't dare stop, yet felt reluctant to leave; Mr. Cao said nothing, so he had no choice but to keep running north. He ran all the way to the north end, and the bicycle was still following! He turned into a small alley — still following! Out of the alley — still following! Heading to Huanghua Gate, he shouldn't have gone through the small alley, and only when he reached the north end of the alley did he realize his mistake; he admitted to himself that he had been a bit flustered, which made him even more annoyed. He ran to the back of Jingshan, and the bicycle turned north toward the back gate and was gone. Xiangzi wiped the sweat from his face. The snow had lightened a little, but among the sleet pellets a few snowflakes had reappeared. Xiangzi seemed to like the snowflakes — drifting freely through the air, not as irritating as the sleet pellets. He turned and asked: "Where to, sir?"

"Still to Mr. Zuo's place. If anyone asks you about me, say you don't know me!"

"Understood!" Xiangzi's heart was pounding, but it wasn't the right moment to ask questions.

At the Zuo residence, Mr. Cao told Xiangzi to pull the cart inside and quickly close the gate. Mr. Cao remained fairly composed, but his expression was not good. After giving Xiangzi his instructions, he went inside. Xiangzi had just pulled the cart into the entryway and set it down when Mr. Cao came back out, accompanied by Mr. Zuo; Xiangzi knew him, and knew that Mr. Zuo was a close friend of the household. "Xiangzi," Mr. Cao's lips moved quickly, "take a car back. Tell the mistress I'm here. Have them come too, by car, in a separate one,"No need for you to come along — wait here for that car. Understand? Good! Tell the mistress to bring the things she needs, and those few paintings from the study. Got it? I'll call her right now, just to tell you again in case she gets flustered and forgets what I said — you can remind her."

"Should I go?" Mr. Zuo asked.

"No need! That person just now may not necessarily have been a detective, but I have a feeling about it and can't afford not to take precautions. Could you call a car first?" Mr. Zuo went to phone for a car. Mr. Cao reminded Xiangzi once more: "When the car arrives, I'll pay here. Tell the mistress to pack quickly — nothing else matters much, just make absolutely sure to bring the child's things, and those few paintings from the study, those few paintings! Once the mistress is ready, have Gao Ma call for a car and come here. Is all that clear? After they leave, lock the front gate, move into the study to sleep — there's a phone there. Can you use a phone?"

"I can't dial out, but I can answer." In truth Xiangzi wasn't very fond of answering the phone either, but not wanting to worry Mr. Cao, he agreed.

"That'll do!" Mr. Cao continued, still speaking quickly: "If anything stirs, don't go open the door! We'll all be gone, leaving just you — they won't let you off! If things look bad, turn off the lights and jump over the back wall into the Wang family's yard. Do you know the Wangs? Good! Hide there for a bit before you leave. Don't worry about my things or your own things — just go over the wall, so you don't get caught! If anything of mine goes missing, I'll make it up to you later. Here's five yuan for now. All right, I'm going to call the mistress — go over it with her again when you see her. No need to mention an arrest; that cyclist just now may or may not have been a detective — don't panic just yet!"

Xiangzi's mind was in a jumble, as though there were many things he wanted to ask, but afraid of losing track of Mr. Cao's instructions, he dared not ask anything more.

The car arrived, and Xiangzi climbed in, dazed and clumsy. Snow was falling steadily — not heavy, not light — and things outside the car were hard to make out clearly. He sat bolt upright, his head nearly touching the roof. He tried to think things through, but his eyes kept drifting to the red arrow on the dashboard, so vivid and bright. The small wiper in front of the driver swung back and forth on its own, clearing the mist from the glass — that was rather interesting too. Just as he was growing tired of watching it, the car had already reached the house, and he stepped out feeling vaguely unsettled.

He was just about to press the doorbell when someone seemed to materialize out of the wall and grabbed him by the wrist. Xiangzi instinctively tried to pull free, but then he got a clear look at the man and went still — it was the very detective who had been on the bicycle earlier.

"Xiangzi, don't you recognize me?" The detective smiled and let go of his wrist.

Xiangzi swallowed, not knowing what to say.

"Don't you remember — you were the one who got dragged up to the Western Hills with us? I'm Platoon Leader Sun. Coming back to you now?"

"Ah, Platoon Leader Sun!" Xiangzi couldn't really remember. When the soldiers had hauled him up into the hills, he hadn't had the presence of mind to notice who was a platoon leader and who was a company commander.
