"You don't remember me, but I remember you — that scar on your face is a good marker. I followed you for quite a while just now; at first I wasn't sure it was you, but no matter how I looked, that scar couldn't be wrong!"

"Is there something you need?" Xiangzi moved to press the doorbell again.

"Naturally there is, and it's important! Shall we go inside and talk?" Platoon Leader Sun — now a detective — reached out and pressed the bell himself.

"I'm busy!" Sweat suddenly broke out on Xiangzi's forehead, and he thought bitterly: "I can't even get away from him — how can I invite him in?"

"Don't worry, I'm here for your own good!" The detective showed a sly smile. When Gao Ma opened the gate, he stepped right in: "Excuse me, excuse me!" Before Xiangzi or Gao Ma could say a word, he pulled Xiangzi along inside, pointing at the gatehouse: "You live here?" He stepped into the room and glanced around: "Tidy little place! You've got it pretty good!"

"Is there something you need? I'm busy!" Xiangzi couldn't stand any more small talk.

"Didn't I tell you it's important!" Detective Sun was still smiling, but his tone was very stern. "I'll be straight with you — this Cao fellow is a subversive. Once they catch him it's a firing squad, and he won't get away regardless. We've met before, and you waited on me back at the barracks; besides, we're both people of the streets, so I'm taking a big risk coming here to warn you! If you're even a step too slow getting out, they'll come and clean out the whole nest — nobody gets away. We work for our rice, why get tangled up with people like them? Am I right?"

"I'd be letting people down," Xiangzi said, still thinking of Mr. Cao's instructions.

"Letting who down?" The corners of Detective Sun's mouth curved up while his eyes turned sharp. "They brought this trouble on themselves — who are you letting down? They dare to act, they dare to face the consequences; we follow along and suffer for it — that's just not worth it! Never mind anything else — lock you up for three months, you who've been free as a wild bird your whole life, stuck in a dark cell, could you take it? And when they go to prison, they've got money to grease palms and won't suffer much; but you, my good brother, with nothing hard in your hand, you'll be chained to the slop bucket for sure! And that's the least of it — if they're lucky enough to spend their way into a few years' hard labor, the authorities will need someone to answer for it, and if they don't use you as a scapegoat I'd be surprised. We haven't wronged anyone, we haven't provoked anyone, and in the end we go to Tianqiao to eat a black bullet — isn't that a raw deal? You're a smart man, and a smart man doesn't take a loss right in front of him. Being loyal — hah! Let me tell you, my friend, there has never been anything in this world that's been fair to us poor working folk!"

Xiangzi was frightened. Remembering the misery of being dragged off by the soldiers, he could well imagine what prison would feel like. "So I should just leave, and not worry about them?"

"You look out for them — who looks out for you?!"

Xiangzi had no answer. He stood there for a moment, and even his conscience nodded along: "All right, I'll go!"

"Just walk away like that?" Detective Sun gave a cold laugh.

Xiangzi was confused again.

"Xiangzi, my good friend! You're too naive! You think a detective like me would just let you walk away?"

"Then —" Xiangzi was so anxious he didn't know what to say.

"Don't play dumb!" Detective Sun's eyes fixed on Xiangzi's. "I'd guess you've got some savings — hand them over and buy yourself a life! I don't even earn as much as you in a month, and I've got to eat, dress, and support a family — I depend entirely on a little extra on the side, and I'm telling you this straight from the heart! Think about it — can I just open my hand and let you go? Brotherhood is brotherhood — if there were no brotherhood, would I have come to warn you? But business is business. If I don't get something out of this, am I supposed to let my whole family starve? No need to waste words between men who know the world — give it to me straight!"

"How much?" Xiangzi sat down on the bed.

"Whatever you've got — no fixed price!"

"I might as well wait and go to prison then!"

"Those are your words? No regrets?" Detective Sun slipped his hand inside his padded robe. "Look at this, Xiangzi! I can arrest you right now — if you resist, I'll shoot! I'll take you away this instant, and forget about money — the clothes on your back will be stripped off the moment you walk through the prison gate. You're a smart man, think it over yourself!"

"If you've got time to squeeze me, why not squeeze Mr. Cao?" Xiangzi finally managed to say after a long struggle.

"He's the main criminal — catch him and there's a reward, but fail to catch him and you're in trouble. You — you, my foolish brother — letting you go is like letting off a fart; killing you is like squashing a bedbug! Pay up and you go your way; don't pay — fine, see you at Tianqiao! No more fuss, let's be direct, a grown man like you! Besides, this money can't all go to me alone — the boys all need a cut, who knows how many coins it'll come to in the end. Such a cheap price for your life and you still won't do it — I'm out of options! How much money do you have?"

Xiangzi stood up, his mind pounding, and clenched his fists.

"Don't try anything — I'm warning you, there's a whole crowd of people outside! Hurry up, the money! I'm doing you a favor — don't be ungrateful!" Detective Sun's expression turned very ugly.

"What did I ever do to anyone?!" Xiangzi said, his voice breaking, and then sat back down on the edge of the bed.

"You didn't do anything to anyone — you just happened to be in the wrong place at the wrong time! A man's got to be born rich; we're both from the bottom. Nothing more to say!" Detective Sun shook his head with an air of boundless resignation. "All right, consider it my wronging you — stop dragging this out!"

Xiangzi thought a while longer and saw no way out. His hands trembling, he pulled the clay savings jar from inside his bedding.

"Let me see!" Detective Sun smiled and snatched the earthen jar, then knocked it against the wall.

Xiangzi watched the coins scatter across the floor, his heart splitting open.

"This is all there is?"

Xiangzi said nothing, left with nothing but trembling.

"Fine. I won't wipe you out completely — friends are friends. But you should know, this money buying a life is a bargain!"

Xiangzi still said nothing, trembling as he moved to roll up his bedding.

"Leave that too!"

"It's so cold…" Xiangzi's eyes blazed with fury.

"I told you don't touch it — don't touch it! Get out!"

Xiangzi swallowed, bit his lip, pushed open the door and walked out.

The snow had already fallen over an inch deep. Xiangzi walked with his head down. Everything was clean and white, with only a trail of large dark footprints left behind him.

12

Xiangzi wanted to find somewhere to sit down and think everything through from beginning to end — even if all he could do afterward was cry, at least he'd know what he was crying about. Things had changed too fast, and his mind could no longer keep up. There was nowhere for him to sit — everywhere was snow. The small teahouses had all put up their doors; it was past ten. Even if one had been open, he wouldn't have gone in — he wanted a quiet place, knowing that the tears circling in his eyes could fall at any moment. Since there was nowhere to sit, he could only walk slowly. But where to go? In this silver-white world there was no place for him to sit, and no place for him to go. In the vast white expanse, only the hungry little birds and the people with nowhere to turn knew what it meant to grieve.

Where to go? That was the question — no need to think about anything else yet! A cheap lodging house? No good — dressed as he was, something would go missing in the night, never mind how terrifying the lice would be. A better inn? He couldn't afford it — he had only five yuan to his name, and that was his entire fortune. A bathhouse? They closed at midnight and didn't allow overnight stays. There was nowhere to go.

It was precisely because there was nowhere to go that he felt his own wretchedness all the more keenly. After all these years scraping by in the city, he had nothing to show for it but the clothes on his back and five yuan — he'd even lost his bedding! From there his thoughts turned to tomorrow — what then? Pull a rickshaw, go back to pulling a rickshaw — hah, and what did pulling a rickshaw get him? No place to sleep and his savings stolen away! Set up a small street stall? With only five yuan as capital, he'd have to buy a carrying pole and baskets from scratch, and besides, what trade could reliably earn enough to eat? Pulling a rickshaw could bring in thirty or forty cents a day on flat ground; a small stall required capital and offered no guarantee of earning enough for three meals. By the time he'd eaten through the capital, he'd go back to pulling a rickshaw — wasn't that just taking off his trousers to pass wind, throwing away five yuan for nothing? He couldn't let go of a single cent of those five yuan — it was his last hope! Go into domestic service? He wasn't cut out for it: waiting on people, he didn't know how; washing clothes and cooking, he didn't know how either! Nothing worked, nothing was possible — he was nothing but a big, dumb, clumsy, useless lump!

Without realizing it, he had come to Zhonghai. Standing on the bridge, the open expanse stretched in all directions, nothing but snowflakes as far as the eye could see. Only then did he seem to realize the snow had not stopped; he touched his head and found his knitted cap already quite wet. There was no one on the bridge — even the policeman on duty had disappeared somewhere — and a few electric lights blinked as if the snowflakes were striking them. Xiangzi looked out at the snow all around him, his heart empty.

He stood on the bridge for a long time. The world seemed to have died — not a sound, not a stir. The grey-white snowflakes seemed to seize their chance, falling in a frantic, light, relentless stream, as if to bury the world before anyone noticed. In that silence, Xiangzi heard the quiet voice of his own conscience. Never mind yourself for now — you still have to go back and check on the Cao household. Only the mistress and Gao Ma left there, not a single man! And hadn't those last five yuan come from Mr. Cao himself? He dared not think any further. He turned and walked back, very fast.

There were some footprints outside the gate, and two fresh car tracks on the road. Could the mistress have already left? Why hadn't that Sun fellow arrested them?

He didn't dare push the gate open, afraid of being caught again. He looked left and right — no one. His heart began to pound. Let's try, he thought — I have no home to go back to anyway, and if I get caught, so be it. He gave the gate a gentle push and it swung open. He walked a couple of steps along the base of the wall and saw a light in his own room — his own room! He nearly wept. He crept over and listened outside the window. A cough from inside — Gao Ma's voice! He pulled open the door.

"Who's there? Oh, it's you! You nearly scared me to death!" Gao Ma pressed her hand to her chest, steadied herself, and sat down on the bed. "Xiangzi, what on earth happened?"

Xiangzi couldn't answer. He only felt as though he hadn't seen her in years, a warm lump rising in his chest.

"What happened?" Gao Ma asked, looking as though she might cry too: "You hadn't come back yet, and the master called to tell us to go to Mr. Zuo's place, saying you'd be right along. You did come back — wasn't it me who opened the gate for you? I saw you had a stranger with you, so I didn't say a word and went straight in to help the mistress pack. You never came inside. The two of us were fumbling around in the dark, the young master already sound asleep and having to be pulled out of his warm bed. We got everything bundled up,I went back to the study to take down the paintings, and you were nowhere to be found the whole time — what's gotten into you? I'm asking you! I rushed through packing up, came out to look for you, and fine, you'd vanished! The mistress was so angry — and half out of worry — she was shaking all over. I had no choice but to call for a car. But we couldn't just do an "empty city" trick and all leave together. Fine, I talked the mistress into it, told her to go ahead and I'd stay and watch. If Xiangzi came back, I'd head straight to the Zuo place; if he didn't, then that's just my fate! What kind of thing is that to say! What happened to you — speak up!"

Xiangzi had nothing to say.

"Say something! What good does standing there like a statue do? What on earth happened?"

"You go!" Xiangzi finally managed to find something to say: "Go on!"

"You'll watch the house?" Gao Ma's anger eased a little.

"When you see the master, tell him — a detective grabbed me, but then, but then, didn't grab me!"

"What kind of thing is that to say?" Gao Ma was so exasperated she almost laughed.

"Listen to me!" Xiangzi's voice turned urgent: "Tell the master to run, fast — the detective said they'll definitely catch him. The Zuo place isn't safe either. Run! Once you're gone, I'll hop over to the Wang place and sleep there for the night. I'll lock the front gate here. Tomorrow, I'll go find my own way. I'm sorry, Master Cao!"

"The more you say, the more confused I get!" Gao Ma sighed. "Alright, I'm going — the young master might be freezing by now, I need to go check. When I see the master, I'll tell him what Xiangzi said — that he should run fast. Tonight Xiangzi will lock the front gate and hop over to the Wang place to sleep; tomorrow he'll go find work. Is that right?"

Xiangzi nodded, deeply ashamed.

After Gao Ma left, Xiangzi locked the front gate and went back inside. The broken clay jar was still lying on the ground; he picked up a piece of tile, looked at it, and tossed it back down as before. The bedding on the bed hadn't been touched. Strange — what on earth had happened? Could it be that Detective Sun wasn't a real detective? Impossible! If Master Cao hadn't sensed some danger, why would he have fled his home? He couldn't figure it out. He couldn't figure it out. Without realizing it, he sat down on the edge of the bed. The moment he sat down, he startled as if frightened and stood back up. He couldn't linger here! What if that man surnamed Sun came back?! His mind raced quickly: he'd let Master Cao down, but Gao Ma was carrying word back telling him to run fast — that was at least something. In good conscience, Xiangzi had never meant to deceive anyone, and he himself had been wronged. His own money was gone first, and there was no way to look after Master Cao anymore. Muttering to himself like this, he began gathering up his bedding.

He hoisted the bedding onto his back, turned off the light, and headed for the back courtyard. He set the bedding down, gripped the top of the wall with his hands, and called out softly: "Old Cheng! Old Cheng!" Old Cheng was the Wang family's rickshaw puller. No one answered, so Xiangzi made up his mind to jump over first and deal with the rest later. He threw the bedding over; it landed in the snow without much sound. His heart pounded for a moment. Then he climbed up the wall and jumped over. He picked up the bedding from the snow and quietly went to find Old Cheng. He knew where Old Cheng stayed. Everyone seemed to be asleep already; the whole courtyard was completely silent. Xiangzi suddenly felt that being a thief wasn't such a difficult thing — he took a little courage and walked with steady steps; the snow was packed firm and made a faint crunching sound. He found Old Cheng's room and coughed once. Old Cheng sounded like he'd just lain down: "Who's there?"

"It's me, Xiangzi! Open the door!" Xiangzi said it in the most natural, gentle way — hearing Old Cheng's voice felt like hearing the comfort of a family member. Old Cheng turned on the light, draped a worn fur jacket over his shoulders, and opened the door: "What's going on? Xiangzi! In the middle of the night!"

Xiangzi went in, set his bedding on the ground, and sat down on top of it, again at a loss for words.

Old Cheng was in his thirties, with flesh on his face and body that was lumpy and hard, with sharp edges. Ordinarily, Xiangzi and he weren't particularly close — they just nodded and exchanged a few words when they met. Sometimes, when Mrs. Wang and Mrs. Cao went out shopping together, the two of them had more chances to sit and drink tea and rest. Xiangzi greatly admired Old Cheng — Old Cheng ran fast, but in a flustered, frantic way, and his hands always seemed unsteady on the shafts. As a person, Old Cheng was quite decent, but because of this flaw, Xiangzi could never fully admire him.

Today, Xiangzi found Old Cheng completely endearing. Sitting there, he couldn't find anything to say, yet inside he felt grateful and warm. A moment ago he had been standing on the bridge at Zhonghai; now he was sitting indoors with someone he knew. The sudden shift left him feeling hollow inside, yet at the same time a little warmth stirred within him.

Old Cheng burrowed back under his covers and pointed to the worn fur jacket: "Help yourself to a smoke, Xiangzi — there's some in the pocket. They're Bieye brand." The "Bieshu" brand cigarettes had been renamed "Bieye" by rickshaw pullers ever since they first appeared.

Xiangzi didn't normally smoke, but this time it seemed impossible to refuse. He took a cigarette and put it between his lips, puffing away.

"What happened?" Old Cheng asked. "Did you quit your job?"

"No," Xiangzi was still sitting on his bedding, "something went wrong! Master Cao's whole family ran off, and I didn't dare stay and watch the place alone!"

"What kind of trouble?" Old Cheng sat up again.

"Hard to explain — whatever it is, it's no small thing. Even Gao Ma left!"

"The whole place wide open, nobody watching?"

"I locked the front gate!"

"Hmm!" Old Cheng thought for a long while. "Should I go let Master Wang know?" He was already reaching for his clothes.

"Tell him tomorrow — the whole thing is just impossible to explain!" Xiangzi was afraid Master Wang would question him.

The thing Xiangzi couldn't explain went like this: Master Cao taught a few hours of classes at a university. There was a student there named Ruan Ming who had always been on good terms with Master Cao and often came to chat with him. Master Cao was a socialist, and Ruan Ming's views were even more radical, so the two got along well. However, their difference in age and position led to some minor friction: from Master Cao's standpoint as a teacher, he should teach conscientiously, and students should complete their coursework properly — personal feelings couldn't be allowed to affect grades. From Ruan Ming's perspective, in a world this broken and chaotic, a young person with ambition should be doing revolutionary work, and grades could wait. He kept up with Master Cao partly because they still had things to talk about, and partly in hopes that their friendship would earn him a passing grade no matter how badly he did on his exams. Idealists in turbulent times often have a reckless streak — history offers no shortage of such forgivable examples.

When exam time came, Master Cao did not give Ruan Ming a passing grade. Ruan Ming's scores were so poor that even if Master Cao had passed him, he would still have had more than enough grounds for suspension. Yet he harbored a particular hatred for Master Cao. He felt Master Cao had no sense of face; and face, in China, carries the same weight as revolution. Because he was eager to do something, Ruan Ming looked down on learning. Because he looked down on learning, he gradually grew accustomed to laziness, wanting to earn everyone's admiration and affection without any effort — after all, his ideas were progressive! Master Cao's refusal to pass him clearly showed a failure to understand an ambitious young person; in that case, there was no point in being friendly in the first place! Since they'd been on good terms and then Master Cao had humiliated him at exam time, he concluded that Master Cao was a two-faced schemer. The grades couldn't be salvaged, the suspension couldn't be fought — he wanted to vent his anger on Master Cao. Since he himself had been expelled, he'd drag a teacher down with him. That way he could make a show of it and demonstrate that he was not someone to be trifled with. Ruan Ming was not to be messed with! Besides, if this whole affair could get him into a new organization, that was still better than having nothing to do.

He compiled what Master Cao had said in lectures and in their private conversations — remarks about political and social issues — and went to the party office to report him: Master Cao was spreading radical ideas among young people.

Master Cao had heard something of this, but found it rather amusing. He knew how half-hearted his own socialism was, and how much his traditional love of art held him back from any drastic action. It was laughable — to be branded a revolutionary mentor! Laughable, and so he didn't pay it much mind, even though students and colleagues all told him to be careful. Composure, however, cannot — in turbulent times — guarantee safety. The winter break was a good opportunity to purge the schools, and detectives began busying themselves with investigations and arrests. Master Cao had noticed on several occasions that someone seemed to be following him. The shadow behind him changed his amusement to seriousness. He had to think it over: for the sake of building a reputation, this was a good opportunity — spending a few days in jail was easier, safer, and just as valuable as throwing a bomb. A stint in prison was a credential for becoming a man of importance. But he refused. He refused to play along and build himself a false reputation. In good conscience, he hated that he couldn't be a true fighter; in good conscience, he also refused to be a counterfeit one. He went to find Mr. Zuo. Mr. Zuo had a plan: "When the time comes, move in with me — they wouldn't dare search my place!" Mr. Zuo knew people; connections were more powerful than the law. "Come stay here for a few days and lie low. We'll let them think they've scared us. Then we'll work through some channels — might cost a bit of money. Once face is satisfied and money changes hands, you can go home and it'll all blow over."

Detective Sun knew that Master Cao often visited the Zuo residence, and knew that when things got tight, Master Cao would certainly go there. They didn't dare offend Mr. Zuo, but they could frighten Master Cao. Drive him to the Zuo place, and then they'd have a chance to squeeze money out of him — and do it with plenty of face. Shaking down Xiangzi hadn't been part of the detectives' plan, but since they'd run into him, it was easy pickings — why not pocket ten or eight yuan on the side?

Right — Xiangzi had simply been in the wrong place at the wrong time. Bad luck. Everyone had their angles, every situation had its cracks — only Xiangzi had nowhere to run, because he was a rickshaw puller. A rickshaw puller swallows coarse grain and sweats blood; he gives his greatest effort for the lowest pay; he stands at the very bottom of the human world, waiting to be struck by every person, every law, every hardship.

By the time the cigarette burned down, Xiangzi still couldn't make sense of any of it. He was like a chicken dangling from a cook's hand — all he knew was that catching his breath was good; he had no other ideas. He very much wanted to talk with Old Cheng, but had nothing to say. His words weren't enough to express what was in his heart. He had tasted every kind of bitterness, yet his mouth wouldn't open — like a mute. He'd saved for a rickshaw, and the rickshaw was gone; he'd saved money, and the money was gone; all his efforts only gave others a chance to bully him! He didn't dare provoke anyone — he even had to dodge stray dogs — and in the end he was still bullied until he couldn't breathe! Never mind the past for now — what about tomorrow? He couldn't go back to the Cao place. Where would he go? "Can I sleep here for the night?" he asked, like a stray dog that had found a sheltered corner and just needed to hold on a little longer — but even for something this small, he had to make sure it wasn't putting anyone out.

"Stay here then — where would you go in this ice and snow? Is the floor alright? You can squeeze up here with me if you want!"

Xiangzi didn't want to squeeze in; the floor was fine.

Old Cheng fell asleep. Xiangzi tossed and turned, unable to sleep at all. The cold from the floor soon turned the mattress icy as a sheet of iron; he curled his legs up, and his calves felt like they might cramp. The cold draft coming through the crack in the door pricked at his head like a swarm of tiny needles. He squeezed his eyes shut hard, pulled the covers over his head — still couldn't sleep. Listening to Old Cheng's snoring, he grew irritable inside, and felt like jumping up and giving Old Cheng a beating just to feel better. It got colder and colder; his throat grew itchy from the chill, yet he was afraid of coughing and waking Old Cheng.

Unable to sleep, he genuinely thought about sneaking out and going back to the Cao place for another look. The whole thing had fallen apart anyway, and there was no one in the courtyard — why not take a few things? The money he'd saved with such difficulty had been taken from him — taken because of the Cao family's business — so why couldn't he go and take a few things? He'd lost money over the Cao family's affairs; getting it back from the Cao place would be perfectly fair, wouldn't it? With that thought, his eyes lit up, and he instantly forgot the cold. Go! That money he'd worked so hard to save — gone — and now to get it back so easily. Go!

He had already sat up, then quickly lay back down, as if Old Cheng were watching him. His heart lurched. No — he couldn't be a thief. He couldn't! Just now he'd washed his hands of it, hadn't done what Master Cao had asked of him, and already owed him an apology. How could he go and steal from him on top of that? He couldn't go! Better to die poor than to steal!

But how did he know others wouldn't go and steal? Who would know if that man surnamed Sun had taken a few things? He sat up again. A dog barked somewhere in the distance. He lay back down. Still couldn't go. Let others steal if they wanted to — his own conscience would be clear. He was this poor already; he couldn't add another dark stain to his heart!

Besides, Gao Ma knew he'd come to the Wang place. If something went missing in the night, it would be pinned on him whether he did it or not! Not only did he no longer want to steal — he was now afraid someone else might break in. If things really did go missing this very night, he could throw himself into the Yellow River and never wash himself clean! He wasn't cold anymore; his palms had even broken into a light sweat. What should he do? Jump back over and keep watch? He didn't dare. His life had been bought with money — he couldn't walk back into the trap. But if he didn't go, and something went missing?

He couldn't think of a solution. He sat up again, hugging his knees, his head nearly touching them. His head felt heavy, his eyes wanted to close, but he didn't dare sleep. The night was so long — there wasn't even a moment for Xiangzi to close his eyes.

He sat there for who knows how long, turning over who knows how many plans. Then suddenly his mind brightened. He reached out and shook Old Cheng: "Old Cheng! Old Cheng! Wake up!"

"What?" Old Cheng was very reluctant to open his eyes. "If you need to pee, there's a chamber pot under the bed."

"Wake up!""Turn on the light!"

"What's going on, a thief?" Old Cheng sat up in a daze.

"Are you fully awake?"

"Yeah!"

"Old Cheng, take a look! This is my bedding, these are my clothes, this is the five yuan Mr. Cao gave me; nothing else, right?"

"That's it, what's the matter?" Old Cheng yawned.

"You're fully awake? These are all my things. I haven't taken a single thing from the Cao family, have I?"

"No you haven't! We're brothers, we've worked in big households for years — could we afford to have sticky fingers? If it's something we should do, we do it; if it's not, we don't; we can't take people's things! Is that what this is about?"

"You saw it clearly?"

Old Cheng laughed: "No mistake! I'm telling you, aren't you cold?"

"I'm fine!"

[1]

 A pledge made across the bridge of the nose, meaning a solemn guarantee.

Thirteen

Because of the snow's glow, the sky seemed to brighten a little earlier than usual. With the year nearly over, many households had bought chickens to fatten up, and the crowing was several times louder than normal. Roosters calling from every direction gave the scene a feel of a prosperous year blessed by timely snow. But Xiangzi had not slept well through the night. In the second half of the night he had dozed off a few times, drifting in and out, rising and falling like something floating on water, his heart unsettled. The more he slept the colder he got. When he heard the roosters crowing on all sides he truly could not hold on any longer. Not wanting to disturb old Cheng, he curled up his legs, stuffed the quilt against his mouth to muffle his coughing, and still did not dare get up. He endured and waited, feeling deeply restless. He finally managed to wait until daylight, when the sound of cart wheels and drivers' shouts came from the street, and he sat up. Sitting was cold too, so he stood, fastened his buttons, and cracked the door open a little to look outside. The snow was not very thick — it had probably stopped falling around midnight. The sky seemed to have cleared, but it was a hazy grey and hard to see clearly, with what looked like a faint grey shadow even over the snow. At a glance, he spotted the large footprints he had left the night before. Though snow had covered them again, the hollows were still clearly visible.

Partly to have something to do, and partly to erase the traces, he made no sound, felt around in the corner of the room for a broom, and went out to sweep the snow. The snow was heavy and not easy to sweep, and he could not find a large bamboo broom right away, so he bent low at the waist and scraped hard. The top layer swept away, but some snow granules clung to the ground as if they had gripped the earth itself. He straightened up twice and swept the entire outer courtyard clean, piling the snow at the base of two small willow trees. He had worked up a light sweat and felt warm and a little more at ease. He stamped his feet and let out a long breath — very long and very white.

He went back inside, put the broom where he had found it, and was thinking about rolling up his bedding. Old Cheng woke up, let out a yawn, and before his mouth had even closed again he spoke: "Not early anymore, is it?" The tone was remarkably complex. When he finished, he wiped the tears from his eyes and reached into his fur jacket pocket for a cigarette. After two puffs he was fully awake. "Xiangzi, don't go yet! Let me go get some hot water and we'll have a nice warm pot of tea. This night must have been rough enough on you!"

"Shall I go?" Xiangzi offered in a friendly way. But the moment the words were out, he thought of the terror of the night before, and his heart suddenly clenched into a knot.

"No, I'll go! I still owe you one!" With that, old Cheng dressed with remarkable speed, not bothering to fasten a single button, just cinching a sash over his worn fur jacket, and ran out with a cigarette dangling from his lips: "Hey! You swept the whole courtyard? You're something else! I owe you one!" Xiangzi felt a little better.

After a while, old Cheng came back carrying two large bowls of sweet congee, along with what seemed like a great many horseshoe flatbreads and small crispy fried dough sticks. "Didn't make tea, have some congee first — come on, eat up. If it's not enough we'll buy more. No money? We can put it on credit. Hard labor means you can't go around with a long face — come on!"

The sky was fully light now, and the room was cold and bright. The two of them sat cradling their bowls and drank with loud, sweet sounds. Neither said a word, and they finished off the flatbreads and fried dough in one go. "So?" Old Cheng picked a sesame seed from his teeth.

"Time to go," said Xiangzi, looking at the bedroll on the floor.

"Tell me — I still don't quite understand what happened." Old Cheng handed Xiangzi a cigarette. Xiangzi shook his head.

After thinking it over, Xiangzi felt he had no choice but to tell old Cheng everything. Haltingly, he went through the events of the previous night. It was a struggle, but what he told was fairly complete.

Old Cheng pursed his lips for a long time, as if trying to make sense of it all. "The way I see it, you should still go find Mr. Cao. You can't just leave things like this, and you can't just let the money go. Didn't you just say Mr. Cao told you that if things looked bad you should run? Well, you got cornered by a detective the moment you stepped off the rickshaw — who's to blame for that? It's not that you weren't loyal. Things went sideways too fast and you had no choice but to save your own skin first. The way I see it, there's nothing to feel bad about toward anyone. Go — go find Mr. Cao, tell him everything from start to finish, the whole truth. I think he won't blame you, and there's even a chance he'll make up your losses. Go on, leave your bedding here and go find him early. The days are short — once the sun's up it'll be eight o'clock. Get moving!"

Xiangzi's heart stirred back to life. He still felt a little guilty toward Mr. Cao, but what old Cheng said made good sense — with a detective pointing a gun at him, how could he have spared a thought for the Cao household? "Go on!" Old Cheng urged again. "I think you got a bit turned around last night. When something urgent hits, anyone can lose their head. The plan I'm giving you now is solid — I've got a few more years on you and I've been through more. Go on, isn't that the sun coming out?"

A ray of morning sun, reflected off the snow, had already lit up the whole city. Blue sky, white snow — light above, light below — between the blue and white a shimmer of golden sparks flashed, so dazzling and exhilarating you could hardly keep your eyes open. Xiangzi was just about to leave when someone knocked at the door. Old Cheng went to look and called from the entryway: "Xiangzi! Someone's here for you!"

Wang Er from the Zuo household next door, his nose dripping with cold, was stamping the snow off his feet in the entryway. When old Cheng saw Xiangzi come out, he said, "Come on inside, all of you." The three of them went into the room together.

"Well, the thing is —" Wang Er rubbed his hands together, "I came to check on the house, but how do I get in? The front gate's locked. The thing is, it's cold after the snow, really cold! The thing is, Mr. Cao, Mrs. Cao — they both left early this morning. To Tianjin, or maybe Shanghai, I'm not sure. Mr. Zuo asked me to come look after the house. The thing is, it really is cold!"

Xiangzi suddenly felt like crying. He had just been about to follow old Cheng's advice and go find Mr. Cao — and Mr. Cao had already left. He stood there in a daze for a long moment, then asked: "Did Mr. Cao say anything about me?"

"The thing is, no. It wasn't even light yet and they were all up, barely had time to say a word. The train — the thing is — left at seven forty. The thing is, how do I get over to that courtyard?" Wang Er was eager to get through.

"Climb over," said Xiangzi. He glanced at old Cheng as if handing Wang Er over to him, then picked up his bedroll.

"Where are you going?" old Cheng asked.

"Ren'he Garage. There's nowhere else to go." That one sentence said everything — the grievance, the shame, and the helplessness in Xiangzi's heart. He had no other option but to go and surrender. Every road was blocked. He could only go out onto the snow-white ground and find that figure like a dark tower — Huniu. He had his pride, his drive, his loyalty, his sense of honor — none of it was worth a thing, because he had the life of a dog.

Old Cheng took over: "Go on your way. Not to mention Wang Er standing right here — you didn't touch a blade of grass in the Cao house. Go on. When you're in this part of town, come in and chat for a while. Maybe I'll hear of something good and put in a word for you. After you leave, I'll take Wang Er over there. Is there coal?"

"Coal, firewood — all in the small room in the back courtyard." Xiangzi hoisted his bedroll onto his shoulder.

The snow on the street was no longer so white. On the road, cart wheels had pressed it down to reveal a glint of ice beneath. On the dirt paths, horses' hooves had already turned it a patchwork of black and white — a sorry sight. Xiangzi thought of nothing, just carried his bedroll and walked forward. He walked straight to Ren'he Garage without stopping. He did not dare pause — he knew that if he stopped, he would lose the nerve to go in. He walked straight inside, his face burning hot. He had prepared a line to say to Huniu: "I'm back. Do what you will. Whatever you decide is fine — I've got no other way." But when he actually saw her, he turned the line over in his mind several times and still could not get it out. His mouth was not that cooperative.

Huniu had just gotten up. Her hair was disheveled, her eyelids a little puffy, and her dark face was covered in small white goosebumps, like a plucked frozen chicken.

"Oh! You're back!" She was extremely warm, her eyes lit up with a smile. "Rent me a rickshaw," said Xiangzi, head down, staring at the traces of unmelted snow on the tips of his shoes.

"Go talk to the old man." She said it quietly, then tilted her head toward the east room.

Old Liu was in his room drinking tea, a large white stove in front of him with flames nearly half a foot high. Seeing Xiangzi come in, he said with a half-annoyed, half-amused look: "You little rascal, you're still alive! Forgotten about me, have you! Count it up — how many days has it been since you came around? How did things go? Did you buy a rickshaw?"

Xiangzi shook his head, a stabbing pain in his chest. "I still need a rickshaw to pull, Fourth Master."

"Hmph, fell through again! Fine, go pick one yourself!" Old Liu poured a bowl of tea. "Here, have a bowl first."

Xiangzi took the bowl and stood in front of the stove, drinking in big gulps. The tea was scalding hot, the fire blazing hot, and he felt a wave of drowsiness. He set the bowl down and was just about to leave when old Liu called him back.

"Wait, what's your hurry? I'll tell you — you've come at just the right time. The twenty-seventh is my birthday and I'm putting up a tent and having guests over. You can help out for a few days — no need to go pull a rickshaw yet. Those others," old Liu gestured toward the courtyard, "can't be relied on. I don't want them fooling around and making a mess. You help out. Do whatever needs doing without waiting for me to tell you. First go sweep the snow. At noon I'll treat you to a hot pot." "Yes, Fourth Master!" Xiangzi had made up his mind — since he was back here, he would leave everything to the Liu father and daughter. However they wanted to use him was fine. He accepted his fate. "What did I say?" Huniu came in at just the right moment, "It's still Xiangzi — everyone else falls a little short."

Old Liu laughed. Xiangzi lowered his head a little further.

"Come here, Xiangzi!" Huniu called him outside. "Here's some money — go buy brooms first, bamboo ones, good for sweeping snow. You need to hurry up and sweep. The tent workers are coming today." In her room, while counting out money for Xiangzi, she said in a low voice: "Stay sharp! Get on the old man's good side! Our business has hope!"

Xiangzi said nothing and felt no anger. He seemed to have given up entirely — he didn't want to think about anything. He would muddle through one day at a time. Eat when there was food, drink when there was drink, work when there was work, keep his hands and feet busy, a few rounds and the day was done. He figured he'd better learn from the donkey at the millstone — know nothing, say nothing, just keep walking in circles.

Yet he could also feel that no matter what, he would never be truly happy. Though he refused to think, refused to speak, refused to lose his temper, something was always lodged in his chest. He could forget it temporarily while working, but whenever he had a moment of idleness he felt it again — soft, yet always the same size. No particular taste to it, yet it choked him, like a sponge. With that thing blocking his chest, he forced himself to work hard, trying to exhaust himself so completely that he could fall into a dead sleep. He handed the night over to dreams and the day over to his hands and feet. He was like a capable corpse. He swept snow, he ran errands, he went to arrange the gas lamps, he washed rickshaws, he moved tables and chairs, he ate the reward meal old Liu gave him, he slept — he knew nothing, said nothing, thought nothing, only dimly aware of that sponge-like thing inside him.

The snow on the ground was swept clean. The snow on the rooftops gradually melted. The tent workers called out to each other as they climbed up and erected the tent frame. The agreed plan was a warm tent covering the whole courtyard — three sides with hanging eaves, three sides with railings, three sides with glass windows. Inside the tent there would be glass partition screens, hanging panels, and every piece of wood wrapped in red cloth. The main entrance and side entrances would all be hung with colored streamers, and the kitchen would be set up in the back courtyard. Old Liu, celebrating his sixty-ninth birthday, wanted to do things in grand style, so first and foremost he needed an impressive tent. The days were short, and the tent workers only managed to put up the frame, the railings, and the cloth by the end of the day. The decorative work inside and the streamers over the doors would have to be hung the next morning. Old Liu flew into a rage over this, his face flushing red with anger. Because of it, he sent Xiangzi to go urge the gas lamp supplier and the cook to make absolutely sure there were no delays. In truth neither of these was at any risk of being delayed, but the old man was not at ease. Xiangzi had barely returned from that errand when old Liu sent him out again to borrow mahjong sets — three or four of them — because there absolutely had to be a good round of gambling on the day. After bringing back the mahjong sets, he was sent out again to borrow a gramophone, because a birthday celebration had to have some noise. Xiangzi's legs did not stop for a single moment, running until eleven o'clock at night. Having gotten used to pulling a rickshaw, walking with empty hands was more tiring than running. By the last trip back, even he — even Xiangzi — could barely lift his feet. "Good lad! You're something! If I had a son like you, I'd gladly give up a few years of my life for it! Go rest — there's more to do tomorrow!"

Huniu, standing nearby, winked at Xiangzi.

The next morning the tent workers came back to finish the job. The decorative panels were hung — painted with battle scenes from "Romance of the Three Kingdoms": the three battles against Lü Bu, the Battle of Changban Slope, the burning of the linked camps, and so on, with painted-face warriors on horseback brandishing swords and spears. Old Liu tilted his head back and looked it all over, feeling quite satisfied. Right after that, the furniture shop arrived to unload the furnishings: eight seating arrangements inside the tent, with skirting, cushions, and chair covers all in embroidered red. A birthday altar was set up in the main hall, with incense burners and candle holders in cloisonné enamel, and four red felt mats laid out in front of the table. Old Liu immediately sent Xiangzi to order a display of apples. Huniu slipped him two yuan on the side and told him to go order birthday peaches and birthday noodles — the peaches were to have a set of the Eight Immortals on top, presented as a gift from Xiangzi. The apples arrived and were arranged right away. Before long the birthday peaches and noodles arrived too, placed behind the apples. The large peaches had red-tipped points with the Eight Immortals figures inserted in them — a very impressive sight.

"Xiangzi sent these — see how thoughtful he is!" Huniu whispered into her father's ear. Old Liu smiled at Xiangzi.

The center of the birthday altar was still missing a large "longevity" character, which was customarily sent by friends and did not need to be prepared by the family. None had arrived yet, and old Liu, growing impatient, was about to lose his temper again: "Every time there's a wedding or funeral for anyone else, I'm always out front. Now it's my turn, and they leave me hanging —""×his mother's!"

"Tomorrow's the twenty-sixth, the banquet hasn't even started yet — what's the rush?" Huniu called out to reassure him. "I like to put everything out all at once; seeing it scattered around like this drives me crazy! I'm telling you, Xiangzi, the gas lamps need to be set up today. If they're not here by four o'clock, I'll skin them alive!"

"Xiangzi, go give them another nudge!" Huniu deliberately leaned on him, always calling out for Xiangzi to do things in front of her dad. Xiangzi said nothing, listened until he understood, then left.

"Not to talk out of turn, old man," she said with a slight curl of her lip, "but if you had a son, he'd have to be either like me or like Xiangzi! Too bad I was born into the wrong body. Nothing to be done about it. Though having a godson like Xiangzi isn't bad at all! Look at him — doesn't make a peep all day, but gets everything done!"

Liu Siye didn't respond, thought for a moment: "Where's the phonograph? Put something on!"

Some beat-up gramophone borrowed from who knows where — every sound it made was like stepping on a cat's tail, piercing right through you! Liu Siye didn't mind at all, as long as there was some noise.

By the afternoon, everything was in order, just waiting for the cook to come the next day to set up the banquet. Liu Siye made his rounds, surveying everything — red and green decorations everywhere — and gave a satisfied nod. That evening, he went to invite the bookkeeper from Tianshun Coal Shop to handle the accounts. The bookkeeper's surname was Feng, a man from Shanxi, meticulous with numbers. Mr. Feng came over right away, had a look around, and sent Xiangzi out to buy two red account books and a sheet of red paper. He cut the red paper into pieces and wrote longevity characters on them, pasting them up in various spots. Liu Siye thought Mr. Feng was truly thorough, and on the spot wanted to play a few rounds of mahjong with him. Mr. Feng knew Liu Siye's reputation and didn't dare take him up on it. The game fell through, and Liu Siye was a bit put out. He rounded up a few rickshaw pullers. "How about a game of dice — you got the guts?"

Everyone was willing to play, just not with Liu Siye — who didn't know he used to run a gambling den!

"What kind of men are you lot, how do you even get through life!" Siye lost his temper. "When I was your age, I didn't have a single coin to my name and I still dared to play — lose first and worry later. Come on!"

"Copper coins?" one of the rickshaw pullers ventured cautiously.

"Keep your copper coins, Liu Si doesn't play games with children!" The old man downed a cup of tea in one gulp and rubbed his bald head. "Forget it, I wouldn't come even if you invited me! Listen, go tell everyone: tomorrow the banquet starts, guests will arrive in the afternoon, everyone must bring their rickshaws in before four o'clock — no going in and out with your rickshaws making a mess! No rickshaw fees tomorrow, bring them in by four. I'm letting you pull for free all day, so at least have the decency to wish me well in your hearts — don't be ungrateful! The day after tomorrow is the main day, nobody pulls a rickshaw. At eight-thirty in the morning, I'll set up for you first — six large bowls, two seven-inch dishes, four side plates, one hot pot. That's treating you right! Everyone wear a long gown — anyone who comes in looking sloppy gets kicked out! Once you're done eating, all of you clear out so I can entertain my guests. The guests get three large sea bowls, six cold cuts, six stir-fried dishes, four big bowls, one hot pot. I'm making this clear upfront — don't go eyeing what's not yours. Guests are guests. I'm not asking anything of you. Anyone with a conscience can give ten copper coins as a gift — I won't think it's too little. If you don't bring a single coin but kowtow three times, I'll accept that too. Just behave yourselves. Understood? In the evening, if you want to come back and eat, return after six o'clock — whatever's left is all yours. Don't come back early! Got it?"

"Some of us pull night shifts, Siye," a middle-aged rickshaw puller asked, "how can we bring the rickshaws in by four?"

"Night shift pullers come back after eleven! Just don't be crowding around the tent when there are people inside! You pull rickshaws — Liu Si is not in the same line of business as you. Understand?"

Nobody had anything to say, but they couldn't find a graceful way to leave, and just standing there felt awkward. Liu Siye's words had left everyone with a knot of resentment inside. Sure, a free day off from rickshaw fees was a perk, but who wanted to eat a free meal without at least putting in forty copper coins as a gift. Besides, Liu Siye's words were so grating — as if his birthday celebration meant they all had to hide away like mice. And on top of that, not being allowed to pull on the twenty-seventh, the main day, right when business was good at year's end — Liu Siye could afford to sacrifice a day's income, but everyone else being forced to "waste" a day was unbearable! They seethed in silence, standing there, and not a single auspicious thought was being wished for Liu Siye in their hearts.

Huniu tugged at Xiangzi, and Xiangzi followed her out.

Everyone's anger suddenly seemed to find an outlet — they all glared at Xiangzi's retreating figure. For the past couple of days, everyone had felt that Xiangzi was the Liu family's lapdog, fawning shamelessly, working without complaint as their general errand boy. Xiangzi had no idea about any of this — he helped the Liu family to keep his own troubled mind occupied. He had nothing to say to the others in the evenings because there was simply nothing to say. They didn't know his grievances, and assumed he was currying favor with Liu Siye, which was why he didn't bother talking to them. Huniu's attentiveness toward Xiangzi stirred a particular sourness in everyone's hearts. Thinking about the current situation — Liu Siye wouldn't let them come and go freely in the banquet tent, yet Xiangzi would surely get to eat well the whole day. They were all rickshaw pullers — why were there such differences between them? Look, Liu's daughter was calling Xiangzi out again! Everyone's eyes followed Xiangzi, and their legs wanted to move too — they all drifted out one by one on some pretext. Liu's daughter was talking to Xiangzi under the gas lamp, and everyone exchanged nods with each other.

[1] Picking the right moment, meaning gauging when the time is right.

[2] Gas lamp, i.e., a coal gas lamp.

[3] To waste time, to idle. Refers to a deliberate act.

[4] Errand boy, i.e., someone who does odd jobs.

Fourteen

The Liu family's celebration was a lively affair. Liu Siye was very pleased to have so many people come to kowtow and wish him a long life. What made him even prouder was that many old friends had come to offer their congratulations. Through these old friends, he could see that his celebration was not only lively but also "refined." Those old friends' clothes and accessories were already out of date, while Siye's fur-lined robe and mandarin jacket were newly made. In terms of livelihood, several of his friends had been better off than him in their day, but now — after two or three decades of change — they had fallen lower and lower, some barely able to eat their fill. Looking at them, then looking at his own banquet tent, the birthday hall, the hanging screens painted with the Battle of Changban, and the three-sea-bowl feast, he felt he had truly risen a head above them — he had "moved up in the world." Even for gambling, he had prepared mahjong tiles, which was far more refined than dice games. Yet amid all this festivity, he also felt a touch of loneliness and sorrow. Accustomed to living alone, he had originally expected that the guests on his birthday would be no more than shopkeepers and clerks from nearby businesses, along with the rough-and-tumble friends of his past. He hadn't expected female guests to show up as well. Although Huniu could entertain them on his behalf, he suddenly felt the loneliness of his own situation — no wife, only a daughter, and one who looked like a man at that. If Huniu had been a son, he would naturally have started a family long ago and had children, and even if he himself were an old widower, perhaps he wouldn't feel so utterly alone. Yes, he lacked for nothing — except a son. The older he got, the less hope there was of having a son. A birthday celebration was supposed to be a joyous occasion, yet it seemed to call for tears. No matter how much he had moved up in the world, with no one to carry on his legacy, wasn't it all for nothing in the end?

In the morning he was in high spirits, accepting everyone's birthday wishes with great self-importance, feeling like a triumphant old hero standing above the rest. By the afternoon, his mood deflated somewhat. Watching the young children brought along by the female guests, he felt envious, jealous, and yet afraid to get close to them — and not getting close made him feel out of sorts. He wanted to lose his temper but held himself back from erupting on the spot, knowing he was a man of the world and couldn't make a scene in front of guests. He just wanted the day to pass quickly so he could stop suffering through it.

There was also one blemish on the occasion — at breakfast when the meal was set out for the rickshaw pullers, Xiangzi nearly got into a fight.

Breakfast was served just past eight o'clock, and the rickshaw pullers were all a bit disgruntled. Even though they'd been let off rickshaw fees the day before, nobody had come empty-handed today — a dime, forty copper coins, whatever it was, everyone had brought some gift money. On ordinary days, they were working men and Liu Si was the yard owner. But today, as far as they were concerned, they were guests and shouldn't be treated this way.

On top of that, they had to leave as soon as they finished eating, and weren't even allowed to take their rickshaws out — right at the end of the year when business was good!

Xiangzi knew perfectly well that he wasn't among those who had to eat and go, but he wanted to eat together with everyone else. For one thing, finishing early meant he could get back to work sooner. For another, it seemed friendlier. Sitting down with the group, everyone transferred their resentment toward Liu Si onto him. The moment they sat down, someone said: "Hey, aren't you a distinguished guest? How come you're sitting with us?" Xiangzi gave a dumb smile, not catching the edge in those words. These past few days he hadn't said a single idle word himself, so his mind seemed a bit slow too. Since they couldn't take it out on Liu Si, they might as well eat as much as they could. The food couldn't be refilled, but the wine — that was unlimited. Birthday wine! Without any prior agreement, they all decided to drink away their frustration. Some drank in sullen silence, others started calling out finger-guessing games. The old man couldn't stop them from that. Seeing everyone drink, Xiangzi felt he couldn't be too out of step with the group, so he had a couple of cups too. As the drinking went on, everyone's eyes turned red and their mouths stopped behaving. Someone said: "Xiangzi, Camel, you've got it good! Eating well all day, waiting on the master and the young mistress! You might as well stop pulling a rickshaw — best to become a live-in servant!" Xiangzi caught a hint of something in those words but didn't take it to heart. From the moment he'd entered Renhe Yard, he had decided not to play the hero anymore — let fate decide everything. Let people say whatever they wanted. He swallowed his anger. Then someone else said: "Xiangzi takes a different path from us — we earn our keep with muscle, but Xiangzi uses inner skill!" Everyone burst out laughing. Xiangzi could tell they were needling him, but he had already swallowed such enormous grievances — why bother with a few idle remarks? He still said nothing. People at the next table saw an opening, some craning their necks to call out: "Xiangzi, when you become the yard owner someday, don't forget your brothers!" Xiangzi still said nothing, and then someone at his own table chimed in: "Say something, Camel!"

Xiangzi's face flushed, and he said quietly: "How could I ever become the yard owner?!" "Hah, why couldn't you — looks like it'll be wedding bells before long!" Xiangzi didn't quite catch the reference — he didn't know what "wedding bells" meant — but instinctively guessed it was pointing at his relationship with Huniu. His face slowly went from red to white, and all the grievances he had suffered before came rushing back at once, piling up in his chest. The days of patient silence seemed impossible to maintain any longer — like water that had been dammed up long enough, it would burst through the moment it found an outlet. Right at that moment, a rickshaw puller pointed at his face and said: "Xiangzi, I'm talking to you — you're really one of those 'dumplings eaten by a mute — you know exactly what's in them,' aren't you. Aren't you, say it yourself, Xiangzi? Xiangzi?"

Xiangzi shot to his feet, face drained of color, and faced the man: "Step outside and say that — you dare?"

Everyone froze. They had indeed meant to needle him, to throw some jabs, but they hadn't prepared for a fight.

A sudden silence fell, like birds in a forest that had suddenly spotted a hawk. Xiangzi stood there alone, towering over everyone else, and felt his own isolation. But with rage burning in his chest, he somehow felt certain that even if they all came at him together, they were no match for him. He added: "Anyone dare to step outside?"

Everyone suddenly came to their senses, and almost in unison: "Alright, Xiangzi, we were just messing with you!"

Liu Siye had seen it all: "Sit down, Xiangzi!" Then, turning to the group: "Don't bully someone just because they're easygoing — push me and I'll kick the lot of you out! Eat up!" Xiangzi left the table. Everyone shot sideways glances at the old man and picked up their bowls. Before long, the chatter started up again — like forest birds after the danger had passed, chirping softly once more.

Xiangzi crouched at the doorway for a long while, waiting for them. If any of them dared to say another word, he'd beat them. He had nothing left anyway — might as well go all out! But they came out in twos and threes and didn't come looking for trouble. Even though the fight hadn't happened, he'd at least let off some steam. Then he thought about it — today's outburst had made enemies of a lot of people. On ordinary days, he already had no close friends, which was why he had no one to confide his troubles to. How could he afford to make more enemies? He felt a little regret. The food he'd just eaten sat heavy in his stomach, a dull ache. He stood up. Forget it — didn't those people who were always fighting and stirring up trouble seem to get along just fine? Did being honest and well-behaved necessarily get you anywhere? With that thought, he sketched out a different path for himself in his mind — the Xiangzi on this path was completely unlike the one he had once hoped to be. This was a Xiangzi who made friends with everyone yet took advantage wherever he could, drank other people's tea, smoked other people's cigarettes, borrowed money and never paid it back, didn't get out of the way of cars, relieved himself wherever he pleased, spent his days outwitting the police, and thought nothing of being hauled off to the precinct for two or three days. Yes, that kind of rickshaw puller existed too, and was happy — at least happier than Xiangzi. Fine then. Honesty, propriety, ambition — since none of it was any use, turning into that kind of rogue wasn't a bad idea. Not just not bad — Xiangzi thought — it even had something of the bold, fearless spirit about it, afraid of nothing under heaven or earth, absolutely never bowing his head and swallowing his losses in silence. That's right! That's how it should be done! Bad men are carved out of good ones. He even felt a twinge of regret that the fight hadn't come off. No matter — there was no rush. From now on, he would bow his head to no one.Liu Si-ye had sharp eyes that missed nothing. Putting together everything he had seen and heard, he had figured out about eighty or ninety percent of it. These past few days, the girl had been unusually obedient — hmph, because Xiang Zi was back! Look at her eyes, always following him around. The old man kept this in mind, and felt all the more desolate and miserable. Think about it — he had no son to begin with, no way to build a proper family with fire and vigor; and now his daughter was going to run off with someone! His whole life's efforts would have been for nothing! Xiang Zi was a decent enough fellow, but as for being both son and son-in-law, he was far from it; a stinking rickshaw puller! He himself had hustled all his life, fought in gang brawls, knelt on iron chains, and in the end he was supposed to let some country bumpkin walk off with his daughter and his whole business? No such luck! And certainly not from Liu Si! Liu Si had been a firecracker since he was young!

More birthday guests arrived around three or four in the afternoon, but the old man had already lost all interest. The more the guests praised him for being hale and hearty and blessed, the more meaningless it all felt.

After the lamps were lit, the guests gradually dispersed, leaving only a dozen or so who lived nearby or were close friends. Looking at the empty tent in the courtyard, bathed in a bluish glow from the water-moon lanterns, and the tables stripped of their tablecloths, the old man felt hollow and bored. It was as if he could see what his own death would look like — nothing more than this, just the festive tent changed to a white mourning tent, no sons or grandchildren in mourning clothes kneeling before the coffin, only a bunch of strangers playing mahjong through the night. He really wanted to drive out the remaining guests; while he still had breath in him, he ought to throw his weight around! But he couldn't quite bring himself to take it out on his friends. His anger took a turn, and the more he looked at his daughter, the more she irritated him. Xiang Zi was sitting in the tent, looking all proper and presentable, the scar on his face gleaming like a piece of jade in the lamplight. The more the old man looked at the two of them, the more it grated on him.

Tiger Girl had always been wild and unruly, but today she had dressed herself up from head to toe, and had to put on airs entertaining guests — both to earn everyone's praise and to show off in front of Xiang Zi. In the morning she had found it rather amusing, but by the afternoon, fatigue set in and she grew irritable, itching to find someone to yell at. By evening, she had not a shred of patience left, her brows knitting themselves together, standing rigid.

It was past seven o'clock. Liu Si-ye was getting a bit drowsy, but too proud to admit his age, he refused to go to bed. Everyone invited him to join a few rounds of cards, but rather than admit he didn't have the energy, he said cards weren't satisfying enough — dice or dominoes were more his style. Nobody wanted to change games midway, so he had no choice but to sit on the side. To perk himself up, he called for more drinks, saying over and over that he hadn't eaten enough, and complaining that the cook had charged too much while the dishes weren't even plentiful. Starting from that point, he overturned all the satisfaction he had felt during the day: the tent, the furniture and seating, the cook, and everything else — none of it was worth what he'd paid, they had all taken him for a fool, all a swindle!

The bookkeeper, Mr. Feng, had by then tallied up the accounts: twenty-five birthday banners had come in, three sets of birthday peaches and birthday noodles, one jar of birthday wine, two pairs of birthday candles, and about twenty yuan in cash gifts. The number of items was not small, but most people had given only forty copper coins or one dime.

Hearing this report, Liu Si-ye flew into a rage. Had he known it would be like this, he should have just prepared plain noodles with stir-fry! People sat down to a three-bowl banquet and gave a dime's worth of face? They were simply taking the old man for a fool! He would never host another event — he couldn't afford to lose money like this! Needless to say, relatives and friends alike all wanted a free meal at his expense; here he was, sixty-nine years old, clever all his life and foolish for once, eaten alive by a bunch of monkeys and scoundrels! The more the old man thought about it, the angrier he got, until even the satisfaction he had felt during the day seemed like his own stupidity. What he thought in his heart came out of his mouth, laced with curses that had long gone out of fashion on the streets.

Some friends had not yet left, and Tiger Girl, wanting to save everyone's face, tried to stop her father from making a scene. But seeing that everyone seemed absorbed in their cards and apparently not paying attention to the old man's muttering, she held her tongue — better not to open her mouth and make things clearer than they needed to be. Let him mutter away; everyone would just let it pass.

But then, unexpectedly, the old man's ranting turned toward her. She was not going to stand for that! He had held his birthday banquet, and she had been running around for days helping out, and now she got nothing but grief — she couldn't let that slide! Sixty-nine or seventy-nine, he still had to be reasonable! She fired right back:

"You wanted to spend money on this yourself, what does it have to do with me?"

The old man met with a counterattack and snapped to attention. "What does it have to do with you? It's all because of you! You think my eyes don't notice things?"

"What did you see? I've been exhausted all day, and now you're taking it out on me at the end of it — hold on! Go ahead, tell me, what did you see?" Tiger Girl's fatigue vanished, and her tongue was razor sharp.

"You don't have to watch me run things with such hungry eyes! See? I've seen it all long ago, hmph!"

"Why would I have hungry eyes?!" She shook her head. "What exactly did you see?"

"Isn't that right there?!" Liu Si pointed into the tent — Xiang Zi was bent over sweeping the floor. "Him?" Tiger Girl's heart gave a jolt; she hadn't expected the old man's eyes to be so sharp. "Hmph! What about him?"

"No need to play dumb when you know perfectly well what's going on!" The old man stood up. "Either him or me, either me or him — I'm telling you straight. I'm your father! I have the right to have a say!"

Tiger Girl hadn't expected things to come to a head so quickly. Her plan had barely been half carried out, and the old man had already called her bluff. What now? Her face flushed red — a dark red, mixed with the remnants of her powder and the bright blue lamplight, like a piece of overcooked pig liver, a complicated and ugly color. She was a bit worn out; this provocation stirred up her temper, and she couldn't think straight, her mind in a muddle. She couldn't just back down like this — even in a muddle she had to come up with something right away. Even the worst plan was better than no plan; she had never backed down in front of anyone! Fine, might as well go all in — win or lose, it all came down to this one blow. "It's just as well to clear everything up today, let's just call it what it is — what are you going to do about it? I want to hear it. And don't say I was trying to provoke you on purpose!"

The card players seemed to hear the father and daughter quarreling, but were reluctant to look away from their hands. To drown out the noise, they slapped their tiles down harder and louder, calling out suits and declaring wins. Xiang Zi had heard enough to understand what was going on; he kept his head down and swept, his mind made up: if it came to blows, he'd fight.

"You're just trying to make me furious!" The old man's eyes were bulging wide. "Make me so angry I die, so you can go throw yourself at him? Don't count on it — I've still got years left in me!" "Stop talking nonsense, what are you going to do?" Tiger Girl's heart was pounding, but her words were hard as stone. "What am I going to do? Didn't I already say — either him or me, either me or him! I'm not going to let a stinking rickshaw puller have everything!"

Xiang Zi threw down the broom, straightened up, fixed his eyes on Liu Si, and asked: "Who are you talking about?" Liu Si burst into wild laughter: "Ha ha, you little punk, you want to rebel? You, I'm talking about you! Get out of here right now! I gave you a chance, treated you well, and you dare to stir up trouble on my turf — do you know who I am? Get out! Don't ever let me see you again — came here looking for a handout, did you, huh?"

The old man's voice was too loud, and it drew several rickshaw pullers over to watch. The card players assumed Liu Si-ye was having another row with a rickshaw puller and still refused to look up.

Xiang Zi was not quick with words; he had a lot he wanted to say, but not a single word made it to his tongue. He stood there blankly, his neck stiff, swallowing hard. "Get out! Get out now! Came here looking for a free ride? I was pulling dirty tricks before you were even born, hmph!" The old man was partly just trying to intimidate Xiang Zi; in his heart he didn't resent Xiang Zi nearly as much as he resented his daughter — even in his anger, he still felt that Xiang Zi was genuinely an honest man.

"Fine, I'll go!" Xiang Zi had nothing to say and figured he'd better leave quickly; no matter what, he was no match for them in a war of words.

The rickshaw pullers had come to watch the excitement, and when they saw Liu Si-ye berating Xiang Zi, they all remembered the scene from that morning and felt a certain satisfaction. But when they heard the old man driving Xiang Zi out, they switched sides — Xiang Zi had worked so hard, and now he was being kicked out the moment he was no longer useful; the old man was turning his back on him, and they felt it was unjust. Some came over and asked: "What's going on, Xiang Zi?" Xiang Zi shook his head.

"Xiang Zi, wait before you go!" A flash of clarity struck Tiger Girl's mind: her plan was largely useless now, and in a crisis speed was everything — she had to grab hold of Xiang Zi fast, or she'd lose both the chicken and the egg. "The two of us are tied together like two grasshoppers on a string — neither one of us can get away! Wait, let me make things clear!" She turned to face the old man: "Let me just say it straight — I'm already pregnant, and it's Xiang Zi's. Wherever he goes, I go. Are you going to give me to him, or are you going to throw us both out? Say the word!"

Tiger Girl hadn't expected things to move so fast, forcing her to play her last card so soon. Liu Si-ye had even less expected things to come to this. But things being what they were, he couldn't back down, especially in front of everyone. "You've got the nerve to say that out loud — my old face is burning with shame for you!" He slapped himself across the face. "Ptui! Shameless!"

The card players put down their hands, sensing something was off, though they were confused and didn't quite understand what was happening, unable to find a way to cut in. Some stood up, some stared blankly at their tiles.

Now that it was all out in the open, Tiger Girl actually felt relieved: "I'm shameless? Don't make me bring up your business — what dirty thing haven't you done? This is my first time, and it's all your fault anyway: men should marry when they're grown, women should wed when they're of age, and you're sixty-nine and haven't figured that out — wasted your life! Since everyone's here," she gestured around, "it's best we sort this out clearly, open and aboveboard! Use this very tent and throw another party while you're at it!"

"Me?" Liu Si-ye's face went from red to white, and he summoned all the toughness of his old rogue days: "I'd rather set fire to this tent than let you use it!" "Fine!" Tiger Girl's lips began to tremble, her voice turning ugly. "I'll pack up my things and leave — how much are you going to give me?"

"The money is mine, I'll give it to whoever I please!" The old man felt a pang of sorrow hearing his daughter talk of leaving, but he steeled himself for the sake of winning this fight.

"Your money? I've been helping you all these years; without me, just think — your money would have all gone to loose women, you know it's true. Let's be honest about this!" Her eyes found Xiang Zi again. "You say something!"

Xiang Zi stood there stiff as a board, without a word to say.

[1] The sound of drums and music at a wedding procession, a metaphor for getting married.

[2] Not caring who it is, meaning regardless of the person.

Fifteen

When it came to fighting, Xiang Zi couldn't hit an old man, and he couldn't hit a woman either. His strength had nowhere to go. As for making a scene, he could only think about it — he couldn't actually do it. As for Tiger Girl herself, he could very well have stamped his foot and run. But given what had just happened, she had fallen out with her father and was willing to go with him; whatever lay beneath the surface, nobody else knew — on the face of it, she was sacrificing herself for Xiang Zi. With everyone watching, he had no way not to show some backbone. He had nothing to say, and could only stand there waiting for things to play out; at the very least he had to do that much, to be worthy of being called a man.

The father and daughter were left glaring at each other with nothing more to say; Xiang Zi kept his mouth shut. The rickshaw pullers, whatever side they were on, found it hard to get a word in. The card players could no longer stay silent — the silence had become unbearable. But all they could do was offer a few surface-level words of comfort, urging both sides not to get so worked up, to talk things over calmly, that nothing was insurmountable. That was all they could say; they couldn't resolve anything, and didn't want to either. Seeing that neither side would budge, well — even a wise judge can't settle a family dispute — they slipped away whenever they got the chance.

Before everyone had fully cleared out, Tiger Girl grabbed Mr. Feng from Tianshun Coal Yard: "Mr. Feng, don't you have some space at your place? Let Xiang Zi stay there for a couple of days. Our business will be settled quickly, we won't take up your space for long. Xiang Zi, you go with Mr. Feng, we'll talk tomorrow and sort out our plans. I'm telling you — when I leave this house, I'm leaving in a bridal sedan chair, nothing less! Mr. Feng, I'm putting him in your hands — I'll come to collect him tomorrow!"

Mr. Feng kept sucking in his breath, reluctant to take on this responsibility. Xiang Zi was eager to get out of there and said: "I won't run away!"

Tiger Girl shot the old man a glare, went back to her room, and began crying in a shrill voice, locking the door from the inside.

Mr. Feng and the others coaxed Liu Si-ye inside as well. The old man put on his tough exterior again and asked everyone to stay for more drinks: "Don't worry, from now on she's she and I'm I, no more quarreling. She can go her way — I'll treat it as if I never had a daughter. I've kept my dignity my whole life, and she's dragged my face through the mud! Twenty years ago, I'd have split the two of them in half! Now, let her do as she pleases; if she thinks she can get a single copper coin from me, not a chance! Not one cent! Not giving it! Let's see how she gets by! Let her find out for herself whether her father is better or some wild man! Don't go yet, have another drink!" Everyone offered a few polite words and hurried to get away from the trouble.

Xiang Zi went to Tianshun Coal Yard.

Things moved quickly, as it turned out. Tiger Girl rented two small north-facing rooms in a large courtyard in Maojia Bay; she immediately found a plasterer to whitewash the walls clean; she asked Mr. Feng to write a few characters for good luck and pasted them up in the room. Once the room was done, she went to arrange for a sedan chair: a sedan chair covered in stars, sixteen musicians,No golden lanterns, no attendants. Everything was arranged,and she had sewn herself a red silk boarding-the-sedan outfit,finishing it before the new year so she wouldn't have to pick up a needle before the fifth day was out. The wedding date was set for the sixth day of the first lunar month,a lucky day that also required no door-avoidance rituals. She handled all of this herself,then told Xiangzi he needed to buy new clothes from head to toe: "You only do this once in a lifetime!"

Xiangzi had only five yuan in his pocket!

Huniu shot him a look: "What? What about the thirty-odd yuan I gave you?"

Xiangzi had no choice but to tell the truth,and told her everything about the Cao household. She blinked at him,half-believing,half-doubting: "Fine,I don't have time to argue with you,let's each follow our conscience! Here's fifteen yuan. If you don't show up on the day looking like a proper groom,you'd better watch out!"

On the sixth,Huniu climbed into the bridal sedan. Not a word exchanged with her father,no brothers to escort her,no friends or relatives to offer congratulations. Only the gongs and drums rang out merrily along the streets in the new year,as the sedan chair moved steadily past Xi'an Gate and Xisi Pailou,drawing looks of admiration and mixed feelings from people in their new clothes—especially the shop assistants.

Xiangzi wore new clothes bought from Tianqiao,his face flushed red,a satin skullcap on his head that had cost thirty cents. He seemed to have forgotten himself,staring blankly at everything,listening to everything,as if he no longer recognized even himself.
