He moved from a coal shop into a freshly whitewashed new room and couldn't quite make sense of it: everything before had been like a coal yard,pile after pile of black. Now he stumbled dazedly into this new room,white enough to dazzle the eyes,with a few blood-red double-happiness characters pasted on the walls. He felt a kind of mockery—something white,hazy,and suffocating. Inside the room stood Huniu's old table,chairs,and bed,while the stove and chopping block were new,and a colorful feather duster was propped in the corner. He recognized the table and chairs,but the stove,the chopping block,and the feather duster all felt strange to him. The mix of old and new things made him think of the past and worry about the future. Everything was arranged by others' hands. He felt both old and new himself,like some kind of ornament,some strange object. He no longer recognized himself. He couldn't think to cry,couldn't think to laugh. His big hands and big feet moved about in this small,warm room like a large rabbit in a tiny wooden cage,red-eyed,looking outward and inward,with legs built for running yet nowhere to run. Huniu wore a red jacket,her face powdered white with rouge,her eyes sliding over him. He didn't dare look at her directly. She too was something both old and new,something strange—a girl yet a woman,feminine yet masculine,human yet like some fierce wild beast. This beast,dressed in a red jacket,had already caught him and was preparing to deal with him at her leisure. Anyone could push him around,but this beast was especially formidable—she would guard him every moment,glare at him,laugh at him,and could hold him tight and drain every last bit of his strength. There was no escape. He took off the satin skullcap and stared blankly at the red knot on top,staring until his eyes blurred—and when he turned his head,the whole wall was covered with little red dots,spinning and jumping,and in the middle,larger and redder,was Huniu's face,wearing an ugly grin. On their wedding night,Xiangzi finally understood: Huniu had never been pregnant. Like a conjurer,she explained it to him: "If I hadn't tricked you like that,how would you have ever nodded your head and given in? I stuffed a pillow in my waistband! Ha ha,ha ha!" She laughed until tears ran down her face: "You big fool! Never mind,I've treated you fairly enough. What kind of person are you,and what kind of person am I? I went and quarreled with my father and followed you here—shouldn't you be thanking heaven and earth?"

The next morning,Xiangzi went out early. Most of the shops had already opened for business,though a few still had their doors shut. The spring couplets on the doors were still bright red,but some of the yellow hanging decorations had been torn to shreds by the wind. The streets were quiet,though there were plenty of rickshaws about,and the pullers seemed a bit more spirited than usual. Nearly all of them wore new shoes,and there were red paper squares pasted on the backs of their rickshaws. Xiangzi envied these pullers,feeling they at least had the look of people celebrating the new year,while he himself had been cooped up like something stuffed in a gourd for days. They were all getting by honestly,while he had no work at all,just drifting idly along the main street. He was uncomfortable being idle,but whenever he thought about planning for tomorrow,he'd have to go discuss it with Huniu—his wife. He was eating out of his wife's hand—this kind of wife! All that height,all that strength,for nothing. His first duty was to wait on his wife,that thing in the red jacket with the tiger teeth,that thing that sucked the life out of a person. He was no longer a man,just a lump of flesh. He had lost himself,only struggling between her teeth like a mouse caught in a cat's jaws. He didn't want to go back and discuss things with her. He had to get away. He made up his mind to leave without a word. There was nothing wrong with that—she was a female demon who played tricks on him with pillows! He felt a burning resentment. He didn't just want to tear that new outfit to shreds,he wanted to put himself,inside and out,into clean water and wash himself thoroughly. He felt as if his whole body was coated in something unclean and nauseating that filled him with disgust from the bottom of his heart. He never wanted to see her face again!

But where to go? He had no destination. When pulling a rickshaw,his legs followed other people's words. Today his legs were free,but his heart was lost. He headed south along Xisi Pailou all the way until he passed through Xuanwu Gate. The road was so straight,and his heart had no room to turn. Out through the city gate,still heading south,he spotted a bathhouse. He decided to go in for a bath.

Stripped bare,looking at his own body,he felt an overwhelming shame. He lowered himself into the pool,and the hot water scalded his whole body until it went slightly numb. He closed his eyes. His skin tingled and softened,as if accumulated filth were radiating outward from within him. He almost didn't dare touch himself. His mind was hollow,and great beads of sweat rolled down his face. Only when his breathing grew somewhat labored did he lazily climb out,his whole body flushed red,like a newborn infant. He seemed afraid to just walk out like that. Wrapping a large towel around himself,he still felt ugly. Though sweat dripped and splattered down,he still felt unclean—the stain in his heart seemed as if it could never be washed away: in Liu Si's eyes,in the eyes of everyone who knew him,he would forever be the man who had stolen another man's woman.

Before the sweat had fully dried,he hurriedly dressed and rushed out. He was afraid of everyone seeing his naked body! Outside the bathhouse,a cool breeze swept over him and he felt a lightness in his body. The streets were much livelier than before. The clear bright sky cast a glow on every face. Xiangzi's heart was still knotted,not knowing where to go. South,then east,then south again,he made his way to Tianqiao. After the new year,by past nine in the morning,the apprentices from the shops had already finished breakfast and come here. Stalls of every kind,performance circles of every sort,had all set up and claimed their spots early. By the time Xiangzi arrived,rings of people had already gathered,with gongs and drums beating inside. He had no heart to watch any of it. He had forgotten how to laugh.

On ordinary days,the stand-up comedians here,the bear trainers,the conjurers,the shulaibao performers,the yangge dancers,the drum-song storytellers,the martial arts demonstrators—all of them could give him genuine joy and make him open his mouth wide in laughter. He was reluctant to leave Beijing,and Tianqiao accounted for at least half the reason. Whenever he caught sight of the mat-shed stalls at Tianqiao and those ring after ring of people,he would think of many funny and lovable things. Now he was too listless to push his way forward. The laughter of Tianqiao no longer had any share in it for him. He moved away from the crowds toward quieter places,yet felt reluctant to leave. No,he couldn't leave this lively and beloved place,couldn't leave Tianqiao,couldn't leave Beijing. Leave? There was nowhere to go. He still had to go back and discuss things with her—with her! He couldn't leave,and he couldn't just idle about. He had to take a step back and think,just as everyone must take a step back when there's no other way. He had endured every kind of humiliation—why dig in his heels over just this one thing? He had no way to undo the past,so he might as well follow the road ahead.

He stopped and stood still,listening to the jumbled sounds of voices and drums. Watching the people,carts,and horses coming and going,he suddenly thought of those two small rooms. The sounds in his ears seemed to fade away,the figures before his eyes seemed to disappear,and only those two small,white,warm rooms with their red double-happiness characters stood squarely before him. Though he had only spent one night there,they felt deeply familiar and close,and even that woman in the red jacket seemed not so easily cast aside after all. Standing at Tianqiao,he had nothing and was nothing. In those two small rooms,he had everything. Go back—only by going back could anything be worked out. Everything about tomorrow was in that little room. Shame,fear,and sorrow were all useless. If he meant to go on living,he had to go where there were answers.

He walked back in one stretch and stepped through the door,probably just as the clock was striking eleven. Huniu had already prepared lunch: steamed buns,stewed cabbage with meatballs,a dish of tiger-skin aspic,and a dish of pickled radish. Everything else was already set out,only the cabbage was still simmering on the fire,giving off a wonderfully appetizing aroma. She had taken off the red jacket and put back on her everyday padded trousers and jacket,but on her head she wore a small red flower made of velvet,with a little gold-paper ingot on it. Xiangzi glanced at her—she didn't look like a new bride. Her every move was that of a long-married woman: nimble,practiced,with a touch of self-satisfaction. Though she didn't look like a new bride,she still made him feel something new. She was cooking,tidying the room. The faint fragrance and warmth in the room were things he had never experienced before. Whatever she was like,he felt he had a home. Every home has something lovable about it. He didn't know what to make of it all.

"Where did you go?" she asked,ladling out the cabbage. "Went to take a bath." He took off his long gown.

"Oh! Next time you go out,say something first! Don't just swing your arms and walk off like that!" He said nothing.

"Can't even manage a grunt? Can't? I'll teach you!"

He grunted—what else could he do? He knew he had married a she-devil,but this she-devil could cook,could keep house,could scold him and help him too,and that left him feeling all wrong no matter what. He started eating the steamed buns. The food was indeed tastier and heartier than usual,but he ate without appetite. Chewing away,he couldn't feel that wolfing-it-down satisfaction he normally had,and he couldn't work up a sweat eating.

After the meal,he lay down on the kang,hands behind his head,staring up at the ceiling. "Hey! Come help wash the dishes! I'm not anyone's servant girl!" she called from the outer room.

Lazily he got up,glanced at her,and went over to help. He was normally very diligent,but now he worked with a suppressed resentment. Back at the rickshaw yard he had often helped her out,but now the more he looked at her the more he disliked her. He had never hated anyone the way he hated her,and he couldn't say exactly why. He was angry,but unwilling to let it out,keeping it all bottled up inside. Since he couldn't make a clean break with her,quarreling was pointless. Moving about in the small room,he felt his entire life was one long grievance.

When everything was tidied up,she looked around the room and sighed. Then she smiled a little. "Well?"

"What?" Xiangzi squatted by the stove,warming his hands. His hands weren't cold,but he didn't know where else to put them,so he warmed them anyway. These two small rooms really did feel like a home,but he didn't know where to put his hands or feet.

"Take me out for some fun? To Baiyun Temple? No,it's getting late. Just a stroll down the street?" She wanted to enjoy the full happiness of being a newlywed. Even if the wedding had been a shabby affair,this kind of free and easy life was actually quite nice—just right for spending more time with her husband and having a few days of carefree fun. Back at her father's place,she had never lacked for food,clothing,or pocket money. She had only lacked a man she could confide in. Now she wanted to make up for that,to walk proudly down the street and through the temple fair with Xiangzi by her side.

Xiangzi refused to go. For one thing,he felt it was shameful to parade around the whole city with a wife in tow. For another,he thought a wife who had come to him this way should be kept at home. It was nothing to be proud of,and the less it was shown off in front of people the better. Besides,once they went out,how could they avoid running into people they knew? Every rickshaw puller in the western half of the city knew about Huniu and Xiangzi. He couldn't go stirring up whispers behind his back.

"Can't we talk it over?" He was still squatting there.

"What's there to talk over?" She moved closer and stood by the stove. He lowered his hands and rested them on his knees,staring blankly at the flames. After a long silence,he said: "I can't just sit around doing nothing!"

"Born to suffer!" She gave a short laugh. "One day without pulling a rickshaw and your body starts to itch,is that it? Look at the old man—he played his whole life and ended up owning a rickshaw yard in his old age. He doesn't pull rickshaws,doesn't sell his labor,he lives by his wits. You should learn from that. What does it get you,pulling a rickshaw your whole life? Let's enjoy ourselves for a few days first and talk about the rest later. Things don't all have to be rushed in these few days—what's the hurry? I'm not planning to quarrel with you these two days,but don't go out of your way to provoke me either!"

"Let's talk it over first!" Xiangzi was determined not to give in. Since he couldn't just stamp his foot and walk out,he had to think about what to do,and first he had to take a stand—he couldn't keep swinging back and forth like a pendulum.

"Fine,go ahead then!" She pulled over a stool and sat down by the stove. "How much money do you have?" he asked.

"There it is,isn't it? I knew you were going to ask that. You didn't marry a wife,you married that money,didn't you?"

Xiangzi felt as if he'd been choked by a gust of wind,and swallowed hard several times. Old man Liu,and the rickshaw pullers at the yard,all believed he had gotten involved with Huniu out of greed for money. And now,She said it herself! Her own car, her own money, lost for no reason, and now buried under her wife's few coins; even eating felt like it had to be forced down his throat! He wanted nothing more than to wrap both hands around her neck and squeeze! Squeeze! Squeeze! Until her eyes rolled back! Squeeze the life out of everything, then slit his own throat. They weren't human, they deserved to die; he himself wasn't human, so he'd die too; nobody needed to think about living!

Xiangzi stood up, wanting to go out for another walk; he shouldn't have come back in the first place. Seeing that Xiangzi's expression was off, she softened a little: "Alright, I'll tell you. I have a total of around five hundred yuan on hand. Between the sedan chair, the deposit — three months' rent [1]

 , plastering the ceiling, making clothes, buying things, and what I gave you, it all came to just under a hundred, with over four hundred left. I'm telling you, don't worry. Let's just enjoy ourselves while we can. You've been pulling that rickshaw and sweating all year round, you deserve to have a few days of fun; and me, after being an old maid all these years, I deserve a few days of happiness too. When the money's almost gone, we'll go back to the old man. If I hadn't picked a fight with him that day, I never would've gotten out. Now my anger has cooled, and a father is still a father after all. He only has one daughter, and you're someone he likes — if we just swallow our pride and apologize, there probably won't be anything we can't get past. How convenient is that! He has money, and we'd be taking it in a perfectly proper way, nothing unreasonable about it at all; beats you going off to be someone's beast of burden! In a couple of days, you go over there first; he might not see you. If he won't see you once, go a second time; give him all the face he wants, and he won't be able to stay cold forever. Then I'll go, say a few nice things to him, and who knows, maybe we can both move back. Once we move back, we'll hold our heads high and nobody will dare look down on us; if we keep putting up with things here, we'll always be a pair of nobodies — don't you think?"

Xiangzi had never thought of this. Ever since Tigress came to the Cao household to find him, he had assumed that once he married her, he'd use her money to buy a rickshaw and go back to pulling it himself. Using his wife's money wasn't exactly dignified, but given the unspeakable nature of their relationship, there was nothing else to be done. He hadn't expected Tigress to have a move like this up her sleeve. Pulling a long face about it — well, it was certainly an idea, but Xiangzi wasn't that kind of person. Thinking it all through from beginning to end, he seemed to understand a little: if you had money of your own, others could take it from you for nothing and you'd have no place to seek justice. But when someone gave you money, you had no choice but to accept it; and once you accepted it, you could no longer consider yourself a person at all — your ambitions meant nothing, your strength meant nothing, you had to go be someone's slave: a plaything for your own wife, a servant for your father-in-law. A person was seemingly nothing at all, just a bird — go out to find your own food and you'd fall into a net. Eat someone else's grain and you had to sit quietly in a cage, singing for them, ready to be sold off at any moment!

He refused to go find Liu Siye. With Tigress, it was a bond of flesh and blood; with Liu Si, there was nothing. He'd already been done wrong by her — he wasn't about to go begging to her father on top of it! "I don't want to sit idle!" He said only this, to avoid wasting words and getting into an argument.

"Born to suffer, are you!" she said, needling and teasing him. "If you don't like being idle, go run a business."

"I don't know how! I won't make any money! I know how to pull a rickshaw, I love pulling a rickshaw!" The veins on Xiangzi's forehead were throbbing.

"Let me tell you — I simply won't let you pull that rickshaw! I won't have you reeking of sweat, stinking up my bed! You have your ideas, I have mine — let's see who can outlast who! You married a wife, but it was my money that paid for it, you didn't spend a single coin. Think about it — between the two of us, who should be listening to whom?"

Xiangzi was again left with nothing to say.

[1]

 Three months' rent, meaning the first month's rent requires paying three months upfront.

Sixteen

Xiangzi could not stand it any longer by the time the Lantern Festival came around.

Huju was in high spirits. She busied herself boiling tangyuan and making dumplings, visiting temple fairs during the day and lantern displays at night. She would not let Xiangzi have any say in things, yet she never let his mouth go empty, always finding new ways to buy or make him something fresh to eat. The courtyard tenement housed seven or eight families, most of them crammed into a single room; some of those single rooms held seven or eight people across several generations. These people pulled rickshaws, ran small street stalls, worked as policemen, or served as domestic help. Everyone had their own affairs and no one had a moment to spare — even the children went out with little baskets, fetching porridge in the morning and picking up coal cinders in the afternoon. Only the very smallest children played or scuffled in the yard with their bottoms frozen red. Furnace ash, dust, and dirty water were all dumped in the courtyard; no one had time to sweep it up, and the center of the yard was frozen solid with ice. The older children, back from collecting coal cinders, used it as a skating rink, shouting and sliding around for fun. The hardest lot fell to the old people and the women. The old had neither clothes nor food and lay on ice-cold kangs, waiting for the young to bring home a little money for a bowl of porridge. The young ones who sold their labor might come back with money or might come back empty-handed, and when they did come back they would lose their tempers and pick quarrels at the slightest excuse. The old could only swallow their tears on empty stomachs. The women had to look after the old, look after the young, and keep the men who earned the money appeased. They went on working as usual even when pregnant, living on cornbread and sweet potato porridge — and not only working as usual, but going out to fetch porridge and taking in extra piecework. On the lucky nights when old and young had eaten their fill and gone to bed, the women still had to sit hunched over a small kerosene lamp washing, sewing, and mending for others. The rooms were so small and the walls so crumbling that cold wind blew in through the cracks on one side and straight out the other, carrying away every last bit of warmth. These women wore nothing but rags, their stomachs holding a bowl or half a bowl of porridge, or perhaps a six- or seven-month fetus as well. They had to work, had to make sure the old and the young ate first. Their bodies were riddled with illness; before thirty they had already lost their hair, yet they could not rest for a single moment, walking from sickness straight into death. When they died, a coffin had to be begged from the "charitable." The young girls, sixteen or seventeen years old, had no trousers and could only wrap themselves in some scrap of cloth inside the room — a natural prison — helping their mothers with the work and rushing through piecework. To go to the outhouse, they had to wait until they were sure no one was in the yard before dashing out like thieves. All winter long they had not seen the sun or the open sky. Those who grew up plain would one day inherit everything their mothers had; those who grew up with some looks knew it themselves — sooner or later their parents would sell them off so they could "go enjoy a good life."

It was in just such a courtyard that Huju felt quite pleased with herself. She was the only one who had food and clothes, who had nothing to worry about, and who could go out for a stroll whenever she liked. She walked in and out with her chin held high, feeling her own superiority and afraid that the others might try to get close to her; she paid no attention to that crowd of suffering people. The peddlers who came to sell things here dealt almost exclusively in the cheapest goods — scraped-off meat scraps, frost-bitten cabbage, raw soybean milk, donkey and horse meat — all coming here to find customers. Since Huju had moved in, vendors selling mutton head, smoked fish, hard sesame cakes, and braised tofu with fried bean curd also began calling out their wares at the gate. She would carry her bowl, chin raised, and bring these snacks inside, while the children watched her with iron-thin fingers in their mouths, as though she were some kind of princess. She had come here to enjoy herself; she could not, would not, and did not want to see the suffering of others.

Xiangzi's first objection was to her behavior — he had grown up poor and knew what hardship meant. He had no desire to eat all those little snacks and begrudged the money spent on them. His second, and more galling, realization was something he had worked out for himself: she would not let him pull a rickshaw, yet she fed him well every day, just like fattening up a cow to squeeze out more milk. He had become entirely her plaything. He had seen it on the street — an old, scrawny female dog, even when out running errands, would pick a fat, strong male. Thinking of this, he felt not only disgust at this kind of life but genuine worry for himself. He knew how a man who sold his labor ought to take care of his body — the body was everything. If he went on living like this, he would one day become a skeleton, still the same size on the outside but hollow within. He shuddered. If he wanted to stay alive, he had to go back to pulling a rickshaw right away — get out and run, run all day, come home and fall straight into a dead sleep, oblivious to everything; stop eating her good food, and stop waiting on her for her amusement. He made up his mind to do exactly that and would not back down again. If she was willing to put up money for a rickshaw, fine; if not, he would rent one and pull it himself. Without a word, once he had decided, he went and rented a rickshaw. On the seventeenth, he started pulling again, renting by the full day. After completing two fairly long fares, he noticed something he had not felt before — his calves were tight and his hip joints ached. He knew the real cause, but to reassure himself he figured it was probably because he had not pulled for over twenty days and his legs had gone stiff; after a few more runs to loosen them up, it would likely be fine.

He took on another fare, this time running in a group — four rickshaws going together. When they picked up their shafts, the other three all deferred to a tall man in his forties to lead the way. The tall man smiled and accepted, knowing the other three rickshaws were all in better shape than his own. But he gave it everything he had; even though he knew he could not outrun the three young men behind him, he refused to use his age as an excuse to slack off. After running more than a li, someone behind him called out a compliment: "Hey, still got it in you? Not bad at all!" He answered between gasps: "Running with you brothers, how could I afford to be slow?" He really was not slow — even Xiangzi had to push himself to seventy or eighty percent just to keep up. His running style, though, was not pretty: being tall, he could not bend at the waist, which seemed to be one solid plank of wood along with his back, so his whole body had to lunge forward; with his torso pitched ahead, his arms looked like they were trailing behind. It looked less like running and more like he was drilling forward while dragging something. Because his waist was rigid, his hips had to do all the work; his feet barely cleared the ground as he twisted urgently forward. He twisted along at a genuinely fast pace, but you could tell just by watching how much effort it cost him. At turns and corners he swung his whole stiff body around in one hard pivot, making the others hold their breath for him; he always seemed to be boring straight ahead with his torso, with no regard for whether the rickshaw could make it through.

When they arrived, sweat dripped steadily from the tip of his nose, his ears, and his lips. He set down the rickshaw and quickly straightened his back, wincing as he did. When he took his money, his hands were shaking so badly he could barely hold onto it.

Having run a fare together made them friends, and the four of them parked their rickshaws in the same spot. Xiangzi and the others wiped off their sweat and went back to joking around as usual. The tall man paced back and forth alone for a while, coughing dryly at length and spitting up a great deal of white foam before he seemed to recover somewhat and began talking to them: "Done for. Where do I find the heart for it anymore — my waist, my legs, none of it has any strength left. No matter how hard I try to straighten up, my legs won't lift. All I can do is feel anxious."

"Those last few steps were still pretty good — you think that was slow?" a short young man in his twenties cut in. "Honestly, the three of us are all pretty strong, and who didn't break a sweat?" The tall man looked a little pleased, yet also a little ashamed, and let out a sigh.

"The way you run, a decent man could still get beaten by you — you believe that?" said another young man. "Age is no joke." The tall man smiled faintly and shook his head. "It's not all about age either, brothers. Let me tell you something true — in our line of work, don't get married. I mean it." Seeing that everyone had leaned in to listen, he lowered his voice a little: "Once you're married, you never get a rest, day or night. Finished. Look at my waist — stiff as a board, not a bit of give left in it. Better not to push too hard either — the moment I grit my teeth I start coughing, and there's a burning feeling right in the pit of my chest. Never mind, in our line of work you've got to be a bachelor your whole damn life. Even the little sparrows pair up two by two, but we're not allowed to start a family. And there's another thing — once you're married, a child every year. I've got five now, all of them with their mouths open waiting to be fed. The rickshaw rental is high, food is expensive, business is hard — what can you do? Better to stay a bachelor your whole life. If the urge gets too strong, go to a brothel, catch a bad disease, and accept your fate. One person — when you die, you just die. But once you've got a family, with all those mouths, big and small, you can't even close your eyes when you go. Don't you think?" He turned to Xiangzi.

Xiangzi nodded but said nothing.

Just then a fare came along. The short man was the first to negotiate the price, but he stepped aside and called out to the tall man: "Elder brother, you take it. This fellow's got five kids at home."

The tall man laughed. "All right, I'll make one more run. Strictly speaking, that's not how it's done. But fine — I'll bring back a few more flatbreads. See you later, brothers." Watching the tall man walk away, the short man muttered to himself: "Scraping by your whole damn life and you can't even get your hands on a wife. Meanwhile those rich households have one man holding four or five women."

"Never mind about them for now," the other young man picked up. "Look at this trade we're in — you really do have to watch yourself. The tall man wasn't wrong. Think about it — what do you get married for? Can you just set her there and look at her like an ornament? No. So there it is — that's the trap. Eating cornbread every day, attacked from two sides, even the strongest young man will be brought to his knees." Hearing this, Xiangzi lifted his rickshaw shafts and said casually, "Let's move south a bit — no fares around here."

"See you around," the two young men said together.

Xiangzi seemed not to hear. He walked along kicking his legs out — his hip joints really were still a little sore. He had half a mind to turn in the rickshaw and call it a day, but he simply could not find the courage to go home. What was waiting at home was not a wife but a demon that sucked men's blood.

The days were slowly getting longer. He drifted around for two or three more runs before it was barely five o'clock. He turned in the rickshaw and lingered a while longer in a teahouse. After two pots of tea he felt hungry and decided to eat his fill before going home. He ate twelve liang of meat pies and a bowl of red bean and millet porridge, then walked slowly home, letting out loud belches as he went. He knew full well there was a thunderstorm waiting for him at home, but he felt calm. He had made up his mind: no arguing with her, no fighting with her — just fall straight into bed, go out and pull the rickshaw again tomorrow as usual, and let her do whatever she pleased.

The moment he stepped through the door, Huju was sitting in the outer room. She gave him one look, her face so dark it looked ready to drip. Xiangzi thought he would try to smooth things over and stretched his long face into a greeting. But he was not used to doing that sort of thing, and he lowered his head and walked into the inner room. She said nothing. The small room was as silent as a deep mountain cave. The coughing, talking, and children's crying from the neighbors in the yard came through with perfect clarity, yet also seemed very far away, like sounds heard from a hilltop in the distance.

Neither of them was willing to speak first. They lay down one after the other without a word, like a pair of great turtles that never made a sound. After sleeping for a while, Huju spoke, her voice carrying a tone that was half annoyed and half amused: "Where did you go? You were gone the whole day."

"Pulling the rickshaw," he said, half asleep, as though something were lodged in his throat. "Oh. Couldn't stand not working up a stinking sweat — you just had to scratch that itch, you glutton for punishment. I stir-fried dishes for you and you don't come home to eat, you go stuffing yourself all over the place instead. Don't push me too far — my father was a tough man, and there's nothing I can't do. If you dare go out again tomorrow, I'll hang myself right in front of you. I say what I mean and I do what I say." "I can't sit idle."

"Can't you go find the old man?"

"No."

"How stubborn."

Xiangzi was truly furious now. He could not hold back the words in his heart any longer. "Pull the rickshaw, buy my own rickshaw — whoever tries to stop me, I'll walk out and never come back."

"Hmm —" She let that sound spin in her nose, long and winding. In it she expressed both self-importance and contempt for Xiangzi, yet inwardly she was also turning something over. She knew Xiangzi was — though very honest — a hard man. Hard men never said things just for show. She had gone to so much trouble to catch him; she could not let him go so easily. He was the ideal man: honest, hardworking and thrifty, strong and healthy. Given her looks and her age, it would not be easy to find another treasure like him. Real skill lay in knowing when to be firm and when to be soft — she had to reel him in a little: "I know you're proud, but you should also know that I truly care for you. If you won't go find the old man, then let's do it this way: I'll go find him. After all, I'm just his daughter — losing a bit of face doesn't matter."

"Even if the old man takes us back, I still have to go pull the rickshaw." Xiangzi said it plainly and finally.

Huju was silent for a long time. She had not expected Xiangzi to be this sharp. His words were simple enough, but they made it clear he was no longer falling into her traps — he was no dumb mule. That was precisely why she found him all the more interesting; she would need to use her wits to hold onto this big fellow who, when pushed too far, would kick back. She could not press him too hard — finding a man like this was no easy thing. She had to alternate between loosening and tightening her grip, so he could never quite slip out of her palm. "All right, if you want to pull the rickshaw, I can't stop you. But you have to swear you won't pull for a private household, and you have to come home every day. You see, if I go a whole day without seeing you, my heart goes all anxious. Promise me — you'll come home early every evening."

Xiangzi thought of what the tall man had said that afternoon. He lay there with his eyes open staring into the darkness, and saw a crowd of rickshaw pullers, small peddlers, and laborers — backs that could not bend, legs that dragged. That would be him someday. But there was no point in making things difficult for her any further. As long as he could go out and pull the rickshaw, he had already won a victory. "I'll always pull for individual fares."He agreed.

Though she said so, she wasn't very eager to go find Liu Siye. Father and daughter had their quarrels in ordinary times, of course, but the situation now was different — it couldn't be cleared up with just a few words, because she was no longer considered part of the Liu family. A married woman always grows somewhat distant from her parents. She didn't dare walk straight back in like she owned the place. What if the old man really turned his back on her? She could make a scene all she wanted, but if he refused to let go of the money, there was nothing she could do. Even with someone there to mediate, when it came down to it, they could only advise her to go home — she had her own family now.

Xiangzi went out to pull his rickshaw as usual, while she paced back and forth alone in the room, several times nearly getting dressed to go find her father, the thought there but her hands too lazy to move. She was torn. For her own comfort and happiness, she had to go back; for her own dignity, it was better not to. If the old man had cooled down, she only needed to bring Xiangzi to Renhe Garage, and naturally he'd have work to do — no more pulling a rickshaw — and she could steadily take over her father's business. Her heart brightened at the thought. But what if the old man held firm to the end? She'd lose face — no, not just lose face, she'd have to accept being a rickshaw puller's wife; she, hmph, no different from that bunch of women in the courtyard. Her heart went suddenly dark. She almost regretted marrying Xiangzi — no matter how hardworking he was, without her father's blessing, he'd be pulling a rickshaw his whole life. Thinking this, she even considered going back to her family home alone and cutting ties with Xiangzi completely, unwilling to lose everything for his sake. Then she thought again of the happiness she had with Xiangzi, something words couldn't describe. She sat at the edge of the kang, dazed and adrift, dwelling on the joy since their marriage; her whole body felt like one great red flower, warm and fragrant, blooming open in the sunlight. No, she couldn't bear to leave Xiangzi. Even if he pulled a rickshaw, even if he begged for food, she'd stay with him forever. Look at those women in the courtyard — if they could bear it, so could she. It passed. She no longer thought about going to the Liu family.

Ever since leaving Renhe Garage, Xiangzi refused to walk down Xi'an Men Avenue again. These past two days pulling his rickshaw, he always headed east the moment he left home, to avoid the west side of the city where Renhe Garage's rickshaws were everywhere — running into them would be awkward. This day, though, after returning the rickshaw, he deliberately passed by the garage entrance — for no other reason than to take a look. Huniu's words were still in his mind, as if he were testing whether he had the courage to return to the garage — assuming Huniu had managed to smooth things over with the old man — and before going back, he'd first see if he dared walk down this street. He pulled his cap down low and kept to the far side of the street from the garage, afraid of being spotted by someone he knew. From a distance he saw the light at the gate, and his heart felt a heaviness he couldn't quite explain. He thought of how he'd first come here, thought of Huniu's temptation, thought of that night of the birthday banquet. All of it was vivid, like pictures floating before his eyes. Among these pictures were others, clear and brief, interspersed between them: the Western Hills, the camels, the Cao residence, the detective — all distinct, all frightening, all connected into one. The pictures were so clear that his mind felt strangely blank, almost as if he were truly looking at a set of paintings and had forgotten he was part of them himself. When he thought of his own connection to it all, his heart grew chaotic — the images suddenly spun in every direction, jumbled and blurred, and he couldn't figure out why he deserved to suffer all this. The time these scenes seemed to span felt both very long and very short; he couldn't tell how old he was supposed to be anymore. He only felt that compared to when he'd first arrived at Renhe Garage, he had aged enormously. Back then, his heart had been full of hope; now, his gut was full of worry. He didn't understand why, but these pictures would never deceive him.

Renhe Garage was right there before him. He stood on the other side of the street, staring blankly at that very bright electric light. As he stared, his heart suddenly stirred. The four golden characters beneath that light — Renhe Rickshaw Garage — had changed! He couldn't read, but he remembered what the first character looked like: like two sticks joined together, neither quite a cross nor a triangle, a simple and strange character. Matching the sound to the character, it was probably "人" (rén). That "人" had changed — it was now "仁", an even stranger character than "人". He couldn't make sense of it. He looked again at the east and west rooms — those two rooms he could never forget — and neither had any light on.

He stood there until he grew impatient with himself, then lowered his head and walked home. As he walked, he wondered — could it be that Renhe Garage had changed hands? He'd have to find out gradually, and for now it was best not to say anything to his wife. When he got home, Huniu was sitting in the room cracking melon seeds to pass the time. "This late again!" Not a trace of warmth on her face. "Let me tell you, I can't go on like this! You go out and you're gone all day, I don't dare budge from this spot — a whole courtyard full of paupers, afraid something'll get stolen. All day long there's no one to talk to. This won't do, I'm not a piece of wood. Think of something, because this isn't working!" Xiangzi said nothing.

"Say something! Are you deliberately trying to make me angry? Do you have a mouth or not? Do you have a mouth or not?" Her words came faster and faster, sharper and sharper, like a string of small firecrackers going off one after another. Xiangzi still had nothing to say.

"Fine then," she was truly agitated, yet also seemed somewhat helpless against him, her face neither crying nor smiling, intensely frustrated but unable to fully vent. "Let's buy two rickshaws and rent them out — you stay home and collect the fees, how about that? How about that?" "Two rickshaws bring in thirty cents a day at most — not enough to eat on! Rent one out, I pull the other one myself, that'll do!" Xiangzi spoke slowly but naturally; the moment rickshaws were mentioned, he forgot everything else.

"What's the difference? You'd still be away from home!"

"This could work too," Xiangzi's ideas all seemed to follow wherever the rickshaw question led, "rent one out, bring in a full day's fee. The other one, I pull half the day and rent it out the other half. If I take the day shift, I head out early and I'm back by three o'clock; if I take the evening shift, I leave at three and come back at night. Perfect!" She nodded. "Let me think about it — if nothing better comes to mind, we'll do it that way."

Xiangzi was very pleased. If this plan worked out, he'd be pulling his own rickshaw again. Even though it would be bought with his wife's money, he could save up slowly and buy another one himself. Only at this moment did he feel that Huniu had her good points after all, and he actually smiled at her — a genuine, innocent smile from the heart, as if it wiped out all the hardships of the past in one stroke, and with that smile he stepped into a new world, as easy and refreshing as changing into a fresh set of clothes.

[1]

撅, meaning to lose, to be defeated.

Remember to take a break when you're tired~

Public account: 古德猫宁李

Learning resource sharing Chapter Seventeen

Xiangzi gradually pieced together the full story about Renhe Garage: Liu Siye had sold off some of the rickshaws and handed the rest over to a well-known rickshaw owner in the west of the city. Xiangzi could guess that the old man had reached his age, and without his daughter to help him, he couldn't keep the business running, so he simply closed it up and took the money to enjoy himself. Where had he gone? That, Xiangzi couldn't find out.

As for this news, he couldn't say whether he should be glad or not. From the standpoint of his own ambitions and pride, since Liu Siye had made up his mind to abandon his daughter, Huniu's plans had completely fallen through; he could go back to honestly pulling his rickshaw and earning his keep, depending on no one. But thinking of Liu Siye's money, it was genuinely a pity; who knew how the old man had managed to spirit it all away — he and Huniu hadn't seen a single copper coin of it.

Still, what was done was done, and he didn't dwell on it much, let alone feel moved by it. The way he saw it, his strength was his own, and as long as he was willing to work hard and earn money, eating wouldn't be a problem. Without any emotion at all, he told Huniu plainly. But she was shaken. Hearing this, she saw her future clearly in an instant — finished! All of it finished! She had no choice but to be a rickshaw puller's wife for the rest of her life! She would never escape this big courtyard! She had imagined her father might remarry, but never that he would simply walk away like this. If the old man had taken a new wife, Huniu could have fought for the inheritance, maybe even worked things out with the stepmother and gotten something for herself — she had plenty of schemes, as long as the old man kept the garage running. She had never imagined he would be so resolute, so ruthless — turning everything into cash and slipping away with it in secret! When she'd fallen out with him before, she'd thought it was just a tactic, that they'd make up before long, because she knew Renhe Garage couldn't do without her; who could have guessed the old man would let go of the garage just like that?!

Spring had sent word of its arrival — the scaly buds on the tree branches were already showing a plump redness. But in this big courtyard, spring did not come first to the treetops, for there was not a single flower or tree here. In this place, the spring breeze first pitted the block of ice in the yard with small pockmarks, blew a fishy, rank smell up from the filth, sent chicken feathers, garlic skins, and scraps of paper swirling into the corners in little eddies. The people of the courtyard had their hardships in every season. The old folks only now dared come out to sit in the warmth of the sun; the young women only now began to shed a little of the coal dust from their noses, showing a patch of reddish-yellow skin; the mothers only now dared, without too much shame, shoo the children out to play in the yard; the little ones only now dared drag a torn scrap of paper as a kite and run freely around the yard without their small black hands cracking open with cold. But the gruel kitchens had stopped their pots, the relief stations had stopped their rice, the charitable had stopped their handouts — as if the poor had all been handed over to the spring breeze and spring light. It was just the time when the spring wheat had turned green as young grass and the old grain stores were running low, and the price of grain had risen as it always did. The days had grown longer too, and even the old folks could no longer lie down early and let dreams deceive their hungry bellies. Spring had come to the world, but in this big courtyard it only brought more hardship. The lice that had grown fat and old — especially vicious — sometimes crawled out from the cotton wadding on the old folks or the children to take in a little of the spring light.

Huniu looked at the half-thawed ice in the yard, at the tattered, worn-out clothes, breathed in that complex smell now carrying a faint warmth, listened to the old folks' sighs and the children's cries, and felt her heart go half cold. In winter, people had all hidden indoors and the filth had been frozen into the ice; now the people were out and everything showed its true face — even the walls of broken brick were shedding dirt, as if getting ready to collapse when the rains came. The whole yard was a riot of color, blooming with the flowers of poverty, many times uglier than in winter. Hmph, it was precisely at a moment like this that she felt she would live here forever; that little money of hers would run out someday, and Xiangzi was nothing but a rickshaw puller.

She left Xiangzi to watch the house and went to Nanyuan to find her aunt, to get news of the old man. Her aunt said that Siye had indeed come to visit once — around the twelfth day of the first lunar month, she thought — first to thank her, and second to tell her he was planning to go to Tianjin, or maybe Shanghai, to travel around. He said: he'd spent his whole life without ever leaving Beijing, which meant he couldn't call himself much of a man, so while he still had some breath in him, he wanted to go out and see the world. Besides, he said, he had no face left to stay in the city, because his own daughter had brought shame on him. That was all her aunt had to report, and her conclusion was even simpler: the old man might have truly gone away, or he might have just said so and be hiding somewhere quiet; who could say.

When she got home, she threw herself face-down on the kang and wept bitterly, without a trace of pretense or cunning, crying for a long while until her eyelids were swollen.

When she was done crying, she wiped her tears and said to Xiangzi: "Fine. You win. I'll just have to go along with you now. I put my bet in the wrong place. Married to you, I'll say no more about it. Here's a hundred yuan — go buy a rickshaw and pull it!"

Here she kept something back in her mind: she had originally planned to buy two rickshaws, one for Xiangzi to pull himself and one to rent out. Now she changed her mind — buy only one, let Xiangzi pull it, and keep the rest of the money in her own hands. With the money in her hands, the power stayed with her too, and she wasn't willing to hand it all over; what if Xiangzi — after all the money had gone into buying rickshaws — had a change of heart? She had to guard against that. Besides, Liu Siye's sudden departure had made her feel that nothing was reliable, that no one could know what tomorrow would bring, and the best thing was to enjoy what you could while you could — keep some money on hand, eat whatever you felt like eating; she had always been used to snacking. She'd live off what Xiangzi earned — he was a top-class rickshaw puller — and use her own money to cover her personal spending. Enjoy the present first. The money would run out someday, but then again, people don't live forever either. Marrying a rickshaw puller — though not by choice — was already lowering herself; she couldn't also go around every day with her hand out to him while having not a single copper of her own. This decision cheered her up a little. Even knowing things would be bad in the future, at least they wouldn't collapse immediately; it was like walking toward sunset — the distance was already dim, but there was still some light just ahead, so she might as well walk a few more steps while the light lasted.

Xiangzi didn't argue with her. One rickshaw was fine — as long as it was his own, he could pull in sixty or seventy cents a day, good days or bad,It was enough to chew on. Not only did he not argue, he even felt somewhat pleased. All the hardship he had endured in the past had been for the sake of buying a rickshaw. Now that he could buy one again, what more was there to say? Naturally, one rickshaw supporting two people would leave no money to spare; the rickshaw would eventually wear out, with no savings set aside to buy a new one — that was dangerous! But since buying a rickshaw had been so difficult, being able to buy one now was reason enough to be satisfied. Why think so far ahead?

Erqiangzi in the courtyard was looking to sell his rickshaw. The previous summer, Erqiangzi had sold his daughter Xiao Fuzhi — nineteen years old — to a soldier for two hundred yuan. After Xiao Fuzhi left, Erqiangzi lived rather lavishly for a while, redeeming everything he had pawned and buying several new outfits, so the whole family was dressed quite neatly. Erqiangzi's wife was the shortest and ugliest woman in the entire courtyard — a protruding forehead, wide jowls, barely any hair on her head, teeth always showing, her face covered in freckles, unpleasant to look at. She too, with reddened eyelids, wept for her daughter while putting on a new blue gown. Erqiangzi had always had a violent temper, and after selling his daughter he drank frequently; with tears brimming in his eyes after drinking, he was especially quick to find fault. Though Erqiangzi's wife wore new clothes and had enough to eat, the joy could not outweigh the suffering — she was beaten nearly twice as often as before. Erqiangzi was over forty and had decided to stop pulling a rickshaw. So he bought a pair of baskets and set up a peddler's load, selling melons, fruits, pears, peaches, peanuts, and cigarettes — a full range of goods. After two months of business, a rough tally of accounts showed not just losses, but heavy ones. He was used to pulling a rickshaw, and had no idea how to handle a business; pulling a rickshaw was a matter of charging ahead and taking your chances — if it worked, it worked, if not, you let it go. Running a small business required patient dealing, which he could not do. Rickshaw pullers knew how to buy on credit, so he could not bring himself to refuse credit to acquaintances; once they owed him, it was hard to collect. This way, good customers never rode with him, while those who did business with him all took advantage of credit and never paid — he had no choice but to lose money. Losing money made him miserable, and misery made him drink more. Drunk, he often quarreled with police officers on the street, and at home he took out his rage on his wife and children. Offending the police, beating his wife — all because of drink. When he sobered up, he was filled with remorse and anguish. Then he would think again: this money had been bought with his daughter's life, and he was throwing it away for nothing, on top of drinking and beating people — he felt he was not fit to be called human. At such times, he could sleep lazily through an entire day, surrendering his misery to dreams.

He decided to give up the business and go back to pulling a rickshaw — he could not let that money go entirely to waste. He bought a rickshaw. When he was drunk, he had no sense of reason whatsoever. When he was sober, he cared deeply about appearances. Because he cared about appearances, he often put on the airs of dignified poverty, keeping everything in proper order. With a new rickshaw and neatly dressed, he considered himself a high-class rickshaw puller who deserved good tea and respectable fares. He could stand at the rickshaw stand, showing off his gleaming rickshaw alongside his white jacket and trousers, chatting with everyone, too proud to hustle for business. One moment he would flick his new blue cloth duster over the rickshaw with a snap, the next he would stamp his new white-soled double-faced shoes, then stand beside the rickshaw gazing at the tip of his nose with a smile, waiting for someone to praise his rickshaw, which would then give him an opening to talk endlessly. He could idle away a day or two like this. But when he actually got a good fare, his legs would not cooperate with his rickshaw and his clothes — he simply could not run fast enough. This too made him deeply miserable. Misery led him to think of his daughter, and there was nothing to do but drink. In this way, all his money drained away, leaving only the rickshaw.

Around the time of the Start of Winter, he got drunk again. The moment he walked through the door, his two sons — one thirteen, one eleven — tried to slip outside. This set him off, and he kicked each of them once. Erqiangzi's wife said something, and he went for her, kicking her in the lower abdomen; she lay on the ground for a long time without making a sound. The two boys panicked — one grabbed a coal shovel, the other snatched a rolling pin, and they fought their father with everything they had. The three of them tangled together, and in the chaos Erqiangzi's wife was trampled several more times. The neighbors came over and with great difficulty pinned Erqiangzi down on the kang, while the two boys held their mother and wept. Erqiangzi's wife regained consciousness, but was never able to get up from the ground again. By the third day of the twelfth lunar month, her breathing stopped — she was wearing the blue gown made when her daughter was sold. Her family refused to accept this and insisted on taking it to court. After friends pleaded with them at length, her family finally relented, but Erqiangzi had to promise to give her a proper burial and pay her family fifteen yuan. He pawned the rickshaw for sixty yuan. When the new year came around, he wanted to sell the rickshaw — he had no hope of redeeming it himself. When drunk, he even thought about selling one of his sons, but absolutely no one wanted them. He had also gone to find Xiao Fuzhi's husband, but the man flatly refused to acknowledge him as a father-in-law, so there was nothing more to be said.

Xiangzi knew the history of this rickshaw and was not keen on buying it. There were plenty of rickshaws around — why buy this particular one, this unlucky rickshaw, the one bought with a daughter's life and sold off only after its owner had beaten his wife to death? Huniu did not see it that way. She wanted to buy it for just over eighty yuan — a bargain. The rickshaw had only been in use for about half a year, and even the color of the rubber and leather had barely changed; moreover, it was genuinely made by Decheng, a reputable workshop in the western part of the city. A rickshaw that was seventy percent new would still cost fifty or sixty yuan elsewhere, wouldn't it? She could not bear to pass up such a deal. She also knew that shortly after the new year, money would be tight everywhere, and Erqiangzi would not be able to get a high price while being in urgent need of cash. She went herself to inspect the rickshaw, negotiated the price with Erqiangzi herself, and handed over the money. Xiangzi had no choice but to wait and start pulling — he said nothing, and it was not his place to say anything, since the money was not his own. Once the rickshaw was bought, he looked it over carefully and found it was indeed solidly built. But he still felt a little uneasy about it. What bothered him most was the black lacquered body paired with white copper fittings — when Erqiangzi had had the rickshaw made, the black and white contrast was meant to look striking; but to Xiangzi it always seemed a bit ominous, like wearing mourning clothes. He very much wanted to replace the fittings with earth-yellow or pale blue ones, something to take away some of that stark, somber look. But he did not discuss it with Huniu, to avoid inviting another round of her complaints.

When he took the rickshaw out, everyone paid special attention to it, and some people even took to calling it "the little widow." Xiangzi felt unsettled. He tried every way he could to stop thinking about it, but the rickshaw was with him day and night, and he was always vaguely uneasy, as if something could go wrong at any moment. Sometimes he would suddenly think of Erqiangzi and his fate, and it felt as though he was not pulling a rickshaw at all, but pulling a coffin. On this rickshaw, he seemed to see ghostly shadows from time to time — or so it felt.

Yet ever since he had started pulling this rickshaw, nothing had actually gone wrong, despite the constant unease in his heart. The weather was getting warmer and warmer — after shedding the padded clothes, one could almost wear just a single layer of trousers and a shirt without needing a lined jacket; Beijing had very little spring to speak of. The days grew so long they almost made a person impatient, and everyone felt drowsy. Xiangzi would head out early in the morning, and by four or five o'clock he already felt he had spent enough of his strength. But the sun was still high in the sky. He did not want to keep running, yet he was reluctant to call it a day, and he wavered, letting out long, lazy yawns.

With the days so long, if Xiangzi felt tired and bored, Huniu at home was even lonelier. In winter, she could warm herself by the stove and listen to the wind outside — though dreary, there was always the small comfort of thinking "it's just as well not to go out." Now the stove had been moved to the eaves, and there was simply nothing to do inside. The courtyard was so filthy and foul-smelling, without a single blade of green grass. Going out to the street, she was wary of the neighbors — even if she went to buy something, she had to go straight there and come straight back, not daring to wander for even a little while. She was like a bee trapped in a room, watching the sunlight outside but unable to fly out. She had nothing in common with the women in the courtyard. They talked about household gossip and petty neighborhood affairs, while she had always been free-spirited and unrestrained — she had no interest in saying or hearing any of that. Their grievances came from the hardships of daily life, and any small thing could bring them to tears; her grievances were a general dissatisfaction with life — she had no tears to shed, but wanted to curse someone out and vent her frustration. She and they could not understand each other, so it was best for each to keep to themselves and not bother exchanging words.

It was not until mid-April that she found a companion. Erqiangzi's daughter Xiao Fuzhi had come back. Xiao Fuzhi's "man" had been a military officer. Wherever he went, he set up a simple household — spending a hundred or two hundred yuan to find a young girl, buying a large plank bed and two chairs, and living comfortably for a while. When the troops were transferred elsewhere, he would walk away without a second thought, leaving the girl and the furniture right where they were. Spending a hundred or two hundred yuan and living like that for a year or so was no loss at all — just think about the mending and washing of clothes, cooking, and other small chores: if you hired a servant, wouldn't that cost ten or more yuan a month including food and wages? Taking a girl like this meant having both a servant and a bedmate, and one who was guaranteed to be clean and disease-free. When he was in a good mood, he might have a floral cotton gown made for her — a matter of just over a yuan. When he was not, he could leave her sitting at home with nothing, and she had no recourse. When he got his marching orders, he felt not the slightest regret about leaving behind the plank bed and a chair or two, because the two months of back rent owed would have to be figured out by her — selling off the plank bed and everything else might not even be enough to cover it.

Xiao Fuzhi was the one who sold the plank bed, paid off the rent, and came home wearing only a floral cotton gown and a pair of silver earrings.

After selling the rickshaw, Erqiangzi had about twenty yuan left after repaying the loan and interest. Sometimes he felt that losing his wife in middle age was deeply pitiable; since no one else pitied him, he would pour himself a drink, treat himself to something good, and wallow in self-pity. At such times, he seemed to have a grudge against money and spent it recklessly. Other times he felt he should work harder at pulling the rickshaw and raise his two sons properly, so there would be something to look forward to in the future. When he thought of his sons this way, he would go out and buy a big pile of food for the two of them to eat. Watching them wolf it all down, he would hold back tears and mutter to himself: "Children without a mother! Poor wretched children! Your father is out here working himself to the bone — it's all for you kids! I don't mind going hungry myself, eating or not eating doesn't matter, the children have to eat their fill first! Eat up! All I ask is that when you grow up, you don't forget me!" At such times, he also spent no small amount of money. Gradually, the twenty-odd yuan was all gone.

With no money left, and when he had been drinking and his temper flared, he would go a day or two without caring whether the children had eaten anything. The children had no choice but to find ways to scrape together a few copper coins to buy something to eat. They would go help carry ceremonial items at weddings and funerals, or follow the dirt carts to pick up scraps of copper and waste paper. Sometimes they could buy a few sesame cakes; sometimes they could only buy a jin of wheat-stubble sweet potatoes and swallow them down, skin, roots, and all. Sometimes between the two of them they had only one large copper coin, so they would buy peanuts or roasted broad beans — not enough to stave off hunger, but something to keep chewing on for a while.

When Xiao Fuzhi came back, the boys saw a family member again. Each of them hugged one of her legs, with nothing to say, only smiling at her through their tears. Their mother was gone — their sister was their mother now.

Erqiangzi showed no particular reaction to his daughter's return. Her coming back just meant one more mouth to feed. But seeing how overjoyed his two sons were, he could not deny that the household needed a woman to cook and wash clothes for everyone. He said nothing — things would just have to take their course.

Xiao Fuzhi was not bad-looking. Though she had been quite thin and small before, after living with the officer she had filled out considerably and grown a bit taller. She had a round face with evenly proportioned features — nothing particularly striking, but solid and pleasant enough. Her upper lip was very short, so whether she was about to get angry or smile, her lips would part first, revealing very white and even teeth. The officer had been especially fond of those teeth. With those teeth showing, she had a slightly simple, vacant look, and at the same time something of a naive charm. This quality made her — like all poor girls who are not unattractive — like a flower or plant: needing only the faintest fragrance or color to be picked up and sold at the market.

Huniu, who had never bothered with anyone in the courtyard, came to regard Xiao Fuzhi as a friend. First, Xiao Fuzhi was reasonably good-looking; second, she still had a floral cotton gown; third, Huniu figured that since she had been with a military officer, she must have seen something of the world — and so Huniu was willing to associate with her. Women do not make friends easily, but once they do, it happens quickly; within a few days the two had become close friends. Huniu loved snacking, and whenever she got hold of some melon seeds or the like, she would always call Xiao Fuzhi over, chatting and laughing while they ate. In the course of their talk, Xiao Fuzhi would smile her simple smile, showing her white teeth, and tell Huniu all sorts of things she had never heard before. Following the officer, she had not exactly lived well, but when he was in a good mood he would take her out to a restaurant or to see a show, so she had plenty of stories to tell — stories that made Huniu envious. She also had many things she could not bring herself to say: to her, these were violations; to Huniu, they sounded like pleasures. Huniu begged her to tell, and she was too embarrassed to speak but also too embarrassed to refuse. She had seen erotic pictures, which Huniu had never laid eyes on. Things of this sort, Huniu would hear once and want to hear again. She came to see Xiao Fuzhi as the most lovable, most enviable, and even most enviable person she knew. After hearing all of it, she would look at herself — her appearance, her age, her husband — and feel that her whole life had been one long injustice. She had never had a youth, and there was nothing to hope for in the future, and now,Xiangzi was again like a dead brick — a solid, immovable lump! The more dissatisfied she was with Xiangzi, the more she loved Xiaofuzi. Xiaofuzi was so poor, so pitiful, yet in her eyes she was someone who had enjoyed life and seen the world — even if she died tomorrow, it wouldn't be a waste. In her view, Xiaofuzi represented everything a woman ought to experience.

Xiaofuzi's hardship seemed invisible to Hunie. Xiaofuzi had come back with nothing, yet she had to — no matter how useless her father was — look after her two younger brothers. Where was she supposed to find money to feed them?

Erqiangzi got drunk and came up with an idea: "If you really care about your brothers, you'll find a way to earn money and feed them! You all count on me — I work like an animal all day long, and I need to eat first. Can I run around on an empty stomach? What if I drop dead from exhaustion — would that amuse you? You're just sitting around idle anyway. You've got what it takes, so why not sell it? What are you waiting for?"

Looking at her drunken father, looking at herself, looking at her two brothers who were as hungry as starving mice, Xiaofuzi had nothing left but tears. Tears couldn't move her father, tears couldn't fill her brothers' stomachs — she had to offer something more real. To keep her brothers fed, she had to sell her own flesh. Holding her little brother, her tears fell into his hair, and he said, "Sister, I'm hungry!" Sister! Sister was a piece of meat, to be given to her brothers to eat.

Hunie not only offered Xiaofuzi no comfort, she was actually willing to help her out: Hunie was willing to put up a little capital to get her dressed up nicely, to be paid back once she earned some money. Hunie was willing to lend her a room, since Xiaofuzi's own place was too filthy, while Hunie's at least had Xiangzi around, and besides, there were two rooms — enough space for everyone to move around. Since Xiangzi wouldn't be back during the day, Hunie was happy to help a friend, and could also see more and learn more about the things she herself lacked and longed to do but couldn't. Each time Xiaofuzi used the room, Hunie set one condition: she had to pay two jiao. Friends were friends, but business was business — for Xiaofuzi's sake, she had to keep the room tidy, which meant both labor and extra expenses. Didn't buying a broom and dustpan cost money? Two jiao was absolutely not too much — it was only because they were friends that she could be so accommodating.

Xiaofuzi showed a faint smile, swallowing her tears.

Xiangzi knew nothing of all this, yet he couldn't sleep well again. Hunie had "done right by" Xiaofuzi, and now she wanted to reclaim from Xiangzi the youth she had lost.

[1]

 过话, meaning to converse.

[2]

 人, here referring to a man. This term is used exclusively in the context of informal relationships between men and women.

Eighteen

By the sixth month, the courtyard tenement was virtually silent during the day. The children would get up early and head out with tattered baskets to scavenge whatever they could find. By nine o'clock, the vicious sun had nearly scorched their bony backs, and they had no choice but to bring back what little they'd gathered and eat whatever the adults could give them. Then the older ones, if they could scrape together the tiniest bit of capital in the world, would go out buying and collecting, pooling together ice chips to sell. If they couldn't find even that much, they'd band together and head outside the city to swim in the moat, casually stealing a few lumps of coal from the train station, or catching dragonflies and cicadas to sell to the children of wealthy families. The younger ones, too timid to venture far, would stay near the trees outside the gate, picking locust worms, digging for "jingang" beetles[1] and the like. With the children gone and the men gone too, the women all stripped to the waist inside their rooms and refused to come out — not out of modesty, but because the ground in the courtyard had been baked hot enough to burn their feet.

Not until the sun was nearly setting did the men and children trickle back one by one. By then the courtyard had wall shadows and a faint breeze, while the rooms had trapped a full day's heat and were like ovens. Everyone sat out in the courtyard waiting for the women to cook. At that hour the courtyard was tremendously lively, like a market with nothing to sell. Everyone had endured the heat all day and came back red-eyed and short-tempered. Stomachs were empty, and every face was drawn tight with irritability. One wrong word and some would beat their children, others would beat their wives. Even when it didn't come to blows, they'd curse to their hearts' content. This commotion went on until everyone had eaten. Some of the small children lay down in the courtyard and fell asleep right there, while others ran out to the street to play wildly[2]. After eating their fill, the adults mellowed considerably, and those who liked to talk gathered in groups of three or five to recount the day's hardships. Those who hadn't eaten — with nothing left to pawn and nowhere to sell anything, even if they had something to pawn or sell — were out of options because darkness had already fallen. The men, regardless of how stifling the room was, buried themselves on the kang without a word, or perhaps let out a loud string of curses. The women, holding back tears, went around to the neighbors begging, knocking on door after door, until they finally borrowed a tattered twenty-copper paper note. Clutching that precious note, they went out to get some mixed-grain flour and cook up a pot of porridge for everyone to eat.

Hunie and Xiaofuzi were outside this rhythm of life. Hunie was pregnant — for real this time. Xiangzi left early every morning, and she never got up until eight or nine o'clock. The belief that pregnant women shouldn't move around too much was a traditional misconception, and Hunie both believed it and used it to assert a certain status: everyone else had to get up early and get to work, but she alone could lie in as long as she pleased. In the evenings, she would take a small stool out to the street gate where there was a breeze and sit there until nearly everyone in the courtyard had gone to sleep before coming back in. She had no interest in chatting with the others.

Xiaofuzi also got up late, but for different reasons. She was afraid of the men in the courtyard looking at her sideways, so she waited until they had all left before daring to step outside her room. During the day she either came to find Hunie or went out for a walk, since she herself was her own advertisement. In the evenings, to avoid the stares of the courtyard residents, she went out again and wandered the streets, and only when she figured everyone had gone to bed would she slip quietly back in.

Among the men, Xiangzi and Erqiangzi were exceptions. Xiangzi dreaded entering the courtyard and dreaded going into the room even more. The constant talk of poverty from the courtyard residents made his heart restless, and he preferred to find a quiet spot to sit alone. As for the room, he felt more and more that Hunie was like a tigress. The little room was so hot and suffocating, and with that tigress added to it, he felt he could barely breathe every time he went in. In the past he'd had no choice but to come home early, to avoid Hunie making a scene. Lately, with Xiaofuzi to keep her company, she didn't keep such a tight rein on him, and he started coming back a little later.

As for Erqiangzi, he had barely come home at all lately. He knew what his daughter was doing for a living and couldn't bring himself to walk through that gate. But he had no way to stop her — he knew he didn't have the means to support his children. He had no choice but to stay away, out of sight, out of mind. Sometimes he resented his daughter — if Xiaofuzi had been a boy, she surely wouldn't have had to suffer this kind of shame. Since she was born a girl, why did she have to be born to him? Sometimes he pitied her — his daughter was selling her body to feed her two younger brothers. Resent her or love her, he was helpless. When he'd been drinking and had run out of money, the resentment and pity both vanished, and he'd come back to ask her for money. At those moments he saw his daughter as something that could earn money, and as her father, asking her for it was perfectly justified. At such times he also thought about dignity: didn't everyone look down on Xiaofuzi? Well, her own father hadn't let her off either — he forced her to hand over money and cursed her up and down, as if performing for an audience — Erqiangzi had done nothing wrong. Xiaofuzi was shameless by nature.

He'd make a scene, and Xiaofuzi wouldn't breathe a word. It was Hunie who half-scolded, half-coaxed him away, though naturally he had to leave with some money in hand. That money was only fit for buying more drink, because if he were sober when he looked at it, he'd go throw himself in the river or hang himself.

On the fifteenth day of the sixth month, the heat had gone mad. The moment the sun came up, the ground was already as if on fire. A layer of grayish haze, neither quite cloud nor quite fog, hung low in the air, making it hard to breathe. There was not a breath of wind. Xiangzi looked up at the grayish-red sky from the courtyard and decided to go out for the late shift — not leaving until after four in the afternoon. If he couldn't make any money, he could keep pulling all the way through to dawn: nighttime, no matter what, was more bearable than daytime.

Hunie urged him to go out, afraid he'd be in the way at home — what if Xiaofuzi brought a customer back? "You think it's any more comfortable staying home? By midday the walls inside are burning hot!"

He said nothing, drank a ladle of cold water, and walked out.

The willow trees along the street looked sickly, their leaves coated with a layer of dust and curled up on the branches. The branches hung limp and listless, too lazy to stir. The road was bone dry without a single drop of water, giving off a pale white glare. Dust on the footpaths rose high into the air and merged with the gray haze above, forming a vicious curtain of hot grit that scorched the faces of passersby. Everything was parched, everything was burning to the touch, everything was suffocating. The whole old city was like a kiln fired through and through, leaving no room to breathe. Dogs lay on the ground with their red tongues hanging out. The nostrils of mules and horses flared unusually wide. Street vendors didn't dare call out their wares. The asphalt road had begun to melt. Even the brass plaques on shopfronts seemed on the verge of melting in the sun. The streets were eerily quiet, broken only by the maddening, monotonous clang of metal from the ironmongers' shops. The rickshaw pullers, knowing full well that if they didn't work they wouldn't eat, were still too listless to drum up business. Some had parked their rickshaws in whatever shade they could find, put up the canopy, and dozed off in the seat. Some had ducked into small teahouses for tea. Some hadn't even brought their rickshaws out at all, and had just come to the street to have a look, to see if there was any possibility of working. Even those who were pulling fares — even the most strapping young men — were willing to swallow their pride and no longer dared to run, only plodding forward with their heads down. Every well became their salvation. No matter how few steps they'd just pulled, the moment they spotted a well they rushed over. If they couldn't get freshly drawn water, they drank alongside the donkeys and horses from the water trough. And some, struck by heatstroke or heat exhaustion, walked a few steps and then pitched forward onto the ground, never to rise again.

Even Xiangzi felt a twinge of fear. After pulling his empty rickshaw a few steps, he felt heat closing in around him from his face all the way down to his feet, and sweat was already running from the backs of his hands. Still, when he spotted a fare, he wanted to take it, thinking that running might at least stir up a little breeze.
