He picked up a fare and set off, and only then did he realize that the heat had reached a point where it permitted no one to work. The moment he ran, he couldn't catch his breath, and his lips went dry. Even when he didn't feel thirsty, he wanted to drink every time he saw water. If he didn't run, the vicious sun cracked the skin on his hands and back. He managed somehow to pull the fare to the destination, his shirt and trousers plastered to his body. He fanned himself with a palm-leaf fan — the air was hot. He had already lost count of how many times he'd drunk cold water, yet he found himself heading to a teahouse again. After drinking two pots of hot tea, he felt somewhat calmer inside. The tea went in through his mouth and sweat immediately poured out of his body, as if his body were hollow and incapable of retaining a single drop of moisture. He didn't dare move again.

After sitting for a long while, he grew restless. Unable to go out, with nothing to do, he felt as though the weather had a personal grudge against him. No — he couldn't give in. He'd been pulling a rickshaw for more than a day, and this wasn't his first summer at it. He couldn't just "soak" a whole day for nothing. He wanted to go out, but his legs truly refused to move. His whole body felt weak and unsatisfied, like after a bath that hadn't quite done the job — plenty of sweat had come out, but he still felt uneasy inside. He sat a while longer, then could sit no more. Either way he was sweating, so he might as well go out and give it a try.

The moment he stepped outside, he realized his mistake. The layer of gray haze had dispersed and the air was less suffocating, but the sunlight was far more ferocious than before. No one dared look up to find where the sun was — there was only the sense of blinding brightness everywhere: in the air, on the rooftops, on the walls, on the ground, everything was a blazing white with a tinge of red bleeding through. From top to bottom it was like one enormous burning glass, every ray of light like the focal point of that glass, hot enough to set things alight. In this white glare, every color was an assault on the eyes, every sound was grating, every smell was laced with the rank, fetid vapor rising from the ground. The streets seemed emptied of people, the roads appeared suddenly much wider, vast and without a trace of cool air, a terrifying expanse of white. Xiangzi didn't know what to do with himself. He lowered his head, pulled his rickshaw, and shuffled forward at a crawl — no plan, no destination, dazed and muddled, his body draped in a layer of sticky sweat that gave off a sour, stale smell. After walking a while, the soles of his feet and his socks and shoes all stuck together, as if he were treading on wet mud — deeply unpleasant. He hadn't intended to drink any more water, but when he came upon a well he couldn't help himself and gulped down another round — not to quench thirst, but seemingly just to enjoy that brief coolness of the well water, a sudden chill passing from his mouth down to his stomach, his pores snapping shut all at once, a shiver running through him — wonderfully refreshing. After drinking, he belched repeatedly, the water threatening to come back up.

Walking a while, sitting a while, he remained too listless to look for fares. By the time noon arrived, he still felt no hunger. He thought about eating something as usual, but the sight of food made him nauseous. His stomach was more or less full of various kinds of water, and sometimes it would gurgle softly inside, like a mule or horse whose belly rumbles after drinking its fill.

Comparing winter and summer, Xiangzi had always thought winter was more frightening. He hadn't imagined summer could be this unbearable. He'd spent more than one summer in the city, and he couldn't remember it ever being this hot. Was the weather hotter than in previous years, or was his own body weakening? The thought of it suddenly cleared his head a little, as if something cool had passed through his mind. His own body — yes, his own body was giving out. He was frightened, but there was nothing he could do. He had no way to drive Hunie away. He was going to turn into Erqiangzi, turn into that tall man he'd encountered before, turn into little Ma's grandfather. Xiangzi was finished.

It was just past one in the afternoon when he picked up another fare. This was the hottest hour of the day,It happened to be the hottest day of that summer, yet he made up his mind to go out and pull a fare. He didn't care how scorching it was under the sun: if he managed to finish a run without any trouble, that would prove his body hadn't given out; and if he couldn't handle the work, well, what more was there to say — he might as well fall dead on that blazing ground.

He had only taken a few steps when he felt a hint of cool breeze, like a sliver of cool air slipping through a crack in the door of a sweltering room. He didn't dare trust himself; he looked at the willow branches along the roadside, and sure enough, they stirred ever so slightly, twice. The street suddenly filled with people — shopkeepers rushed out from their stores, each clutching a palm-leaf fan over their head, looking around in every direction: "A cool breeze! A cool breeze! The cool breeze has come!" Everyone was nearly jumping up and shouting. The willows along the road suddenly seemed like angels, delivering a message from heaven: "The willow branches are moving! Dear God, please send us more cool wind!"

It was still hot, but people's hearts were much calmer. The cool breeze, even just a little of it, gave everyone a great deal of hope. After a few gusts, the sunlight wasn't as fierce — one moment bright, the next slightly dimmer, as if a sheet of drifting sand were floating above. Then the wind suddenly picked up, and the willow branches, which had been motionless for so long, swayed and danced as if they had just received some wonderful news, their limbs seeming to grow longer. After one gust, the sky darkened and dust flew up into the air. As the dust settled a little, ink-black clouds appeared on the northern horizon. Xiangzi's sweat was gone; he glanced north, stopped the rickshaw, and put on the rain cover. He knew that summer rain could come without a moment's warning.

He had just finished putting on the rain cover when another gust hit, and the black clouds, rolling like waves, had already blotted out half the sky. The heat rising from the ground mixed with the cool wind, carrying a raw, dry smell of earth — neither cool nor hot. The southern half of the sky was still bright and sunny, while the northern half was dark as ink, as if some great calamity were approaching and everything had been thrown into a panic. Rickshaw pullers scrambled to put on their rain covers, shopkeepers rushed to take down their hanging signs, street vendors frantically packed up their stalls, and pedestrians hurried forward at a quickened pace. Another gust of wind. When it passed, the shop signs, the street stalls, and the pedestrians all seemed to have been swept away by the wind — gone without a trace, leaving only the willow branches whipping wildly.

The clouds hadn't yet covered the whole sky, but the ground was already very dark, as if the blazing brightness of midday had suddenly turned to night. The wind carried scattered raindrops, lurching east and west as if searching for something on the ground. Far to the north, a red flash of lightning seemed to peel back a piece of the black cloud, revealing a vast patch of blood-red sky. The wind died down a little, but it was sharp and biting, making people shiver. After one such gust, everything seemed at a loss, even the willows standing in uneasy suspense, waiting for something. Another flash, right overhead, and bright white raindrops came pelting down hard, kicking up clouds of dust that carried a faint smell of rain. A few large drops smacked Xiangzi on the back, and he shuddered twice. The drops stopped, and the black clouds spread evenly across the entire sky. Then another gust — fiercer than before — the willow branches flew sideways, dust scattered in all directions, and rain streaked down; wind, dust, and rain all merged together into one mass, cutting across every direction in a cold, grey blur, wrapping everything inside it, making it impossible to tell what was tree, what was ground, what was cloud — chaos in all directions, noise everywhere, everything lost in confusion. The wind passed, leaving only straight curtains of rain falling from sky to earth, not visible as individual drops but as one solid sheet, one wave after another; from the ground, countless arrows seemed to shoot upward, and from the rooftops, ten thousand waterfalls came cascading down. Within minutes, sky and earth were indistinguishable — a river from the sky pouring down, rivers on the ground flowing sideways, forming a dim, murky, sometimes blindingly white world of water.

Xiangzi's clothes were soaked through long ago, not a single dry spot on his body; even through his straw hat, his hair was completely wet. The water on the ground was over his ankles, making every step a struggle; the rain above hammered his head and back, lashed across his face, and wrapped around his legs. He couldn't lift his head, couldn't open his eyes, couldn't breathe, couldn't take a step. He felt rooted to the water, with no idea where the road was, no sense of what lay ahead, behind, or to either side — only the bone-chilling water pouring over every part of him. He was beyond knowing anything; there was only a faint warmth somewhere in his chest and the sound of rain filling his ears. He wanted to set the rickshaw down, but didn't know where to put it. He wanted to run, but the water gripped his legs. Half dead, half alive, he lowered his head and dragged himself forward one step at a time. The passenger seemed to have died in the seat, not making a sound, leaving the puller to fight for his life in the water.

The rain eased a little, and Xiangzi straightened his back slightly and let out a breath: "Sir, shall we find shelter and wait it out?"

"Keep going! What do you think you're doing, leaving me stranded here?" the passenger shouted, stamping his feet.

Xiangzi really wanted to just set the rickshaw down and find somewhere to take cover. But looking at himself, water streaming off every inch of his body, he knew that if he stopped he would shake himself to pieces. He clenched his teeth and splashed forward through the water, heedless of how deep or shallow it was. He had barely gone far when the sky darkened for a moment, then flashed bright, and the rain blinded his eyes again.

He delivered the passenger, who didn't give him a single extra copper. Xiangzi said nothing — he barely had enough left in him to hold on to his life.

The rain let up for a while, then came down again, lighter than before. Xiangzi ran straight home in one go. He huddled by the fire and tried to warm himself, shaking like a leaf in a storm. Huniu mixed him a bowl of ginger and sugar water, and he drank it down in one go, clutching the bowl like a man in a daze. When he finished, he burrowed under the covers and knew nothing more, drifting in and out of sleep, the sound of rain rushing in his ears.

By past four o'clock, the black clouds began to show signs of exhaustion, flashing weakly with a dull, faded light. Before long, the clouds in the west split open, their dark peaks edged with golden yellow, wisps of white mist drifting below; the lightning moved south, trailing a few faint rumbles of thunder. After a while longer, sunlight broke through the gaps in the western clouds, turning the rain-soaked leaves into a sheet of golden green. In the eastern sky hung a double rainbow of seven colors, both ends planted in the dark clouds, its arch framing a patch of clear blue sky. The rainbow soon faded, and not a single black cloud remained in the sky. The washed blue sky and the washed world below seemed like a new, cool, and beautiful world just born out of darkness. Even above the puddles in the courtyard, a few dragonflies of various colors had appeared.

Yet aside from the children running barefoot after those dragonflies, the people of the courtyard had no time to appreciate the clear sky after the rain. The back eave wall of Xiao Fuzi's room had collapsed in one section, and the three sisters were busy pulling up the bed mat to plug the hole. The courtyard walls had crumbled in several places, but no one had time to deal with that — everyone was focused on their own rooms: some had steps too low and water had flooded inside, so people worked together with dustpans and broken bowls to bail it out. Some had a side wall fall in and were trying to patch it up. Some had roofs leaking like watering cans, soaking everything inside, and were busy moving things out to dry by the stove or on the windowsill. While the rain was still falling, they had huddled in those rooms that could collapse and bury them alive at any moment, entrusting their lives to heaven; after the rain, they tallied up and dealt with their losses. Though a heavy rain might knock half a copper off the price of a catty of grain, that was no compensation for what they had lost. They paid rent, yet no one ever came to fix the houses; only when a place became completely uninhabitable would one or two plasterers show up and loosely patch it with plain mud and broken bricks — ready to collapse again. Fall behind on rent, and the whole family would be thrown out, their belongings seized. The houses were falling apart, the houses could kill people, and nobody cared. Their little money could only rent places like this; broken, dangerous — they had it coming.

The greatest loss was falling sick from the rain. Adults and children alike spent their days and nights on the streets looking for work, and summer downpours could drench them at any moment. They all earned their living through physical labor, always drenched in sweat, and the northern rainstorms were so sudden, so cold, sometimes mixed with hailstones the size of walnuts; icy raindrops striking wide-open pores could at the very least put them flat on the kang with a fever for a day or two. When children fell sick, there was no money for medicine; a single rainstorm could push the corn and sorghum higher in the fields, but it could also kill off many of the city's poor. When adults fell sick, it was even worse; after the rain, poets composed verses about dewdrops on lotus leaves and double rainbows; in poor households, a sick adult meant the whole family went hungry. One rainstorm might drive a few more people into prostitution or petty theft, send a few more to prison; with an adult sick, the children were better off stealing or selling their bodies than starving. The rain falls on the rich and on the poor; on the righteous and on the unrighteous. In truth, the rain is not fair, because it falls upon a world that has no fairness.

Xiangzi fell ill. He was not the only sick person in the courtyard.

[1]

 金刚, referring to the pupa of the locust tree caterpillar.

[2]

 撒欢, originally describing the joyful running and frolicking of animals, also used to describe the same behavior in young children.

Nineteen

Xiangzi slept in a daze for two days and nights, and Huniu panicked. She went to the Goddess Temple and prayed for a folk remedy: besides a pinch of incense ash, there were two or three herbal ingredients. After they poured it down his throat, he did open his eyes and looked around, but after a while he fell asleep again, mumbling something unintelligible. Only then did Huniu think to send for a doctor. After two acupuncture needles and a dose of medicine, he came to his senses, and the moment he opened his eyes he asked, "Is it still raining?"

When the second dose of medicine was ready, he refused to take it. He resented spending the money, and he hated himself for being so useless — imagine getting sick from a single rainstorm. He wouldn't drink that bitter brew. To prove he didn't need medicine, he wanted to get up and dressed right away. But the moment he sat up, his head felt weighted down by a boulder, his neck went limp, stars burst before his eyes, and he collapsed again. There was nothing more to say. He took the bowl and swallowed the medicine.

He lay in bed for ten days. The longer he lay there, the more anxious he became. Sometimes he buried his face in the pillow and wept without a sound. He knew he couldn't go out and earn money, which meant every expense had to come out of Huniu's pocket. Once her money ran out, everything would depend on his one rickshaw. Given Huniu's love of spending and eating, he couldn't keep up with her — and on top of that, she was pregnant. The more he couldn't get up, the more his mind wandered, and the more he brooded, the worse he felt, and the harder it was to recover. The moment he was barely out of danger, he asked Huniu, "Where's the rickshaw?"

"Don't worry, it's rented out to Ding Si."

"Oh!" He wasn't at ease about his rickshaw — he was afraid Ding Si, or anyone else, might damage it. But since he couldn't get out of bed himself, of course it had to be rented out. Could it just sit idle? He calculated in his head: if he pulled it himself, he'd bring in at least fifty or sixty cents a day, good days or bad. Rent, coal, rice, firewood, charcoal, lamp oil, tea, water — never mind new clothes — that would just barely cover two people, and only if they were careful, not the way Huniu spent without a care. Now, with only a little over ten cents a day coming in from the rental, he was losing forty or fifty cents every day, not counting medicine. What if the illness dragged on? Yes, he couldn't blame Er Qiangzi for drinking, couldn't blame those poor friends of his for going off the rails — pulling a rickshaw was a dead end. No matter how hard you worked, how determined you were, you just couldn't afford to start a family, get sick, or let anything go wrong. Hmph! He thought about his first rickshaw, the money he'd scraped together — what had he done to deserve losing it? He hadn't been sick, hadn't started a family — it was just gone, cruelly and without reason. Being good didn't help, being bad didn't help. This road led only to death, and there was no telling when it would come — you'd never know until it did. At that thought, his grief turned to despair. To hell with it — if he couldn't get up, he'd just lie there. That's all there was to it. He stopped thinking about anything and lay there quietly. But before long he couldn't stand it anymore. He wanted to get up right away and go back to his bitter struggle. The road might be a dead end, but the human heart was alive — right up until they nailed the coffin shut, hope never stopped. But he couldn't stand up. With nothing else to do, helplessly, almost begging, he said a few words to Huniu:

"I said that rickshaw was unlucky — it really is unlucky."

"Just get better. Always going on about the rickshaw — you're rickshaw-crazy!"

He said nothing more. Right, he was rickshaw-crazy. Ever since he'd started pulling, he'd believed the rickshaw was everything. Turns out...

As soon as he felt a little better, he got out of bed. He looked in the mirror and didn't recognize the person staring back: a face full of stubble, temples and cheeks sunken in, eyes like two deep hollows, and that scar covered in wrinkles. The room was stifling hot, but he didn't dare go out to the courtyard — partly because his legs felt boneless, and partly because he didn't want anyone to see him. Not just in this courtyard, but at every rickshaw stand in the east and west of the city, everyone knew Xiangzi was a top-notch young man. Xiangzi couldn't be this wretched, sickly ghost. He refused to go out. But staying inside, he felt suffocated. He wished he could eat himself strong in one gulp and go back to pulling the rickshaw. But illness does what it wants — it comes and goes on its own terms.

After resting for about a month, without waiting to be fully recovered, he got back on the rickshaw. He pulled his cap down low so people wouldn't recognize him, allowing himself to run at a slower pace. "Xiangzi" and "fast" were inseparable — he couldn't just plod along openly and let people look down on him.

His body hadn't fully healed, but he was greedy to take on more fares to make up for what he'd lost during his illness. After a few days, the sickness came back. This time dysentery came with it. He was so frustrated he slapped himself in the face. It was no use — his stomach felt like it was touching his spine, and still the diarrhea wouldn't stop. By the time the dysentery finally let up, his legs could barely manage to squat down and stand back up, let alone think about running.

He rested for another month. He knew Huniu's money was probably almost gone. By the fifteenth of the eighth month, he decided to go back out. If he got sick again this time, he swore he'd go drown himself in the river.

During his first illness, Xiao Fuzi often came by to check on him. Xiangzi had never been able to out-talk Huniu, and his heart was so pent up, so he sometimes exchanged a few words with Xiao Fuzi. This infuriated Huniu. When Xiangzi wasn't home, Xiao Fuzi was a good friend. When Xiangzi was home, Xiao Fuzi was — in Huniu's mind — "coming to flirt. Shameless!" She pressured Xiao Fuzi to pay back the money she owed her, and declared, "From now on, don't you dare set foot in here again!"

Xiao Fuzi had lost the place where she received clients, and her own room was so run-down — a sleeping mat propped against the back wall — she had no choice but to go register at the "relocation agency." But the "relocation agency" had no use for someone like her. They dealt in "female students" and "young ladies from good families" — high-end connections, big money — they didn't want someone as ordinary as her. She was out of options. She thought about going to work in a brothel, but without any capital of her own, she couldn't run her own operation, so she'd have to be bonded to an establishment. But that would mean losing all her freedom — who would look after her two younger brothers? Death was the simplest and easiest thing, and living was already like being in hell. She wasn't afraid to die, but she didn't want to die, because she wanted to do something braver and greater than dying. She wanted to see both her brothers earning their own living before she died in peace. She would die sooner or later, but she had to die in a way that saved two lives. After thinking it over and over, she had only one path: sell herself cheap. Anyone willing to come to her little room certainly wouldn't pay much — fine, whoever came was fine, as long as they paid something. This way, she saved on clothes and makeup too. The men who sought her out didn't dare hope she'd be dressed up in any particular style — they paid for what they could afford. She was very young, and that itself was already a bargain.

Huniu's body had become quite inconvenient. She was afraid to even go out to buy things, worried something might go wrong, and Xiangzi was gone all day, and Xiao Fuzi wouldn't come over — she was as lonely as a dog tied up inside. The lonelier she got, the more she resented things. She assumed Xiao Fuzi's cut-rate business was deliberately meant to spite her. She wasn't going to take that lying down. She sat in the outer room with the door wide open and waited. When someone headed toward Xiao Fuzi's room, she'd raise her voice and make cutting remarks, embarrassing them and making things difficult for Xiao Fuzi. When Xiao Fuzi's clients dwindled, she was pleased. Xiao Fuzi knew that if this kept up, everyone in the courtyard would gradually side with Huniu and drive her out. She was only afraid, not daring to get angry — someone who had fallen to her circumstances knew to put facts before anger and tears. She brought her little brother over and knelt before Huniu. She said nothing, but her expression made it clear: if this bow wasn't enough, she herself wasn't afraid to die — but she'd make sure no one else got to live either. The greatest sacrifice is enduring humiliation, and the greatest endurance of humiliation is preparation for resistance.

Huniu was at a loss. No matter how she thought about it, something felt off — but with that big belly of hers, she didn't dare start a fight. Since she couldn't go at it physically, she had to give herself a way out: she'd just been teasing Xiao Fuzi, who'd have thought it would be taken seriously — Xiao Fuzi was just too literal-minded. With that explanation, they became friends again, and Huniu went back to looking out for Xiao Fuzi as before.

From the Mid-Autumn Festival onward, Xiangzi became more careful in everything. Two bouts of illness had taught him he was not made of iron. The ambition to earn more money hadn't entirely faded, but repeated blows had made him see clearly just how feeble an individual's strength really was. A tough man sometimes had to grit his teeth, but even gritting your teeth could make you spit blood. Though the dysentery had cleared up, his stomach still cramped from time to time. Sometimes, just when his legs had found their stride and he thought about picking up the pace, a rope-twisting pain would seize his gut, and he'd slow down — sometimes even stop dead in his tracks, head bowed, belly sucked in, forcing himself to endure. When he was pulling a solo fare it was manageable, but when running in a group, he'd suddenly stop without warning, baffling everyone around him, and leaving himself deeply embarrassed. He was only in his twenties and already making a fool of himself like this — what would it be like when he was thirty or forty? At that thought, a wave of cold sweat broke over him.

For the sake of his health, he very much wanted to go back to pulling a contract rickshaw. After all, that kind of work had its breathing room — you had to run fast when running, but the rest periods were long too, and it was easier overall than pulling random fares. But he knew perfectly well that Huniu would never let him go. Once you had a family, you lost your freedom, and Huniu was especially fierce about it. He accepted his bad luck. For half a year — from autumn into winter — he just half-coped and half-struggled, not daring to be careless, not daring to slack off, his heart heavy and stifled, head down, grinding away. Head down — he no longer dared to be as recklessly carefree as before, as if nothing mattered. As for earning money, he still made more than the average rickshaw puller. Unless his stomach was in the grip of cramps, he never willingly let a fare slip by. He pulled whoever needed pulling, and he never picked up bad habits. Deliberately inflating prices, switching passengers mid-route, holding out for only the best fares — none of that rubbed off on him. This meant he worked harder, but money came in every day without fail. He didn't cut corners, so he didn't run into trouble either.

But the money coming in was too little to save anything. In with the left hand, out with the right — clean every time. He didn't even dare think about saving anymore. He knew how to be frugal, but Huniu certainly knew how to spend. Huniu's confinement was due in the second month of the new year. From the start of winter, her belly had become visible, and she liked to deliberately stick it out to show off her importance. Looking at her own stomach, she could barely be bothered to get off the kang. All the cooking was handed over to Xiao Fuzi, and naturally the leftovers and scraps went home with Xiao Fuzi for her brothers to eat. That alone cost quite a bit. Besides meals, she also had to have snacks. The more her belly showed, the more she felt she needed to eat well — she couldn't let her mouth go without. She not only bought little odds and ends at any moment, but also told Xiangzi to bring something back for her every day. Whatever Xiangzi earned, she spent. Her demands rose and fell with his income. Xiangzi couldn't say a word. When he'd been sick, he'd spent her money — so fair was fair, of course he had to spend on her now. The moment Xiangzi tightened the purse strings even slightly, she'd immediately fall ill. "Being pregnant is nine months of sickness — what do you know?" What she said was true enough. As the new year approached, she had even more ideas. Unable to move around herself, she sent Xiao Fuzi on errand after errand to buy things. She resented not being able to go out, yet pampered herself too much to go out, and staying in made her restless — so the only comfort was to have more things brought in to look at. She kept saying it wasn't for herself but out of concern for Xiangzi: "You've been grinding away all year — aren't you going to eat something decent? Since your illness you've never fully gotten your strength back. If you don't eat something at year's end, are you going to wait until you're shriveled up like a dried-out bedbug?" Xiangzi couldn't argue back, and didn't know how to. But once the food was cooked, she'd eat two or three big bowls of it herself. After eating, with no exercise, she'd feel bloated, and clutching her belly she'd insist it was the baby acting up.

After the new year, she absolutely refused to let Xiangzi go out in the evenings — she might go into labor at any moment, and she was frightened. It was only now that she began to reckon with her real age. Though she still wouldn't say it outright, she no longer told him, "I'm only 'a little' older than you." All her fussing left Xiangzi bewildered. The continuation of life was nothing more than having children, and Xiangzi couldn't help feeling a little glad about it — even if he had no need for a child at all, that simple yet mysterious word "Dad" that would one day attach itself to him was enough to make even the hardest heart close its eyes and think. No matter how you thought about it, that word always moved you. Xiangzi, clumsy and plain, couldn't think of anything good or admirable about himself. But when he thought of that wondrous word, he suddenly felt his own worth — as if it didn't matter that he had nothing, as long as there was a child, life wouldn't be empty. At the same time, he wanted to provide for Huniu and care for her as best he could. She was no longer just "one" person now. Even if she was very annoying, in this matter she deserved full credit. But no matter how much credit she deserved, her way of carrying on was truly unbearable. She changed her mind every other moment, seeing ghosts and spirits everywhere, making a constant fuss, while Xiangzi had to go out and earn money, needed his rest — even if the money could be spent freely, he still needed one solid night's sleep to go back out and haul away the next day. She wouldn't let him go out at night, and she wouldn't let him sleep properly either. He had no say in anything, drifting through the days in a daze, not knowing what to do. Sometimes he felt joy, sometimes anxiety, sometimes frustration. Sometimes he felt ashamed of his joy, sometimes he consoled himself in his anxiety, sometimes he found joy in his frustration. Emotions spun in circles inside him, leaving the simplest of men completely disoriented. Once, he actually pulled a fare past the destination, forgetting where the passenger had hired him to go.

Around the Lantern Festival, Huniu decided to have Xiangzi go fetch the midwife,She could no longer hold on. The midwife came and told her it wasn't time yet, explaining the signs to watch for when labor was near. She endured two more days, then started making a fuss again. The midwife was called back — still not time. She wept and screamed that she wanted to die, that she couldn't bear the torment any longer. Xiangzi was helpless, and to show he was doing his best, he gave in to her demands and stopped pulling the rickshaw for the time being.

The commotion dragged on until the end of the month, and even Xiangzi could see that the time had truly come — she no longer looked like herself. The midwife came again and gave Xiangzi a quiet warning: there was likely going to be a difficult birth. Huniu's age, a first pregnancy, a lack of exercise, and a very large baby from all the rich food she'd eaten during the pregnancy — taken together, a smooth delivery was out of the question. On top of that, she'd never been examined by a doctor, and the baby's position had never been corrected. The midwife had no skill for that kind of procedure, but she could say: I'm afraid it's going to be a breech birth.

In this courtyard tenement, the birth of a child and the death of a mother were things people had long since come to speak of in the same breath. But Huniu faced more danger than the others. The other women kept working and moving right up until the day of delivery, and since they didn't eat enough, their babies weren't very large, which actually made delivery easier. Their danger lay in the neglect that came after birth — Huniu was the exact opposite. Her advantages were her undoing.

Xiangzi, Xiaofuzi, and the midwife kept watch over her for three days and three nights. She called upon every god and spirit she knew and made countless vows — none of it did any good. In the end her voice had gone hoarse, and she could only murmur softly, "Mama, oh Mama." The midwife was out of options, everyone was out of options, and it was Huniu herself who came up with the idea: she told Xiangzi to go outside Desheng Gate to fetch Auntie Chen the Second — a renowned toad spirit medium. Auntie Chen wouldn't come for less than five yuan. Huniu pulled out her last seven or eight yuan: "Good Xiangzi, go quickly! Don't worry about the money! Once I'm better, I'll be a good wife to you! Go, go quickly!"

Auntie Chen arrived with her "spirit boy" — a yellow-faced, heavyset man of about forty — just around lamp-lighting time. She was about fifty, dressed in a blue silk jacket, wearing red pomegranate flowers in her hair along with a full set of gold-plated jewelry. Her eyes were fixed in a glassy stare. She washed her hands upon entering, then lit the incense. She kowtowed first herself, then sat behind the incense table, staring blankly at the rising smoke. Suddenly her whole body shuddered in one great tremor, her head drooped, her eyes closed, and she stayed motionless for a long while. The room was so quiet you could have heard a pin drop, and even Huniu bit down and dared not make a sound. Slowly, Auntie Chen raised her head and nodded, looking around at everyone. The "spirit boy" tugged at Xiangzi, urging him to kowtow quickly. Xiangzi wasn't sure whether he believed in spirits or not, but felt that kowtowing couldn't hurt. In a daze, he lost count of how many times he bowed. When he stood up, he stared at those glassy "spirit" eyes and the bright red glow of the burned-down incense, breathing in the smoke, his heart clinging to a vague hope that something good might come from all this ceremony. He stood there in a stupor, cold sweat forming in his palms.

The toad spirit medium spoke in a slow, aged voice, with a slight stammer: "No, no, no need to worry! Draw a, a, a charm to hasten the birth!"

The "spirit boy" quickly handed over a sheet of yellow cotton paper, and the medium made several passes over the incense flame, then used her own saliva to draw on the paper.

When the charm was finished, she stammered out a few more words: the general meaning was that Huniu owed a debt to this child from a past life, and so she had to suffer a little. Xiangzi was too dazed to make much sense of it, but he felt a creeping fear.

Auntie Chen let out a long, wide yawn, closed her eyes and sat stiffly for a moment, then opened them as if waking from a deep dream. The "spirit boy" quickly relayed the medium's words. She seemed quite pleased: "The spirit was in a good mood today — very talkative!" She then instructed Xiangzi on how to get Huniu to swallow the charm, and gave him a pill to be taken along with it.

Auntie Chen was eager to stay and see the charm take effect, so Xiangzi had to prepare some food for her. He handed this task to Xiaofuzi. Xiaofuzi went out and bought hot sesame paste flatbreads and braised pork knuckle in sauce. Auntie Chen still complained there was no wine.

Huniu swallowed the charm. Auntie Chen and the "spirit boy" finished eating. Huniu was still writhing and crying out. After more than an hour of this, her eyes began to slowly roll back. Auntie Chen still had an idea — calm as ever, she told Xiangzi to kneel before a tall stick of incense. Xiangzi had little faith left in Auntie Chen. But since he'd already spent five yuan, he might as well try all her methods. Since he wasn't going to beat her, he'd just go along with what she said — on the off chance it worked.

He knelt rigidly before the tall incense, not knowing which spirit he was praying to, but trying to feel sincere. Watching the flame flicker, he pretended to see shapes in it and prayed silently. The incense burned lower and lower, dark streaks appearing in the flame. He bowed his head down, hands on the ground, drifting in and out of drowsiness — he hadn't slept properly in two or three days. His neck suddenly went limp and he startled awake. Looking up, he saw the incense had burned down to almost nothing. Without thinking about whether it was time to stand, he slowly pushed himself up from the ground, his legs half numb.

Auntie Chen and the "spirit boy" had already slipped away.

Xiangzi had no time to be angry. He rushed over to Huniu, knowing things had reached the worst possible point. Huniu was only gasping in great gulps of air, no longer able to make a sound. The midwife told him to try to get her to a hospital — she had exhausted every method she knew.

Something inside Xiangzi seemed to crack open all at once. He opened his mouth wide and wept. Xiaofuzi was also in tears, but being in the position of someone there to help, she kept a slightly clearer head. "Brother Xiang! Don't cry yet! Let me go to the hospital and ask?"

Without waiting to see if Xiangzi had heard, she wiped her tears and ran out.

She was gone for about an hour. When she ran back, she was too out of breath to speak. Leaning against the table, she coughed dryly for a long while before she could get the words out: a doctor's house call was ten yuan, just for a look, not including delivery. Delivery was twenty yuan. If it was a difficult birth, she'd have to go to the hospital, and that would cost several tens of yuan. "Brother Xiang! What do you want to do?!" Xiangzi had no way out. All he could do was wait — whoever was meant to die would die.

Ignorance and cruelty were among the things on display here. The reasons for that ignorance, that cruelty, lay elsewhere.

At midnight, Huniu breathed her last, taking a dead child with her.

[1]

 Averaging out evenly.

[2]

 Top of the line, first-rate.

Twenty

Xiangzi's rickshaw was sold!

Money flowed like water — his hands could no longer hold it back. The dead still had to be carried out, and even having a death notice written cost money.

Xiangzi moved about like a man who had lost his mind, watching everyone bustle around him, doing nothing but reaching into his pocket for money. His eyes were a frightening red, crusted with yellowish-white discharge at the corners. His ears felt deaf. He stumbled along after the others in a daze, not knowing what he himself was doing.

Walking behind Huniu's coffin out through the city gate, he came back to himself a little, though his mind still couldn't take in anything. There was no funeral procession to speak of — just Xiangzi and Xiaofuzi's two younger brothers, each holding a thin stack of paper money, scattering it along the road for the ghosts that might block the way.

Stumbling along in a stupor, Xiangzi watched the pallbearers bury the coffin. He didn't cry. His mind felt like it was on fire, burning his tears dry — he wanted to cry but couldn't. He stared blankly, barely aware of what was happening. It wasn't until the foreman came over to settle up that he thought to go home.

Xiaofuzi had already tidied the room. He came back and collapsed onto the kang, too exhausted to move. His eyes were dry and wouldn't close. He stared blankly at the ceiling with its water stains from old leaks. Unable to sleep, he sat up. He glanced around the room and couldn't bear to look. He didn't know what to do with himself. He went out and bought a pack of "Yellow Lion" cigarettes. Sitting on the edge of the kang, he lit one — he didn't even want to smoke. Staring at the thin curl of blue smoke rising from the tip, tears suddenly streamed down his face, one after another. He thought not only of Huniu but of everything. He'd been in the city for years, and this was what all his effort had come to — just this, just this. He couldn't even cry out loud. The rickshaw, the rickshaw — the rickshaw was his livelihood. He'd bought one, lost it. Bought another, sold it. Three times up, three times down, like a shadow he could never quite grasp, while he bore all that hardship and humiliation for nothing. Gone, all of it gone, even his wife gone. Huniu had been difficult, but without her, how could he call it a home? Everything in the room had been hers, yet she herself was buried outside the city walls. The more he thought, the more bitter he felt. Anger dried up his tears. He smoked that cigarette hard, forcing himself to smoke it even though he didn't want to. When it was finished, he held his head in his hands, his mouth and chest burning, wanting to let out a wild howl and spit out all the blood in his heart just to feel some relief.

At some point, Xiaofuzi came in and stood at the chopping block in the outer room, staring at him.

He looked up sharply and saw her, and the tears came rushing back. At that moment, even if he'd seen a dog, he would have cried. His heart was so full of grievance that he needed a living creature to release it to. He wanted to talk to her, to get a little sympathy. But there was too much to say, and his mouth wouldn't open.

"Brother Xiang!" She moved a little closer. "I've got everything tidied up."

He nodded, too overwhelmed to thank her. Courtesy in the midst of grief is hollow. "What are you going to do now?"

"Hm?" He seemed not to have understood at first, but then he did, and shook his head — he couldn't think about plans right now.

She took two more steps forward. Her face suddenly flushed, and she showed a few white teeth, but the words wouldn't come. Her life had forced her to forget shame long ago, yet when it came to something real and serious, she was still a woman with a true heart — a woman's heart is bound up, in large part, with her sense of shame. "I was thinking…" That was all she managed to say. She had so much to say, but the moment her face went red, it all scattered and she couldn't recall any of it.

True words are rare in this world, and a woman's blush says more than a great many of them. Even Xiangzi understood what she meant. In his eyes, she was the most beautiful woman alive — beautiful to the bone. Even if she were covered in sores, her flesh rotted away, she would still be beautiful to him. She was beautiful, she was young, she was strong-willed, she was hardworking and thrifty. If Xiangzi were to remarry, she would be the ideal person. He wasn't thinking about remarrying right away — he couldn't think about anything right now. But since she was willing, and since the pressure of life was forcing her to bring it up without delay, he seemed to have no way to refuse. She herself was such a good person, and she had helped him so much. He could only nod. He truly wanted to go over and hold her and cry his heart out, cry away all the grief, and then work hard together with her going forward. In her, he saw all the comfort a man could get from a woman, and all the comfort he deserved. He wasn't much of a talker, but with her, he felt like talking freely. With her listening, his words wouldn't be wasted. A nod from her, or a smile, was the most satisfying answer he could imagine — it made him feel he truly had a "home."

Just at that moment, Xiaofuzi's second younger brother came in: "Sister! Dad's here!"

She frowned. She had just pushed open the door when Erqiangzi was already in the courtyard. "What were you doing in Xiangzi's room?" Erqiangzi's eyes went wide, his feet tangling over each other as he lurched and swayed toward her: "You sell yourself and that's not enough — you're going to let Xiangzi have you for free? You shameless thing!"

Xiangzi heard his own name and rushed out, standing behind Xiaofuzi. "I'm talking to you, Xiangzi," Erqiangzi slurred, trying to puff out his chest but unable to even stand steady. "I'm talking to you, Xiangzi — do you call yourself a man? You can take advantage of anyone, but you'd take advantage of her? What kind of thing are you!"

Xiangzi didn't want to bully a drunk, but the frustration built up inside him made it impossible to control his anger. He stepped forward. Four red eyes locked onto each other, as if sparks might fly between them through the air. Xiangzi grabbed Erqiangzi by the shoulder and flung him away like a child, sending him sprawling far across the ground.

A guilty conscience, fueled by drink, turns to violence — Erqiangzi's drunkenness had been partly an act to begin with. The fall sobered him up halfway. He wanted to fight back, but knew he was no match for Xiangzi. Just walking away quietly didn't sit right either. He sat on the ground, unwilling to get up, yet unable to just stay there. His mind was a mess, so his mouth just ran: "What business is it of yours how I discipline my own children? You hit me? Your grandmother! Who do you think you are!"

Xiangzi didn't bother to answer back, just stood quietly waiting for him to make a move.

Xiaofuzi held back her tears, not knowing what to do. Trying to reason with her father was useless, but watching Xiangzi beat him didn't sit easy with her either. She searched her whole body and scraped together a dozen or so copper coins, which she handed to her brother. Her brother normally wouldn't dare go near their father, but seeing him knocked to the ground today gave him a little courage. "Here, take it and go."

Erqiangzi squinted and took the money, hauling himself up while muttering: "The lot of you, sons of bitches! Push the old man too far and I'll get a knife and slaughter the whole bunch of you!" Nearly at the gate, he shouted back: "Xiangzi! We've got a score to settle[1] — we'll meet outside!"

After Erqiangzi left, Xiangzi and Xiaofuzi went back inside together.

"I have no choice," she said to herself, just that one sentence — a summary of all her hardships, and carrying within it an endless hope: if Xiangzi was willing to marry her, she would have a way forward.

Xiangzi, after all that had just happened, found himself seeing many dark shadows gathering around her.He still liked her, but he couldn't take on the responsibility of supporting her two younger brothers and a drunken father. He didn't dare think that with Tigress dead he would be free; Tigress had her good points too, at least she had helped him a great deal financially. He didn't dare think about Xiao Fuzi eating off him, yet it was absolutely true that her whole family couldn't earn their own keep. Love or no love, the poor have to let money decide, and "romantics" are only born into wealthy families.

He began to pack up his things.

"You're moving out, aren't you?" Xiao Fuzi's lips had gone completely white.

"Moving out!" He steeled his heart. In a world without fairness, the poor rely on hardheartedness to hold on to their personal freedom — that tiny, tiny bit of freedom.

She gave him one look, then lowered her head and walked out. She felt no hatred, no anger, only despair.

Tigress's jewelry and better clothes had all gone into the coffin with her. What remained were only some worn-out garments, a few pieces of wooden furniture, and some pots, bowls, and ladles. Xiangzi picked out a few of the better clothes and set them aside; the rest, clothes and household items alike, he sold. He called in a junk peddler and sold everything for a flat price of a little over ten yuan. He was in a hurry to move out, in a hurry to be rid of all these things, so he had no patience to find several buyers and haggle slowly over prices. The peddler packed everything up and left, and the room held nothing but his own bedroll and those few set-aside clothes, lying on the bare kang. With the room completely empty, he felt somewhat relieved, as if he had shaken off many entanglements and could now go wherever he pleased. But before long, he found himself thinking about those things again. The table had been carried away, yet its legs had left marks on the floor — small mounds of fine dust pressed against the base of the wall, forming little square shapes. Looking at these impressions, he thought of the objects, thought of the people — all gone now, like a dream. No matter whether the things were good or bad, no matter whether the people were good or bad, without them, the heart had nowhere to rest. He sat down on the edge of the kang and pulled out another "Yellow Lion" cigarette.

Along with the cigarette, he pulled out a tattered bill. Half-consciously, he emptied out all his money; for the past two days he hadn't bothered to count it up. He pulled out a whole pile — silver coins, paper bills, copper coin notes, copper coins, a bit of everything. It was quite a pile, but when he counted it, it came to less than twenty yuan. Adding the ten-odd yuan from selling the things, his total wealth came to just over thirty yuan.

He set the money on the kang bricks and stared at it, not knowing whether to cry or laugh. The room held no people, no things — only himself and this pile of worn, grimy money. What was it all for?

He let out a long sigh, stuffed the money into his chest with resignation, then gathered up his bedroll and those few clothes and went to find Xiao Fuzi.

"Keep these clothes for yourself. Leave the bedroll here for now — I'll find a good rickshaw yard first, then come back for it." Not daring to look at Xiao Fuzi, he kept his head down and said all this in one breath.

She said nothing, only murmured a couple of sounds in reply.

Xiangzi found a rickshaw yard, came back for his bedroll, and saw that her eyes were swollen from crying. He didn't know what to say, but he managed to squeeze out these two sentences: "Wait for me. When I've made something of myself, I'll come back. I will, for sure."

She gave a small nod and said nothing.

Xiangzi rested only one day before going back to pulling his rickshaw as before. He no longer threw himself into the work with burning eagerness, but he didn't deliberately slack off either — he just drifted along day after day, indifferent but not resentful. After drifting like this for about a month, he felt a kind of calm inside. His face had filled out a little, but it no longer had that ruddy glow; his complexion was yellowish, not visibly robust, yet not showing any real weakness either. His eyes were bright but expressionless, always gleaming as if full of energy, yet as if seeing nothing at all. He was like a tree after a storm, standing quietly in the sunlight, not daring to stir. He had never been much of a talker, and now he was even less inclined to open his mouth. The weather had grown quite warm, and the willow branches were hung with tender leaves; sometimes he would park his rickshaw in the sun, head bowed, lips moving slightly in a murmur to himself, and sometimes he would tilt his face up to the sunlight and doze off for a bit. Unless he absolutely had to speak, he barely exchanged a word with anyone.

But he had taken up smoking. The moment he sat down on his rickshaw, his big hand would reach under the chest pad. Lighting a cigarette, he would draw and exhale very slowly, his eyes following the smoke rings upward, staring blankly, then giving a small nod, as if he had made out some meaning in them.

When he pulled his rickshaw, he still moved more nimbly than most rickshaw pullers, but he no longer pushed himself to the limit. At turns and corners, going up and down slopes, he was especially careful — almost excessively careful. If someone tried to race him, no matter how they taunted or provoked him, he would keep his head down without a word and go on at his own unhurried pace. He seemed to have seen through what pulling a rickshaw really meant, and no longer expected any glory or praise from it.

At the yard, though, he had made friends. Even if he didn't talk much, even silent geese like to fly in flocks. If he hadn't made friends, the loneliness would have been more than he could bear. Whenever he pulled out his cigarette case, he would pass it around to everyone in a circle. Sometimes when people saw only one cigarette left in his case and felt embarrassed to reach for it, he would say simply: "I'll buy more." When the others gambled, he no longer hung back on the sidelines as before — he would come over and watch, and sometimes put down a bet, winning or losing without caring, as if only to show everyone that he was one of them and understood that after days of hard work people needed to enjoy themselves. When they drank, he joined them too; he didn't drink much, but he would spend his own money to buy food and drink for everyone. Things he had once looked down on, he now found had some point to them — since his own path had led nowhere, he had no choice but to admit that others were doing things right. When friends had weddings or funerals, he had once not known how to show goodwill; now he would chip in forty copper coins, or go in on a group gift. Not only did he contribute money, he would go in person to pay his respects or offer congratulations, because he had come to understand that these occasions were not just a waste of money but involved human obligations that had to be fulfilled. At such times people truly wept or truly laughed — it wasn't empty commotion.

Those thirty-odd yuan, however, he didn't dare touch. He got a piece of white cloth and, with clumsy hands and a thick needle, sewed the money inside it, keeping it always against his skin. He had no desire to spend it, nor any desire to buy a rickshaw again — he simply kept it on him as a precaution. Who could know what disasters might come? Illness, unforeseen misfortune — any of it could strike at any time, and one had to be prepared. A person isn't made of iron — he had come to understand that.

Just before the Start of Autumn, he took on a monthly hire again. This time it was easier than any household job he had worked before; if it hadn't been, he wouldn't have taken it. He knew how to choose his work now — he would take a monthly hire only if it suited him; otherwise, pulling individual fares was perfectly fine too. He was no longer burning with eagerness to get into a wealthy household the way he used to be. He had learned that his body was something to be taken care of; a rickshaw puller who pushed himself to the limit — as he once had — would only lose his life without gaining anything in return. Experience teaches a person to be a little more careful with himself, because there is only one life.

This time his place of work was near the Yonghe Temple. His employer, surnamed Xia, was in his fifties, educated and well-mannered; at home he had a wife and twelve children. He had recently taken a concubine and, not daring to let his family know, had deliberately chosen a quiet location to set up a separate little household. In this small household near the Yonghe Temple lived only Mr. Xia and his new concubine; besides them there was a maidservant and a rickshaw puller — Xiangzi. Xiangzi liked this job very much. To start with the courtyard: there were only six rooms in all, three occupied by Mr. Xia, one used as a kitchen, and the remaining two as servants' quarters. The courtyard was very small; along the south wall grew a half-grown jujube tree with a dozen or so half-reddened jujubes hanging from its tips. When Xiangzi swept the yard, two or three strokes of the broom nearly covered it from one end to the other — very convenient. There were no flowers or plants to water; he thought about tidying up the jujube tree, but he knew how willful jujube trees were, growing crooked and twisted and refusing to be trained, so he left it alone.

There wasn't much other work either. Mr. Xia went to his office in the morning and didn't return until five in the afternoon, so Xiangzi only had to take him there and pick him up; once home, Mr. Xia never went out again, as if he were in hiding. The concubine went out more often, but she always came back around four o'clock to let Xiangzi go and fetch Mr. Xia — and once he was fetched home, Xiangzi's day was done. Besides, the places the concubine went were no more than the Dongdan Market and Zhongshan Park; after dropping her off, Xiangzi had long stretches of free time. All of this Xiangzi handled as if it were child's play.

Mr. Xia kept a very tight fist and wouldn't part with a single coin easily; coming and going, he kept his eyes straight ahead, as if there were no people and no things on the street. The concubine, on the other hand, was free-handed, going out to buy things every few days; if it was food and she didn't like it, she gave it to the servants; if it was household goods, when the time came to buy new ones she would first give the old ones to the servants, so she could go to Mr. Xia and ask for money. Mr. Xia's entire mission in life seemed to be to devote every ounce of his energy and every coin of his money to the concubine; beyond that, he had no life and no pleasures of his own. His money could only leave his hands through the concubine — he wouldn't spend it himself, let alone give it to others. It was said that his legal wife and twelve children lived in Baoding and sometimes went four or five months without receiving a single coin from him.

Xiangzi disliked Mr. Xia: he went about all day long hunched over, neck drawn in, slipping in and out like a thief, eyes fixed on his own toes, never making a sound, never spending money, never smiling — even sitting in the rickshaw he looked like a scrawny monkey. Yet on the rare occasions when he did say a word or two, he managed to say exactly the wrong thing, as if everyone else were a scoundrel and only he himself was a gentleman of culture and propriety. Xiangzi didn't like people like that. But he had learned to treat a job as just a job — as long as the monthly pay came in, why bother about anything else? Besides, the concubine was quite generous, often giving him food and other things. He told himself to let it go and just think of it as pulling a monkey around who had no sense of human feeling. As for the concubine, Xiangzi simply regarded her as a woman who would occasionally give him a little extra money and didn't feel any particular warmth toward her. She was far better-looking than Xiao Fuzi, and she was soaked in perfume and powder, wrapped in silks and satins — Xiao Fuzi couldn't compare. Yet despite her beauty and fine dress, for some reason every time he saw her he thought of Tigress; there was always something about her that resembled Tigress — not the clothes, not her looks, but some quality of manner or air that Xiangzi couldn't find the right words to describe. He only felt that she and Tigress were, to use the best words he could think of, the same kind. She was very young, twenty-two or twenty-three at most, yet she carried herself with a seasoned assurance, nothing like a newly married woman — just like Tigress, who had never had a young girl's shyness or gentleness. She had permed hair and wore high heels, and her clothes were cut to help her walk with a sharp, angular sway. Even Xiangzi could see that, for all her fashionable dress, she lacked the bearing of an ordinary respectable lady. Yet she didn't seem to have come from a brothel either. He couldn't figure her out. He only felt she was somewhat frightening, the way Tigress had been frightening. But Tigress hadn't been this young, hadn't been this beautiful; so Xiangzi feared her even more, as if she carried within her all the fierceness and venom of every woman he had ever known. He simply didn't dare look her in the eye.

After spending some time there, he grew even more afraid of her. When he took Mr. Xia out, Xiangzi never saw him spend money on anything; yet Mr. Xia did occasionally go shopping — to a large pharmacy to buy medicine. Xiangzi didn't know what medicine he bought; but whenever he came home with it, the couple seemed especially pleased, and even the barely-breathing Mr. Xia appeared noticeably livelier. After two or three lively days, Mr. Xia would go back to barely breathing again, and his back would be bent even lower — very much like a live fish bought off the street, which thrashes about energetically when first put in water, then soon settles down again. The moment Xiangzi saw Mr. Xia sitting in the rickshaw looking like a dead man, he knew it was time for another trip to the pharmacy. He didn't like Mr. Xia, yet every time they went to the pharmacy he couldn't help feeling sorry for this old scrawny monkey. And whenever Mr. Xia came home carrying his packet of medicine, Xiangzi would think of Tigress, and feel a misery he couldn't quite name. He didn't want to hold a grudge against the dead, but looking at himself and then at Mr. Xia, he couldn't help resenting her; whatever else might be said, his body was no longer as strong as it used to be, and Tigress bore a large share of the blame.

He thought seriously about quitting. But to quit over something so beside the point seemed unreasonable; puffing on a "Yellow Lion," he muttered to himself, "Why stick your nose in other people's business?"

[1] To set aside a matter for the time being and deal with it later.

[2] A jointly agreed-upon gift.Twenty-One

When chrysanthemums were going out of season, the concubine bought four pots of flowers, and when the maidservant Yangma accidentally dropped one of them, a quarrel broke out between them. Yangma was from the countryside and fundamentally regarded flowers and plants as nothing of importance; still, since she had broken someone else's belongings, however unimportant they might be, it was her own carelessness, so she didn't dare say a word. But when the concubine kept on making a scene, hurling one coarse, wild insult after another without stopping, Yangma's temper could no longer be held down, and she answered back. A country woman in a rage doesn't know how to measure her words — she shook loose everything she had and let out the most unrestrained curses she knew. The concubine stamped her feet and screamed for a while,Tell Yang Ma to pack up and get out immediately.

Xiangzi never came over to mediate. His mouth wasn't made for breaking up fights, let alone a quarrel between two women. When he heard Yang Ma call Xia Taitai a back-alley whore, ridden by a thousand men and groped by ten thousand, he knew Yang Ma's time here was finished. At the same time he could see that if Yang Ma was done, he'd be done too. Xia Taitai probably wouldn't keep a servant who knew her history. After Yang Ma left, he waited to be dismissed. He figured the moment a new maid arrived would be the moment he'd have to pack his bags. But he didn't lose any sleep over it. Experience had made him cool about starting and leaving jobs. There was no point getting emotional about it.

After Yang Ma left, though, Xia Taitai was surprisingly warm toward Xiangzi. With no maid around, she had to go to the kitchen herself. She gave Xiangzi money and sent him out to buy groceries. When he came back, she told him what needed peeling and what needed washing. He peeled and washed while she cut the meat and cooked the rice, chatting with him as she worked. She wore a pink thermal top over a pair of dark trousers, her feet slipped into a pair of white satin embroidered slippers. Xiangzi kept his head down and worked with clumsy hands, not daring to look at her, yet wanting to. Her perfume kept flooding into his nostrils, as if telling him he had no choice but to look, the way a fragrant flower lures bees and butterflies.

Xiangzi knew the dangers of women, and he knew their pleasures. One Huniu was enough to make any man both fear women and be unable to give them up. And Xia Taitai was far beyond anything Huniu could compare to. Xiangzi couldn't help stealing a couple of glances at her. If she was as fearsome as Huniu, she still had qualities that made her a hundred times more desirable.

Two years ago, Xiangzi would never have dared look at her like that. Now he didn't much care. For one thing, having already been tempted by a woman, he had no way to restrain himself anymore. For another, he had gradually fallen into the rut of a "rickshaw puller." What other rickshaw pullers considered right, he now considered right too. Since his own efforts and self-discipline had failed, everyone else's way of doing things must have its reasons. He had no choice but to be a "rickshaw puller," whether he liked it or not. Being different from the crowd got you nowhere. So if grabbing a little advantage was what ordinary suffering people considered fair, why should Xiangzi pass one up? He looked at this woman twice. Yes, she was just a woman. If she was willing, Xiangzi had no way to refuse. He didn't quite believe she could be that shameless, but what if she was? If she didn't make a move, Xiangzi certainly wouldn't. But if she gave him the slightest sign, he had no plan. Had she already given him a sign? Why else would she let Yang Ma go without immediately hiring someone new, and ask only Xiangzi to help with the cooking? Why wear so much perfume just to go to the kitchen? Xiangzi didn't dare decide anything, didn't dare hope for anything, yet somewhere inside he faintly wanted to decide something, wanted some kind of hope. He felt like he was having an unreal but pleasant dream, knowing it was a dream yet wanting to keep dreaming it. Some heat in life was forcing him to admit he was no good, and hidden inside that worthlessness was the greatest pleasure — or perhaps the greatest misery. Who cared!

A flicker of hope stirred some courage. A little courage fanned a great heat. A fire burned in his chest. There was nothing low about this. Neither he nor she was low. Desire was equal!

A flicker of fear woke his reason. A little reason doused the fire in his heart. He almost wanted to run away right then. There was only misery down this road. Anyone who went down it was bound to make a fool of themselves!

Hope one moment, fear the next — his heart shook like a man with malaria. It was worse than when he'd been with Huniu. Back then he'd known nothing, like a young bee blundering into a spider's web for the first time. Now he knew how careful he should be, and he knew how bold he could be. He was inexplicably sliding forward, yet clearly and fully aware of the danger of falling!

He didn't look down on this concubine, this secret prostitute, this beauty. She was everything, and she was nothing. If there was any way he could justify himself, he thought, it was that old scrawny monkey of a Xia Xiansheng who was truly despicable and deserved whatever bad came to him. With a husband like that, nothing she did was wrong. With an employer like that, he — Xiangzi — could do whatever he liked without it mattering. His courage grew.

But she paid no attention to whether he'd looked at her or not. When the meal was ready, she ate alone in the kitchen. When she finished, she called out to Xiangzi: "Go eat. When you're done, wash up the dishes. This afternoon when you go pick up the master, pick up tonight's groceries on the way back so we don't have to go out again. Tomorrow's Sunday, the master will be home and I'm going out to find a maid. Do you know anyone you could recommend? Good maids are so hard to find! Alright, go eat before it gets cold!"

She said it all very naturally and matter-of-factly. That pink thermal top suddenly seemed — in Xiangzi's eyes — somehow much plainer. He felt a pang of disappointment, and from disappointment came shame. He saw himself clearly: he was no longer a man of ambition. Not just lacking ambition — he was a bad person. He shoveled down two bowls of rice in a daze and felt utterly hollow. He washed the dishes, went to his room, and smoked one "Yellow Lion" cigarette after another without counting.

That afternoon when he went to pick up Xia Xiansheng, he felt, for no clear reason, an intense hatred for this old scrawny monkey. He really wanted to pull the rickshaw at a wild pace and then let go of the shafts and send the old man flying. Only then did he understand something he'd once witnessed at a wealthy household — the master's third concubine and the eldest young master had been carrying on, and when the master found out, the young master had nearly poisoned him to death. Xiangzi had always thought the young master was just young and foolish. Now he finally understood why that master had deserved to die. Still, he had no desire to kill anyone. He just found Xia Xiansheng irritating and hateful, with no way to punish him. He deliberately bounced the rickshaw shafts up and down to give the old monkey a good shaking. The old monkey said nothing. Xiangzi felt worse for it. He had never done anything like this before, and even when he had some reason to, he couldn't forgive himself. The regret left him indifferent to everything. Why go looking for trouble? Whatever you said, he was a rickshaw puller. Do the job well and that was that. What was the use of thinking about anything else?

His heart grew calm. He put the whole fruitless episode out of his mind. When it occasionally came back to him, he found it almost funny.

The next day, Xia Taitai went out to look for a maid. She came back shortly with someone on a trial basis. Xiangzi gave up any lingering thoughts, but no matter how he turned it over in his mind, something felt off.

Monday after lunch, Xia Taitai sent the trial maid away — too dirty, she said. Then she told Xiangzi to go buy a jin of chestnuts.

He came back with a jin of roasted chestnuts and called out from outside the door.

"Bring them in," she said from inside.

Xiangzi went in. She was at the mirror powdering her face, still wearing that pink thermal top, but with a light green skirt now. She saw Xiangzi come in through the mirror and turned around quickly, giving him a smile. In that smile Xiangzi suddenly saw Huniu — a young and beautiful Huniu. He froze where he stood. His boldness, his hope, his fear, his caution — all of it was gone. All that remained was a breath of heat, large or small, holding his whole body up. That breath carried him forward when he moved forward, and back when he stepped back. He had no will of his own anymore.

The next evening, he hauled his bedroll back to the depot.

The thing he had always feared most and found most shameful, he now blurted out to everyone with a laugh — he couldn't piss properly!

Everyone rushed to tell him what medicine to buy or which doctor to see. Nobody found it shameful. They all sympathized and offered advice, and with slightly flushed faces told their own stories with a certain pride. Several of the younger ones had paid money for this kind of illness. Several of the middle-aged ones had picked it up for free. Several who had done monthly hire work had experiences of varying degrees but the same nature. Several who had done monthly hire work had no personal experience of this particular thing, but had other stories about their employers that were well worth telling. This little ailment of Xiangzi's opened everyone up, and they spoke to him like old friends. He himself forgot his shame, though he took no pride in it either. He bore the illness with calm acceptance, not much different from catching a chill or getting a touch of heatstroke. When the pain came, he felt a slight regret. When it eased up, he'd think back on the sweetness of it. Either way, he wasn't anxious. Life's experience had taught him to take his own existence lightly. What was the use of being anxious?

This remedy, that folk cure — it cost him over ten yuan altogether, and the illness never fully cleared up. Carelessly, whenever he thought it was better he'd stop taking the medicine. When the weather turned overcast or the seasons changed, his joints would ache, and he'd take some medicine for it, or just tough it out, never treating it as anything serious. If fate had already ground him down to the bottom, what did his body matter? Once he made peace with that, even a fly finds pleasure on a dung heap — so what about a full-grown living person?

After the illness passed, he was almost a different person. He was still as tall, but that upright spirit was gone. He let his shoulders droop forward deliberately, his mouth hung slack, a cigarette dangling from his lips. Sometimes he tucked a half-smoked cigarette behind his ear — not for convenience, but just to look the part.He still didn't talk much, but when he did open his mouth he'd try to squeeze out something clever, and even if it didn't come out quite right, he had that attitude about him. With a loose heart, his posture and manner became careless and slouching.

Still, compared to most rickshaw pullers, he couldn't be called truly bad. When he sat alone and thought about who he used to be, he still wanted to be better, still couldn't accept just sliding away like this. Even if striving had been useless, throwing himself away wasn't exactly smart either. At times like these, he'd think again about buying a rickshaw. He'd already spent over ten of his thirty-odd yuan on medicine — money wasted. But with twenty yuan as a base, he still had more hope than those who had nothing at all. Thinking this way, he'd feel a strong urge to throw away the half-pack of "Yellow Lions" he hadn't finished, swear off cigarettes and alcohol, and clench his teeth to save money. From saving money he'd think about buying a rickshaw, and from buying a rickshaw he'd think about Xiao Fuzi. He felt he'd let her down. Since leaving the compound, he'd never once gone to see her, and not only had he not made anything of himself, he'd gone and picked up a filthy disease.

But whenever he was with friends, he'd smoke as usual, drink when the chance came, and forget Xiao Fuzi completely. Among friends, he never took the lead in anything, but when others wanted to do something, he couldn't very well not join in. The day's hard work and a belly full of grievances could only be temporarily forgotten by talking and joking with them. The comfort of the moment drove away any lofty ambitions. He wanted to enjoy himself for a while, then sleep a dead sleep until the world went dark. Who wouldn't want that, when life was so dull, so painful, so hopeless? The festering sores of life could only be numbed for a while by the poison of cigarettes, alcohol, and women. Fight poison with poison — everyone knew that one day the poison would reach your heart. But who had a better idea to replace it?!

The less effort he made, the more he pitied himself. He used to be afraid of nothing. Now he looked for ease and comfort. Wind or rain, he wouldn't take the rickshaw out. A little soreness in his body and he'd rest for two or three days at a stretch. Self-pity made him selfish. He wouldn't lend a single coin of his money to anyone, keeping it all to cushion himself on bad-weather days when he couldn't work. He'd share cigarettes and drinks, but never lend money. He was more delicate and pitiable than anyone else. The more idle he was, the lazier he became. With nothing to do he'd get bored, so he constantly needed some entertainment or something good to eat. Whenever he thought he shouldn't be wasting his time and money like this, there was always a ready-made line in his heart, forged from so much experience: "Didn't I used to try hard? What good did it do me, even a little?" Nobody could argue against that line. Nobody could explain it away. So who could stop Xiangzi from going downhill?!

Laziness makes a person short-tempered. Xiangzi now knew how to glare at people. At the depot managers, at the police, at anyone — he decided he was done being meekly accommodating. When he'd worked hard and given his all, he'd never been treated fairly. Now he knew how precious his sweat was. He'd shed as little of it as possible. If anyone tried to take advantage of him, they could forget it. He'd set the rickshaw down wherever he felt like it, too lazy to move, never mind whether it was a proper place to stop. If a policeman came to interfere, he'd use his mouth but not his body, dragging things out as long as he could. When he finally saw he had no choice but to move, his mouth would be even busier — he'd curse. If the policeman wouldn't take the cursing, then a fight was fine too. Xiangzi knew he was strong. He'd beat the policeman first and go to jail after — no loss in that. In a fight, he felt his own strength and ability again. Driving his force into another man's flesh, he saw a kind of light, as if the sun had suddenly grown brighter. Saving up his strength to be ready for a fight — he'd never even thought of such a thing before. Now it had actually become a fact, and it was something that could make his heart feel good for a while. When he thought about it, how funny it all was!

Never mind a bare-handed policeman — even the cars that bullied their way through the streets didn't scare him. When a car came head-on, churning up all the dust on the road, Xiangzi wouldn't budge, no matter how the horn blared, no matter how impatient the passengers were. The car had no choice but to slow down. Once it slowed, Xiangzi moved aside, having swallowed far less dust. He used the same trick when a car came from behind. He'd worked it out: cars didn't dare hurt people, so why move aside early,How could it not stir up all the dust? The police were there specifically to clear the way for cars, afraid they wouldn't go fast enough or kick up enough dust. Xiangzi wasn't a cop, so he wasn't about to let cars run wild. In the eyes of the police, Xiangzi was a top-grade troublemaker, but they didn't dare mess with troublemakers. The laziness of the downtrodden is the natural result of effort gone to waste, and the defiance of the downtrodden carries a certain justice.

When it came to passengers, he showed absolutely no courtesy. He'd go exactly as far as agreed, not one step more. If they agreed on the hutong entrance, and then wanted him to go inside the hutong, forget it! The passenger would glare, and Xiangzi would glare back even harder. He knew how much those gentlemen in Western suits feared getting their clothes dirty, and he also knew how arrogant and stingy those gentlemen in Western suits mostly were. Fine, he was ready for that. If it came to a fight, he'd reach out and grab the sleeve of their fifty or sixty yuan suits and at least leave a big black handprint on them. After giving them that handprint, they'd still have to pay up. They knew how much strength was in that big hand, that one grip had already squeezed their thin little arms until they ached.
