He still ran at a decent pace, but he wouldn't push himself for nothing. If a passenger urged him on, his big feet would drag along the ground: "Faster? How much extra?" No pleasantries — what he was selling was blood and sweat. He no longer hoped they'd be generous enough to tip a little more. You get what you pay for, and things had to be settled upfront before he'd put in the extra effort.

As for the rickshaw, he no longer cared for it the way he once had. Since his desire to buy his own had cooled, he felt indifferent toward other people's rickshaws. A rickshaw was just a rickshaw. Pulling it meant earning enough for food and the rental fee, and that was the end of it. Not pulling it meant no rental fee to pay, so as long as he had enough money for one day's food, there was no need to take it out. That was the extent of the relationship between a man and his rickshaw. Of course, he still wouldn't deliberately damage someone else's vehicle, but he didn't go out of his way to protect it either. Sometimes another rickshaw puller would accidentally scrape it, and he wouldn't fly into a rage and argue with the man. He'd calmly pull it back to the depot, and if the damage called for fifty cents, he'd put out twenty and be done with it. If the depot owner wouldn't accept that, fine, there was always one last resort: a fight. If the depot owner wanted to fight, Xiangzi was game.

Experience is the fertilizer of life. Whatever experiences you have, that's what kind of person you become. You can't grow peonies in the desert. Xiangzi had fallen completely into the rut. He was no better than the other rickshaw pullers, and no worse. He was just a rickshaw puller through and through. This way, he felt more at ease than before, and others found him easier to get along with. Crows are all the same shade of black, and he had no wish to be the one with white feathers.

Winter came again. The yellow wind blowing in from the desert could freeze many people to death in a single night. Listening to the wind, Xiangzi buried his head under the covers and couldn't bring himself to get up. Only when the wind stopped its howling and wailing would he drag himself up, unable to decide whether to go out or take the day off. He was too lazy to grab the ice-cold rickshaw handles, and dreaded the wind that choked you and turned your stomach. The fierce wind died down at sunset, and only after four o'clock did it finally go still, with a faint reddish glow of evening light breaking through the murky yellow sky. He forced himself to pull together, hauled the rickshaw out, tucked his hands in his sleeves, leaned his chest against the top of the handles, and shuffled along listlessly, a half-smoked cigarette dangling from his lips. Before long it was dark. He wanted to pick up a couple of fares quickly and call it a night early. Too lazy to light the lamps, he waited until the police along the road had reminded him four or five times before finally lighting them.

In front of the Drum Tower, he snagged a fare under the lamplight and headed east. He didn't even take off his thick padded robe, just trotted along in a muddled way. He knew it looked bad, but so what if it looked bad. Would looking proper get him a few extra coins? This wasn't really pulling a rickshaw, it was just getting by. Sweat appeared on his forehead, yet he still wouldn't take off his long robe. If he could muddle through, he'd muddle through. Turning into a small hutong, a dog apparently took issue with the sight of someone pulling a rickshaw in a long robe and started chasing and barking at him. He stopped the rickshaw, turned around gripping his cloth duster, and chased after the dog with all his might. He drove the dog completely out of sight and then waited a while longer to see if it dared come back. It didn't dare. Xiangzi felt a bit better: "Damn it! Did you think I was scared of you?" "What kind of rickshaw puller are you supposed to be? Answer me!" the passenger in the rickshaw asked irritably.

Xiangzi's heart stirred — that voice sounded familiar. The hutong was very dark, and though the rickshaw lamp was lit, the light only reached downward, so he couldn't make out who was sitting in the rickshaw. The passenger wore a large hood, with mouth and nose wrapped in a thick scarf, only two eyes showing. Xiangzi was still trying to figure it out when the passenger spoke again: "Aren't you Xiangzi?"

Xiangzi understood. The person in the rickshaw was Liu Siye! A rush of heat surged through his whole body, and he didn't know what to do.

"Where is my daughter?"

"Dead!" Xiangzi stood there in a daze, not knowing whether it was himself or someone else who had said those two words.

"What? Dead?"

"Dead!"

"Falling into your hands, how could she not die?!"

Xiangzi suddenly found himself again: "Get down! Get down! You're too old to take a beating from me. Get down!"

Liu Siye's hands trembled as he climbed down. "Where is she buried? I'm asking you!" "None of your business!" Xiangzi picked up the rickshaw and walked away.

He had gone quite a distance when he looked back. The old man — like a large dark shadow — was still standing there.

[1]

To show off, to act clever.

Twenty-Two

Xiangzi forgot which way he was heading. He held his head high, gripped the cart handles tight, eyes bright, striding forward; just walking, with no thought of direction or destination. His heart felt light, his body felt free, as if all the bad luck he'd suffered since marrying Tigress had been spat right onto Liu Fourth's face. He forgot the cold, forgot about drumming up business, he just wanted to keep walking, as if somewhere ahead he would find his old self again — that Xiangzi who was unencumbered, pure, driven, always pushing forward. Thinking of that dark figure standing in the alley, that old man, it seemed there was nothing more to say — to defeat Liu Fourth was to defeat everything. He hadn't thrown a single punch or landed a single kick on the old bastard, yet the old man had lost his only family, while Xiangzi was free as a bird. Who could say this wasn't justice? If the old man didn't drop dead from rage, he'd be close enough to it! Liu Fourth had everything, Xiangzi had nothing; and yet now, Xiangzi could still pull his rickshaw with a smile, while the old man couldn't even find his daughter's grave! Fine then — no matter how many silver dollars you've got piled up, no matter how big your temper, you couldn't break this penniless wretch who scrapes by on two meals a day!

The more he thought about it, the happier he felt. He truly wanted to sing out loud, to let the whole world hear this victory song — Xiangzi is alive again, Xiangzi has won! The cold night air cut at his face, but he felt no chill, only exhilaration. The street lamps cast a cold light, yet inside Xiangzi's chest there was a warmth spreading through him, light everywhere, illuminating his future. He hadn't smoked in hours and had no desire to. From now on, no tobacco, no alcohol — Xiangzi was going to start fresh, work hard again, be his own man. Today he had beaten Liu Fourth, and he would beat him forever; Liu Fourth's curses would only drive Xiangzi to greater success, greater hope. One foul breath expelled, and from now on Xiangzi would breathe nothing but fresh air. He looked at his own hands and feet — wasn't he still young? Xiangzi would stay young forever. Let Tigress die, let Liu Fourth die, while Xiangzi lived on — happy, strong, alive. All the wicked would get what they deserved, all of them would die: the soldiers who stole his rickshaw, Mrs. Yang who starved her servants, Tigress who cheated and crushed him, Liu Fourth who looked down on him, Detective Sun who swindled him, Second Mistress Chen who made a fool of him, Mrs. Xia who led him astray… they would all die, and only the faithful Xiangzi would live, live forever! "But Xiangzi, you've got to work hard from now on!" he told himself. "Why wouldn't I work hard? I've got ambition, I've got strength, I'm young!" he answered himself. "With this weight off my chest, who can stop Xiangzi from building a life? Put what I've been through on anyone else and see if they'd be happy, see if they wouldn't slide downhill. All that's behind me now. Tomorrow you'll see a new Xiangzi, better than before, much better!"

Muttering to himself, he put more spring in his step, as if to prove his own words — no empty talk, I really do have this body. So what if he'd been sick, so what if he'd done things he wasn't proud of. Change your heart and your body follows, no question about it! He'd worked up a sweat and his mouth was dry, he wanted a drink of water, and only then did he realize he'd reached the Back Gate. He didn't bother with a teahouse — he left his cart at the parking area west of the city gate and waved over a small boy carrying a large clay pot and yellow sand-glazed bowls, and drank two bowls of tea that tasted like dishwater. Terrible stuff, but he told himself this was what he'd be drinking from now on, no more spending money on good tea and good food. Having made that decision, he went ahead and ate something too — something hard to swallow — as the start of his new life of hard work and endurance. He bought ten pan-fried buns stuffed with nothing but cabbage stalks, the dough thick and gritty on the outside. No matter how bad they tasted, he got them all down. When he finished, he wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. So where to now?

In his heart, there were only two people he could turn to, two people he could rely on. If he was going to work hard and make something of himself, he had to find them both — Little Fortunate and Mr. Cao. Mr. Cao was a good man, surely he would forgive him, help him, give him sound advice. Follow Mr. Cao's guidance, and with Little Fortunate's support alongside — him working outside, her managing inside — they would surely succeed, surely succeed, there was no doubt about it!

Who knew whether Mr. Cao had come back yet? Never mind, tomorrow he'd go to North Long Street to ask around; if he couldn't find out there, he'd go to the Zuo residence and ask. As long as he found Mr. Cao, everything else could be sorted out. All right — tonight he'd pull the rickshaw, and tomorrow he'd go find Mr. Cao. Once he found him, he'd go see Little Fortunate and tell her the good news: Xiangzi hadn't made it yet, but he was determined to, and they'd work toward it together, side by side!

With this plan settled, his eyes shone like a hawk's, sweeping in all directions. The moment he spotted a fare, he flew over before the price was even agreed and stripped off his heavy cotton jacket. When he ran, his legs weren't quite what they used to be, but a surge of heat carried his whole body and he gave it everything he had! Xiangzi was still Xiangzi — when Xiangzi ran flat out, nobody else could match him. Every fare he got, he took off like a shot, as if possessed. The sweat poured out of him freely. After each run he felt lighter, his legs regaining that spring, and he wanted to keep going, like a thoroughbred that hasn't run its fill, still pawing the ground after it's been pulled up. He didn't stop until one in the morning. Back at the depot, after paying the cart fee, he still had over ninety cents left.

He slept straight through till daylight. He rolled over and opened his eyes — the sun was already high. Rest after exhaustion is the sweetest pleasure. He got up and stretched, his joints cracking pleasantly, his stomach feeling completely hollow, desperate for something to eat. After eating, he smiled and told the depot owner, "Taking a day off, got things to do." He had it all figured out: one day off, get everything sorted, start the new life tomorrow. He headed straight for North Long Street, just to try his luck — what if Mr. Cao had already come back? As he walked, he prayed silently: please let Mr. Cao be back, don't let me come all this way for nothing! If the first thing goes wrong, everything goes wrong! Xiangzi has changed — surely heaven will look after him now?

At the gate of the Cao residence, his hand trembled as he pressed the bell. Waiting for someone to come open the door, his heart was pounding out of his chest. Standing before this familiar gate, he didn't stop to think about everything that had happened here before — he only hoped that when the door opened, he'd see a familiar face. He waited, beginning to wonder if perhaps no one was home, why else would it be so quiet, so quiet it was almost unsettling. Then suddenly there was a sound from inside and he actually flinched. The door opened, and mixed in with the sound of it was the most precious, most warm and dear "Oh!" — Gao Ma! "Xiangzi? Haven't seen you in ages! How did you get so thin?" Gao Ma herself had put on a little weight. "Is the master home?" Xiangzi couldn't think of anything else to say.

"He's home. Look at you — only care about the master, as if the rest of us don't exist! Didn't even ask how we're doing! You really are something — always just one track, like a carpenter with only one cut in him! Come in! Things going all right for you?" She walked inside as she talked.

"Hah! Not great!" Xiangzi gave a small smile.

"Um, sir," Gao Ma called outside the study, "Xiangzi's here!"

Mr. Cao was inside moving his narcissus to follow the sunlight. "Come in!" "Right, you go on in — we'll talk later. I'll go let the mistress know. We've all been thinking about you! A simple soul has his own kind of luck, don't you underestimate it!" Gao Ma chattered as she went inside.

Xiangzi entered the study. "Sir, I'm here!" He wanted to say something more polite but the words wouldn't come.

"Ah, Xiangzi!" Mr. Cao stood in the study wearing a short jacket, a gentle, clean smile on his face. "Sit down! So —" he thought for a moment. "We've been back for a while. Old Cheng told me you were at — right, the Renhe depot. Gao Ma even went to look for you once, but couldn't find you. Sit down! How have you been? Things going all right?"

Xiangzi's tears were about to fall. He never talked about his feelings with others, because his words were made of blood, buried deep in his heart. He steadied himself for a long moment, trying to turn that blood into simple words and let them flow out. Everything was there in his memory — think of it and it all came back. He needed to arrange it slowly, put it in order. He was about to tell a living history, and though he didn't understand its meaning, the string of hardships was real and clear.

Mr. Cao could see he was gathering his thoughts. He quietly sat down and waited.

Xiangzi sat with his head bowed for a long while, then suddenly looked up at Mr. Cao, as if to say that if he couldn't find someone to listen, he might as well not speak at all.

"Go ahead," said Mr. Cao with a nod.

Xiangzi began to tell his story, starting from how he had come to the city from the countryside. He hadn't meant to go back that far, but without saying it, his heart couldn't feel at ease and the story wouldn't feel complete. His memory was built from sweat and blood and pain — not something to toss out casually, and once he started he didn't want to cut off the beginning or the end. Every drop of sweat, every drop of blood had flowed from his life, so every single thing was worth telling.

Coming to the city, how he did hard labor, then how he switched to pulling a rickshaw. How he saved up and bought his own cart, how he lost it… all the way up to where things stood now. Even he found it strange — how could he talk for so long, and feel so good doing it? One thing after another, all of it seemed to want to leap out of his heart. The events seemed to find their own words, one sentence following the next, each one real, each one both dear and sad. His heart couldn't hold those things back, and his words had no way of stopping. Not a moment of hesitation or confusion — it was as if he wanted to pour out his whole heart in one breath. The more he talked the better he felt, forgetting himself, because he was already contained in those words, in every sentence — the driven, wronged, suffering, fallen him. When he finished, his forehead was damp with sweat, and his heart was empty, emptied out in the most comfortable way, like the hollow lightness after fainting and breaking into a cold sweat.

"Now you want me to give you some advice?" Mr. Cao asked.

Xiangzi nodded. Having said everything, he seemed reluctant to open his mouth again. "Still going to pull a rickshaw?"

Xiangzi nodded again. He didn't know how to do anything else.

"If you're still going to pull a rickshaw," Mr. Cao said slowly, "then there are really only two options. One is to save up and buy your own cart, the other is to rent one for now and keep working — isn't that right? Since you have no savings, borrowing money to buy a cart means paying interest, so what's the difference? Better to just rent for now. And it's still better to do a monthly hire — the work is steady and your meals and lodging are covered. I think you should come back here. I sold my cart to Mr. Zuo, so if you come, you'd need to rent one. What do you say?"

"That would be wonderful!" Xiangzi stood up. "You don't hold that business against me, sir?" "What business?"

"That time — when you and the mistress both ran to the Zuo residence!"

"Oh!" Mr. Cao laughed. "Who remembers that! I panicked a bit back then. The mistress and I went to Shanghai for a few months, but it really wasn't necessary. Mr. Zuo had already sorted it out, and that Ruan Ming has even become an official now, treats me well enough. You probably didn't know any of that. Forget it — I don't think about it at all. Let's get back to our business: that Little Fortunate you mentioned just now — what's to be done about her?"

"I have no idea!"

"Let me think: if you marry her and rent a room outside, it still doesn't add up — rent, coal, lamp oil, heating, it all costs money, not enough. If she comes with you to work, how likely is it that things line up so neatly — you pulling a rickshaw, her as a maidservant — not easy to arrange! That's a tricky one." Mr. Cao shook his head. "Don't take this the wrong way — is she really reliable?" Xiangzi's face flushed red, and after struggling for a long moment he managed to say, "She had no choice but to do what she did. I'd stake my life on it — she's a good person! She…" His thoughts churned inside him: many different feelings had knotted into one, then suddenly threatened to split apart and burst out all at once. He had no more words.

"Well, in that case," Mr. Cao said hesitantly, "the only way might be if I can make room for both of you here. You'd have one room to yourself, and the two of you would share one room — the housing problem could be solved. The question is whether she can do washing and household work. If she can manage some tasks, she could help Gao Ma. The mistress is expecting a baby before long, and Gao Ma is stretched a bit thin on her own. As for her — she'd eat here at my expense, but I wouldn't pay her wages. What do you think?"

"That would be wonderful!" Xiangzi smiled with genuine simplicity.

"However, I can't decide this entirely on my own — I'll need to discuss it with the mistress!"

"Of course! If the mistress isn't sure, I'll bring her over so the mistress can meet her!" "That works too," Mr. Cao laughed, not having expected Xiangzi to be so thoughtful. "Let's do this — I'll mention it to the mistress first, and you bring her over in a few days. Once the mistress gives her approval, we'll call it settled!"

"Then sir, I'll be going?" Xiangzi was eager to find Little Fortunate and share this good news — news he hadn't even dared to hope for.

Xiangzi left the Cao residence at around eleven o'clock, the most pleasant time of a winter day. It was an especially clear and beautiful day, the blue sky without a single cloud, sunlight streaming down through the crisp dry air, carrying a refreshing warmth. The crowing of roosters, the barking of dogs, and the calls of street vendors all carried far, and across the street you could hear bright, clear sounds falling like the cry of a crane from the sky. All the rickshaws had their canvas hoods folded back, the brass fittings glinting gold. On the footpath, camels moved with slow, steady steps,Cars and trams raced through the center of the street, people and horses moved about on the ground, white doves flew overhead, and the whole old city was everywhere alive with motion within stillness — chaotic in a satisfying way, quiet in a satisfying way, a world of sound and a thousand kinds of life, all spread beneath a clear bright blue sky, with trees standing silently everywhere.

Xiangzi's heart wanted to leap out of his chest and fly straight up into the sky, to soar among the white doves! Everything had fallen into place: the job, the wages, Xiao Fuzi — all settled beautifully in just a few words, more than he could have ever imagined! Look at this sky, so clear and dry, just like the straightforward, open nature of northerners. When good things happen, even the weather turns fine — he felt he had never seen such a lovely winter day. To express his joy in a more tangible way, he bought a frozen persimmon and bit into it, filling his mouth with ice! The biting cold spread from his teeth slowly down through his chest, sending a shiver through his whole body. He finished it in a few bites, his tongue a little numb, his heart at ease. He strode off to find Xiao Fuzi. In his mind he could already see that crowded courtyard, that little room, and the person he loved — all he lacked was a pair of wings to carry him there in an instant. As long as he could see her, everything from before could be wiped clean, and a whole new world could begin. His urgency now surpassed even what he had felt going to see Mr. Cao. His relationship with Mr. Cao was one of friends and master and servant, each doing good by the other. She was more than a friend — she would give her whole life to him, and two people from hell would wipe away their tears, smile, and walk forward hand in hand. Mr. Cao's words could move him, but Xiao Fuzi could move him without saying a word. He had spoken honestly to Mr. Cao; he would say even more heartfelt things to Xiao Fuzi, things he could never say to anyone else. She, right now, was his life — without her, nothing else mattered. He could not just work for his own food and drink; he had to rescue her from that little room, and then live with her in a clean, warm place, as happy, dignified, and close as a pair of little birds! She could forget about Er Qiangzi, forget about her two younger brothers — she had to come and be with Xiangzi. Er Qiangzi was perfectly capable of earning his own keep, and those two brothers could manage together, pulling a rickshaw between them or finding some other work; but Xiangzi — he couldn't do without her. His body, his spirit, his livelihood — there wasn't a part of him that didn't need her. And she needed a man like him just as much.

The more he thought about it, the more eager and happy he felt; there were so many women in the world, but not one was as good or as right for him as Xiao Fuzi! He had been married, had had his secret encounters; he had known the beautiful and the plain, the old and the young; but none of them had ever taken hold of his heart — they were just women, not companions. True, she was not the pure and spotless girl he had once imagined, but it was precisely because of that that she was more pitiable, and more able to help him. A simple country girl might be perfectly innocent, but she would never have Xiao Fuzi's capability and good sense. Besides, what about himself? There were plenty of dark spots in his own heart too! So the two of them were just right for each other — neither one above the other, like a pair of cracked jars that could still hold water, perfectly matched to sit side by side.

No matter how he looked at it, this was the most fitting thing in the world. Having thought all this through, he turned to practical matters: first, draw a month's wages from Mr. Cao, buy her a padded robe, get her shoes and feet sorted out, then bring her to meet Mrs. Cao. Dressed in a new, plain long padded robe, clean from head to toe — with her looks, her age, her bearing, she would surely make a good impression, surely win Mrs. Cao's favor. No question about it!

By the time he arrived, he was drenched in sweat. Seeing that old broken gate felt like seeing a hometown he hadn't returned to in years — the broken gate, the crumbling walls, the few dry yellowed weeds on top of the gatehouse, all of it dear to him. He went through the gate and headed straight for Xiao Fuzi's room. He didn't bother to knock, didn't bother to call out — he grabbed the door and pulled it open. The moment it opened, he instinctively stepped back. A middle-aged woman was sitting on the kang, wrapped in a tattered quilt because there was no fire in the room. Xiangzi stood frozen in the doorway, and a voice came from inside: "What's the matter! Bringing news of a death? Why do you just barge into someone's room without a word?! Who are you looking for?" Xiangzi didn't feel like speaking. The sweat on his body suddenly went cold; he held onto the broken door, not yet daring to abandon all hope: "I'm looking for Xiao Fuzi!" "Don't know her! Next time you're looking for someone, ask first before you go pulling doors open! Xiao Fuzi, Da Fuzi — what do I know!"

He sat at the gate for a long while, his mind blank, forgetting what he had come for. Slowly a little came back to him — just a little, no bigger than Xiao Fuzi herself — and she walked back and forth through his mind like a paper figure on a revolving lantern, going round and round without purpose; he seemed to have forgotten what she meant to him. Gradually her image grew smaller, and his heart had a little more room to move. Only then did the pain set in.

When the outcome of something is still unknown, people always hope for the best. Xiangzi told himself that maybe Xiao Fuzi had just moved, that nothing worse had happened. It was his own fault — why hadn't he come to see her more often? Shame drives a person to act, to make up for their mistakes. Best to go and ask around. He went back into the courtyard and found an old neighbor to inquire with. He got no clear news. Still unwilling to give up hope, too anxious even to eat, he thought of going to find Er Qiangzi — or even the two brothers would do. Those three men were always out on the streets; they couldn't be that hard to find.

He asked everyone he met — at rickshaw stands, in teahouses, in courtyards — walking and asking all day long until his legs gave out, and still no news.

That evening he returned to the rickshaw yard, his body utterly exhausted, but still unable to let the matter go. After a whole day of disappointment, he no longer dared to hope for anything. People who suffer die easily, and people who die in suffering are easily forgotten. Could it be that Xiao Fuzi was already gone? Taking a step back — even if she hadn't died, it was possible that Er Qiangzi had sold her off again, to somewhere very far away; and that would be worse than death!

Tobacco and drink became his companions again. How could a man think without smoking? And how could he stop thinking without drinking himself senseless?

[1]

 Skin, not scorched.

[2]

 Gritty — the feeling of eating poorly made, impure dough that crunches like sand between the teeth.

Chapter 23

Xiangzi wandered the streets like a lost soul and ran into little Xiao Ma's grandfather. The old man was no longer pulling a rickshaw; his clothes were thinner and more ragged than before. He carried a willow stick over his shoulder, a large clay teapot hanging from the front and a worn wicker basket from the back, the basket holding some sesame flatbreads, fried dough sticks, and a large brick.

He still recognized Xiangzi.

As they talked, Xiangzi learned that little Xiao Ma had been dead for more than half a year. The old man had sold the broken-down rickshaw and now spent his days selling tea and flatbreads and pastries at the rickshaw stands. He was still as kind and warm as ever, but his back had bent considerably, his eyes watered in the wind, and his eyelids were perpetually red, as though he had just been crying.

Xiangzi drank a bowl of his tea and briefly told him a little of the grief in his heart. "You thought you could make it on your own?" the old man said, sizing up Xiangzi's words. "Who doesn't think that? But who ever actually makes it? Back in my day, I was strong, I had a good heart, I kept at it all these years — and look at me now! Strong body? Even a man made of iron can't escape the net that's been cast over us. Good heart? What good does it do! Good deeds rewarded, evil deeds punished — there's no such thing! When I was young, I was truly warm-hearted, treated other people's troubles as my own. Did it help? No! I even saved lives — people jumping into rivers, people hanging themselves — I saved them all. Was I rewarded? No! I'll tell you, I'll probably freeze to death one of these days. I've figured it out: for people who do hard labor, trying to make it on your own is harder than climbing to heaven. What can one person do on their own[1]? You've seen grasshoppers, haven't you? Alone, one can jump pretty far — but let a child catch it and tie a string to it, and it can't even fly. But when they swarm together in great masses, hmm, one swarm can strip a whole field of crops clean, and nobody can do a thing about it! Isn't that right? And me, with my good heart — I couldn't even keep my own little grandson alive. He got sick, I had no money for proper medicine, and I watched him die in my arms! Say no more, say no more about any of it! — Tea here! Who wants a hot bowl?"

Xiangzi truly understood now: Liu Si, Mrs. Yang, Detective Sun — none of them would suffer any punishment just because he cursed them; and he himself would gain nothing just because he had tried hard. On his own, relying only on himself — just as the old man said — he was nothing but a grasshopper with a string tied to it. What good were wings?

He had no desire at all to go to the Cao household anymore. If he went there, he would have to keep striving, and what was the point of striving? He might as well drift along carelessly: when there was nothing to eat, pull the rickshaw out; when he had enough for the day, rest for the day, and let tomorrow take care of itself. This was not just one way of doing things — it was the only way. Saving money, buying a rickshaw, only to have it taken away by someone else — what was the point? Why not enjoy what pleasures there were to be had?

Besides, if he had found Xiao Fuzi, he would still have needed to keep working — not for himself, but for her. Since he couldn't find her, just like this old man who had lost his grandson, who was he working for? He told the old man about Xiao Fuzi too; he had come to think of him as a true friend.

"Who wants a hot bowl?" the old man called out first, then turned his mind to Xiangzi's situation: "The way I figure it, there are only two possibilities: either Er Qiangzi sold her to someone as a concubine, or she ended up in the white house. Hmm, most likely she's in the white house! Here's why — Xiao Fuzi, from what you just told me, has been married before, so it's not easy for someone to want her; men looking to buy a concubine want someone untouched. So there's probably an eight-in-ten chance she's in the white house. I'm nearly sixty, I've seen plenty: if a strong young rickshaw puller doesn't show up at the stand for a day or two, go look for him — either he's landed a monthly hire, or he's flat on his back in the white house; if a rickshaw man's daughter or wife suddenly disappears, seven or eight times out of ten she's ended up there too. We sell our sweat, our women sell their flesh — I understand, I know! Go look for her there. Not hoping she's really there, but still — Tea here! Who wants a hot bowl?!" Xiangzi ran all the way to outside Xizhimen.

The moment he passed through the outer gate, he felt the openness of the space. Bare trees stood along the roadside with not a single bird on their branches. Gray trees, gray earth, gray houses — all standing silently beneath a grayish-yellow sky; looking across this expanse of gray, he could see the desolate western hills in the distance. North of the railway tracks lay a stretch of woodland, with a few low buildings beyond the trees. Xiangzi reckoned this must be the white house. He looked at the woods — not a stir; looking further north, he could make out some low-lying wetland beyond the zoo, uneven ground with only a few withered cattails and broken reeds remaining. There was no one outside the small buildings, no movement at all. Everything near and far was so quiet that he wondered whether this was really the notorious white house. He steeled himself and walked toward the buildings. Straw curtains hung over all the doorways — newly hung ones, still yellowish and slightly glossy. He had heard people talk about this place: in summer, the women here would sit bare-backed outside their rooms, calling out to passersby. Those who came to visit would sing brothel songs[2] from a distance, showing they were no strangers to the place. Why was it so quiet now? Could it be that no business was done here in winter?

He was still puzzling over this when the straw curtain on one of the end rooms stirred, and a woman's head appeared. Xiangzi gave a start — at first glance, the face looked very much like Huniu's. He thought to himself: "I come looking for Xiao Fuzi, and if I find Huniu instead, that would really be something out of a nightmare!"

"Come in, you big silly!" the head spoke, but the voice was nothing like Huniu's — hoarse and raspy, much like the old quack medicine seller he had often heard at Tianqiao, hoarse yet urgent.

There was nothing in the room but the woman and a small kang. The kang had no mat, but there was a small fire burning inside it, and the smell was stifling and foul. An old quilt lay on the kang, its edges as greasy and dark as the bricks of the kang itself. The woman was around forty, her hair disheveled, her face unwashed. She wore a pair of lined trousers on the bottom and a small blue cotton jacket on top, unbuttoned. Xiangzi had to bow his head low just to get through the door, and the moment he stepped inside she threw her arms around him. The little cotton jacket was already open, and from her chest hung a pair of extraordinarily long and large breasts.

Xiangzi sat down on the edge of the kang, since standing left no room to straighten his neck. He was actually glad to have run into her — he had often heard people mention that the white house had a woman called "White Flour Sack," and this must be her. The nickname came from those two large breasts. Xiangzi got straight to the point and asked if she had seen a Xiao Fuzi; she didn't know. Xiangzi described what Xiao Fuzi looked like, and the woman thought for a moment: "Yes, yes, there was someone like that! Young, always showing a few white teeth when she smiled — yes, we all called her Little Tender Meat here."

"Which room is she in?" Xiangzi's eyes suddenly widened with a fierce light. "Her? She's long gone!" "White Flour Sack" pointed outside. "Hanged herself in the woods!"

"What?"

"Little Tender Meat was well-liked after she came here. But she couldn't quite take it — she was very frail. One evening, at lamp-lighting time — I remember it clearly, because I was sitting at the door with two or three of the other women. Ah, it was just at that moment that a customer came and went straight to her room; she didn't like sitting at the door with us — she even got beaten for it when she first arrived,Later she got a name for herself, and everyone left her alone in her room — at least those who came to see her never went to anyone else. After about the time it takes to finish a meal, the client left and headed straight for that grove of trees. We didn't notice anything, and nobody went into her room to check on her. It wasn't until Old Chagan went to collect payment from her that they found a man lying in the room, stark naked, sleeping like a log. Turns out he'd been drunk. Little Tenderness had stripped the client of his clothes, put them on herself, and run. She was a clever one. If it hadn't been dark, she couldn't have escaped even if her life depended on it. In the dark, dressed as a man, she fooled everyone. Old Chagan immediately sent people out to search everywhere — hmph, they went into the grove, and there she was, hanging. When they cut her down, she was already gone, but her tongue hadn't stuck out much, and her face didn't look bad — she was still pretty even in death! It's been several months now, and the grove has been perfectly quiet at night — she hasn't come out to haunt anyone. How kind of her!…" Xiangzi didn't wait for her to finish. He staggered out. He walked to a burial ground, a square plot planted with pine trees, with a dozen or so grave mounds among them. The sunlight was already faint, and inside the pine grove it was even dimmer. He sat down on the ground, which was covered with dry grass and pine pollen. There was no sound at all, only a few mountain magpies in the trees crying out in long, mournful calls. This couldn't possibly be Xiaofuzi's grave — he knew that — yet his tears fell in streams, one after another. There was nothing left, not even Xiaofuzi — she too had gone into the earth. He had been a fighter, Xiaofuzi had been a fighter, and now all he had left were useless tears, while she had become a hanged ghost. A mat, buried in the wasteland where the destitute dead were thrown — that was the end of a lifetime of struggle.

Back at the rickshaw yard, he fell into a stupor and slept for two days. He had no intention of going to the Cao household anymore — there was no need to even send word. Mr. Cao couldn't save Xiangzi's life. After sleeping for two days, he pulled his rickshaw out again. His mind was a complete blank. He no longer thought about anything, no longer hoped for anything. He only came out to suffer for the sake of his stomach — once his stomach was full, he went to sleep. What was there to think about? What was there to hope for? Watching a bony, angular dog waiting beside a sweet potato cart for scraps of skin and roots, he understood that he himself was just like that dog — his whole day's movement was just to scrounge for sweet potato skins and roots to eat. Getting by was everything. There was no need to think about anything else.

Humanity had lifted itself out of the animal kingdom, yet even now people drove their own kind back into it. Xiangzi was still in that city of culture, but he had turned into a beast. It was not his fault in the slightest. He had stopped thinking, so even if he killed someone, he bore no responsibility. He no longer had hope. He just drifted downward in a daze, sinking into a bottomless pit. He ate, he drank, he whored, he gambled, he was lazy, he was cunning — because he had lost his heart; his heart had been torn out by others. All that remained was that tall frame of flesh, waiting to rot, destined for the wasteland where the destitute dead were thrown.

Winter passed, and the spring sunshine was nature's gift of clothing to everyone. He rolled up his padded clothes and sold them all. He wanted to eat well, drink well — no need to keep winter clothes, and no need whatsoever to plan on seeing another winter. Be happy today, and die tomorrow! What did winter matter! If he was unlucky enough to still be alive when winter came, he'd deal with it then. Before, whenever he thought ahead, he thought about his whole life. Now he only cared about the present. Experience had taught him that tomorrow was just a continuation of today, that tomorrow inherited today's grievances. Selling his padded clothes made him feel wonderfully free — what couldn't you do with cash in hand? Why hold onto them and wait for a winter that could choke you to death with a single gust of wind?

Gradually, it wasn't just clothes — he wanted to sell everything, anything he didn't need right away he got rid of immediately. He loved watching his possessions turn into money that he spent himself. Once he spent it, it couldn't fall into anyone else's hands — that was the safest way. Sell things, and buy again when needed. If there was no money to buy, then simply do without. Not washing his face, not brushing his teeth — none of that really mattered. It saved money and saved trouble. Who was he keeping up appearances for? Better to wear ragged clothes and have flatbread rolled with braised meat in your belly — that was real! With good things inside your stomach, even if you died you'd have some substance to you, not like a rat that starved to death. Xiangzi — that proud, dignified Xiangzi — had become a thin, filthy low-class rickshaw puller. His face, his body, his clothes — he washed none of them. Sometimes he went more than a month without a haircut.

He stopped caring about his rickshaw too. New or old didn't matter — as long as the rental fee was low, it was fine. When he picked up a fare, the moment there was the slightest advantage to be had, he'd dump the passenger halfway. If the passenger objected, he'd glare back, and if it came to a fight, spending two days at the police station was nothing. Pulling the rickshaw alone, he walked very slowly — he begrudged his own sweat. But when running in a pack with other rickshaws, if he was in the mood, he'd sprint for a stretch, just to leave the others behind. In those moments he was also very good at causing trouble — cutting off other rickshaws, deliberately making sharp turns, blocking the rickshaw behind him, suddenly shoving the one in front — he could do it all. Before, he had believed that pulling a rickshaw meant carrying a human life, and one moment of carelessness could mean someone's death. Now he deliberately wanted to cause harm. What did it matter if someone got thrown off — everyone deserved to die. He had reverted to his old silence. Without a word, he ate, he drank, he made trouble.

Words are how people exchange opinions and convey feelings. He had no opinions, no hopes — what was the point of talking? Except for haggling over fares, he kept his mouth shut from morning to night. His mouth seemed to exist solely for eating, drinking tea, and smoking. Even when drunk he made no sound — he would go sit in some out-of-the-way place and weep. Almost every time he got drunk he would go to the grove where Xiaofuzi had hanged herself and shed his tears. After crying, he would go stay at the white house. When he sobered up, his money was gone and his body had caught a disease. He had no regrets. If there were times when he did feel regret, it was regret that he had ever tried so hard, been so careful, been so honest. Whatever deserved to be regretted was all in the past. Now there was nothing left to regret.

Now, whatever way he could get an advantage, that was what he did. Bumming an extra cigarette off someone, passing a counterfeit coin when buying something, eating a few extra pieces of pickled vegetables with his soybean milk, putting in a little less effort on a fare while squeezing out one or two extra coins — all of it gave him satisfaction. He gained an advantage, someone else took a loss — yes, this was a kind of revenge. Slowly he expanded on this. He learned to borrow money from friends with no intention of paying it back. If pressed, he could turn shameless. At first, nobody suspected him at all — everyone knew him as a man of dignity and integrity, so the moment he opened his mouth, he got the loan. He exploited this remnant of his reputation to borrow money everywhere. Borrowing felt like picking up something for free, and whatever he borrowed he spent right away. When people came to collect, he would put on a pitiful expression and beg for more time. If that didn't work, he would go borrow another 20 cents, pay back 15 cents of the debt, and spend the remaining 5 cents on wine first. Before long, he couldn't borrow a single coin from anyone, and he began to swindle money. Every household he had ever worked for, he went to visit — whether the master or the servants, he would spin a tale of lies and cheat a few coins out of them. If there was no money, he would beg for some old clothes, and the moment he had the clothes they too became money, and the money immediately became tobacco and wine. He kept his head down scheming, thinking up tricks — a good scheme could bring in more money than a whole day of pulling a rickshaw, with less effort and more income. He felt it was an excellent deal. He even went to find Gao Ma at the Cao household. He waited at a distance for Gao Ma to come out shopping, and the moment he saw her, he was at her side in almost a single step, calling out to her in the most winning tone.

"Oh! You scared me to death! I thought — who could it be — Xiangzi! What happened to you?" Gao Ma's eyes went wide as saucers, as if she were looking at some strange creature.

"Don't even ask!" Xiangzi lowered his head.

"Didn't you and the master work everything out? How come you just disappeared without a word? I even asked Old Cheng about you — he said he hadn't seen you. Where on earth did you go? The master and mistress have both been worried!"

"I was terribly sick, nearly died! Put in a word for me with the master — help me out for now, and once I'm better I'll come back to work!" Xiangzi delivered the story he had long since prepared — simply, movingly.

"The master isn't home. Would you like to come in and see the mistress?"

"No need! Look at the state of me! Just put in a word for me!"

Gao Ma brought out two yuan for him. "The mistress is giving you this. She says to get some medicine quickly!"

"Yes! Thank the mistress for me!" Xiangzi took the money, already calculating in his mind where to spend it. The moment Gao Ma turned away, he headed for Tianqiao and had himself a full day of fun.

Slowly he made the rounds of all the households, then made a second round — but this time it was far less effective. He could see that this approach couldn't be relied on for long. He needed another idea, something easier than pulling a rickshaw. Before, his one and only hope had been the rickshaw. Now he despised it. Of course he couldn't completely cut ties with the rickshaw all at once, but as long as he had some way to scrape through three meals, he refused to touch the handlebars. His body was lazy, but his ears were sharp. The moment he caught wind of something, he'd be there ahead of everyone else. Citizens' delegations, petition groups — anything where someone was paying, he'd do it. 30 cents, 20 cents — he was happy to spend a day waving a flag and wandering with the crowd. He felt that no matter what, it beat pulling a rickshaw. The pay wasn't much, but at least it didn't take any real effort. Flag in hand, head down, cigarette dangling from his lips, a half-smile on his face, he walked along with everyone else without a word. When it was absolutely necessary to shout something, he would open his mouth wide and make no sound at all — he was protective of his voice. He had no desire to exert himself in anything, because in the past he had spent his strength and gotten not the slightest benefit from it. In these flag-waving, slogan-shouting moments, if any danger arose, he was the first to run, and he ran fast. His life could be ruined by his own hands — he would never sacrifice anything for anyone else. Those who strive for themselves also know how to destroy themselves — these are the two ends of individualism.

[1] Bèng'r, meaning skill, prospect, future.

[2] Yáo diào, a popular tune sung in brothels.

Remember to take a break when you're tired~

Public account: Gude Maoning Li

Study resource sharing 24

It was again the season for pilgrims to climb the mountain and burn incense, and the weather had turned blazing hot.

Vendors selling paper fans seemed to have all crawled out of nowhere at once, carrying their boxes, the string bells on the boxes jangling to catch people's attention. Along the roadside, green apricots were already being sold by the pile, cherries gleamed a dazzling red, clusters of golden bees settled on basins of rose dates, glass powder lay in large porcelain basins giving off a milky glow, the stalls selling pea jelly and cold starch noodles were kept spotlessly neat, displaying condiments of every color. People had changed into light, bright-patterned single-layer clothes, and the streets were suddenly full of color, like so many long rainbows scattered down among the people. Street cleaners worked harder than ever, constantly splashing water on the roads, yet the fine dust still swirled up into the air, making people irritable. And yet among the fine dust there were those long, trailing willow branches and the nimble, restless swallows, which made people feel a certain freshness all the same. Weather that left you not knowing quite how to feel — everyone let out long, lazy yawns, tired yet somehow content.

Yangge troupes, lion dancers, road-clearing processions, five-tiger-staff performers, and all manner of other groups made their way up the mountain one after another. Beating gongs and drums, carrying trunks and boxes, waving apricot-yellow banners, one group following another, they stirred up an unusual excitement throughout the whole city, leaving people with a feeling both distant and intimate, leaving behind sounds and dust in the air. Those going to the festival and those watching it all felt a certain warmth, devotion, and excitement. The festivity of troubled times comes from superstition, and the only comfort for the foolish is self-deception. These colors, these sounds, the clear clouds filling the sky, the dust covering every street — they gave people spirit, gave them something to do: those who climbed the mountain climbed, those who strolled the temple fair strolled, those who went to see flowers went to see flowers… and at the very least one could stand by the roadside and watch the excitement, and murmur a few words of prayer.

With the heat like this, it seemed to wake the ancient capital from its spring dream. There were places to go everywhere, and everyone thought of something to do. The warmth drove flowers, plants, fruit trees, and human pleasures all upward together. The green willows and fresh reeds of the North and South Lakes drew young people with harmonicas, men and women setting small boats loose under the willow shade or drifting among the tender lotus, blowing love songs with their lips while their eyes exchanged kisses. The peonies and tree peonies in the parks invited poets and refined gentlemen to stroll slowly back and forth, fanning themselves with prized paper fans. When their legs grew tired, they would sit before the red walls and beneath the green pines, drinking a few cups of clear tea just sufficient to stir a pleasant melancholy, stealing glances at the well-bred young ladies and celebrated beauties passing by. Even the places that had always been quiet were visited by the warm breeze and sunny days, which brought tourists just as they brought butterflies. The peonies of Chongxiao Temple, the green reeds of Taoranting, the mulberry groves and rice paddies of the Natural History Museum — all drew voices and the shadows of parasols. Even the Temple of Heaven, the Confucius Temple, and the Yonghe Lama Temple had a faint stir of liveliness amid their solemnity. Those who loved to travel and the students went to the Western Hills, to the hot springs, to the Summer Palace — to travel, to roam freely, to collect specimens, to scrawl words on rocks. Even the poor had places to go: Huguo Temple, Longfu Temple, White Pagoda Temple, the Earth God Temple, Flower Market — all were livelier than usual. All kinds of potted flowers were displayed in vivid color along the roadsides, and for one or two copper coins you could bring "beauty" home. At the soybean milk stalls, the pickled vegetables gleamed as brilliantly as a great flower, with bright red chili peppers arranged at the top.Eggs were cheap right now, and the deep-fried egg dumplings, golden and crispy on the outside with a soft tender center, made people's mouths water. Tianqiao was even more lively — newly built tea pavilions with canvas awnings stood one beside another, their white tablecloths and gaudy sing-song girls facing the old pines atop the Temple of Heaven wall across the way. The gongs and drums rang out for seven or eight hours straight, and the dry, crisp weather made them sound especially sharp and clear, stirring up people's hearts. Prostitutes found it easy to dress up — a single floral cotton dress was enough to look pretty and show off the curves of their bodies. Those who preferred peace and quiet also had their places to go: in front of Jishuitan, outside Wanshou Temple, the clay pits east of the city, the Baishi Bridge to the west — all good spots for fishing, where small fish would brush against the tender reeds and make them tremble. After fishing, the pig's head meat, stewed tofu, white spirits, and salted soybeans at a roadside teahouse could leave a person drunk and full; then, carrying a fishing rod and a catch of small fish, one could stroll along the willow-lined bank, treading through the last rays of the setting sun, and pass leisurely through the ancient city gate.

There was fun everywhere, noise everywhere, color and life everywhere. The sudden burst of early summer heat was like a magic charm, filling every corner of this old city with an enchanted power. It paid no mind to death, to disaster, to hardship — when its time came, it unleashed its force and lulled a million hearts into a trance, making them sing its praises as if in a dream. It was filthy, it was beautiful, it was old, it was full of life, it was chaotic, it was leisurely, it was lovable — it was the great Beijing of early summer.

It was precisely at this time of year that people longed for news — news entertaining enough to drive away boredom, news worth reading two or three times without growing tired of it, news you could go out and witness for yourself after putting down the paper. The days were so long and bright and clear!

And such news arrived. The trams had barely pulled out of the depot when the newsboys were already shrieking in their high-pitched voices, chasing after people in every direction: "News about Ruan Ming's execution, news about the nine o'clock parade through the streets!" One copper coin, one copper coin, then another — all snatched up by small dark hands. On the trams, in the shops, in the hands of passersby, every sheet of paper spoke of Ruan Ming: Ruan Ming's photograph, Ruan Ming's history, interviews with Ruan Ming, large characters and small, illustrations with captions, entire pages devoted to Ruan Ming. Ruan Ming was on the trams, in the eyes of pedestrians, on the lips of everyone in conversation — it seemed as though there was no one else left in the old city, only Ruan Ming. Ruan Ming was parading through the streets today, Ruan Ming was being shot today! Valuable news, ideal news — not only was his name on everyone's lips, but in a little while they could actually see him in person. Women rushed to get dressed up; old men headed out early, afraid their slow legs would leave them at the back of the crowd; even schoolchildren thought about skipping half a day of class to get a look. By half past eight, the streets were already packed with people — excited, hopeful, jostling, clamoring — waiting to see this living piece of news. Rickshaw pullers forgot to drum up business, shops fell into disorder, street vendors couldn't be bothered to call out their wares — everyone was waiting for the prison cart and Ruan Ming. This was a people whose history had seen Huang Chao, Zhang Xianzhong, and the Taiping Heavenly Kingdom — a people who knew how to be slaughtered and who loved to watch slaughter. Execution by gunshot seemed too simple; what they loved to hear about was death by a thousand cuts, decapitation, flaying, being buried alive — hearing such things gave them a pleasure like eating ice cream, a delight that made them shiver slightly. But this time, on top of the shooting, there was a parade through the streets, and they were almost grateful to whoever had come up with the idea, giving them the chance to see a half-dead man bound to a cart, a feast for their eyes. Even if they weren't the presiding official at the execution, it was close enough. These people had no sense of right or wrong, no understanding of good and evil, no ability to distinguish truth from falsehood — they clung tightly to a set of moral codes and liked to be called civilized people; yet they loved to watch their fellow human beings be cut to pieces, with the same cruelty and relish as a child slaughtering a small dog. If power were ever placed in their hands, any one of them could order a city massacre, pile up the breasts and feet of women into small mountains — that would be their idea of a grand achievement. Since they hadn't obtained that kind of power, they were content for now to watch more pigs and sheep and people being killed, to get a taste of it. And if even that was out of reach, they would curse a child with "a thousand cuts" and "ten thousand cuts" just to vent the venom in their hearts.

The sky was a brilliant, clear blue, the red sun hung high in the east, a few light breezes stirred the willow branches along the roadside. On the east sidewalk, a large patch of shade was packed with people — old and young, men and women, ugly and handsome, fat and thin, some dressed up in the latest fashions, some wearing nothing but a thin undershirt — all chatting and laughing, full of anticipation, craning their necks to the south or north every now and then. When one person craned their neck, everyone followed, and all their hearts beat a little faster at once. Like this, they pressed further and further forward, the crowd gradually spilling onto the edge of the road, forming a wall of flesh, with only a mass of heads of varying heights bobbing about. Police came out in squads to maintain order — they blocked, they shouted, and sometimes they grabbed a child who looked like a lump of mud and landed a couple of punches on them, drawing roars of laughter from the crowd. They waited, waited patiently, legs already aching from standing, yet unwilling to leave empty-handed. Those in front refused to move, and those newly arrived from behind kept pushing forward, sparking disputes — no hands were raised, just mouths going at it, trading insults, with everyone else cheering them on. Children grew impatient and got slapped by adults; pickpockets made their hauls while those who'd been robbed cursed at the top of their lungs. Noise, shouting, uproar all around — no one would budge, and the more people gathered, the less anyone would move, all united in their eagerness to see the half-dead prisoner.

Suddenly, everyone fell quiet. A squad of armed police appeared in the distance. "They're coming!" someone shouted. Immediately the crowd erupted in noise, and the whole mass of people surged forward like a machine, an inch at a time, then another inch. Coming! Coming! Every pair of eyes lit up, every mouth was saying something, a sea of voices, the smell of sweat filling the entire street — the people of this land of propriety and ritual, burning with passion to watch a man be killed.

Ruan Ming was a short, slight figure, hands bound behind his back, sitting in the cart like a sickly little monkey, head bowed, a white placard more than two feet long stuck behind him. The crowd noise rolled like ocean waves, each wave pushing the next, and everyone pursed their lips in disappointment: so this was the little monkey? This was all there was to it, so limp and dull? Head down, face deathly pale, not a single sound? Someone had an idea: "Hey fellas, let's give him a cheer!" And immediately, from all sides, came shouts of "Bravo!" — like cheering for a female performer on stage, contemptuous, malicious, hateful. Ruan Ming still made no sound, didn't even lift his head. Some people were genuinely frustrated, genuinely disgusted by such a spineless prisoner, and pushed to the edge of the road to spit at him. Ruan Ming still didn't move, showed no reaction whatsoever. The longer people watched, the less satisfying it was, yet the less willing they were to leave. What if he suddenly called out, "In twenty years I'll be a hero again"? What if he asked a wine shop for two jugs of spirits and a plate of braised meat? No one would move — they had to see what he would do in the end. The cart passed, and still they followed; he'd shown nothing so far, but who could say he wouldn't catch his breath at Danpailou and belt out a few lines from The Fourth Son Visits His Mother? Follow him! Some followed all the way to Tianqiao. Even though he never did anything to impress or satisfy them, they had watched him take the bullets with their own eyes — that, at least, made the trip worthwhile.

In the midst of all this excitement, Xiangzi walked alone with his head down, moving slowly along the base of the Deshengmen city wall. When he reached Jishuitan, he looked around in all directions. No one was there. He moved slowly, stepping as lightly as he could, toward the edge of the lake. Reaching the water's edge, he found an old tree, leaned his back against the trunk, and stood there for a while. Listening to make sure there were no voices nearby, he quietly sat down. A reed leaf stirred, or a small bird suddenly called out, and he would leap to his feet in alarm, sweat breaking out on his forehead. He listened, he looked — nothing stirred in any direction — and he slowly sat down again. This happened several times before he grew accustomed to the rustling of the reeds and the birdsong, and decided not to be startled anymore. He stared blankly at the water channel beyond the lake, where small fish with eyes bright as tiny pearls gathered and scattered, came and went; sometimes a fish would carry a piece of tender duckweed on its head, sometimes it would blow a few bubbles from its mouth. Along the edge of the channel, some tadpoles that had already grown legs held their bodies upright, swaying their large black heads. The water suddenly flowed faster, sweeping the small fish and tadpoles downstream, their tails tilting sideways as they went with the current, but the current also brought a new group struggling to hold their ground. A water scorpion darted past at great speed. The current gradually steadied, the small fish regrouped and opened their tiny mouths to nibble at a floating green leaf or a bit of grass. Larger fish lurked in the depths, occasionally breaking the surface with their backs before quickly turning and diving back down, leaving a swirl and a scattering of ripples on the water. A kingfisher skimmed the surface like an arrow, and all the fish, large and small, vanished — only duckweed remained on the water. Xiangzi stared at all of this blankly, as if he saw it and yet didn't see it. Absentmindedly he picked up a small stone and tossed it into the water, sending up a splash that scattered the duckweed, and he gave a violent start, so frightened he nearly jumped to his feet again.

After sitting for a long while, he stealthily reached one large dark hand to his waist and felt around. He nodded, his hand resting there; after a moment, he drew out a wad of banknotes, counted them, then tucked them back with great care.

His mind was entirely occupied with that money — how to spend it, how to keep others from finding out, how to enjoy it while staying safe. He was no longer thinking for himself; he had become an appendage of the money, subject to its every command.

The origins of this money had already determined where it would go. Money like this could not be spent openly. This money, and the person who held it, both feared the light of day. While everyone else was out in the streets watching Ruan Ming, Xiangzi hid at the quiet base of the city wall, trying to find an even quieter, darker place to go. He didn't dare walk through the busy streets anymore, because he had sold out Ruan Ming. Even sitting alone by the still water, leaning against the city wall where no one passed, he didn't dare raise his head — it was as though a ghost were always following him. Ruan Ming, fallen in a pool of blood at Tianqiao, lived on in Xiangzi's heart, lived on in the banknotes at his waist. He felt no regret — only fear, fear of that ghost that followed him everywhere, at every moment.

After Ruan Ming came to power, he had enjoyed quite a few of the things he had previously believed should be overthrown. Money can lead a person into corrupt company, cause them to abandon high ideals, and willingly descend into hell. He put on fine Western suits, went to brothels, gambled, and even took up opium. When his conscience stirred, he told himself it was the wicked society that had ensnared him, that it wasn't entirely his own fault; he admitted his behavior was wrong, but blamed it on the overwhelming temptations of society, which he had no power to resist. One thing led to another, and when his money ran short, he thought again of those radical ideas — not to rouse himself to act on them, but to use them to get more money. Turning ideas into cash was much like, back in his student days, trying to get a passing grade through his relationship with a teacher without doing any real work. A lazy person's ideas cannot coexist with integrity — everything that can be converted into money will sooner or later be sold. He accepted a stipend. Organizations eager to spread revolution could not afford to be too careful in selecting their fighters; anyone willing to join was welcomed as a comrade. But those on the payroll had to show some results, by whatever means — the organization wanted reports. Ruan Ming couldn't just take the money and do nothing. He got involved in organizing rickshaw pullers. As for Xiangzi — he was already an old hand at waving the flag and beating the drum, and so Ruan Ming came to know Xiangzi.

Ruan Ming sold his ideas for money; Xiangzi accepted ideas for money. Ruan Ming knew that when the moment came, he could sacrifice Xiangzi. Xiangzi had never made such a calculation, but when the moment came, that's exactly what he did — he sold out Ruan Ming. Those who work for money are vulnerable to the lure of more money; loyalty cannot be built on money. Ruan Ming believed in his own ideas and used the radicalism of those ideas to excuse all his own vile behavior. Xiangzi listened to what Ruan Ming said and found it very reasonable, but he also found Ruan Ming's lifestyle very enviable — "If I had more money, I'd enjoy myself for a few days too! Just like that Ruan fellow!" Money had degraded Ruan Ming's character; money had dazzled Xiangzi's eyes. He sold Ruan Ming for sixty yuan. Ruan Ming wanted the power of the masses; Xiangzi wanted more of the same pleasures Ruan Ming had enjoyed. Ruan Ming's blood was spilled on the stipend money; Xiangzi stuffed the banknotes into his waistband.

He sat there until the sun had shifted to the west and the reeds and willows on the lake were draped in golden-red light before Xiangzi finally stood up and walked westward along the base of the city wall. Cheating people out of money — he had grown used to that. Selling a person's life — this was the first time. And yet he had found what Ruan Ming said so very reasonable! The openness of the space along the city wall and the towering height of the wall itself made him more and more afraid with every step. When he spotted a few crows on a rubbish heap, he wanted to give them a wide berth, afraid of startling them into giving him a few ominous cries. Reaching the west side of the city wall, he quickened his pace and slipped out through Xizhimen like a dog that had stolen food. A place where someone could keep him company at night, numb him, take away his fear — that was the ideal place to go, and the white house was exactly that kind of place.

By autumn, Xiangzi's illness no longer allowed him to pull a rickshaw, and his reputation had fallen so low that no one would rent him one. He became a regular at cheap lodging houses. At night, two copper coins would buy him a place to lie down. During the day, he did whatever odd jobs would earn him enough for a bowl of porridge. He couldn't beg on the streets — a man of his size, and no one would feel charitable toward him. He didn't know how to mark up his body and go to temple fairs to beg for money, because he'd never been taught the tricks of disguising his sores into something pitiable enough to move people. As for theft, he didn't have the skills for that either — thieves had their own networks and connections. He could only earn his own keep by his own efforts, with no other support or help to rely on. He had struggled for himself, and for himself he had completed his own destruction. He waited to draw that final breath — a dead man still breathing, with individualism as his soul.This soul will rot away in the earth along with his body.

Ever since Beiping was designated a former capital, its grandeur, craftsmanship, food, language, and police officers… had slowly drifted outward, seeking people and wealth that commanded the same authority as the Son of Heaven. The Westernized Qingdao now had Beiping-style instant-boiled mutton; the bustling Tianjin could hear the low, mournful cry of "Hard-faced — steamed buns" in the dead of night; in Shanghai, Hankou, and Nanjing, there were police officers and runners who spoke the Beijing dialect and ate sesame paste flatbreads; jasmine tea traveled from south to north, passed through double-smoking in Beiping, then headed south again; even the pallbearers sometimes boarded trains to Tianjin or Nanjing to carry the coffins of high officials and nobles.

Beiping itself, however, was gradually losing its original grandeur. Pastry shops might still sell flower cakes past the ninth day of the ninth month, but vendors of sweet rice balls might pull out of the market come autumn; century-old shops two or three hundred years in business suddenly thought to hold anniversary celebrations, using the occasion to hand out flyers advertising deep discounts… Economic pressure drove the grandeur elsewhere to seek its fortunes, for dignity alone couldn't put food on the table. Even so, weddings and funerals on the whole still preserved the old rites and pomp — marriages and burials seemed, after all, to warrant attention and at least some degree of ceremony. The ceremonial staffs, musical instruments, bridal sedans, and official canopies for weddings and funerals were still something no other city could match. The pine cranes and pine lions used in funeral processions, the paper-crafted figures, sedans, and horses, the full ceremonial retinue for wedding processions, and the twenty-four-piece musical ensembles still cut an official and imposing figure on the streets, calling to mind the splendor and bearing of more peaceful times.

Xiangzi's livelihood depended largely on these surviving rites and customs. When there was a wedding, he carried flags and parasols for the family; when there was a funeral, he held flower wreaths and memorial banners. He felt no joy and shed no tears — he simply accompanied the family through the streets for a dozen or so copper coins. Dressed in the green uniform or blue robe provided by the pallbearer company or the bridal sedan shop, wearing an ill-fitting black hat, he could temporarily cover his tattered clothes and look a little more presentable. When a wealthy household held an event and required all hands to shave their heads and wear boots, he had the chance to make himself clean and tidy from head to toe for once. His venereal disease made it hard to walk, so it suited him just fine to carry a flag or a pair of memorial banners, shuffling slowly along the side of the road.

Yet even at this, he was no good hand. His golden days were behind him. Having failed to build a life for himself through rickshaw pulling, everything had gone the way of his faded hopes — amounting to nothing. A man of his size, and yet he would scramble to carry a single flying-tiger flag or a pair of short, narrow memorial banners, refusing to touch the heavier red parasols or "silence" placards. He would even compete and squabble with old men, children, and even women. He refused to take the slightest loss.

Carrying that little thing, he walked with his head down and his back hunched, a cigarette butt picked up off the road dangling from his lips, shuffling along listlessly. When everyone stopped, he might still be walking; when everyone had already moved on, he might still be standing there a moment longer. He seemed not to hear the gong signals giving orders. He never looked to see whether the spacing ahead and behind was even, or whether the ranks to the left and right were straight. He walked his own walk, head bowed, as if lost in a dream or pondering some profound truth. The gong-beaters in red and the ceremonial marshals waving silk flags hurled just about every foul word they knew at him: "You little bastard! I'm talking to you, Camel! What the hell, fall in line!" He seemed not to hear even that. The gong-beater came over and struck him with the gong mallet. He rolled his eyes and gazed around in a daze. No matter what the gong-beater said, he kept his eyes on the ground, looking to see if there was a cigarette butt worth picking up. The dignified, the proud, the dreaming, the self-serving, the individualistic, the strong, the great Xiangzi — no one knew how many funerals he had seen others through, nor when or where he himself would be buried: buried, this degenerate, selfish, hapless spawn of a sick society, this ghost at the dead end of individualism.
